\section{Flaws in standard base representations}
% OUTLINE 4.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section intro does:
% - Opens the chapter's argument at its first step: similarity is computed on a representation, so any flaw in the representation is carried into every similarity value.
% - So the representation is examined before the similarity measure; Section 4.3 then examines the measure itself.
% - Write a representation as a map $\phi: \mathcal{M} \to \mathbb{R}^{d}$ from molecules to $d$ features.
% - Every pairwise quantity in this chapter has the form $D(\phi(m_a), \phi(m_b))$, so it can be no better than $\phi$.
% - A representation can fail a task in two ways, and each subsection is an instance of one:
%   - It throws information away: two different molecules get the same vector, $\phi(m_a) = \phi(m_b)$ with $m_a \neq m_b$; or one feature stands for several unrelated substructures (hash collisions).
%   - It keeps information the task does not need: many of the $d$ features do not vary on the dataset, or vary without relation to $\mathbf{y}$.
% - One further problem concerns the reader, not the model: a feature from a learned embedding has no chemical meaning a chemist can check.
% - One observation is not a flaw of any single representation: different representations disagree about which molecules are close, so each one sees only part of the chemistry.
%
% Why standard representations have these flaws:
% - They are built once, for all of chemistry, before any dataset or task is known.
%   - ECFP hashes every circular substructure of any molecule into a fixed-length bit vector \citep{rogers2010}.
%   - Descriptor sets are fixed lists of computed properties chosen for general use \citep{Todeschini2009, Mauri2006}.
%   - Learned embeddings are trained on large unlabelled corpora, not on the property being predicted.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on pretraining corpora and objectives of molecular foundation-model embeddings (e.g. masked-SMILES pretraining) versus the downstream QSAR endpoint.
% - One dataset usually covers a small region of chemical space (often a few chemical series), so most of a general-purpose representation describes chemistry that is not in the dataset.
% - The features that matter differ from task to task; a fixed representation cannot know which ones they are.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how much of a general-purpose fingerprint's bit space a single QSAR dataset (congeneric series) actually occupies.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on task-agnostic versus task-specific molecular representations for QSAR (fixed fingerprints versus representations selected or learned per endpoint).
% - Some of these flaws are already stated in the literature, without counts:
%   - In common real-world molecular datasets the least frequent substructures usually appear in only a few compounds, often a single one \citep{Dablander2024}.
%   - The pooled vector of a graph neural network is "obscure real numbers" that cannot be interpreted directly by human experts \citep{Dablander2024}.
% - What this chapter adds: a count of each flaw on the thesis datasets, and a link from each flaw to what it does to similarity (Sections 4.3 and 4.4).
%
% How the evidence is gathered (method framing for the whole section):
% - Small molecules only in this chapter; hERG Ki, Caco-2, LogD and QM9 are the main datasets.
% - Representations for the other domains are described in Chapter 5, "Representations by domain".
% - One small, simple statistic per flaw, computed on the full feature matrix of each dataset.
% - Most of the statistics fit no model: they are properties of the representation on that dataset alone.
%   - Why it matters: Chapter 3 found that the representation governs clean performance and the model architecture governs robustness. A statistic that fits no model keeps the model out and isolates the representation.
% - Each count is a property of a representation on one dataset, not of the representation in general.
%   - State this once here so the subsections do not repeat it.
% - TODO(results): one sentence per flaw summarising its count across datasets, once the tables in 4.2.1 to 4.2.5 are final; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Preview of the five subsections (one line each in the text):
% - 4.2.1: most features do not vary on a given dataset (constant and near-constant features).
% - 4.2.2: hash collisions, where one fingerprint bit stands for several unrelated substructures.
% - 4.2.3: molecules the representation cannot tell apart (identical vectors for different molecules, often stereoisomers).
% - 4.2.4: learned embedding features cannot be read chemically.
% - 4.2.5: different representations carry different information (their pairwise distances disagree).
%
% Links to:
% - Chapter 2, "Molecular Representations in QSAR": each representation family is defined there; point back, do not re-define.
% - Chapter 2, "Molecular similarity and activity cliffs": the similarity assumption is defined there; this section gives representation-level reasons it can fail.
% - Section 4.3: a distance sums over all $d$ features, e.g. $D(\mathbf{x}_a, \mathbf{x}_b) = \sum_{j=1}^{d} |x_{aj} - x_{bj}|$, so the flaws here become flaws in the distance.
% - Section 4.5, "What a representation would need": each flaw here gives one requirement there.
%   - Vary on the dataset; tell the molecules apart; be readable; draw on more than one representation.
% - Chapter 5: the hybrid answers each flaw with one design choice.
%   - Non-varying and task-irrelevant features: within each representation $k$, only the $b_k$ features with the highest importance $s^{(k)}_j$ are kept, with $\sum_k b_k = B$.
%   - One representation's blind spots: the hybrid draws features from all $K$ representations, $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$, $|S_k| = b_k$.
%   - Readability: the blueprint records which features were chosen from which representation; this helps a chemist only where those features have chemical names.
% - Why it matters: if the flaws belong to the representation, no choice of similarity measure alone can fix them.
% - Why it matters: that is the case for changing the representation itself, which is the job of Chapter 5.
%
% DIAGRAM: one overview schematic for the section. Left: a molecule mapped to a long feature vector. Right: five small panels, one per subsection. (a) Most features constant across the dataset (a feature that is zero for every molecule). (b) Two different substructures hashed to the same bit. (c) Two stereoisomers mapped to the same vector. (d) An embedding feature labelled only by an index number. (e) Two representations ranking the same molecule pairs by distance in a different order.
% ---- END NOTES 4.2 ----


\subsection{Most features do not vary on a given dataset}
% OUTLINE 4.2.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What the section argues:
% - A general-purpose fingerprint such as ECFP4 folded to 2048 bits is designed to cover all of chemistry \citep{rogers2010}.
% - One dataset often comes from a few chemical series. On it, most of those bits hardly change between molecules.
% - Some bits are off in every molecule. A few are on in every molecule. Many more are on in only a handful of molecules.
% - The features that matter for a task are a small subset of the ones that vary.
% - Which subset matters depends on the task.
% - This section measures only how many features vary at all.
% - Whether the varying features relate to the task is the subject of "Different representations carry different information" and of Chapter 5.
% - Already in prose: nothing yet; the section holds only a placeholder.
%
% Definitions (one dataset, one representation k):
% - Feature matrix $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$. Feature $j$ is the column $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{:,j}$.
% - Share of the most common value: $p_j = \max_v \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathbb{1}[x_{ij} = v]$.
% - Constant feature: $p_j = 1$, meaning one value in every molecule.
% - Near-constant feature at threshold $\tau$: $p_j \ge \tau$.
% - Proposed threshold: $\tau = 0.95$, meaning one value covers at least 95% of the molecules.
% - For a binary bit, $f_j = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i x_{ij}$ is the fraction of molecules with the bit on.
% - Then $p_j = \max(f_j, 1-f_j)$. The bit is near-constant when $\min(f_j, 1-f_j) \le 1-\tau$.
% - Variance of a binary bit: $\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{x}_{:,j}) = f_j(1-f_j)$.
% - The maximum is $0.25$, at $f_j = 0.5$. A bit on in 1% of molecules has variance $0.01 \times 0.99 = 0.0099$.
% - Fraction of features that vary: $\rho_k(\tau) = \frac{1}{d_k}\,\big|\{\, j : p_j < \tau \,\}\big|$.
% - Standard practice: Kuhn and Johnson flag "near-zero variance" predictors with two numbers \citep{kuhn2013applied}.
%   - First number: count of the most common value divided by count of the second most common value.
%   - Second number: the number of distinct values as a percentage of $n$.
%   - A predictor is flagged when the first is large and the second is small.
%   - The widely used software default is a ratio above $95/5 = 19$ and fewer than 10% distinct values.
%   - For a binary bit the ratio is $p_j/(1-p_j)$, so a ratio of 19 is exactly $p_j = 0.95$.
%   - A binary bit has 2 distinct values, which is under 10% of $n$ whenever $n > 20$. So for bits their rule reduces to $p_j > 0.95$.
%   - TODO(lit): confirm the wording and page of the near-zero-variance rule in Kuhn and Johnson (2013), data pre-processing chapter, before quoting its cut-offs.
% - A "mostly zero" rule (at least 95% of values equal 0) is not the same as $p_j \ge 0.95$ for continuous descriptors. State which rule is used.
% - For bits the two rules agree only when the common value is 0.
%
% Planned statistic (not results):
% - For every molecular dataset and every base representation, count three things: $d_k$; the number of constant features; the number of features with $p_j \ge 0.95$.
% - Table: one row per dataset, one column per representation, each entry "constant / near-constant / $d_k$".
% - Count on the same feature matrices the full runs use.
% - Optional second count: repeat inside each training fold. The hybrid search sees only training molecules, and a feature that varies on the whole dataset can be constant on one fold's training set.
% - TODO(results): constant and near-constant feature counts per dataset and representation (ECFP4, atom pairs, topological torsions, RDKit path fingerprint, Mordred, physicochemical descriptors, learned embeddings); numbers come from the full runs.
% - Hypothesis to state as a hypothesis, not a finding: sparse hashed fingerprints (ECFP4, topological torsions) will have the most near-constant bits.
% - Second hypothesis: dense learned embeddings will have few or none.
% - DIAGRAM: one panel per dataset. x-axis: the features of each representation, sorted by the minority-value fraction $\min(f_j, 1-f_j)$. y-axis: that fraction, on a log scale. Horizontal line at 0.05 marks the near-constant cut. One line per representation.
% - DIAGRAM (alternative): stacked bar per representation, showing constant, near-constant and varying features as shares of $d_k$, one facet per dataset.
%
% Why it matters for distances:
% - Tanimoto: $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{|\mathbf{a}\wedge\mathbf{b}|}{|\mathbf{a}\vee\mathbf{b}|}$.
%   - A bit that is always off adds nothing to either count, so it has no effect.
%   - A bit that is always on adds 1 to both numerator and denominator for every pair.
%   - That pushes every similarity toward 1 and narrows the range of similarities.
% - Euclidean or Manhattan distance on raw features: a constant feature adds 0 to every pairwise distance, so it has no effect.
% - Near-constant features distort distances once features are standardised.
%   - Standardised value of a binary bit: $z = \frac{x - f_j}{\sqrt{f_j(1-f_j)}}$.
%   - The rare molecules get $|z| = \sqrt{(1-f_j)/f_j}$. For $f_j = 0.01$ this is about 9.95.
%   - Squared Euclidean contribution between a molecule with the bit on and one with it off: $\frac{1}{f_j(1-f_j)}$.
%   - This is about 101 for $f_j = 0.01$, against 4 for a bit with $f_j = 0.5$.
%   - So one rare bit can outweigh about 25 common bits in the distance, whether or not it relates to the task.
% - The hybrid's own distances standardise each feature on the training molecules first. So this effect applies to it too, unless the rare bits are left out.
% - So the argument rests on the near-constant count, not the constant count.
% - The brain-dump phrase "exponentially more irrelevant information" is not accurate. Rephrase:
%   - Each feature unrelated to the task adds one more term to the sum inside the distance.
%   - The growth is linear in the number of such features, not exponential.
%   - As such terms grow in number, the nearest and farthest neighbours become harder to tell apart \citep{beyer1999nearest}.
%   - Keep the full argument for "Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations".
%
% Why it matters for models:
% - A constant feature carries no information for any model. It only costs memory and time.
% - Near-constant features raise the number of features without adding many molecules that differ on them.
% - When features outnumber labelled molecules, predictions can get worse.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the effect of rarely-set fingerprint bits on QSAR model performance at small training-set sizes.
% - Tree-based importance (gain, TreeSHAP) gives a constant feature a score of zero, because no tree can split on it.
% - A near-constant feature can still get a nonzero score from a few molecules. This is one route to overfitting in feature selection.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on bias of tree-based feature importance toward or against rare binary features.
%
% How the hybrid handles this (method facts, checked against the code):
% - The hybrid applies no separate low-variance or sparsity filter before selection.
% - A representation contributes only features whose importance score is above zero. Features scored exactly zero are never chosen.
% - So constant features never enter the hybrid, without any filter.
% - For a representation wider than 2000 features, the tree-based importance methods first keep the 2000 features with the highest raw variance. Features outside those 2000 score zero.
% - That step is a variance filter by rank, not by a threshold, and it exists for speed.
% - One consequence: a representation can supply fewer than its allocated $b_k$ features, if fewer than $b_k$ score above zero.
% - Why it matters: the method removes non-varying features as a side effect of choosing by task importance, rather than by an unsupervised rule.
%
% Existing alternatives to point to:
% - Sort and Slice replaces hash folding. It keeps the substructures that occur most often in the training set, so rare and absent substructures are left out by construction \citep{sns}.
% - Sort and Slice chooses by frequency, not by the task.
% - Dablander's thesis discusses that the least frequent substructures tend to appear in only a few compounds \citep{Dablander2024}. TODO(lit): find and confirm the page numbers in Dablander (2023) for this statement before citing it.
%   - That statement is about substructures before hashing. This section counts bits after folding to 2048.
% - Unsupervised filters remove features with low variance or high sparsity before modelling.
% - They do not use the labels, so they cannot tell a rare bit that matters from one that does not.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how often low-variance or sparsity filters are applied as preprocessing in published QSAR pipelines.
%
% Links to:
% - "Hash collisions: one bit, several substructures": the bits that do vary may each stand for more than one substructure.
% - "Molecules the representation cannot tell apart": if too few bits vary, different molecules can get identical vectors.
% - "Different representations carry different information": varying is necessary but not enough; the varying features still have to relate to the task.
% - "What a representation would need": built from the features that vary on this dataset and relate to this task.
% - Chapter 5, "From base representations to a hybrid": the hybrid chooses $B$ features by task importance, and features that never vary cannot be chosen.
% - Chapter 6, "Similarity in a hybrid": distances there are computed on standardised hybrid features, so the rare-bit effect above is relevant.
% - Why it matters: this is the first and simplest reason a fixed general-purpose representation is wasteful on one dataset.
% - Why it matters: it motivates choosing features per dataset and per task, which is what the hybrid does.
% ---- END NOTES 4.2.1 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{4.2.1: constant and near-constant feature counts (Table 4.1).}


\subsection{Hash collisions: one bit, several substructures}
% OUTLINE 4.2.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: nothing yet in this subsection, only a placeholder. Tanimoto on ECFP as the standard similarity is introduced in 4.3, so it is not re-introduced here.
% What folding is:
% - ECFP gives each circular substructure (an atom and its neighbourhood) one integer identifier, built by repeatedly relabelling each atom from its neighbours' labels \citep{Morgan1965,rogers2010}.
% - ECFP4 means radius 2 (diameter 4 bonds). Each atom contributes one identifier at radius 0, one at radius 1 and one at radius 2.
% - The identifiers are hashed into a large integer space, 32-bit in the original description \citep{rogers2010}.
% - A model needs a fixed-length vector, so the identifiers are folded into $L$ bits: bit index $= h(\text{id}) \bmod L$.
% - The ECFP4 used in this thesis has radius 2, $L = 2048$ bits, and is binary (bit on or off, no counts).
%   - The other hashed fingerprints used later (atom pair, topological torsion, RDKit path fingerprint with paths of 1 to 7 bonds, Avalon) are also 2048 bits and binary.
% - Folding is many-to-one. Two different substructures can land on the same bit. This is a bit collision.
% - The model then sees one feature that stands for several unrelated substructures.
% Collisions follow from counting:
% - Let $M$ be the number of distinct substructure identifiers across the dataset and $L$ the number of bits.
% - Occupied bits can be at most $L$. So if $M > L$, at least $M - L$ identifiers are lost into bits already used (pigeonhole principle).
% - Under uniform hashing, the chance that a given identifier shares its bit with at least one other identifier is $P(\text{shared}) = 1 - (1 - 1/L)^{M-1} \approx 1 - e^{-(M-1)/L}$.
%   - Scale example only, not a data result: $M = L = 2048$ gives $P \approx 1 - e^{-1} \approx 0.63$.
% - Expected number of occupied bits for $m$ identifiers: $\mathbb{E}[\text{occupied}] = L\left(1 - (1 - 1/L)^{m}\right)$.
% - Expected number of identifiers lost to collisions: $m - \mathbb{E}[\text{occupied}]$.
% - Inside one molecule, collisions are uncommon. With $m$ of order tens of identifiers and $L = 2048$, $P(\text{shared}) \approx 1 - e^{-m/L}$ is a few percent.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature reporting the typical number of bits set per drug-like molecule in 2048-bit ECFP4 (for example the ChEMBL average).
% - Across a dataset, $M$ is much larger than for one molecule. So one bit can mean one substructure in one molecule and a different substructure in another.
% How to measure it on a dataset (planned statistic, not yet computed):
% - Compute the unfolded Morgan identifiers for each molecule, and record the bit each one folds to.
% - Per molecule: $c_i$ = number of bits set in the folded 2048-bit vector. Report the median over molecules.
% - Per dataset: for each occupied bit $j$, $u_j$ = number of distinct identifiers that fold into it.
% - Collision share of occupied bits $= \frac{|\{j : u_j > 1\}|}{|\{j : u_j \ge 1\}|}$.
% - Also report the spread of $u_j$: its maximum, and the share of occupied bits with $u_j \ge 3$.
% - Planned datasets: hERG Ki, Caco-2, LogD.
% - TODO(results): median bits set per molecule, collision share of occupied bits, and the distribution of identifiers per bit on hERG Ki, Caco-2 and LogD; numbers come from the planned statistic once it is run.
% - DIAGRAM: histogram with x-axis = number of distinct substructures folded into one bit ($u_j$) and y-axis = number of bits, one panel per dataset. Optional inset: two substructures that share one bit, drawn as molecule fragments.
% Consequence 1, interpretation:
% - A bit that a model ranks as important (for example by a tree's gain) cannot be read as one substructure.
% - Mapping a bit back to substructures only lists the substructures present in the molecules examined. A new molecule can switch the same bit on through a different substructure.
% - Why it matters: the hybrid selects single features by importance (Chapter 5). A selected ECFP bit in a blueprint is ambiguous to a chemist unless its colliding substructures are listed.
% - Links to: 5.1.4 Blueprints and what they tell a chemist.
% - Links to: 4.2.4. Embeddings are unreadable because a feature has no chemical name at all. A collided bit is unreadable because it has several names.
% Consequence 2, similarity:
% - Tanimoto on folded bits: $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{|\mathbf{a}\wedge\mathbf{b}|}{|\mathbf{a}\vee\mathbf{b}|}$ \citep{Bajusz2015}.
% - A collision can create a shared bit from two different substructures. The two molecules then look more alike than they are.
% - A collision can also merge two substructures that both molecules share into one bit. This lowers both the numerator and the denominator.
% - So folded Tanimoto differs from Tanimoto on the unfolded identifier sets. The direction and size of the difference must be measured, not assumed.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing Tanimoto similarity on folded versus unfolded ECFP, including the effect of fingerprint length on similarity values and nearest-neighbour rankings.
% - Option for a further measurement, not in the outline and for you to decide: the Spearman correlation between folded and unfolded Tanimoto over all molecule pairs, and how many of each molecule's five nearest neighbours change.
% - Links to: 4.3 Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations.
% - Links to: 4.4.2 Noise detection by neighbours, because the neighbour sets there come from this similarity.
% Consequence 3, prediction:
% - A collided feature mixes the signals of unrelated substructures. For the model, this acts as noise inside the feature.
% - Sort & Slice replaces folding. It keeps the $L$ substructures most frequent in the training set, one feature each, so there are no collisions. It matched or beat hash folding on the tasks tested \citep{sns}.
% - Filtered circular fingerprints choose substructures before folding, which keeps them interpretable. They improved either prediction or runtime \citep{Gtlein2016}.
% - MinHash fingerprints (MHFP6) keep the circular substructures but encode them with MinHash instead of folding. They were designed for nearest-neighbour search \citep{Probst2018}.
% - Folding and its alternatives are also discussed in a DPhil thesis \citep{Dablander2024}.
%   - TODO(lit): find the page numbers of the folding and collision discussion in Dablander2024 before citing a specific claim from it.
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: the most widely used molecular representation loses the identity of single features before any model is fitted.
% - Why it matters: this is a flaw in the representation, not in the model. Changing the model cannot undo it.
% - Links to: 4.2.3. There, whole molecules merge into one vector. That is the molecule-level version of the same many-to-one mapping.
% - Links to: 4.5 What a representation would need (one feature, one meaning).
% - Links to: Chapter 5. The hybrid can select ECFP bits next to named descriptors. Collisions are one argument for preferring a named descriptor when both are equally important. This is an argument only, not a result.
% ---- END NOTES 4.2.2 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{4.2.2: planned statistic, not run.}


\subsection{Molecules the representation cannot tell apart}
% OUTLINE 4.2.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this subsection shows:
% - Claim: a standard representation can map two different molecules to exactly the same vector.
% - If $\mathbf{x}_i = \mathbf{x}_j$ for molecules $i \neq j$, every model $f$ must give $f(\mathbf{x}_i) = f(\mathbf{x}_j)$.
% - If their measured labels differ ($y_i \neq y_j$), part of the error is fixed before any model is trained.
% - This is a property of the representation, not of the model or the data size.
% - Why it matters: this error cannot be removed by a better model, more tuning, or more data in the same representation. Only a change of representation can remove it.
% - Links to: 4.2.1 and 4.2.2 (features that do not vary, bits shared by several substructures); 4.3 (similarity of 1 between different molecules); Chapter 5 (combining representations can split a tie).
% Three causes of a repeated vector (counted separately):
% - The dataset holds the same molecule twice, e.g. two SMILES spellings of one structure. Detected by a shared InChIKey. Not the representation's fault.
% - Featurisation failed and returned an all-zero row, e.g. no 3D conformer could be generated, or the SMILES could not be parsed. A pipeline fault, not a property of the representation.
% - Two genuinely different molecules the representation cannot separate. Only this case is a flaw of the representation.
% - Sub-count of the third case: molecules that differ only in stereochemistry. Test: their canonical SMILES become identical once stereochemistry is removed.
% Why stereoisomers tie:
% - Most commonly used molecular fingerprints do not encode stereochemistry (cis/trans double bonds, enantiomers, diastereomers) \citep{orsi2024map4c}.
%   - ECFP/Morgan fingerprints hash circular atom environments of the 2D molecular graph \citep{rogers2010}.
%   - In this thesis the Morgan fingerprints are built with chirality switched off, which is the library default. TODO(lit): cite the RDKit documentation for the Morgan fingerprint generator's default includeChirality = False, with \citep{rdkit}.
%   - Computed 2D descriptor vectors (physicochemical descriptors, Mordred-type descriptors) are mostly functions of the 2D graph, so enantiomers get identical values. TODO(lit): search for literature on which 2D molecular descriptors are invariant to stereochemistry.
% - Stereoisomers can differ in measured activity, so a stereo-blind representation forces one prediction onto molecules with different measured values. TODO(lit): search for literature on enantiomer potency differences (eudismic ratio) in drug activity.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on stereoisomer (enantiomer/diastereomer) differences in hERG channel inhibition potency.
% - Public Ki data in ChEMBL contains undifferentiated stereoisomers \citep{Kramer2012}, so the problem also enters through the labels, not only the features.
% - MACCS keys (166 predefined substructure keys) are coarse, so they can also tie molecules that differ in more than stereochemistry. TODO(lit): add the MACCS key definition paper (Durant et al. 2002, "Reoptimization of MDL keys for use in drug discovery", J. Chem. Inf. Comput. Sci.) to refs.bib.
% Learned and 3D representations are not immune:
% - Message-passing graph neural networks can distinguish at most what the 1-dimensional Weisfeiler-Leman graph isomorphism test distinguishes \citep{xu2019gin, morris2019wl}.
%   - So some non-isomorphic molecular graphs receive the same embedding.
% - A SMILES language model ties stereoisomers if the stereochemistry markers ("@", "/", "\") are removed from the SMILES before tokenising.
%   - TODO(results): number of hERG molecules that the SMILES-transformer tokeniser maps to identical token ids; numbers come from the full runs.
% - 3D representations can separate diastereomers, since their conformers differ in shape.
% - 3D descriptors built only from interatomic distances cannot separate mirror-image enantiomers: every distance is the same in both.
% The measurement (method):
% - For each dataset and each representation, group molecules whose feature vectors are identical, compared exactly (byte for byte, no tolerance).
% - Each tied group is assigned one cause, checked in this order: all rows all-zero; all rows share one InChIKey (same molecule twice); otherwise a genuine tie.
%   - InChIKey keeps stereochemistry, so two stereoisomers count as two different molecules.
% - Report per representation: number of molecules in a tied group; how many are genuine ties; how many of these are stereo-only; how many are all-zero rows; how many are repeated inputs.
% - Also report ties after concatenating two representations: two molecules share a row of $[\mathbf{X}^{(a)}, \mathbf{X}^{(b)}]$ only if they share a row in both.
%   - Concatenation can split a tied group but never create one.
%   - If two representations tie the same molecules (e.g. two stereo-blind representations), concatenating them fixes nothing.
% - Also report ties after concatenating all $K$ representations in a dataset's pool, $[\mathbf{X}^{(1)}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}]$. No hybrid built from that pool can have fewer ties.
% - Best score any model could reach, given the ties (regression):
%   - Tied group $G$ must share one prediction; the squared-error optimum is the group mean $\bar{y}_G = \frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{i \in G} y_i$.
%   - $\mathrm{RMSE}_{\min} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G}\sum_{i \in G}(y_i - \bar{y}_G)^2}$, in the dataset's label units (e.g. pKi).
%   - $R^2_{\max} = 1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_{\min}^2 / \mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{y})$.
%   - Also report the spread of labels inside each tied group, the standard deviation of $y$ within $G$, and its maximum over groups.
% - Binary classification scored by AUROC: a tied active and a tied inactive must get the same score, so that pair counts 0.5.
%   - $\mathrm{AUROC}_{\max} = 1 - \frac{1}{2}\frac{\sum_G p_G (|G| - p_G)}{n_{+} n_{-}}$, with $p_G$ actives in group $G$ and $n_+, n_-$ actives and inactives in the dataset.
% - Classification scored by accuracy: the best a model can do in a tied group is predict its most common label.
% - No model is fitted; this is a count on the feature matrices.
% - Datasets: hERG Ki, LogD, Caco-2. Planned extension: QM9 and the MDR1 ADME dataset.
% - The same count applies to antibody sequence representations; there most repeated vectors come from the same sequence appearing twice, not from genuine ties.
% Results to place here:
% - TODO(results): table, one row per representation, one column per dataset: molecules in tied groups (count and %), of which stereo-only; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): best reachable RMSE and $R^2$ per representation on hERG Ki, and the largest label spread (pKi) inside a tied group; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): which representations give zero ties, and whether the stereo-blind representations all tie the same molecules; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Context for the label spread: the estimated experimental uncertainty of public Ki data is a mean error of 0.44 pKi and a standard deviation of 0.54 pKi \citep{Kramer2012}.
%   - A tied group whose labels differ by more than this is not explained by measurement noise alone.
% - DIAGRAM: one pair of hERG stereoisomers drawn side by side, their identical fingerprint bits, and their two measured pKi values; caption states the prediction any model must give (their mean).
% - DIAGRAM: bar chart, x-axis representation, y-axis % molecules in a tied group, bars split into stereo-only and other genuine ties; one panel per dataset.
% Activity cliffs as a consequence:
% - Activity cliff: a pair of structurally similar molecules with a large difference in activity \citep{Maggiora2006, Dablander2023}.
% - Cliffs are a known reason QSAR models disappoint; valid data points on a cliff can look like outliers \citep{Maggiora2006}.
% - Machine learning models of many kinds perform worse on activity-cliff molecules \citep{vanTilborg2022, Dablander2023}.
% - Structure-activity landscape index for a pair: $\mathrm{SALI}_{ij} = \frac{|y_i - y_j|}{1 - \mathrm{sim}(i,j)}$ \citep{guha2008sali}.
%   - A tied pair has $\mathrm{sim}(i,j) = 1$, so SALI is undefined (infinite) whenever $y_i \neq y_j$.
%   - So a tie with different labels is the extreme cliff: zero distance in the representation, non-zero activity difference.
% - A tie is the worst case of a wider problem: molecules that are close but not identical in the representation get close predictions from any smooth model.
% - Why it matters: some cliffs are not in the chemistry but made by the representation. Changing the representation can remove them; no model choice can.
% - Why it matters: any method that uses nearest neighbours (noise detection, predicting a molecule's activity from its nearest neighbours, 4.4) sees a tied neighbour at distance 0 with a different label, and may flag a correct label as noise.
% - Links to: 4.3 (similarity of 1 does not mean same molecule); 4.4 (neighbour-based noise detection); Chapter 5 (a hybrid can add features from a representation that separates the tie, e.g. a 3D one; a hybrid of only stereo-blind representations cannot).
% - Links to Chapter 5: a hybrid keeps $b_k$ features, not all $d_k$, so it can re-introduce ties the full representation did not have. To tell these apart, count ties three ways: the full representation, only the features the hybrid kept from it, and the whole hybrid $\mathbf{H}$.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on activity cliffs formed by stereoisomers ("chirality cliffs", stereo-activity cliffs).
% ---- END NOTES 4.2.3 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{4.2.3: shared-vector census (herg\_ki, logd, caco2).}


\subsection{Learned embeddings cannot be read chemically}
% OUTLINE 4.2.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: descriptors (PDV) have a named physicochemical meaning per feature; frozen embeddings / last-layer outputs do not.
%
% What the point is:
% - A feature is "chemically readable" if a chemist can say what property or substructure that single feature measures, without looking at the model.
% - Three kinds of base representation sit on a scale of readability: descriptors, substructure keys/fingerprints, learned embeddings.
% - Descriptors: feature $j$ is a named quantity (e.g. logP, TPSA, count of nitro groups) computed by a fixed formula.
%   - The descriptor set used here is 200 RDKit 2D descriptors \citep{rdkit}; general reference for descriptor definitions \citep{Todeschini2009}.
% - Substructure keys: each bit is a predefined substructure pattern, so bit $j = 1$ means "pattern $j$ is present".
%   - MACCS keys: 166 predefined patterns, originally built for substructure searching \citep{Durant2002}.
% - Hashed circular fingerprints (ECFP): each bit can be traced back to the atom environments that set it \citep{rogers2010}.
%   - Readable only up to hash collisions: one bit can stand for several unrelated substructures (Links to: "Hash collisions: one bit, several substructures").
% - Learned embeddings: feature $j$ is coordinate $j$ of a vector produced by a pretrained network.
%   - Its only name is "dimension $j$ of representation $k$".
%
% How the embeddings used in this thesis are built (why one dimension has no meaning):
% - Mol2vec: each Morgan substructure (radius 1) has a learned 300-dimensional vector from word2vec \citep{Jaeger2018}.
%   - The molecule vector here is the mean over the substructures found in the vocabulary: $\mathbf{z} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}(m)|}\sum_{u \in \mathcal{U}(m)} \mathbf{v}_u$.
%   - The original paper sums instead of averaging \citep{Jaeger2018}; the two differ only by a per-molecule scale.
%   - The inputs are substructures, but after averaging, one coordinate mixes contributions from every substructure in the molecule.
% - Frozen SMILES transformers: the SMILES string is tokenised, passed through the network, and the final-layer token states are averaged over non-padding tokens: $\mathbf{z} = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T} \mathbf{h}_t$.
%   - Two such models are used: ChemBERTa-77M-MTR, 384 features \citep{Chithrananda2020, Ahmad2022}; and a smaller BERT pretrained on ChEMBL, 256 features.
%   - TODO(lit): find the citation for the ChEMBL-pretrained BERT ("Pushing the Boundaries of Molecular Property Prediction for Drug Discovery with Multitask Learning BERT Enhanced by SMILES Enumeration").
%   - Each coordinate mixes all tokens, through all attention layers.
% - GNN-derived vectors: pooled node states are real numbers with no direct chemical interpretation \citep{Dablander2024}.
%   - Check the exact wording and page in Dablander's thesis before quoting it directly.
% - The same issue holds for the protein/antibody language-model embeddings used in the non-molecular datasets.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on interpretability of individual dimensions of protein language model embeddings (ESM-2 / antibody language models).
%
% A simple argument that the axes need not be meaningful:
% - Take any orthogonal matrix $\mathbf{Q}$ ($\mathbf{Q}^\top\mathbf{Q} = \mathbf{I}$) and rotate the embedding: $\mathbf{Z}' = \mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Q}$.
% - Every Euclidean distance is unchanged: $\|\mathbf{z}_a\mathbf{Q} - \mathbf{z}_b\mathbf{Q}\|_2 = \|\mathbf{z}_a - \mathbf{z}_b\|_2$.
%   - So a method that uses only distances (nearest neighbours, RBF kernels) sees the same molecules whether or not the axes are rotated.
%   - A single coordinate of $\mathbf{Z}$ is therefore not a fixed object; a descriptor's coordinate is.
% - Feature selection is not rotation-invariant: it keeps individual coordinates $S_k \subset \{1,\dots,d_k\}$.
%   - Which embedding coordinates the hybrid keeps depends on a basis that the pretraining chose arbitrarily.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on whether pretrained molecular embedding dimensions align with known physicochemical descriptors (probing studies of chemical language models).
%
% Planned statistic (method only; not run yet):
% - For each embedding feature $z_j$, take its largest absolute Spearman correlation with any of the 200 descriptors $x_l$ over the $n$ molecules of a dataset: $r^{\max}_j = \max_l |\rho_S(z_j, x_l)|$.
% - Report the number of embedding features with $r^{\max}_j > 0.8$.
%   - A feature close to a known descriptor can be "read" through that descriptor.
% - Report per dataset and per embedding, on the same datasets as the constant-feature and shared-vector counts in this chapter.
% - TODO(results): count of embedding features with $r^{\max}_j > 0.8$, per embedding and dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: histogram of $r^{\max}_j$ per embedding (x axis: largest |Spearman| with any descriptor, 0 to 1; y axis: number of features), one panel per embedding, dashed line at 0.8.
% - Limitation to state: high correlation with one descriptor is necessary, not sufficient, for a chemical reading.
%   - A feature could track a mix of several descriptors and still correlate weakly with each one.
%
% Where explanation methods fit:
% - Feature attribution (e.g. SHAP, gradient methods) says which input features a model used \citep{JimenezLuna2020}.
%   - If those features are embedding dimensions, the explanation still ends at "dimension $j$".
% - Explanations are more useful to chemists when they map to atoms, substructures or named properties \citep{Wellawatte2023}.
% - Interpretation of QSAR models is itself hard to benchmark \citep{Matveieva2021}.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on atom-level attribution for transformer SMILES models (attention-based or token-level attributions mapped back to atoms).
%
% This is a trade-off, not a reason to drop embeddings:
% - Embeddings can still carry information the descriptors miss.
% - Readability and predictive value are separate questions.
% - The point here: when a model relies on embedding features, the chemist learns less about why.
%
% Why it matters:
% - Why it matters: the hybrid picks features by importance and records which features were chosen, by representation and index.
%   - That record is chemically useful only for named features (descriptors, substructure keys, traceable fingerprint bits).
% - Why it matters: a task-specific feature set dominated by embedding dimensions can predict well but gives little chemical insight.
%   - One dominated by descriptors or keys can point to properties or substructures linked to the endpoint.
% - Links to: Chapter "Building Adaptive Hybrid Representations", sections "Blueprints" and "Blueprints and what they tell a chemist" (a blueprint names chosen descriptors/substructures; embedding features stay unnamed).
% - Links to: "Different representations carry different information" (why embeddings stay in the pool despite this).
% - Links to: "What a representation would need" (readability is one of the listed requirements).
% ---- END NOTES 4.2.4 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Sec "Component representations".
It is worth noting that some of these features are more straightforward than others. For example, PDV's carry very clear physicochemical properties such that if you examined one of it's features, you'd know exactly the type of chemical information it contains. Other representations are frozen embeddings, or the last layer of a network. While these features may be found to contain useful informative for the prediction task at hand, they do not tell us what type of chemical information, if any, they relate to. 


\subsection{Different representations carry different information}
% OUTLINE 4.2.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.2.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this subsection does:
% - Claim: two representations of the same molecules can disagree about which molecules are close to which.
% - If they disagree, each carries information the other lacks, or weights the same information differently.
% - This is the reason to combine representations. It sets up Chapter 5.
% - Scope here: small molecules only. The other domains' representations (proteins/antibodies, reactions, nucleotides, cancer genomics) are described in Chapter 5, Representations by domain.
% - Already in prose: nothing in this subsection yet. The previous subsection (descriptor features have chemical names, embedding features do not) is the lead-in.
% What each family encodes (small molecules):
% - Circular fingerprints (ECFP): hashed presence of atom neighbourhoods up to a set radius; local substructure \citep{rogers2010}.
% - Substructure-key fingerprints (MACCS): a fixed dictionary of predefined fragments; presence or absence only.
% - TODO(lit): search for the original definition of the MACCS structural keys (the 166-key public set) for a citation.
% - Atom-pair fingerprints: pairs of atom types plus the shortest-path (bond count) distance between them; describes the global layout of the molecular graph.
% - TODO(lit): search for the original atom-pair fingerprint paper (Carhart, Smith and Venkataraghavan, 1985) and add it to the bibliography.
% - Physicochemical descriptor vectors (RDKit descriptors, Mordred): computed whole-molecule properties such as logP, polar surface area, atom and ring counts, charges; each feature is named and continuous.
% - TODO(lit): search for the Mordred descriptor calculator paper (Moriwaki et al., 2018) for a citation.
% - Graph-kernel features: Weisfeiler-Lehman relabelling of the molecular graph (5 iterations); each feature counts how often one relabelled subtree pattern occurs in the molecule.
% - TODO(lit): search for the Weisfeiler-Lehman graph kernel paper (Shervashidze et al., 2011) for a citation.
% - mol2vec: substructure identifiers embedded like words in a sentence, trained on a large unlabelled set of molecules \citep{Jaeger2018}.
% - Pretrained transformer embeddings (SMILES or graph models): learned from large unlabelled corpora; dense, and features have no chemical name.
% - DIAGRAM: Table 4.2, one row per representation family: what it encodes, feature type (binary / count / continuous / learned), typical number of features, weaknesses found in Sections 4.2.1 to 4.2.4.
% - Model for Table 4.2: the representation-comparison table in Dablander's thesis \citep{Dablander2024} (check the table number before referring to it).
% - From the literature: which fingerprint ranks molecules closest to human judgement depends on the task. ECFP4/ECFP6 and topological torsion do best when ranking diverse structures; atom pairs do best when ranking very close analogues \citep{OBoyle2016}.
% How overlap is measured here (distance agreement):
% - Idea: compare the geometry each representation induces, not the features themselves. Features of two representations cannot be matched one to one, but molecules can.
% - Step 1: standardise each feature of $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$ to mean 0 and variance 1. A constant feature contributes 0 to every distance.
% - Step 2: draw one random set $P$ of molecule pairs $(i,j)$ with $i \neq j$, with a fixed seed. Up to 5,000 pairs. The same pairs are reused for every representation, so the distance vectors line up element by element.
% - Step 3: for each representation, the Euclidean distance on each pair: $d^{(k)}_{ij} = \lVert \mathbf{x}^{(k)}_i - \mathbf{x}^{(k)}_j \rVert_2$. This gives a vector $\mathbf{d}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{|P|}$.
% - Step 4: agreement between representations $k$ and $l$: $A_{kl} = |\rho_S(\mathbf{d}^{(k)}, \mathbf{d}^{(l)})|$, where $\rho_S$ is the Spearman rank correlation. $A_{kk} = 1$.
% - Reading it: $A_{kl} \approx 1$ means both representations order molecule pairs from near to far in the same way.
% - Reading it: $A_{kl} \approx 0$ means one representation's distances say nothing about the other's.
% - It is a rank correlation, so it ignores differences in scale. A 2048-bit fingerprint and a 200-feature descriptor vector have very different distance ranges.
% - It needs no labels. It measures overlap of geometry, not overlap of predictive content.
% - Consequence for concatenation: a Euclidean distance on a concatenated vector sums squared differences over both feature sets, $d^2_{ij}(\mathbf{H}) = \sum_k \lVert \mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i,S_k} - \mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{j,S_k} \rVert_2^2$. Two representations with high $A_{kl}$ add the same ordering twice.
% - Lineage: correlating two distance matrices over the same objects goes back to the Mantel statistic \citep{Mantel1967}.
% - Lineage: the same idea in neuroscience is representational similarity analysis, comparing representations through their distance matrices over shared stimuli \citep{Kriegeskorte2008}.
% - Only the correlation is reported here. No permutation test.
% - Alternative measure to name: centred kernel alignment, $\mathrm{CKA}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}) = \frac{\mathrm{HSIC}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L})}{\sqrt{\mathrm{HSIC}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K})\,\mathrm{HSIC}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})}}$, with $\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{X}^{(k)}\mathbf{X}^{(k)\top}$ and $\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{X}^{(l)}\mathbf{X}^{(l)\top}$ \citep{Kornblith2019}.
% - CKA still works when there are more features than samples \citep{Kornblith2019}. It is not computed in this thesis.
% A second view: agreement of predictions:
% - A task-aware view: correlate the out-of-fold predictions of the same model trained on each representation, $|\rho_S(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}, \hat{\mathbf{y}}^{(l)})|$.
% - Two representations can have different geometry but make the same predictions, if the task depends only on a part they share.
% - The reverse also happens: similar geometry, different predictions.
% - Choosing each dataset's final set of representations (Chapter 5) looked at this prediction correlation next to each representation's own score. The choice was made by hand. Point forward; do not repeat it here.
% Results to place:
% - TODO(results): matrix of distance agreement $A_{kl}$ for every representation pair on each dataset (hERG Ki, LogD, antibody thermostability, TCGA lung adenocarcinoma vs squamous cell carcinoma), with the median over pairs and the most and least alike pair per dataset; numbers come from the full runs on the final representation sets.
% - TODO(results): whether descriptor-type representations agree more with each other than with fingerprints or embeddings; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: heatmap of $A_{kl}$ for one small-molecule dataset, representations ordered by family (fingerprints, descriptors, graph kernel, embeddings); optional second panel with the same for a non-molecular dataset.
% - DIAGRAM (optional): scatter of $d^{(k)}_{ij}$ against $d^{(l)}_{ij}$ over the sampled pairs, one panel for the most-alike pair and one for the least-alike pair.
% Why it matters / links:
% - Why it matters: if agreement were near 1 everywhere, combining representations would add nothing.
% - Why it matters: low agreement means there is something to combine.
% - Why it matters: low agreement also means "similar molecules" depends on the representation chosen. Section 4.3 (Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations) takes this up.
% - Caveat: low agreement shows difference, not usefulness. A representation can differ because it carries noise.
% - Caveat: whether the differing part predicts the label is decided by the hybrid's importance scores $s^{(k)}_j$ (Chapter 5).
% - Links to: Chapter 5, Combining representations: what exists. Concatenation and ensembling both assume the representations carry complementary information.
% - Links to: Chapter 5, The search space. The budget $B$ and allocation $b_k$ decide how much of each representation to keep.
% - Links to: Chapter 5, The search space. Redundancy between representations is one reason allocating per representation differs from pooling all features and taking the top $B$.
% - Links to: Section 4.5, What a representation would need. Keep what each family adds, drop what duplicates.

\section{Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations}
% OUTLINE 4.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope of this section:
% - Section "Flaws in individual representations" (4.2) covered flaws in the vectors themselves. This section covers what happens when two vectors are compared to give one similarity or distance number.
% - Main point: a similarity measure compares every feature. It cannot tell which features matter for the property being predicted.
%   - So features irrelevant to the task become part of the similarity.
% - Already in Chapter 2 (Molecular similarity and activity cliffs): the similar property principle, its dependence on representation and coefficient \citep{Maggiora2014}, the activity-cliff definition, the MoleculeACE thresholds, and the Aggarwal high-dimension result. Cross-reference them; do not restate.
% - Already in prose: the eight distance formulas for continuous descriptors, and the opening remark that ECFP Tanimoto is the default.
%   - The sentence "ECFPs do not individually distinguish each molecule" belongs to "Molecules the representation cannot tell apart" (4.2.3). Link to it rather than repeating it.
% - Once the representation is fixed, the similarity measure is fixed with it. The task is never consulted.
%
% Definitions (add Tanimoto to the list already in prose):
% - Binary Tanimoto: $a = |\mathbf{a}|$ and $b = |\mathbf{b}|$ are the on-bits in each molecule; $c = |\mathbf{a}\wedge\mathbf{b}|$ are the shared on-bits.
%   - $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{c}{a + b - c}$ \citep{Bajusz2015}.
% - Count (min/max) Tanimoto for count fingerprints: $T_{\text{count}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{\sum_j \min(a_j,b_j)}{\sum_j \max(a_j,b_j)}$.
% - Dot-product form of Tanimoto: $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}}{\|\mathbf{a}\|^2+\|\mathbf{b}\|^2-\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}}$.
%   - It equals the binary form on 0/1 features.
%   - On count features it gives a different value from the min/max form. Say which form is used.
%   - It never exceeds 1, because the denominator minus the numerator is $\|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}\|^2 \ge 0$.
%   - On features with negative values (e.g. z-scored descriptors) it can be negative, so $1 - T$ can exceed 1.
% - Distance from similarity: $d = 1 - T$.
% - Tanimoto ignores bits that are off in both molecules (joint absences). Euclidean and cosine do not treat 0 as special.
% - Mahalanobis distance (the existing bullet has no formula): $d_M(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) = \sqrt{(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^\top \boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})}$.
%   - $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}$ is singular when any feature is constant, or when there are more features than molecules ($p > n$).
%   - It then needs a pseudo-inverse or a shrinkage estimate of $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}$.
%   - Links to: "Most features do not vary on a given dataset" (4.2.1). Constant features make Mahalanobis undefined without that fix.
% - Bajusz et al. found Tanimoto a sound choice among fingerprint similarity coefficients \citep{Bajusz2015}.
%   - The argument here is not about which coefficient to use. It is about which features go into it.
% - Bit-string similarity values depend partly on molecular size \citep{Flower1998}.
%   - Larger molecules set more bits, so their similarity values are shifted systematically.
%   - That is a second effect with nothing to do with the task.
%
% How irrelevant features dilute Tanimoto (use this in place of the "exponential" claim):
% - Split the features into those relevant to the task ($R$) and the rest ($I$).
%   - $u_R$ = number of bits on in either molecule within $R$ (the union); $c_R$ = number on in both.
%   - Define $u_I$ and $c_I$ the same way within $I$.
% - Binary Tanimoto is $T = \frac{c_R+c_I}{u_R+u_I}$.
% - This is a weighted average of the two parts: $T = w\,T_R + (1-w)\,T_I$, with $w = \frac{u_R}{u_R+u_I}$, $T_R = c_R/u_R$, $T_I = c_I/u_I$.
% - Meaning: the relevant part counts only in proportion to its share of the on-bits.
%   - Example: if 10 of 60 on-bits are relevant, the task-relevant similarity is $1/6$ of the score.
% - Bits that are off for every molecule in the dataset (4.2.1) do not change Tanimoto, because joint absences are ignored.
%   - They are harmless for Tanimoto. They are not harmless for Euclidean or cosine after z-scoring, if any variation remains.
% - Bits that are on for every molecule (a shared scaffold, common atom environments) are shared by every pair.
%   - If all irrelevant on-bits are of this kind, $T_I = 1$ and $T = w\,T_R + (1-w)$.
%   - Every pair is pushed toward 1, and the spread of $T$ shrinks roughly by the factor $w$.
% - Why it matters: a fixed threshold such as $T \ge 0.55$ or $T \ge 0.9$ means something different on each dataset.
%   - It depends on how many shared irrelevant bits that dataset's molecules carry.
%
% How irrelevant features dilute Euclidean distance:
% - Squared distance is a sum over features: $d^2(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) = \sum_{j\in R}(x_j-y_j)^2 + \sum_{j\in I}(x_j-y_j)^2$.
% - After z-scoring, each irrelevant feature adds on average $\mathbb{E}[(z_{ij}-z_{i'j})^2] = 2$ for two independent molecules $i, i'$.
%   - So $\mathbb{E}[d^2] = d_R^2 + 2m$, where $m = |I|$ is the number of irrelevant features.
% - The irrelevant part also varies from pair to pair. For independent features its standard deviation grows like $\sqrt{m}$.
%   - Once that exceeds the spread of $d_R^2$ across pairs, the nearest-neighbour order is set mostly by irrelevant features.
% - Constant features add 0 to Euclidean distance. The harm comes from features that vary but are unrelated to the task.
% - The number of terms grows linearly with the number of features, not exponentially.
%   - Links to: the paragraph in "The exploratory learning-with-noisy-labels study" (4.4.1) that says "exponential growth in the number of irrelevant pairwise interactions". Replace that phrase with the linear argument above.
% - The established high-dimension result is distance concentration \citep{Beyer1999}.
%   - With $p$ features and under broad conditions on the feature distributions: $\frac{D_{\max}-D_{\min}}{D_{\min}} \to 0$ in probability as $p\to\infty$.
%   - $D_{\max}$ and $D_{\min}$ are the distances from one query point to its farthest and nearest data point.
%   - $L_1$ and fractional norms keep more contrast than $L_2$ as $p$ grows \citep{Aggarwal2001}. (Already cited in Chapter 2; use here for the $L_1$ versus $L_2$ point only.)
% - Hubness: in high dimension a few points appear in many other points' $k$-nearest-neighbour lists \citep{Radovanovic2010}.
%   - $N_k(i)$ = number of other molecules that have $i$ among their $k$ nearest neighbours.
%   - Its distribution becomes right-skewed. Measure it with the skewness of $N_k$.
%
% Scaling choices that change the answer silently:
% - Z-scoring a binary bit with prevalence $p_j$ divides it by $\sqrt{p_j(1-p_j)}$.
%   - One differing bit then adds $\frac{1}{p_j(1-p_j)}$ to $d^2$: 4 at $p_j=0.5$, about 500 at $p_j=0.002$.
%   - Rare bits dominate the distance.
%   - Rare bits are also the ones most affected by hash collisions and by which molecules happen to be sampled.
% - The existing sentence "All features are standardised before distance computation" is under the continuous-descriptor heading.
%   - Say that it applies to continuous descriptors. Say separately what is done for binary or count fingerprints.
% - When two z-scored blocks are joined side by side, each adds squared distance in proportion to its width.
%   - Block $k$'s share of $\mathbb{E}[d^2]$ is $d_k / \sum_{k'} d_{k'}$.
%   - A 2048-bit fingerprint joined to a 128-feature embedding gets $2048/2176 \approx 94\%$ of the squared distance.
%   - Links to: Chapter 6, "Similarity in a hybrid". There each z-scored block is divided by $\sqrt{d_k}$ under Euclidean distance, or by $d_k$ under Manhattan ($L_1$) distance, so width stops acting as weight.
%
% Errors compound from representation to similarity to the method using it:
% - Molecules the representation cannot tell apart (4.2.3) have $\mathbf{x}_i = \mathbf{x}_{i'}$, so $d = 0$ and $T = 1$ whatever their activities.
%   - Any neighbour-based method treats a real activity difference between them as noise.
% - Hash collisions (4.2.2) put different substructures on the same bit.
%   - $c$ then counts shared bits that are not shared chemistry, so $T$ is too high.
% - The chain: representation error, then similarity error, then wrong neighbours, then a wrong prediction, noise flag or applicability-domain call.
%   - Each step inherits the errors of the step before and adds its own.
% - Activity cliffs (defined in Chapter 2) are counted relative to one representation, one similarity measure and one threshold.
%   - The same dataset gives a different set of cliffs under a different representation, because "similar" is defined differently.
% - Why it matters: cliffs make the property look as if it changes sharply between similar molecules.
%   - Part of that may be the measure calling molecules "similar" on features that do not matter for the property.
%   - Links to: Chapter 2, where cliffs are described as easy to confuse with label noise.
%
% Similarity depends on which representation is used:
% - The same pair can be near under ECFP4 and far under physicochemical descriptors (Chapter 2 makes this point in general).
%   - Nothing in the data says which one is right for a given task.
% - Neighbour overlap between representations $A$ and $B$ for molecule $i$: $J_i = \frac{|\mathcal{N}^A_k(i)\cap\mathcal{N}^B_k(i)|}{|\mathcal{N}^A_k(i)\cup\mathcal{N}^B_k(i)|}$, averaged over $i$, with $k = 5$.
% - Agreement of all pairwise distances between two representations: $\rho_S(\{d^A_{ii'}\}, \{d^B_{ii'}\})$ over the same molecule pairs.
%   - Links to: "Different representations carry different information" (4.2.5). Do not repeat its numbers here.
% - Neighbourhood behaviour: a representation suits a property if small distances go with small property differences \citep{Patterson1996}.
%   - Test: rank correlation between $d(\mathbf{x}_i,\mathbf{x}_{i'})$ and $|y_i-y_{i'}|$ over sampled pairs.
% - Say which question is asked, because the answers can disagree:
%   - All pairs: does distance track property difference across the whole dataset?
%   - Nearest neighbours only: does predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours work?
%   - The nearest neighbours can carry signal even when the all-pairs correlation is near zero.
% - Similarity with no task attached is not wrong. It answers "do these molecules look alike?", not "will they behave alike on this property?".
%   - That gap motivates choosing features for the task (Chapter 5).
%
% Results slots (full runs only):
% - TODO(results): rank correlation of distance against absolute property difference over sampled pairs, per dataset and per representation; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): accuracy of predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours, next to the all-pairs correlation, same datasets; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): agreement between fingerprint distance and maximum-common-substructure distance, per fingerprint; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): number of molecules in activity cliffs at the MoleculeACE thresholds and at a looser threshold, per dataset and per representation; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): relative contrast $(D_{\max}-D_{\min})/D_{\min}$ per query molecule and skewness of $N_5$, on raw Tanimoto and on z-scored Euclidean; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): five-nearest-neighbour overlap $J$ between ECFP4, physicochemical descriptors and one learned embedding; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: two panels, one dataset. Left: absolute activity difference (y) against ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity (x), sampled pairs, binned by similarity (median and interquartile range per bin). Right: the same pairs with similarity under a second representation. Should show that high-similarity pairs still differ widely in activity, and that the two representations disagree about which pairs are similar.
% - DIAGRAM (optional): schematic of the weighted-average formula. Two fingerprints with relevant and irrelevant bits coloured, showing $T$ pulled toward $T_I$ as irrelevant on-bits increase.
%
% Literature gaps:
% - TODO(lit): search for literature stating that ECFP4 (Morgan radius 2) with Tanimoto is the default similarity in QSAR and virtual screening (a benchmark or survey).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing count-based (min/max) and binary Tanimoto on Morgan fingerprints for nearest-neighbour retrieval.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature measuring distance concentration or hubness on molecular fingerprints specifically.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on task-specific or feature-weighted molecular similarity (similarity weighted or learned for one property endpoint).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing the set of activity cliffs found in one dataset under different fingerprints or similarity measures.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how z-scoring sparse binary fingerprint bits changes Euclidean nearest neighbours.
%
% Links:
% - Why it matters: every method in "Consequences for methods that rely on similarity" (4.4) takes a similarity as input: noise detection by neighbours, prediction from neighbours, applicability domain, cluster-based splits.
%   - If the similarity is diluted, those methods inherit the dilution, however well they are designed.
% - Links to: "What a representation would need" (4.5). Requirement from this section: compute similarity on features chosen for the task, with each block's weight controlled rather than set by its width.
% - Links to: Chapter 6, "Similarity in a hybrid", which tests whether a hybrid chosen for the task gives a better similarity.
% ---- END NOTES 4.3 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Sec "Similarity extension".
While the hybrids are being tested as feature sets for standard predictive tasks, the original motivation for this whole adaptive feature selection proccess was the task of similarity. 
% TODO: need sources for this
Within small molecule research, the most standard way to measure how similar two molecules are is the Tanimoto coefficient between two ECFPs representations of those molecules. There's a handful of problems with this approach. Firstly, ECFPs do not indvidually distinguish each molecule. 

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Ch 6 Sec 6.2.
\subsubsection{Distance metrics for continuous molecular descriptors}

% [CR 939-952] the eight distance metrics, carried whole.
The choice of distance, or similarity metric has a strong impact on performance of distance-based noise estimation methods. We evaluate eight distance metrics appropriate for continuous molecular descriptors:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Euclidean ($L_2$): Standard geometric distance, $d(x, y) = \sqrt{\sum_i (x_i - y_i)^2}$
    \item Manhattan ($L_1$): Sum of absolute differences, $d(x, y) = \sum_i |x_i - y_i|$, more robust to outlier features
    \item Cosine: Angle-based similarity, $d(x, y) = 1 - \frac{x \cdot y}{\|x\| \|y\|}$, invariant to magnitude
    \item Chebyshev ($L_\infty$): Maximum absolute difference, $d(x, y) = \max_i |x_i - y_i|$
    \item Canberra: Weighted $L_1$ normalized by magnitude, $d(x, y) = \sum_i \frac{|x_i - y_i|}{|x_i| + |y_i|}$
    \item Correlation: $d(x, y) = 1 - \rho(x, y)$, where $\rho$ is the Pearson correlation
    \item Bray-Curtis: Ecological dissimilarity measure, $d(x, y) = \frac{\sum_i |x_i - y_i|}{\sum_i |x_i + y_i|}$
    \item Mahalanobis: Covariance-adjusted distance accounting for feature correlations
\end{itemize}

All features are standardised (zero mean, unit variance) before distance computation to ensure equal contribution across features with different relative scales.

% TODO: This subsection replaces the confirmation report's entire ch.4, 'Exploring techniques for
% TODO: learning with noisy labels for QSAR' [CR 666-776]. That study was preliminary; the outline
% TODO: keeps only its motivating finding here and defers the real measurement to ch.6.
% TODO: Not carried over: the 12-method table [CR 681-715], the HetGP kernel table [CR 725-740],
% TODO: the method-effectiveness table [CR 746-765], and the DivideMix / curriculum results.
% TODO: They are recoverable from Confirmation_Report/paper.tex if ch.6 wants them.
% [CR 670] the exploratory LNL study and its headline finding.
% TODO(xref): 'In Chapter 3' -- old ch.3 is now ch.3 (robustness and uncertainty); now a \ref.

Numerous studies have explored Learning with Noisy Labels (LNL) in the broader context of ML, as well as specifically for QSAR models. \citet{Song2022} provide a comprehensive review of Deep LNL, identifying five main approaches: (i) modifying architectures to better handle noise in training; (ii) employing regularization to reduce overfitting to noise; (iii) adjusting loss functions to be less sensitive to outliers or label flips; (iv) post-processing to adjust the weights for noisy data; and (v) sample selection to select presumably clean data to pre-train. \citet{Cortes2015} examined 12 different algorithms across 12 datasets and 10 noise levels, finding that no single algorithm was overall best at maintaining predictive performance in the presence of noise; certain algorithms perform better or worse at different levels of noise. This is a recurring theme in LNL research: there is no universal solution to handling noise with experimental endpoints.
% TODO make this flow
There are a wide variety of approaches to Learning with Noisy Labels (LNL), spanning sample selection, meta-learning, curriculum learning, and iterative refinement \citep{Song2022}. Cheminformatics data presents additional challenges in the form of high-dimensional molecular representations and dealing with a wide variety of chemical domains with various similarities and relationships. In this chapter, we explore a set of 12 LNL methods in the context of QSAR modeling. We examine the impact of uncertainty quantification, molecular distance metrics, and alternative loss functions in the hopes of improving performance under label noise. We tested 221 total configurations across LNL methods, loss functions, distance metrics, and molecular representations. Notably, we found that the inclusion of distance metrics substantially hurts performance and that molecular representation choice explains even less variance under LNL methods. We also identified the following LNL techniques which showed promise on the tasks at hand: heteroscedastic GP, curriculum learning, and sample selection methods. The work was meant to connect the representation and uncertainty quantification insights found in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} with more classical noise mitigation techniques as a means of determining broader research directions. 

% [CR 721] the twenty worst configurations all used a distance metric.
One of the most prominent outcomes was that distance-augmented methods performed significantly worse than ones that did not incorporate any sense of distance or molecular similarity. An ANOVA test was run on the different adapted factors and shows that distance metric usage is the dominant factor in variance in $R^2$.. When ranking the performance of methods, all 20 worst-performing configurations with respect to noise robustness used distance metrics, not a single one achieved both high baseline ($R^2 > 0.6$) and high noise retention ($> 80\%$). This pattern was observed across standard distance types (Euclidean, Manhattan, Cosine, Mahalanobis) and a chemistry-aware metric (Tanimoto coefficient). 

% [CR 779] why a single representation may lack the information distance needs.
% [XREF 2026-09-27] DONE: 'Building upon the results in Chapter 4' reworded as suggested here.
% NOTE: [CR 781-782], which sat between this paragraph and the next in the confirmation
% report, is now background in Sec 2.3. Cross-reference it rather than repeating it.
% [XREF 2026-09-27] NEW LANGUAGE: was 'Building upon the results in Chapter 4 which identified'.
Building upon the exploratory study above, which identified the use of distance or similarity measures as a major area of weakness with respect to learning with noisy labels and noise mitigation, we decided to investigate alternative featurization techniques. There are multiple possible explanations behind the poor performance of distance metrics, first being that a singular molecular representation lacks sufficient information for distance calculations, with and possibly even without noise. These representations all encode different types of molecular information, PDVs encode global molecular properties, ECFPs contain substructure information, and MPNNs such as MHG-GNNs capture 3D structural features. These single sources of data may not be sufficient for robust similarity estimation. Additionally, some of these representations are sparse and contain a large number of features that are not relevant to molecules of a given domain. 

% [CR 784] the uninformative-dimensions argument -- the hinge of the whole second half.
The main issue we identified here is that when a large proportion of dimensions are uninformative, similarity measures become dominated by those uninformative features, causing exponential growth in the number of irrelevant pairwise interactions as dimensionality increases. This is what we sought to address by developing a representation with informative features and low dimensionality. We do so by creating a budget-constrained hybrid representation, which takes high-importance features from a variety of sources. These hybrid representations concatenate the top-ranked features by importance from a selection of base representations. 


\subsection{Noise detection by neighbours}
