% - Defaults: $k = 10$, features standardised on the training molecules, Manhattan distance.
% - Repeat measurements of the same molecule are never used as each other's neighbours.
%   - Otherwise a molecule's own duplicate would agree with its label, and the score would partly compare the label with itself.
%
% Why it inherits the similarity flaw:
% - Two assumptions are stacked: the distance ranks the right molecules as neighbours, and those neighbours' labels are clean.
% - Links to: Section 4.2. Many features do not vary or are unrelated to the task, so part of the neighbour list is set by irrelevant features.
% - The neighbours come from the same noisy training set. When every label carries noise, $\bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(i)}$ carries noise too.
%   - Under independent Gaussian noise of variance $\sigma_\epsilon^2$, the noise variance of the neighbour mean is $\sigma_\epsilon^2/k$. It is smaller than the molecule's own $\sigma_\epsilon^2$, but not zero.
%   - This assumes the neighbours' true labels equal the molecule's true label. Any real label difference between neighbours adds to the error.
% - If all labels are noisy to a similar degree, there is no clean majority for a noisy label to stand out from. This was the confirmation report's own explanation.
% - Activity cliffs look like noise to these scores. A real, large label gap between near-identical molecules gives a high $r_i$ \citep{Maggiora2006}.
%   - Links to: the activity-cliff counts in Section 4.3. Every cliff molecule is a possible false alarm for a neighbour-based detector.
% - Why it matters: a detector that removes cliff molecules removes the molecules that carry the most structure-activity information.
% - Hubs: in high-dimensional spaces a few points appear in many other points' neighbour lists \citep{Radovanovic2010}.
%   - Consequence, not yet measured on these datasets: one noisy hub label shifts many neighbour means. The hub statistic is planned in Section 4.3.
% - Under some conditions on the data, the nearest and farthest distances become nearly equal as the number of features grows \citep{Beyer1999}. The neighbour list then carries little information.
%
% How the thesis tests it (methods, not results):
% - Noise is injected into training labels only, so the molecules whose labels were changed are known.
% - Detection quality is graded against the known changed molecules:
%   - Average precision: the area under the precision-recall curve for the score $r_i$. This is the main number because it needs no cut-off.
%   - Precision at $m$, where $m$ is the number of changed molecules: the share of the $m$ highest-scoring molecules whose label was really changed.
%   - AUROC: the probability that a changed molecule scores higher than an unchanged one. 0.5 means no better than chance.
% - Detection is not defined when every label is perturbed, for example Gaussian noise added to all labels, because no molecule is unchanged.
% - Downstream effect: recovery $= \frac{m_{\text{cleaned}} - m_{\text{noisy}}}{m_{\text{clean}} - m_{\text{noisy}}}$, where $m$ is the dataset's own metric (e.g. $R^2$, Spearman $\rho$, RMSE, sign-corrected).
%   - 1 = full recovery. 0 = no change. Below 0 = cleaning made the model worse.
%   - Recovery is not reported when the clean-to-noisy gap is below 2% of the clean score. A near-zero denominator makes the ratio meaningless.
% - The confirmation report used the same recovery ratio with $R^2$.
% - The space in which neighbours are found is varied separately from the model's input features:
%   - Tanimoto distance on ECFP4.
%   - Each model's two best single representations.
%   - The hybrid.
%   - A forest proximity: the share of trees in which two molecules land in the same leaf.
%   - $k \in \{5, 9, 10, 20\}$.
%   - Manhattan or Euclidean distance.
%   - Features optionally weighted by importance.
% - Planned control, not yet run: neighbours drawn at random. If fingerprint neighbours do no better than random ones, the distance adds nothing to detection.
% - Comparison methods that use no distance: a z-score filter on the labels alone, and out-of-fold model residuals.
% - Links to: Chapter 6, which recomputes these scores with neighbours found in a hybrid and under noise types other than Gaussian.
% - TODO(results): detection average precision and AUROC for each neighbour space and distance, against the random-neighbour control. Numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): recovery for neighbour-based removal, weighting and smoothing, against the methods that use no distance. Numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Bigger picture:
% - A neighbour-based detector can only be as good as the similarity it uses. Tuning $k$ or the removal cut-off cannot fix a neighbour list that is wrong.
% - The confirmation report found that methods using no distance held up better. That points at the distance, not at the idea of cleaning labels.
% - Why it matters: if the features that define a neighbour were chosen for the task, the neighbour list should improve. This motivates Section 4.5 and Chapter 5.
% - Links to: Section 4.4.1, the same pattern in the wider noisy-label study.
% - Links to: Section 4.4.3, other methods built on nearest neighbours.
% - DIAGRAM: one dataset at one noise level. x-axis: neighbour residual $r_i$. y-axis: size of the injected noise $|\epsilon_i|$. Activity-cliff molecules in a separate colour. Shows that noisy molecules and cliff molecules overlap.
% - DIAGRAM: detection average precision (y-axis) against noise level $\sigma$ (x-axis). One line per neighbour space: ECFP4 Tanimoto, best single representation, hybrid, random neighbours. A dashed line marks the chance level, which equals the share of changed labels.
% ---- END NOTES 4.4.2 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from the conclusions of old Ch 6 (confirmation report). The second
% paragraph describes hybrids built with greedy allocation, which is cut.
% [CR 1144,1146] the confirmation report's own honest limits on neighbour-based noise
% estimation and on hybrids. Survives the re-run because it is a qualitative claim.
% TODO(xref): 'conclusions of Chapter 4' -> the LNL study, now Sec 5.1.
Overall, we see in agreement with conclusions of Chapter 4 on the use of distance metrics in LNL methods, the concept of using a sample's neighbors to determine it's ground truth is flawed. It is likely all labels are too uniformly noisy for a methodology like this. Uncertainty-based methods show potential at lower noise, but their estimation performance degrades with higher noise. Sampling weighting methods generally proved effective, and one estimator using exponential sample weighting produced very strong recovery across both noise levels.  

Hybrid representations had mixed results. For sample removal, hybrids showed advantages at high noise, but struggled at low noise. They showed consistently positive recovery rates for neighbor-based estimation, which roughly falls in line with the idea that a low-dimension, high-quality information representation benefits neighbor-based reasoning. The feature budget in this experiment was restricted to 50-100 features, which may limit its advantages. This range was successful at standard molecular property prediction, but potentially not for certain noise mitigation or estimation tasks. Distance-based methods remain challenging under Gaussian noise regardless of representation, suggesting the core issue is the noise distribution rather than representation quality alone. This merits an investigation into mitigation of noise from other distributions or noise injection strategies.


\subsection{Other standard uses of similarity}
% OUTLINE 4.4.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.4.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope: the other routine jobs in cheminformatics that assume "similar molecules have similar properties". A flawed similarity (Sections 4.2 and 4.3) reaches all of them. For each: what it computes, where similarity enters, what goes wrong.
% - Already in prose: the noise-cleaning methods that use neighbours (4.4.1, 4.4.2). This section widens the argument beyond noise.
% Common thread:
% - All uses below rest on the similarity principle: structurally similar molecules tend to have similar properties \citep{Maggiora2014}.
% - The usual default similarity is Tanimoto on a binary fingerprint such as ECFP4 \citep{rogers2010, Bajusz2015}.
%   - $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{|\mathbf{a}\wedge\mathbf{b}|}{|\mathbf{a}\vee\mathbf{b}|}$; distance $d = 1 - T$.
% - The fingerprint is fixed before the task is known.
% - The same features and the same distance are used whatever property is being predicted.
% - So each use inherits the flaws from 4.2 and 4.3: features that never vary on the dataset, features irrelevant to the task, and representations that disagree about which molecules are neighbours.
% - Why it matters: these uses make decisions that are hard to check later: which molecules to buy, which predictions to trust, which molecules go in the test set.
% Similarity search and virtual screening:
% - Rank a library by similarity to one or more known actives; test the top of the list \citep{Willett1998}.
%   - Score for library molecule $i$ against query set $Q$, max fusion: $\max_{q\in Q} T(\mathbf{x}_i,\mathbf{x}_q)$.
% - Flaw it inherits: every fingerprint bit counts equally in $T$, including bits unrelated to activity at the target.
% - Neighbourhood behaviour: a descriptor is useful only if a small distance implies a small property difference \citep{Patterson1996}.
%   - DIAGRAM: $|y_i - y_j|$ against $d(i,j)$ for sampled pairs; a good descriptor leaves the top-left corner empty (large property gap at small distance). Reuse the planned 4.3 diagram (D4.1) rather than draw a new one.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how much ligand-based virtual screening performance changes with the choice of fingerprint and similarity coefficient, across benchmark target sets.
% k-nearest-neighbour prediction:
% - Predict a molecule's property from the labels of its $k$ nearest training molecules.
%   - $\hat{y}_i = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j\in N_k(i)} y_j$, where $N_k(i)$ is the set of $k$ training molecules closest to $i$ under $d$.
%   - Distance-weighted version: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j\in N_k(i)} w_{ij}\,y_j}{\sum_{j\in N_k(i)} w_{ij}}$, with $w_{ij} = 1/d(i,j)$.
% - The model has no parameters beyond $k$ and $d$.
% - So its accuracy is a direct test of the similarity itself.
% - In this thesis $k = 5$ by default (fewer only when a training fold has under 25 molecules; $k=\min(5,\lfloor\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{train}}}\rfloor)$).
% - Flaw it inherits: as dimension grows, the nearest and farthest points can become almost equally far away, so "nearest" carries little meaning \citep{Beyer1999}.
%   - Relative contrast: $\frac{d_{\max} - d_{\min}}{d_{\min}} \to 0$ as dimension $\to \infty$, under the conditions stated in \citet{Beyer1999}.
% - Hubness: in high dimensions a few points appear in the $k$-neighbour lists of many others \citep{Radovanovic2010}.
%   - Implication: a few labels, and their noise, are copied into many predictions.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. The similarity objective of the hybrid search is this prediction: distance-weighted, five neighbours, scored by the Spearman correlation between predicted and true labels on held-out molecules. kNN is also one of the downstream models in Chapter 5.
% Applicability domain:
% - The set of inputs where a model's predictions can be trusted.
% - Usually defined by distance to the training set \citep{Tropsha2010}.
%   - Common form: $D(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j\in N_k(\mathbf{x})} d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_j)$ over training molecules; flag the prediction if $D(\mathbf{x}) > \theta$.
% - Evidence it can work: similarity to the training set separates accurate from inaccurate QSAR predictions \citep{Sheridan2004}.
% - Flaw it inherits: if task-irrelevant features dominate the distance, $D$ measures structural novelty, not how likely the model is to be wrong.
% - How it is checked in Chapter 6: Spearman correlation between $D(\mathbf{x})$ and absolute error $|y - \hat{y}|$ on held-out molecules, within each fold, then the spread across folds.
% - Why it matters: an applicability domain is how users and regulators decide whether to believe a prediction. TODO(lit): search for the OECD principles for the validation of QSAR models, principle 3 (a defined domain of applicability).
% - TODO(results): distance-against-error correlation for the base representations and the hybrid; numbers come from the full runs (Chapter 6).
% Activity cliffs:
% - A pair of structurally similar molecules with a large difference in activity \citep{Maggiora2006, Stumpfe2012}.
%   - Rule: pair $(i,j)$ is a cliff if $\mathrm{sim}(i,j) \ge \tau$ and $|y_i - y_j| \ge \delta$.
%   - Structure-activity landscape index: $\mathrm{SALI}_{ij} = \frac{|y_i - y_j|}{1 - \mathrm{sim}(i,j)}$ \citep{Guha2008}.
%   - One benchmark uses similarity above 0.9 on any of three similarity measures and at least a tenfold potency difference \citep{vanTilborg2022}. Check the exact definition in the paper before quoting.
% - Cliffs are where QSAR models make their largest errors, and similarity-based methods do no better there \citep{Maggiora2006, vanTilborg2022, Dablander2023}.
% - Two-way dependence on the representation:
%   - Which pairs count as cliffs depends on $\mathrm{sim}$. A different fingerprint gives a different set of cliffs.
%   - A cliff under ECFP4 may be two molecules the fingerprint cannot tell apart (links to: 4.2.2 hash collisions; 4.2.3 molecules the representation cannot tell apart).
% - Why it matters: when only neighbours are consulted, a true cliff looks the same as a noisy label.
%   - So neighbour-based noise detection (4.4.2) will flag true cliffs as noise.
% - Links to: Chapter 6 reports kNN prediction error on cliff and non-cliff molecules separately. Its cliff rule is defined in the Chapter 6 methods, not here.
% Clustering and clustering-based splits:
% - Clustering groups molecules by fingerprint distance.
%   - Example: Butina clustering picks cluster centres with a Tanimoto cutoff \citep{Butina1999}.
% - Scaffold splits group molecules by Bemis-Murcko scaffold \citep{Bemis1996}.
%   - They avoid the fingerprint but still define "similar" by one structural rule.
% - Both are used to build train/test splits so test molecules are not near-copies of training ones.
% - Flaw it inherits: if the distance does not track the property, a cluster split can be hard or easy for reasons unrelated to structure.
%   - So the reported generalisation gap depends on the representation used to split, not only on the model.
% - Links to: 5.3.1 Datasets and splitting (the splits in this thesis are group-aware; say which grouping is used).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing scaffold, cluster and time splits for QSAR evaluation, and how much the estimated error changes between them.
% Diversity selection:
% - Pick a subset of a library that covers chemical space, for screening or for a training set.
%   - MaxMin: repeatedly add $\arg\max_{i\notin S}\min_{j\in S} d(i,j)$ to the selected set $S$ \citep{Snarey1997}.
% - Flaw it inherits: "diverse" under a fixed fingerprint means diverse in bits, not in the property.
%   - A subset can be diverse in fingerprint space and redundant for the task.
% - Limit: a task-aware distance needs labels. Diversity selection before any data exists is a case the hybrid cannot help.
% Federated and multi-site prediction:
% - FLuID is a federated scheme in which each member's model acts as a teacher and knowledge is shared through distillation \citep{Hanser2025}; general review of federated learning for molecules: \citep{Hanser2023}.
% - In the implementation used in this thesis, each site's prediction is weighted by how close the query molecule is to that site's training data (mean distance to 8 nearest neighbours in the site's own feature space).
%   - TODO(lit): confirm in \citet{Hanser2025} how the published method weights each site's prediction by distance, and which distance it uses.
% - Flaw it inherits: the weighting is only as good as the distance. The site chosen as "closest" may not be the one that predicts best.
% - Links to: 5.3.5 Applied settings: OPIG and federated.
% Summary for the section:
% - Every use above takes the fingerprint and the distance as given.
% - None of them asks whether the features are relevant to the task.
% - The shared remedy is to change what goes into the distance, not the method built on top of it.
% - Why it matters: one change to the representation reaches all of these uses at once. Whether it helps each one is an empirical question for Chapter 6.
% - Links to: 4.5 What a representation would need (features chosen for the task, irrelevant ones removed before any distance is taken); Chapter 6, Similarity in a hybrid, which tests kNN prediction, applicability domain and cliffs in the hybrid space.
% - DIAGRAM: one schematic. A base fingerprint box, then a distance box, then arrows fanning out to search, kNN, applicability domain, cliffs, splits, diversity, federated weighting. Mark the single shared input to show that one flaw reaches all of them.
% ---- END NOTES 4.4.3 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{4.4.3: kNN, applicability domain, cliff detection, clustering, federated weighting.}


\section{What a representation would need}
% OUTLINE 4.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Role of this section:
% - Short bridge section: turn the flaws in 4.2-4.4 into a list of requirements, then say the adaptive hybrid representation is built to meet them.
% - One requirement per flaw; each requirement is a property of the feature set, not of a model.
% - Already in prose/comments: the origin story and the search axes (brain-dump text). Use the origin story here; leave the axes to Chapter 5.
% - DIAGRAM: three-column table or flow graphic. Column 1 = flaw (and the section it came from). Column 2 = requirement. Column 3 = where Chapter 5 handles it. One row per requirement below.
%
% The underlying assumption being repaired:
% - Similarity-based methods assume similar molecules have similar properties; Patterson et al. called this neighbourhood behaviour \citep{Patterson1996}.
% - That assumption is only as good as the space the distance is taken in.
% - The flaws in 4.2-4.3 are flaws of the space, not of the assumption itself.
% - So the proposed fix is to change the space (the features), not to drop similarity.
% - Links to: 4.3 (distance against activity difference), 4.4 (methods that inherit the flaw).
%
% Requirement 1, task-specific:
% - A fixed fingerprint such as ECFP encodes every substructure it finds, whatever the target \citep{rogers2010}.
% - Which features matter depends on the property being predicted.
% - The same molecule pair can be close for one property and far for another.
% - Requirement: features chosen for the task, where the task is a supervised model fit on a training set with labels $\mathbf{y}$.
% - Requirement: features chosen from training data only (inside each fold), so the choice never sees the test molecules.
% - Links to: 4.2.1 (features that do not vary), 5.2.1 (from base representations to a hybrid).
%
% Requirement 2, compact (irrelevant features removed before any distance):
% - Squared Euclidean distance splits over features: $d^2(\mathbf{x}_i,\mathbf{x}_j) = \sum_{r\in R}(x_{ir}-x_{jr})^2 + \sum_{u\in U}(x_{iu}-x_{ju})^2$, with $R$ = task-relevant features and $U$ = irrelevant ones.
% - The second sum adds variation with no relation to $\mathbf{y}$.
% - The more features in $U$, the larger its share of every distance.
% - Under broad conditions, as the number of dimensions grows, the nearest and farthest neighbours of a query become almost equally far away \citep{Beyer1999}.
% - Their measure is relative contrast: $(d_{\max}-d_{\min})/d_{\min} \to 0$ as dimension grows.
% - A related high-dimensional effect: a few points become the nearest neighbour of many others (hubness) \citep{Radovanovic2010}.
% - Tanimoto on bit vectors: $T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \frac{c}{a+b-c}$, with $a$, $b$ = bits set in each molecule and $c$ = bits set in both \citep{Bajusz2015}.
%   - A bit that is 0 in every molecule does not change $T$.
%   - A bit set in both molecules of a pair adds 1 to $a$, $b$ and $c$: $T \to \frac{c+1}{a+b-c+1}$.
%   - That moves the pair closer to $T=1$; a bit set in almost every molecule does this to almost every pair and compresses the range of similarities.
%   - So near-constant features are not harmless for Tanimoto, even though all-zero ones are.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how irrelevant or noisy features reduce k-nearest-neighbour accuracy (feature weighting or feature selection for kNN).
% - Requirement: a budget $B$ = total number of features kept, far smaller than the summed width $\sum_k d_k$ of the base representations.
% - Requirement: $B$ is not fixed in advance; different datasets and models may prefer different sizes, so $B$ is searched.
% - Links to: 4.2.1, 4.3, 5.2.2 (the search space), 5.2.3 (why each axis is swept).
%
% Requirement 3, several representations, not one:
% - Which representation performs best varies with the dataset and the model \citep{Deng2023}.
% - Base representations disagree about which molecules are neighbours (4.2.5).
% - Some base representations cannot tell certain molecules apart (4.2.3).
% - Requirement: draw features from $K$ base representations $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$, so one representation's blind spot can be covered by another.
% - Requirement: decide how the budget is shared between representations, $b_k$ with $\sum_k b_k = B$.
% - The thesis tests two ways of sharing it: a quota per representation, and one pooled ranking of all features weighted by how well each representation performs alone. Chapter 5 compares them.
% - How many representations enter the hybrid at all is a separate choice, also searched.
% - Result: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$, $|S_k| = b_k$.
% - Under a quota, $S_k$ = the $b_k$ features of representation $k$ with the highest importance $s^{(k)}_j$.
% - Plain concatenation $[\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(K)}]$ meets this requirement but fails requirement 2.
% - So combining and filtering have to happen together.
% - Closest prior work fuses fingerprints by feature importance \citep{Turkina2025}; point to 5.1.1 for the comparison, do not repeat it here.
% - Links to: 5.1.1 (combining representations: what exists), 5.1.3 (the adaptive hybrid representation).
%
% Requirement 4, usable both as model input and as a distance space:
% - The same feature set should serve a downstream model and a nearest-neighbour calculation.
% - Distance in the hybrid: $d_{\mathbf{H}}(i,j) = d(\mathbf{H}_{i,:}, \mathbf{H}_{j,:})$, for any distance from 4.3.
% - Two ways to get a similarity space: (a) build a hybrid for prediction and take distances in it; (b) search for the hybrid that scores well at similarity directly.
% - For (b), the score is how well each molecule's activity is predicted from its five nearest neighbours in the hybrid.
% - Neighbours come only from other scaffold groups, so a molecule is not predicted from its own close analogues.
% - Neighbours are weighted by inverse distance: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} w_{ij}\, y_j}{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} w_{ij}}$, $w_{ij} = 1/d_{\mathbf{H}}(i,j)$.
% - No downstream model is fit for this score.
% - Score for regression: Spearman $\rho(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$.
% - Score for binary classification: $2(\mathrm{AUROC}-0.5)$, so that chance is 0, as for a correlation.
% - A second similarity-type score: Spearman correlation between Euclidean distance in the hybrid and maximum-common-substructure distance, over pairs of molecules.
% - Links to: 6.2.1 (similarity in a hybrid), 4.4.2 (noise detection by neighbours).
%
% Requirement 5, readable where the base features are readable:
% - Learned embeddings cannot be read chemically (4.2.4); descriptors and substructure bits can.
% - Requirement: record which features were chosen and from which representation, so a chemist can inspect them.
% - That record is the blueprint: the list of (representation $k$, feature $j$) pairs in $\mathbf{H}$ and the allocation $(b_1,\dots,b_K)$.
% - Why it matters: selected descriptors or substructure bits can point to chemistry linked to the property, or show whether the selection is chemically sensible.
% - Links to: 5.1.4 and 5.2.6 (blueprints).
%
% What the requirements do not ask for:
% - No new representation learning; existing base representations are reused as they are.
% - No particular model; the hybrid is an input, and any model can be fit on it.
% - Not limited to small molecules; any supervised task with several vector representations of each sample qualifies.
% - Links to: 5.1.5 (beyond small molecules).
%
% What feature selection cannot fix (state honestly, carry to limitations):
% - A selected hashed bit still merges every substructure that hashes to it (4.2.2); selection cannot split a collision.
% - Molecules identical in every base representation stay identical in the hybrid (4.2.3).
% - Selection needs enough labelled molecules to estimate importance; small datasets limit it.
% - Links to: 5.10 (limitations).
%
% Bridge to Chapter 5 (origin story):
% - The first version selected task-relevant features only to compute similarity.
% - The selected feature sets also worked as ordinary model inputs in that proof of concept.
% - That made the selection itself the method: the adaptive hybrid representation.
% - Questions this leaves, answered in Chapter 5: how to score a feature ($s^{(k)}_j$); how to split the budget across representations ($b_k$); how to score a candidate hybrid; how large $B$ should be.
% - Why it matters: Chapter 4 argues the space is the problem; Chapter 5 builds a space from the task; Chapter 6 tests whether that space makes similarity-based noise handling work.
% ---- END NOTES 4.5 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Sec "Similarity extension" (author notes).
% Similarity background
% TODO: find compass file on this

% Just need to formalise this

% TODO: Go back to original LNL experiments (explained in confirmation report, find this) - copy over the evidence that similarity-based approaches just automatically fail
% I think that molecular similarity as we often do it (ECFP4/Tanimoto or other variations of that) is flawed and applications that this concept of similarity (in my case noise detection and mitigation) suffer as a result

 % Context for similarity and my motivating hypothesis: I think that molecular similarity as we often do it (ECFP4/Tanimoto or other variations of that) is flawed and applications that this concept of similarity (in my case noise detection and mitigation) suffer as a result
 %    - Take a look at ECFP4 as an example. Let's say you're working with molecules in a particular domain, how many of those features are going to useless? First, you can get numbers on in a given dataset how many features have no difference between the molecules. Some will differ, but will have nothing to do with the task at hand. There's definitely useful features in that set of 2048, but they're probably quite sparse, and when you take a similarity metrix that does a vector by vector comparison, and some of your features are irrelevant, you have exponentially more irrelevant pieces of information (is this true? phrase this better). I thought a much more efficient approach would be to select useful features for that task (I define task as some kind of supervised model fit on a training set) and you specify how many features you want, and you use that to calculate similarity. Now I implemented a proof-of-concept version of this and realised that these customised feature sets (hybrids) work as standard inputs to the model, irregardless of similarity. Then the main question was how on earth do I select the best features? How do I score how meaningful a feature is (feature importance), if I have lots of base reps, how do I know how many features from each to select (allocation strategies), once I have a candidate, how do I score it to see if its useful (internal evaluator). Then the other axes I test for the purposes of seeing if this works: does it work on different downstream evaluators, and what happens when I modify/control the budget (number of features). 
 %        - Compare to other feature selection algorithms but explain why this is different - you could just pool all the features (and I've tried this, pull up my results on it), but it's not actually as effective as looking at them on a representation-level. It turns out if you have 10 reps you're considering, and you limit it to the top 2,3, 5, SOME datasets prefer that. Others prefer all 10. It's more complex than a simple feature selection algorithm if you really want to optimise it, and my results show why
 %        - This use case is specifically taking existing reps that say vastly different things about the molecule (PDV vs ECFP4 vs graph based embeddings vs sequence based embeddings) and tailor selecting a set of features from all of these to the very specific task at hand
 %    - Two concepts of using similarity: make a normal hybrid and use it for similarity OR modify the objective that the hybrid search optimises for to make it similarity-aligned (called "similarity hybrids")


\section{Conclusions}
% OUTLINE 4.6: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 4.6 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Purpose of this section:
% - Short. One paragraph per research question from the introduction, then one bridge paragraph to Chapter 5.
% - It restates findings. It adds no new evidence. Citations should be ones already used earlier in the chapter.
% - The section is empty in thesis.tex now, so none of this repeats existing prose.
% - Most chapter results are not final yet. Each "claim to restate" below is written as a claim the full runs must support before it goes in.
%
% The argument in one line:
% - A fixed representation, compared with a fixed measure, cannot know which features matter for a given property.
% - Every method that compares molecules to each other inherits that limit.
% - The similarity assumption holds only relative to a chosen representation and measure \citep{Maggiora2014}.
% - The chapter's claim: the usual representation and measure are a poor choice for the datasets studied.
%
% Question 1: do a standard representation's features vary, and can it tell molecules apart?
% - Claim to restate: on a given dataset, many features of a general-purpose fingerprint are constant or nearly constant.
%   - Near-constant, defined once: feature $j$ of representation $k$ is near-constant if $\max_v \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathbb{1}[X^{(k)}_{ij} = v] \ge 0.95$.
%   - Fraction of near-constant features: $\phi^{(k)} = \frac{1}{d_k}\sum_{j=1}^{d_k} \mathbb{1}[\text{feature } j \text{ is near-constant}]$.

% - Claim to restate: several representations give different molecules identical vectors.

\subsection{Combining representations: what exists}
% OUTLINE 5.1.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.1.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: conjoint fingerprints, structural plus bioactivity fingerprints, fingerprint fusion by importance, early/late/intermediate fusion, kernel learning, the ~5,000-feature example, "no single representation wins", forward compatibility.
% Bib gap: these keys are cited in the existing prose but have no entry in refs.bib: Stahlschmidt2022, Hughes1968, Bellman1961, Steyaert2023, Wang2021, Zhang2024, Gonen2011, FernandezDelgado2014, Cichonska2018, Valsecchi2020. Entries for Stahlschmidt2022 and Hughes1968 are returned with these notes. The rest still need finding.
% What "combining" means here:
% - Many prior combinations of molecular representations are plain concatenation.
%   - The features of two or more representations go side by side in one matrix.
%   - $\mathbf{X}_{\text{concat}} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}, \mathbf{X}^{(2)}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times D}$, where $D = \sum_{k=1}^{K} d_k$.
%   - The multimodal fusion literature calls this "early fusion". One downstream model is trained on the joined matrix \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}.
%   - Nothing is removed, so width grows with every representation added.
% - Fusion vocabulary, for one line in the text: early fusion joins inputs, intermediate fusion joins learned internal representations, late fusion joins predictions \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}.
%   - The method in this thesis works at the input level, like early fusion.
%   - The difference: it selects features before it joins them.
% - Prior molecular work that combines and filters at the same time: fusing six fingerprints by feature importance \citep{Turkina2025}.
%   - That work stays with fingerprints, where every feature has a name.
%   - The hybrid also takes learned embeddings and descriptor sets.
%   - TODO(lit): read Turkina2025 and check whether it splits the feature budget per fingerprint or ranks all fingerprint bits together. This decides how close it is to the hybrid.
% The observation that motivates the method:
% - Working hypothesis: a concatenation of two representations usually scores between its two parts, not above the better one.
%   - As an inequality for representations A and B under a higher-is-better metric $m$: $\min(m_A, m_B) \le m_{A \oplus B} \le \max(m_A, m_B)$, where $A \oplus B$ is the concatenation.
%   - Proposed reason: concatenation adds the irrelevant features of both parts along with the useful ones.
%   - The weaker representation's irrelevant features dilute the stronger one's useful features.
% - TODO(results): where each two-representation concatenation lands relative to its two parts (per dataset, per model); numbers come from the full runs.
%   - DIAGRAM: one panel per dataset. x-axis lists representation pairs. Each pair shows three points: score of A, score of B, score of the concatenation. A shaded band spans the two single scores, so it is clear whether the concatenation lands inside or outside it.
% - Link to dimensionality: with a fixed number of samples $n$, adding features past some point lowers the mean accuracy of a classifier \citep{Hughes1968}.
%   - Ratio to state in words: samples per feature $= n / D$.
%   - At a few hundred molecules and thousands of features, this ratio is below 1.
% - Link to distance: if most concatenated features are irrelevant, a distance over all $D$ features is mostly made of irrelevant differences.
%   - Euclidean case: $d(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')^2 = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{R}} (x_j - x'_j)^2 + \sum_{j \notin \mathcal{R}} (x_j - x'_j)^2$, where $\mathcal{R}$ is the set of task-relevant features.
%   - The second sum grows with the number of irrelevant features.
%   - Links to: Chapter 4, sections on flaws in similarity (same argument for a single fingerprint). Keep this to one bullet here.
% Feature selection, the other half of "combine and filter":
% - Feature selection keeps a subset $S \subseteq \{1, \dots, D\}$ with $|S| = B \ll D$.
%   - It keeps original features; it does not transform them.
% - Standard three-way split of selection methods \citep{Guyon2003, Saeys2007}:
%   - Filter: score each feature without a model (for example, correlation or mutual information with $\mathbf{y}$), then keep the top $B$.
%   - Wrapper: search over subsets and score each subset by training a model on it \citep{Kohavi1997}.
%   - Embedded: the model selects while it trains (for example, an L1 penalty or tree split gain).
% - Where the hybrid sits in that split (your own framing, not a claim from the literature):
%   - Within each representation, features are ranked by model-based importance scores (tree split gain, SHAP values, DeepLIFT attributions). This is embedded in character.
%   - Across representations, budgets and allocations are compared by cross-validated scores. This is wrapper in character.
% - Main structural difference from textbook feature selection: selection is done per representation, after the budget is split across representations.
%   - Budget split: $b_k$ features for representation $k$, with $\sum_k b_k = B$.
%   - Hybrid: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$, where $S_k$ holds the top $b_k$ features of representation $k$ ranked by $s^{(k)}_j$.
%   - Pooled selection, by contrast: one ranking over all $D$ features.
%   - In a pooled ranking, a wide representation (for example, a 2048-bit fingerprint) can crowd out a narrow one (for example, about 200 descriptors).
%   - Importance scores from different representations are on different scales. A pooled ranking compares numbers that are not directly comparable.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on the bias of tree-based feature-importance scores toward features of different type or cardinality (e.g. split-gain and impurity-importance bias toward high-cardinality or continuous features).
%   - One of the allocation methods in the search does use a pooled step: each representation first contributes a candidate slice sized by its standalone performance, then all candidates are ranked together and the top $B$ kept. The comparison belongs in Section 5.2, not here.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on group feature selection and structured sparsity (group lasso, when features come in predefined blocks). This is the nearest classical relative of per-representation budgets.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on multi-view feature selection (selecting features jointly across several views of the same samples), especially at small sample sizes.
% - TODO(lit): too broad for one citation. Background review of feature-selection methods in cheminformatics and QSAR (filter, wrapper, embedded, as applied to fingerprints and descriptors).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on feature selection applied to concatenated molecular fingerprints or descriptors before QSAR modelling (whether selection after concatenation recovers or beats the best single representation).
% - TODO(lit): find and verify Xu, Tao and Xu 2013 (multi-view learning survey; naive concatenation of views overfits on small samples) and Ponzoni et al. 2017 (hybridising feature selection and feature learning). The old outline names both. Neither is in refs.bib.
% - Note for the text: selection must happen inside each cross-validation fold to avoid optimistic bias. The argument and its citations belong in Section 5.2 or the Discussion, not here.
% Why it matters:
% - Why it matters: concatenation alone does not solve "no single representation wins". A bigger matrix is not a better one if most of what is added is irrelevant to the task.
% - Why it matters: classical feature selection does not know the features come in blocks with different meaning, scale and width. The hybrid keeps the block structure and filters inside each block.
% - Why it matters: selection keeps the original features, so every retained feature traces back to one representation.
%   - This is not true of PCA or learned fusion.
%   - Links to: 5.1.4 Blueprints and what they tell a chemist.
% - Gap to state: little prior molecular work combines learned embeddings (graph, language model) with fingerprints and descriptors under a fixed feature budget chosen per task.
%   - TODO(lit): search for molecular property prediction studies that select features from concatenated learned embeddings plus fingerprints plus descriptors, to confirm this gap before stating it.
% - Links to: 5.1.2 Other ways to use several representations (PCA, ensembling, classical selectors compared directly).
% - Links to: 5.1.3 The adaptive hybrid representation (the method this section motivates).
% - Links to: 5.2.1 From base representations to a hybrid (formal definition of $b_k$ and $S_k$).
% ---- END NOTES 5.1.1 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] was old Sec "Review of existing approaches".
% TODO: review
% [CR 786-788] the confirmation report's related-work paragraphs (conjoint fingerprints,
% bio-activity fingerprints, feature-importance fusion).
Prior research in this field shows promise, with conjoint fingerprints achieving stronger performance on binding affinity prediction than alone across a range of models \citep{Xie2020}. Augmenting structural-based fingerprints like ECFP4 with bio-activity based fingerprints has also been successful at improving both predictive performance and scaffold-hopping \citep{Laufktter2019}. A very similar fusion strategy, in which features are selected from six non-hashed fingerprints and are combined by feature importance score within individual models, consistently improves predictive accuracy over the original fingerprints \citep{Turkina2025}. 

% TODO: review
The majority of the work in this space deals with combining relatively standard fingerprints and linear representations like SMILES \citep{Xie2020, Laufktter2019}. However, graph-based representations capture important structural information that is often lost in fingerprints. We explore using GNN embeddings within these hybrid, fusion methodologies. We further expand on the mechanisms used to compute feature importance, concatenate features, and use these hybrids within similarity quantification and noise mitigation tasks. We also explore adding on smaller sets of structural features to a single base representation, referred to as augmentation. 

% [KB 19] the KIRBy draft's fusion taxonomy: early/late/intermediate fusion, MKL.
% Citation keys are KIRBy's own and are NOT in refs.bib.
% TODO: review and the li and deng defini
So far, the most common strategy for combining feature sets has been concatenation, which requires only a single downstream model \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}. It works best when feature sets are homogeneous. When they are not, concatenation degrades \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}, and \cite{Deng2023} called the practice ``misleading'' when the goal is to measure the contribution of individual representation types. Late fusion trains a separate model per source and aggregates predictions \citep{Steyaert2023}, avoiding the dimensionality problem. But cross-source feature interactions are lost. To recover cross-source interactions requires intermediate fusion (MOGONET \citep{Wang2021}, cross-attention \citep{Zhang2024}), but at the cost of end-to-end training on all sources; adding a new source means retraining from scratch. No interpretable feature subsets emerge from multiple kernel learning \citep{Gonen2011}, which operates entirely at the kernel level. There remains, as Steyaert et al.\ noted, no decisive evidence that any of these strategies is broadly superior \citep{Steyaert2023}.

% [KB 21] the dimensionality argument: ~5000-d input on a few hundred samples.
% TODO: review
In practice, most research groups default to one representation and ignore the rest. ECFP is common in small molecules, while deep learning groups may opt for embeddings like MoLFormer or ChemBERTa. ESM-2 embeddings are common for antibodies, and can be broken up by chain. While there is some research that compares these representations, there is not much that focuses on combining them beyond simple concatenation. Concatenating just three standard sources, however (ECFP4 at 2,048 dimensions, ESM-2 at 1,280, Mordred descriptors at ${\sim}1{,}600$), gives a ${\sim}5{,}000$-dimensional input for a dataset that may contain only a few hundred labeled samples. At these sample-to-feature ratios, more features mean worse predictions \citep{Hughes1968, Bellman1961}. Models sensitive to distance, such as kNN and SVM, are affected most severely \citep{FernandezDelgado2014}.

% [KB 23] forward compatibility as a design requirement.
The pace of new model development shows no sign of slowing, and extends well beyond small molecules into protein language models \citep{Lin2023, Elnaggar2022}, encoders built for antibodies \citep{Ruffolo2021, Olsen2022}, histopathology foundation models \citep{ElNahhas2025}, and gene expression. First, these new models may prefer different feature sets, and that's what this methodology can provide. But beyond that, new models can be fit and converted into frozen embeddings and used as further input into this farmework. 

% TODO: Outline Sec 5.2 also names Xu, Tao and Xu 2013 (naive concatenation of views overfits on
% TODO: small samples), Valsecchi et al. 2020 (prediction-level fusion) and Ponzoni et al. 2017
% TODO: (hybridizing feature selection and feature learning). Xie et al. 2020 is already cited above.

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Ch 5 Introduction.
% [KB 15] the 'no single representation wins' evidence base, md -> tex.
A comparison of 62,820 models in molecular property prediction benchmarks showed that classical RDKit2D descriptors matched or outperformed learned features from graph neural networks and pretrained language models on the majority of datasets \citep{Deng2023}. Descriptor-based models occupied 73\% of top-three positions on 11 benchmarks \citep{Jiang2021}, and in 1,310 ChEMBL assays, feedforward networks on ECFP fingerprints outperformed graph convolutions and SMILES-based recurrent models \citep{Mayr2018}. The input often matters more than the model. Yet we still lack a systematic way to exploit this. No single representation wins. The same pattern holds outside small-molecule chemistry: no single protein language model consistently achieves the highest performance on all tasks \citep{Kenlay2024, Schmirler2024}; for antibodies, general-purpose models sometimes outperform those built for the domain \citep{Kenlay2024}. In computational pathology, no individual foundation model dominates on clinical endpoints \citep{ElNahhas2025}, and in cancer genomics, integrating additional omics layers can negatively impact performance when the data types are not properly combined \citep{Steyaert2023}.

% [KB 17] the breadth of the representation landscape.
For small molecules alone, the landscape now includes extended-connectivity fingerprints, physicochemical descriptor sets exceeding 1,600 dimensions, graph neural network embeddings \citep{Yang2019, Fang2022}, language models trained on billions of SMILES strings \citep{ross2022}, image-based encodings (molecules rendered as 2D images) \citep{Zeng2022}, and stereoelectronics-infused molecular graphs \citep{Boiko2025}. The same is true for proteins (ESM-2 \citep{Lin2023}, ProtTrans \citep{Elnaggar2022}), antibodies (AntiBERTy \citep{Ruffolo2021}, AbLang \citep{Olsen2022}), histopathology \citep{ElNahhas2025}, and multi-omics cancer profiles \citep{Steyaert2023}. A recent review counted over 40 molecular foundation models published between 2020 and 2024 \citep{Song2026}. Increasingly, new models appear faster than we can compare them \citep{Bommasani2021}. The choice is not getting simpler. Different sources presumably encode distinct aspects of the same biology. Noisy or incomplete on their own, single data modalities somehow give more sensitive and robust predictions when combined with independent signals from other modalities \citep{Steyaert2023}. An ensemble of frozen embeddings from the top four pathology foundation models, El Nahhas et al.\ found, achieved a mean AUROC 1.2\% higher than the best individual model on 31 clinical tasks \citep{ElNahhas2025}.

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Ch 5 Sec 5.7.
% [KB 133] the discussion paragraph on why compression helps -- information bottleneck.
% TODO: this is Discussion material; it may belong in Sec 6.3 (Why it helps) or ch.8.
Concatenation, the most common method for combining feature sets, gives the full pool that KIRBy starts from, but on biological sample sizes it can degrade rather than help \citep{Bellman1961, Stahlschmidt2022}. What late fusion gains by training separate models per source \citep{Steyaert2023} it loses in cross-source feature interactions. Those interactions can be recovered through intermediate fusion (MOGONET \citep{Wang2021}, cross-attention \citep{Zhang2024}), but only at the cost of retraining whenever a new source appears. At the kernel level, multiple kernel learning \citep{Gonen2011} (e.g., pairwiseMKL for drug bioactivity \citep{Cichonska2018}) can learn source weights, but no interpretable feature-level selections result. Consensus methods \citep{Valsecchi2020} combine predictions, not features, and so cannot exploit cases where one source's noise is another's signal. Instead, KIRBy works at the feature level: it selects features that span source boundaries, and because any new encoder's output is just another matrix in the pool, the approach is modular by construction. Perhaps the closest analogy in the optimization literature is the CASH problem \citep{Thornton2013} (the joint selection of algorithm and hyperparameters), extended here to include the input encoding as an additional search dimension. Tishby and Zaslavsky \citep{Tishby2015} showed why compression helps: the information bottleneck principle states that optimal representations maximize mutual information with the target while minimizing mutual information with the input. In effect, the budget forces the model to keep only what matters. The feature count controls the trade-off between informativeness and dimensionality.


\subsection{Other ways to use several representations}
% OUTLINE 5.1.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.1.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope: the other ways to use several representations at once, and how each one differs from selecting features.
% - Already in prose (end of 5.1.1): concatenation, late fusion, intermediate fusion, multiple kernel learning, consensus methods. The bullets below add the maths and the merge-versus-select contrast only.
%
% Shared setup:
% - Pool of $K$ base representations for the same $n$ molecules: $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$.
% - Full concatenation: $\mathbf{X} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times D}$, with $D = \sum_k d_k$.
% - Every method below answers one question: how to get from $D$ features, often thousands, to something a model can learn from with $n$ labels, often hundreds.
% - Three families merge something. Dimensionality reduction merges features. Ensembling keeps representations separate and merges predictions. Multiple kernel learning keeps them separate and merges similarities.
% - A fourth family, selection, keeps a subset of the original features. The hybrid belongs to this family.
% - DIAGRAM: four panels, same $K=3$ representations as input to each. (a) PCA: all features flow into $B$ mixed components, one model. (b) Ensemble: one model per representation, predictions combined. (c) Multiple kernel learning: one kernel per representation, weighted sum, one kernel model. (d) Hybrid: $b_k$ original features taken from each representation, one model. Mark in each panel what the model sees and what must be computed for a new molecule.
%
% Dimensionality reduction, which merges features:
% - PCA on the centred pool: $\mathbf{C} = \frac{1}{n-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$. Keep the top $B$ eigenvectors $\mathbf{W}_B \in \mathbb{R}^{D\times B}$. New inputs: $\mathbf{Z} = \mathbf{X}\mathbf{W}_B \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times B}$ \citep{Jolliffe2016}.
% - First direction: $\mathbf{w}_1 = \arg\max_{\|\mathbf{w}\|=1} \mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{C}\mathbf{w}$. Each later direction maximises variance subject to being orthogonal to the earlier ones.
% - Each component $z_m = \sum_{j=1}^{D} w_{jm}x_j$ mixes every feature from every representation.
% - Consequence: a component cannot be traced to one substructure or descriptor, or to one representation. A chemist cannot read a PCA feature.
% - PCA never sees $\mathbf{y}$. It keeps the directions with the most variance, not the directions that predict the label.
% - Low-variance components can matter as much for regression as high-variance ones \citep{Jolliffe1982}. Dropping them by variance can throw away the signal.
% - Scale: raw variance depends on units, so descriptors with large values would dominate. Standardising each feature first (PCA on the correlation matrix) removes the unit effect.
% - Width still matters after standardising: every feature then carries unit variance, so a representation with more features contributes more total variance and tends to dominate the leading components.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on applying PCA to concatenated binary fingerprints mixed with continuous descriptors (scaling, and whether PCA suits binary data).
% - Cost for a new molecule: PCA needs all $K$ representations computed, plus the dense $D\times B$ projection.
% - Other merging methods exist: kernel PCA, autoencoders, UMAP and t-SNE. The last two are mostly used for visualisation, not as model inputs.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on dimensionality reduction of combined molecular fingerprints and embeddings used as model input in QSAR (not visualisation).
% - Contrast with selection: the hybrid is $\mathbf{H} = \mathbf{X}_{:,S}$ with $S \subset \{1,\dots,D\}$ and $|S| = B$. Every feature of $\mathbf{H}$ is an original feature.
% - Methods note: the comparison concatenates all representations, standardises each feature on the training fold, reduces by PCA to $B$ components (the hybrid's budget), and trains one model.
% - Methods note: PCA is fit inside each cross-validation fold, on training molecules only, and run at every budget and every downstream model the hybrid uses.
% - TODO(results): PCA at matched budget against the hybrid and against the best single representation; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Ensembling, which merges predictions:
% - Train one model $f_k$ on each representation. Combine: $\hat{y}(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} w_k f_k(\mathbf{x}^{(k)})$, with $w_k \ge 0$ and $\sum_k w_k = 1$. The plain mean uses $w_k = 1/K$.
% - Why ensembles help: members make errors that are partly independent, so combining them cancels some of the errors \citep{Dietterich2000}.
% - Combining rules in the comparison: mean, median, 20% trimmed mean, mean after dropping members outside an interquartile-range fence, mean after dropping members more than 3 scaled median absolute deviations from the median, mean weighted by each member's inner cross-validation score, and mean of the 3 best members on inner cross-validation.
% - Majority vote is added for classification with hard class labels.
% - Two ways to build the ensemble, both in the comparison. (1) The same model type on every representation. (2) Each representation gets its own best model, chosen on inner cross-validation.
% - Two representation sets: the top 3 representations by inner cross-validation, or all of them.
% - A further version lets the ensemble choose its own representation set and combining rule on inner cross-validation. The hybrid also chooses its own recipe from training data, so this is the like-for-like comparison.
% - All ensemble choices use only the training molecules of each outer fold (inner cross-validation, group-aware). Nothing is chosen using held-out scores.
% - An oracle is also reported: the best single model and representation pair read off the held-out fold. It is an upper bound, not a fair competitor.
% - Each member sees one representation. No single model can combine a feature from $\mathbf{X}^{(1)}$ with a feature from $\mathbf{X}^{(2)}$.
% - Cost for a new molecule: $K$ representations computed and $K$ models run, for as long as the model is in use.
% - The hybrid pays once, for the search, then deploys one model. It still needs every representation its selected features come from. Its saving at prediction time holds only if it draws on fewer representations than the ensemble does.
% - Related idea: the random subspace method trains each tree on a random subset of features \citep{Ho1998}. That is selection inside an ensemble, but the subsets are random, not chosen for the task.
% - TODO(results): ensemble against hybrid, with accuracy and prediction-time cost (representations computed, models deployed) side by side; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Multiple kernel learning, which merges similarities:
% - Combined kernel: $k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}') = \sum_{k=1}^{K}\eta_k\, k_k(\mathbf{x}^{(k)},\mathbf{x}'^{(k)})$, with $\eta_k \ge 0$ \citep{Gonen2011}.
% - Weights are learned per representation, not per feature. A representation is used whole or down-weighted whole.
% - Works only with kernel models (SVM, Gaussian process, kernel ridge). Tree ensembles and neural networks cannot use it directly.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Multiple kernel learning is a learned similarity, as the similarity hybrid is, but it weights whole representations where the hybrid chooses individual features.
%
% Selecting from the pooled features (the family the hybrid belongs to):
% - Standard split: filter methods score each feature on its own, wrapper methods score feature subsets with a model, embedded methods select during training \citep{Guyon2003}.
% - mRMR, a filter method: add features one at a time. Each step picks the feature with the highest $I(x_j;\mathbf{y}) - \frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{x_i\in S} I(x_j;x_i)$, i.e. relevance to the label minus mean redundancy with the features already chosen \citep{Peng2005}.
% - Recursive feature elimination, a wrapper method: fit the model, drop the lowest-ranked features, repeat until $B$ remain \citep{Guyon2002}.
% - These methods treat the $D$ pooled features as one flat list, with no notion of which representation a feature came from.
% - The hybrid splits the budget by representation first: $b_k$ features from $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$, with $\sum_k b_k = B$. It then ranks by importance $s^{(k)}_j$ within each representation.
% - Why the split matters: a per-representation budget can express "use only 2 or 3 representations" or "use all of them" directly. A flat ranking cannot.
% - TODO(results): how many representations the best hybrid draws on, per dataset (5.2.3); numbers come from the full runs.
% - Methods note: the comparison runs the hybrid, mRMR, recursive feature elimination, PCA and random selection at the same budget $B$, on the concatenated pool, inside each fold.
% - Methods note: mutual information in mRMR is estimated with a $k$-nearest-neighbour estimator. Because of cost, mRMR is run only up to a budget of 200 features.
% - Methods note: recursive feature elimination uses a random forest to rank features, removing about a tenth of the remaining features per step. The downstream model is a random forest, so this comparison covers regression datasets only.
% - TODO(results): hybrid against mRMR, recursive feature elimination, PCA and random selection at matched $B$; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Prior work questions selecting features using the labels. Unsupervised Sort & Slice beat selection schemes based on mutual information \citep{sns}, and Dablander raised the risk that label-based selection overfits \citep{Dablander2024}.
%   - TODO(check): confirm the page in Dablander2024 (the outline gives p. 138; not re-read).
%   - Links to: 5.9 Discussion (overfitting), which answers this directly.
%
% Shrinking the feature space, and models that want different features:
% - Every method above except ensembling also reduces width, from $D$ to about $B$ with $B \ll D$. Each ensemble member still sees all $d_k$ features of its representation.
% - Fewer features than samples matters when $n$ is small: compare the ratio $n/B$ with $n/D$.
% - Distance-based models (kNN, SVM with an RBF kernel) use every feature in the distance, so irrelevant features add noise to every distance.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on kNN accuracy as irrelevant features are added (distance concentration in high dimensions, e.g. "when is nearest neighbour meaningful").
% - Tree ensembles choose one split feature at each node \citep{Breiman2001}, so irrelevant features can be partly ignored.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on random-forest accuracy as the fraction of irrelevant features grows.
% - Neural networks sit in between. They learn their own input weights, but with small $n$ they can overfit on wide inputs.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on neural-network sample efficiency on high-dimensional tabular inputs with small $n$ (e.g. tabular deep learning against gradient-boosted trees).
