\subsection{The adaptive hybrid representation}
% OUTLINE 5.1.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.1.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: the framework takes frozen feature matrices and outputs a task-specific feature set; the NGBoost PDV-vs-Avalon example; the "kitchen sink" argument against pooling everything; four research questions from the confirmation report.
% - TODO(check): the existing prose says irrelevant features give "exponentially more noise" in a pairwise comparison. For a distance that sums over features, the irrelevant part grows linearly with the number of irrelevant features, not exponentially (formula under "Why filter before a distance"). Reword.
% - TODO(check): the existing prose uses the "KIRBy" name. Per thesis naming, KIRBy appears only in the code-availability appendix; call the method the "adaptive hybrid representation" here.
% - TODO(check): the research questions in the existing prose are the confirmation-report set (noise robustness, distance-based mitigation, importance weighting, sample weighting). They do not match this chapter's scope. Suggested replacements are at the end of these notes.
%
% The idea in one line:
% - Definition: an adaptive hybrid representation is a feature set built by selecting up to a fixed number of features from several base representations of the same inputs.
% - The selection is made for one dataset and one downstream model.
% - It is an input, not a model. Any standard supervised model can be trained on it.
% - "Adaptive" means it is re-derived per task. A different dataset, label, split or downstream model gives a different hybrid.
% - Name: "adaptive hybrid representation", "hybrid" for short, throughout the thesis.
%
% Notation (shared with Methods):
% - $K$ base representations $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$, $k=1,\dots,K$, all describing the same $n$ molecules/samples; labels $\mathbf{y}$.
% - Budget $B$: the maximum total number of features in the hybrid.
% - Allocation $b_k$: the number of features given to representation $k$, with $\sum_k b_k = B$.
% - Importance of feature $j$ in representation $k$: $s^{(k)}_j$, computed on training data only.
% - $S_k$: the indices of the top-$b_k$ features of representation $k$, ranked by $s^{(k)}_j$.
% - Hybrid: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times \sum_k |S_k|}$, with $|S_k| \le b_k$.
% - Features with zero importance are not taken, so the hybrid can have fewer than $B$ features: $\sum_k |S_k| \le B$.
% - Contrast with plain concatenation: $\mathbf{X}^{\text{cat}} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}]$, width $D = \sum_k d_k$. Typically $B \ll D$.
%
% Where the idea came from (her origin story, keep it short):
% - The first goal was a better similarity: keep the features relevant to the task and compute distances on those alone.
% - A proof of concept showed the selected feature sets also worked as ordinary model inputs, apart from similarity.
% - The selection itself became the method. Similarity became one of its uses (Chapter 6).
% - Links to: Chapter 4, section "What a representation would need", which ends at this idea.
%
% Why filter before a distance (the similarity motivation as maths):
% - Manhattan distance between two feature vectors: $d(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}) = \sum_{j=1}^{D} |a_j - b_j|$.
% - Split the features into task-relevant ($R$) and irrelevant ($I$): $d = \sum_{j\in R}|a_j-b_j| + \sum_{j\in I}|a_j-b_j|$.
% - The second sum has $|I|$ terms, so it grows roughly in proportion to $|I|$.
% - When $|I| \gg |R|$ the irrelevant sum decides which molecules count as nearest. "Nearest" then stops meaning "similar for this task".
% - Selecting features first removes most of $I$ before any distance is taken.
% - The idea relies on the similarity principle: similar structures tend to have similar properties \citep{Maggiora2014}.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the loss of nearest-neighbour contrast as irrelevant dimensions are added (e.g. Beyer et al. 1999, "When is nearest neighbor meaningful?"; Aggarwal, Hinneburg and Keim 2001 on distance metrics in high-dimensional space).
%
% The questions the idea raises (each becomes a swept axis in Methods):
% - How useful is a single feature? This is feature importance, $s^{(k)}_j$.
%   - Three model-based methods: total split gain in a LightGBM model \citep{ke2017lightgbm}; TreeSHAP values from a random forest; DeepLIFT attributions from a small feed-forward neural network.
%   - Importance is computed separately within each representation, on the training fold only.
% - How many features should each representation get? This is allocation, $b_k$.
%   - Each representation is first scored on its own by cross-validation. Call that score $r_k$.
%   - The score is put on a scale where $0$ means "no better than a trivial prediction".
%   - Error metric (RMSE): $r_k = \max\!\left(0,\ 1 - \text{RMSE}_k / \text{RMSE}_{\text{mean}}\right)$, where $\text{RMSE}_{\text{mean}}$ comes from predicting the training-set mean.
%   - Correlation metric (Spearman): $r_k = \max(0, \rho_k)$.
%   - AUROC: $r_k = \max\!\left(0,\ 2(\text{AUROC}_k - 0.5)\right)$.
%   - A representation no better than the trivial prediction gets weight zero and no features.
%   - Weights: $w_k = f(r_k)$. The allocation methods differ only in $f$: linear ($w_k = r_k$), squared ($w_k = r_k^2$), softmax ($w_k = e^{r_k/T} / \sum_l e^{r_l/T}$, with temperature $T$), or exponential decay in the representation's rank.
%   - Budget split: $b_k \approx B \cdot \frac{w_k}{\sum_{l} w_l}$. Shares are floored and the leftover features go one at a time to the largest fractional parts (largest-remainder rounding).
%   - A representation with a non-zero weight keeps at least one feature. No representation gets more features than it has.
% - How many representations take part? Only the top 2, 3 or 5 by standalone score, or all of them.
% - How is a candidate hybrid judged during the search? By an internal evaluator: a model fitted on the candidate to score it.
%   - It is either the downstream model itself, or a fixed stand-in (random forest, LightGBM, neural network).
%   - Scoring a feature subset by training a model on it is the wrapper approach to feature selection \citep{Kohavi1997}.
% - How large should the hybrid be? This is the budget $B$. It is a maximum, and several values are tried.
% - Does the answer depend on the downstream model? Each downstream model gets its own search. Tree ensembles and neural networks are both tested.
% - Why it matters: none of these choices has one right answer across datasets. The chapter's results sections show this axis by axis.
% - Links to: Methods sections "The search space" and "Why each axis is swept".
%
% Why allocate per representation instead of pooling every feature:
% - Standard feature selection ranks all $D$ features of one pooled matrix $\mathbf{X}^{\text{cat}}$ together \citep{Guyon2003}.
% - Pooling has three problems here.
%   - Representations differ in width by orders of magnitude, e.g. 2048-bit fingerprints next to about 200 descriptors. Wide representations can crowd out narrow ones.
%   - Importance scores are not on a common scale across feature types (binary bits, continuous descriptors, embeddings).
%   - A representation's standalone score is information about its whole block of features. Pooling does not use it.
% - Allocating per representation first decides how much each representation gets, then picks features within each one.
% - The search also includes one pooled allocation method.
%   - Each representation offers candidate features in proportion to its squared weight. The pool of candidates is about twice the budget.
%   - One importance fit on the pooled candidates ranks them together, and the top $B$ are kept.
%   - So per-representation and pooled selection are compared directly, not assumed.
% - Her observation: some datasets do best with only the top 2 or 3 representations, others with all of them. A single pooled ranking cannot express that choice.
% - TODO(results): per-representation allocation vs pooled allocation, and which number of representations wins on each dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Links to: section "Other ways to use several representations" (PCA, ensembling, classical feature selection).
%
% What it is for:
% - Intended for representations that describe the molecule in different ways: physicochemical descriptors, substructure fingerprints such as ECFP4, graph neural network embeddings, language-model embeddings.
% - The hybrid keeps the task-relevant part of each.
% - Selection also shrinks the input. A smaller input costs less to train on.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how specific model families (e.g. k-nearest neighbours, Gaussian processes, SVMs) degrade as irrelevant input features are added.
% - Named features (descriptors, substructure bits) stay traceable in the hybrid. This is what makes blueprints possible (next subsection).
% - Why it matters: it uses several representations without choosing one in advance, and without training on the full concatenated width $D$.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, sections "Different representations carry different information" and "Most features do not vary on a given dataset".
%
% Two ways to use a hybrid for similarity (both measured in Chapter 6):
% - (a) Ordinary hybrid, then similarity. Build the hybrid for prediction as usual, then compute distances between molecules on its features. The search is unchanged.
% - (b) Similarity hybrid. The search is scored differently: by how well each molecule's activity is predicted from its 5 nearest neighbours in the candidate feature space. No downstream model is fitted.
%   - Neighbour prediction: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j \in N_5(i)} w_{ij}\, y_j}{\sum_{j \in N_5(i)} w_{ij}}$, with $w_{ij} = 1/d(\mathbf{h}_i,\mathbf{h}_j)$.
%   - $N_5(i)$: the 5 molecules nearest to $i$ under distance $d$ on the hybrid features, taken only from other scaffold groups. So a molecule is never predicted from its own scaffold group.
%   - Default distance: Manhattan.
%   - Score for regression: Spearman correlation $\rho(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$.
%   - Score for binary classification: $2(\text{AUROC} - 0.5)$. This puts chance at $0$, the same zero point as a correlation.
%   - Related published measure: the modelability index, MODI \citep{Golbraikh2014}. MODI counts whether each compound's single nearest neighbour is in the same activity class. The score here is a graded, regression form of the same idea.
% - Difference: (a) asks whether features good for prediction also give a good similarity. (b) asks for a good similarity directly.
% - Why it matters: it tests whether the Chapter 4 failures of fingerprint similarity can be repaired by choosing the features.
% - Links to: Chapter 6, section "Similarity in a hybrid".
%
% Research questions for this chapter (suggested replacements; she decides):
% - RQ1: Does a hybrid built from several base representations score better on the downstream task than the best single representation?
% - RQ2: Which search choices matter (importance method, allocation method, number of representations, internal evaluator, budget)? Does the best choice differ between datasets and between downstream models?
% - RQ3: Does per-representation allocation do better than pooling all features, and better than concatenation, PCA and ensembling?
% - RQ4: Do the selected features (the blueprint) make chemical sense, and what do they say about the task?
% - RQ5: Does the method carry over to other domains (antibodies, reactions, nucleotides, gene expression) and to applied settings (benchmark tasks from collaborators, federated learning)?
% - Note: the similarity and noise questions move to Chapter 6.
%
% Figures:
% - DIAGRAM: several base representation matrices side by side, different widths and colours. Within each, features ranked by importance. The budget $B$ drawn as a bar split into $b_1,\dots,b_K$. The top features from each joined into a narrow hybrid matrix $\mathbf{H}$. Side panel: the pooled concatenation $\mathbf{X}^{\text{cat}}$ of width $D$ for contrast.
% - DIAGRAM: two panels for the two similarity uses. (a) predictive search, hybrid, then a distance computed on it. (b) search scored by 5-nearest-neighbour prediction, giving a similarity hybrid. The same molecules shown in both, with one molecule's neighbours circled.
%
% Literature TODOs:
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on supervised feature weighting or selection for molecular similarity searching, e.g. weighted fingerprints for virtual screening.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the combined algorithm selection and hyperparameter optimisation (CASH) problem and AutoML systems that also search over input feature sets, to frame the hybrid search as extending that search to the input representation.
% ---- END NOTES 5.1.3 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] was old Sec "Adaptive feature selection".
% TODO: how do I properly reference something? noise robustness
% TODO: is this right??? I'm making shit up with NGBoost.
Here, we present KIRBy (Knowledge Integration by Representation), a framework that takes a collection of feature matrices for a given data set and outputs a customised feature set optimised for a specific prediction task. The inputs can be any type of frozen feature vector, such as fingerprints, descriptor sets, foundation model embeddings, gene expression signatures, histopathology features. Within a given domain, these individual vectors typically contain its own type of information. For example, substructure fingerprints like ECFP containing information about the presence of chemical substructures, while a PDV contains information about the molecule's specific physciochemical properties. It's a well-established phenomenon when it comes to machine learning, there's rarely ever a one-size-fits-all approach. Take the noise robustness study above as evidence, NGBoost's predictive performance on QM9 was only ever strong with given PDV input features. However, on Caco-2, NGBoost preferred Avalon, with PDV as a close second. The model NGBoost itself produces better fits when given physicochemical properties, yet this preference can be overidden by a different prediction task. Avalon likely captures features that are pertinent to the log efflux ratio. 

% TODO: is it fair to say exponential noise here? 
But as important as the selection of useful features is, it is also important to filter out the useless features. We could throw the kitchen sink at the models and pool together features across a wide array of representations. Not only would that pose computational challenges, but it also introduces a huge set of irrelevant features that function as noise in downstream applications. Take pairwise comparisons between feature vectors. If the majority of your features are noise relative to the task at hand, a pairwise comparison of two feature vectors ends up with exponentially more noise. With all of that in mind, I decided to develop a method that selects the best features for a given data set and model. In theory, it should effectively filter out noisy features and isolate the feature which carry the most relevant information. 

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Sec 4.1 (research questions).
% [CR 792-798] the four research questions the second half was built to answer.
% TODO: the outline redistributes these -- Q1 and Q3 -> ch.5, Q2 and Q4 -> ch.6. Decide
% whether they stay here as a roadmap or move.
This research aims to address the following questions: 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Do hybrid representations achieve higher noise robustness (retention percentage) than their base representations?
    \item Do the selected hybrid features help stabilize distance-based noise mitigation?
    \item Does feature importance weighting improve effectiveness of hybrid representation creation, model training, and noise mitigation tasks?
    \item When sample removal is suboptimal, can sample weighting or label smoothing provide superior noise handling under Gaussian corruption?
\end{enumerate}
% TODO: Outline: 'This is the motivation for the method; it is exploratory, not a reported result,
% TODO: and the noisy-label question is measured properly in Chapter 6.' Say so explicitly.


\subsection{Blueprints and what they tell a chemist}
% OUTLINE 5.1.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.1.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What a blueprint is:
% - Already in prose: none. The section currently holds only a placeholder todo.
% - A blueprint is the record a finished hybrid search leaves behind. It says which base representations went in, how the budget was split between them, and how to rebuild the same hybrid.
% - It holds two kinds of information.
%   - The recipe: the representation pool, the budget $B$, the allocation method, the importance method, the internal evaluator, the number of representations allowed, the number of folds, the random seed, the metric and its direction, the objective (predictive or similarity), and the augmentation settings.
%   - The record of what was chosen: the budget each representation received in each fold, $b_k^{(m)}$ for fold $m$, and a score per representation that sets its share of the budget.
% - The selected features themselves, $S_k^{(m)} \subseteq \{1,\dots,d_k\}$, are not stored feature by feature for the base representations. Rebuilding the hybrid from the recipe on the same data gives them back, together with their importance ranks.
%   - Exception: for the extra features added by augmentation, the indices kept in each fold are stored directly.
% - Share of the budget given to representation $k$: $\pi_k = b_k / B$, with $\sum_k \pi_k = 1$. This is the coarsest reading of a blueprint and needs no feature names at all.
% - The finer reading is feature by feature. Every feature of $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ traces back to a pair (representation $k$, feature index $j$) and to its importance score $s^{(k)}_j$.
% - Scope note: how blueprints are stored and rebuilt belongs in the Blueprints subsection of the hybrid-search methods. This subsection is about why a record of selected features matters to a chemist.
%
% Which features can be read chemically:
% - Named physicochemical descriptors computed with RDKit \citep{rdkit}. Each selected feature is a property with a name, e.g. MolLogP (calculated logP), TPSA (topological polar surface area), NumHDonors, NumAromaticRings, FractionCSP3.
% - MACCS keys: each key is a fixed, predefined substructure query, so a selected key names a substructure directly \citep{Durant2002}.
% - ECFP bits: each bit is a hashed circular atom environment of radius $r$ (radius 2 for ECFP4) \citep{rogers2010}.
%   - Given a molecule that sets the bit, the bit maps back to an atom environment that can be drawn on that molecule.
%   - Caveat: hashing folds many environments onto one bit. The environment drawn from one example molecule may not be the only one behind that bit.
%   - Links to: "Hash collisions: one bit, several substructures" (Chapter 4).
% - Named feature sets exist outside small molecules too: antibody structure and geometry descriptors, and pathway or gene-set scores in the genomics datasets. The same argument applies to them.
% - Features that cannot be read chemically: learned embeddings (graph, language-model and sequence embeddings).
%   - For these, the blueprint can only say "dimension $j$ of embedding $k$".
%   - That still says which pretrained model supplied signal for this task, and how much of the budget it earned ($\pi_k$).
%   - It says nothing about which chemistry.
%   - Links to: "Learned embeddings cannot be read chemically" (Chapter 4).
% - Consequence: a hybrid with a large share of named features can be interpreted. A hybrid made mostly of embedding features mostly cannot.
% - The mix is set by the search, not chosen by the user.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on interpreting hashed Morgan/ECFP fingerprint bits by mapping them back to atom environments, and on the limits of doing so.
%
% Direction 1: hypothesis generation:
% - The features the search keeps are the ones that predicted the label on the training folds.
% - On a binding or activity dataset, recurring substructure keys or ECFP environments point to parts of the molecule that may be involved in the activity.
% - Example of the reasoning (not a result): if a basic-nitrogen environment and a high-logP descriptor were both kept on a hERG dataset, that would agree with known hERG blocker features. It would be a starting point for a chemist, not a finding.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on hERG blocker pharmacophore features (basic amine, lipophilicity, aromatic rings).
% - This gives a chemist something to test: make or pick molecules that differ in the feature and check the activity.
% - Caveat: selected means predictive, not causal. A kept feature may stand in for a correlated property that does the real work.
% - Caveat: correlated features share importance. Among near-duplicate descriptors (e.g. several logP-like or size-like descriptors), which one is kept can be arbitrary and can change between folds.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on how correlated features split or swap importance in tree-based and permutation feature importance.
%
% Direction 2: sanity check on the selection:
% - The same record can be read the other way round. If the kept features make chemical sense for the endpoint, that supports the claim that the selection finds real signal and is not fitting noise.
% - Example of what counts as sensible (not a result): lipophilicity and polar surface area descriptors being kept for a permeability or logD endpoint.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature linking TPSA and logP to passive membrane permeability (Caco-2).
% - The opposite also counts. Kept features with no plausible link to the endpoint are a warning sign.
% - Kept features that change completely from fold to fold are also a warning that the selection may be overfitting.
% - Interpretation methods can themselves be checked: benchmarks with synthetic endpoints, where the contributing substructures are known in advance, exist for QSAR interpretation \citep{Matveieva2021}.
% - Stability puts a number on "changes from fold to fold". Treat each fold's selected set as one subset out of $M$ folds and use the stability index of \citet{Nogueira2018}:
%   - $\hat{\Phi} = 1 - \dfrac{\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p} s_j^2}{\frac{\bar{k}}{p}\left(1 - \frac{\bar{k}}{p}\right)}$
%   - $p$ = total number of candidate features in the pool, $p = \sum_k d_k$.
%   - $s_j^2 = \frac{M}{M-1}\hat{q}_j(1-\hat{q}_j)$ = unbiased variance, across the $M$ folds, of whether feature $j$ was selected; $\hat{q}_j$ = fraction of folds that selected feature $j$.
%   - $\bar{k}$ = mean number of features selected per fold (equal to $B$ when every fold fills the budget).
%   - $\hat{\Phi} = 1$: the same features are picked in every fold. $\hat{\Phi} \approx 0$: agreement no better than chance for subsets of that size.
%   - The stability results themselves belong in the blueprint results chapter, not here.
%
% Why it matters:
% - Why it matters: most model interpretation in drug discovery happens after the model is fitted, using attribution maps and feature-attribution scores \citep{JimenezLuna2020}. A blueprint gives interpretation at the input instead: the model only ever sees the features that were kept.
% - Why it matters: this fits the argument that a model should, where possible, be interpretable by construction rather than explained after the fact \citep{Rudin2019}.
% - A small budget of named features is a step in that direction. The model on top of it may still be a black box.
% - Why it matters: a chemist gets a short list of features for their task out of thousands of candidates. A single 2048-bit fingerprint gives no such shortlist.
% - Why it matters: the output is more than a better score. It is also information about the problem.
% - Limitation to flag early: interpretability depends on which representations are in the pool. A pool made only of embeddings gives a blueprint with no chemistry in it.
%
% Links:
% - Links to: the Blueprints subsection of the hybrid-search methods (what is stored, and how a hybrid is rebuilt from it).
% - Links to: "Results: blueprints on specific datasets" (case studies checking whether the kept features match the known chemistry).
% - Links to: the overfitting discussion in the complexity results. The sanity-check direction is one argument on the "not overfitting" side.
% - Links to: "Applied settings: OPIG and federated". Reusing a recipe found on a large dataset on a smaller one is one practical use of a blueprint.
% - Links to: "Beyond small molecules". Named features exist in other domains, so the interpretability argument carries over.
% - DIAGRAM: one example blueprint in two panels. Left: stacked bar of the budget share $\pi_k$ per representation. Right: top 10 named features by importance, with MACCS and ECFP features drawn as highlighted substructures on an example molecule. Here a schematic; the real figure belongs in the results.
% - TODO(results): which named features the best hybrids keep on the case-study datasets, and whether they match the known chemistry; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Other uses of a task-specific similarity (the outline puts these here; they overlap with "Other standard uses of similarity" in Chapter 4, so decide where they live):
% - Predicting each molecule's activity from its nearest neighbours in the hybrid space.
% - Applicability domain: whether a new molecule is close enough to the training set to trust a prediction.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on distance-to-training-set applicability-domain methods in QSAR.
% - Detecting noisy labels by comparing each molecule with its neighbours (Chapter 6).
% - Clustering molecules to build group-aware train/test splits.
% - Weighting partners by similarity in federated learning.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on similarity-weighted client aggregation in federated learning for drug discovery.
% - Scoring or conditioning in generative molecular design.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on similarity-based scoring functions in generative molecular design.
% ---- END NOTES 5.1.4 ----

\subsection{Beyond small molecules}
% OUTLINE 5.1.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.1.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What the method needs from a dataset:
% - Already in prose (5.1.3): the inputs can be any frozen feature vector, e.g. fingerprints, descriptors, foundation-model embeddings, expression signatures. This section says what that generality means and where it stops.
% - The search needs only $n$ labelled samples ($\mathbf{y}$) and $K \ge 2$ representations of the same samples, each a fixed-length vector per sample.
%   - Formally: each representation is a map $\phi_k$ from a sample to a vector, $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_i = \phi_k(\text{sample}_i) \in \mathbb{R}^{d_k}$.
%   - The vectors are stacked into $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$, with row $i$ referring to the same sample in all $K$ matrices.
%   - Nothing in the search uses chemistry. It sees $K$ matrices, $\mathbf{y}$, a group label per sample for the folds, and a metric.
%   - The output is $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ with $|S_k| \le b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k = B$.
%   - $|S_k|$ can be below $b_k$: a representation stops contributing once its remaining features have zero importance, so the hybrid can be narrower than $B$.
%   - $\mathbf{H}$ is an ordinary table, so any standard model can take it.
% - Consequence: the method applies to supervised learning on tabular data in general, wherever several encoders exist for the same inputs.
% - Frozen means the encoders are not retrained. A new encoder adds one more matrix $\mathbf{X}^{(K+1)}$, and the search decides whether any of its features are picked.
% - What does not transfer: the chemistry-specific parts of the thesis.
%   - The substructure reading of blueprints (5.1.4).
%   - The small-molecule similarity and noise work (Chapters 4 and 6).
%   - The feature selection itself transfers.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, "No single method fits every dataset". The same search gives a different hybrid per domain, dataset and model.
%
% Domains used in this thesis (beyond small molecules):
% - Antibodies: heavy- and light-chain sequences.
%   - Representations: one-hot per position; BLOSUM substitution encoding; general protein language models (ESM-2, ESM-C, ESM3, ProtT5, SaProt); antibody language models (AntiBERTy, AbLang2, AbRoBERTa, IgT5).
%   - Different summaries of one language model's output count as separate representations: mean over the whole sequence, mean over CDR residues only, mean over early or middle layers.
%   - Tasks: expression from a mutational scan, melting temperature, humanness (human vs non-human therapeutic antibodies), and binding to HER2 (trastuzumab variants).
%   - Why it matters: this is the domain with the most work after small molecules, and it has published benchmarks to compare against (5.7).
%   - TODO(results): hybrid vs best single representation on each antibody dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Chemical reactions: yield prediction from high-throughput experiment plates, built on the Buchwald–Hartwig C–N coupling data \citep{Ahneman2018}.
%   - Representations: condensed graph of reaction fingerprints; reaction language-model fingerprints \citep{Schwaller2021} and a second reaction language model; one-hot of each component choice; molecular fingerprints, descriptors and embeddings of the reactants, or of the difference between products and reactants.
%   - Status: proof of concept.
%   - TODO(results): hybrid vs best single representation on the two reaction datasets; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Nucleotides: binary tasks on DNA sequences from the revised Nucleotide Transformer benchmark \citep{DallaTorre2025}.
%   - Tasks: promoter detection (TATA-box promoters) and an H3K4me3 histone-mark task.
%   - Representations: k-mer counts ($k$ = 4 to 6), tetranucleotide frequencies, and DNA foundation models (Nucleotide Transformer v2, HyenaDNA, GROVER, Mistral-DNA; DNABERT-2 on the histone task).
%   - A k-mer count vector has $4^k$ features, e.g. $4^6 = 4096$ for $k=6$.
%   - Status: proof of concept.
%   - TODO(results): hybrid vs best single representation on the two nucleotide datasets; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Cancer genomics (TCGA): lung adenocarcinoma vs squamous cell carcinoma, and breast cancer PAM50 subtype (4 classes) \citep{Parker2009}.
%   - Representations: expression foundation models (e.g. Geneformer \citep{Theodoris2023}), methylation, copy number, miRNA, protein arrays (RPPA), and whole-slide pathology image embeddings.
%   - Here the representations are different measurement types, not different encoders of one input. The method treats both cases the same way.
%   - TODO(results): hybrid vs best single representation on the two TCGA datasets; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Split rule per domain, one line each (detail belongs in 5.3.1):
%   - reactions: grouped by additive;
%   - antibodies: grouped by sequence similarity, mutation position or edit distance from the wild type, depending on the dataset;
%   - nucleotides: grouped by chromosome;
%   - TCGA: grouped by tissue source site.
% - DIAGRAM: one row per domain. Left: the raw input (molecule, antibody chain pair, reaction, DNA sequence, patient sample). Middle: the $K$ representation matrices. Right: the same "hybrid search" box outputs $\mathbf{H}$. Point: only the left column changes between domains.
%
% One-hot vs learned embeddings:
% - One-hot of a sequence with $L$ positions and alphabet size $A$ gives $d = L \cdot A$ features, with exactly one 1 per filled position.
%   - Antibody example: padded to 150 heavy + 130 light positions with $A = 20$, so $d = 280 \times 20 = 5{,}600$.
%   - At most 280 of these are 1 in any sequence, i.e. at most $280/5600 = 5\%$ of features.
%   - Reaction component one-hot: one indicator per possible choice of each component. The Buchwald–Hartwig design has 15 aryl halides, 4 ligands, 3 bases and 23 additives \citep{Ahneman2018}.
%   - That gives $15+4+3+23 = 45$ features, with exactly 4 ones per reaction.
% - Why one-hot is hard for this method:
%   - positions that never change across the dataset have zero variance and so zero importance, e.g. conserved framework positions in antibodies;
%   - each remaining feature marks one residue at one position, so its importance rests on few samples;
%   - features with zero importance are never taken, so a sparse representation can contribute fewer features than its allocation $b_k$.
% - One-hot is still in the final representation set of some antibody datasets (e.g. melting temperature).
%   - Possible reason (untested): in mutational-scan and single-lineage datasets the variation sits at a few positions, so a few one-hot features carry the signal.
% - Recommendation: prefer language-model sequence embeddings over raw one-hot.
%   - An embedding is dense.
%   - Its width $d_k$ is fixed regardless of $L$.
%   - Most of its features vary across samples.
% - Neural networks are less robust to uninformative features than tree models on tabular data \citep{Grinsztajn2022}.
%   - Why it matters: one reason to drop features before modelling rather than leave the choice to the model.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing one-hot position encodings with protein language model embeddings as inputs for antibody property prediction at small sample sizes.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing k-mer count features with DNA language-model embeddings for promoter and histone-mark classification.
%
% Requirements for a dataset:
% - Enough samples.
%   - Within each training fold, importance scores and each representation's standalone score (which sets the allocation) come from an internal 80/20 split.
%   - With 5 folds, the internal validation part holds about $0.2 \times 0.8\,n = 0.16\,n$ samples, e.g. about 32 samples at $n = 200$.
%   - At that size, differences between representations can be smaller than the fold-to-fold spread.
%   - TODO(results): the sample size below which hybrids stop beating the best single representation; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Representations whose features carry importance.
%   - If every representation already scores poorly, no representation has features worth selecting, and the hybrid has nothing to combine.
%   - Seen in this work on some low-accuracy tasks.
%   - TODO(results): an example low-accuracy task where the hybrid did not help; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Representations that differ from each other.
%   - If all $K$ encode the same information, a hybrid can at best match the best one.
%   - Links to: 4.2.5, "Different representations carry different information".
% - Labels not dominated by measurement noise.
%   - No feature set can predict better than the label noise allows.
%   - Links to: Chapter 6, and Chapter 7, "Handling noise realistically".
% - Frozen inputs cap what can be learned.
%   - If the signal needs end-to-end training on raw inputs, selecting features from fixed encoders cannot supply it.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing frozen pretrained embeddings with end-to-end trained models on quantum-chemical property prediction (e.g. QM9).
% - Why it matters: this list tells a reader in another field whether to try the method.
% - Why it matters: it also sets up the Limitations section of this chapter (5.10).
% - Links to: 5.3.2, "Representations by domain" (full representation sets); 5.7 (antibody results); Chapter 7, "Future directions" (other modalities).
% ---- END NOTES 5.1.5 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{5.1.5: other domains; requirements for a dataset.}


\section{Methods: the hybrid search}
% OUTLINE 5.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: none at this heading (5.2 has only the outline pointer; the pipeline paragraph and fold-local preprocessing sit in 5.2.1).
% What this section does:
% - Opens the methods chapter for the hybrid search: one paragraph plus the overview figure, then points to 5.2.1 to 5.2.6.
% - Goal of the search in one line: for a given dataset, downstream model and feature budget, find the set of features, taken from several base representations, that predicts best.
% - The hybrid is a new representation built for one task. It is not a new model.
% - The downstream model is unchanged; only its input changes.
% - Budget $B$ is a maximum, not a target. A hybrid may use fewer than $B$ features (see Step 4).
% Inputs and output (shared notation):
% - Inputs: $K$ base representations $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$ of the same $n$ molecules; labels $\mathbf{y}$; group labels for splitting (e.g. scaffold groups); a metric; the task type (regression or classification); a list of budgets.
% - Output: a hybrid $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ with $|S_k| \le b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k = B$, so $\sum_k |S_k| \le B$.
% - Output also includes a blueprint: the settings needed to rebuild the same hybrid on new labelled data (detail in 5.2.6).
% - One best hybrid is kept for each pair (downstream model, budget). The best is not picked across models.
% The search in four steps (one per box of the overview figure):
% - Step 1, score each representation on its own: fit a model (the internal evaluator) on $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$ alone, score it on held-out rows, giving $r_k$.
%   - Scores are rescaled so that 0 means "no better than a trivial predictor".
%   - Error metric: $\tilde{r}_k = \max\left(0,\ 1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_k / \mathrm{RMSE}_0\right)$, where $\mathrm{RMSE}_0$ is the error of predicting the training mean. MAE uses the same rule with the training median as the trivial predictor.
%   - Correlation metric: $\tilde{r}_k = \max(0, \rho_k)$.
%   - AUROC or concordance index: $\tilde{r}_k = \max\left(0,\ 2(\mathrm{AUROC}_k - 0.5)\right)$ (0 at chance, 1 at perfect).
%   - Accuracy or F1: $\tilde{r}_k = \max\left(0,\ \frac{m_k - m_0}{1 - m_0}\right)$, where $m_0$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class.
%   - Why: a representation that adds nothing then gets no share of the budget, whatever the metric.
% - Optional restriction before Step 3: keep only the $N$ representations with the highest $\tilde{r}_k$, with $N \in \{2, 3, 5, K\}$. This representation count is its own axis of the search.
% - Step 2, score each feature within each representation: importance $s^{(k)}_j$ of feature $j$ in representation $k$. The options are tree-based gain, SHAP values for trees, or a neural attribution (DeepLIFT); detail in 5.2.2.
% - Step 3, split the budget across representations (allocation): weights $w_k = g(\tilde{r}_k)$, then $b_k \approx B \, \frac{w_k}{\sum_{k'} w_{k'}}$.
%   - Rounding is by largest remainder, so the $b_k$ are integers summing to $B$. No $b_k$ can exceed $d_k$.
%   - $g$ is the allocation method. Linear: $g(r)=r$.
%   - Squared: $g(r)=r^2$.
%   - Softmax: $w_k = \exp(\tilde{r}_k / T)$ with $T=1$.
%   - Rank-based decay: $w_k = \exp(-0.3\,\rho_k)$, where $\rho_k = 0$ for the best-scoring representation, 1 for the next, and so on.
%   - One variant pools candidates instead of fixing a quota per representation. Each representation contributes its top $\approx 2B\, w_k/\sum_{k'} w_{k'}$ features, using squared weights, to a shared pool. One importance ranking over the whole pool then keeps the top $B$ (detail in 5.2.2 and 5.2.3).
% - Step 4, select and concatenate: $S_k$ = the $b_k$ features of representation $k$ with the highest $s^{(k)}_j$.
%   - A representation stops at its last feature with $s^{(k)}_j > 0$. So $|S_k| \le b_k$, and the hybrid can be narrower than $B$.
%   - This is by design: features with zero importance are not padded in, because they would be picked in effect by their position in the matrix.
%   - Exception: if no feature of a representation scores above zero, its full quota $b_k$ is taken.
% - Then score the candidate hybrid: fit the downstream model on the training rows, score on held-out rows, average over folds.
% Where each step happens (leakage control):
% - The data are split into group-aware folds (5 by default). Molecules from one group never sit on both sides of a split.
% - Inside each fold's training rows, a second group-aware split holds out about 20% of the rows (one fold of a 5-fold group split).
% - Steps 1 to 4 (standalone scores, feature importances, allocation, selection) use only this inner split.
% - The fold's held-out rows are touched only when the finished hybrid is scored.
% - The comparison reported against single representations uses nested cross-validation. The whole search runs inside each outer training fold. The winning hybrid and each single representation are then scored on the same untouched outer fold.
% - Why: choosing features is itself model selection. Scoring the winner on the rows used to choose it is biased upwards \citep{Cawley2010, Varma2006}.
% - Selecting features on the full data before cross-validation gives error estimates that are too optimistic \citep{Ambroise2002}.
% The search space as a product:
% - Each candidate hybrid is one configuration: (representation count, importance method, allocation method, internal evaluator, downstream model, budget).
% - Internal evaluator = the model used to score representations in Step 1. It can be the downstream model itself or a fixed other model (random forest, LightGBM or a DNN; detail in 5.2.2).
% - Number of configurations per dataset, fold, objective and augmentation setting: $N_{\text{config}} = |\mathcal{C}| \times |\mathcal{I}| \times |\mathcal{A}| \times |\mathcal{E}| \times |\mathcal{M}| \times |\mathcal{B}|$ (count, importance, allocation, evaluator, model, budget).
% - TODO: fill $N_{\text{config}}$ from Table 5.1 once the final axis values are fixed; state it with the number of model fits ($N_{\text{config}}$ times the number of folds).
% - Every configuration in the grid is scored. This lets later chapters say which axis moves the score on which dataset (5.2.3).
% - Selection target: $c^* = \arg\max_{c}\ \frac{1}{F}\sum_{f=1}^{F} m\big(\mathbf{y}_{\text{val},f},\ \hat{\mathbf{y}}_{c,f}\big)$ for metric $m$ (sign flipped for error metrics), over $F$ folds, taken separately for each (downstream model, budget).
% - The default objective is predictive score. The same search can instead optimise a similarity-based score (used in Chapter 6).
% - One objective per search; the objective is not swept inside it.
% Roadmap sentence (one bullet per subsection):
% - 5.2.1: the steps above in full, with preprocessing inside each fold.
% - 5.2.2: every axis and its values (Table 5.1).
% - 5.2.3: evidence that datasets and models disagree on each axis, which is why each is swept.
% - 5.2.4: augmentation, keeping one dominant representation and adding a few features from the others.
% - 5.2.5: a fast setting with fewer axis values, and adaptive pruning of weak values partway through the folds.
% - 5.2.6: blueprints and mapping selected features back to chemistry.
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: Chapter 4 argues that a fixed representation carries many features that do not vary or do not relate to a given task. The search keeps only features that earn their place, per task.
% - Why it matters: filtering and combining happen in one step. Plain concatenation combines without filtering.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on concatenating molecular fingerprints and descriptors as model input, and whether the concatenation beats each part.
% - Why it matters: every selected feature keeps its source representation and its importance rank, so the hybrid can be read back (blueprints, 5.2.6). A merged projection such as PCA cannot be read back this way.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (flaws in fixed representations and similarity); 5.1.3 (motivation for the adaptive hybrid representation); Chapter 6 (the same search with a similarity objective and under label noise); 7.3 (no single setting fits every dataset).
% - The method needs no new model architecture. It works with any tabular inputs and any supervised model.
% - Links to: 5.1.5 (transfer to antibodies, reactions and nucleotides), which rests on the previous bullet.
% - Cost: the search is paid once per dataset and set of representations. Changing the representation list or the model means a new search (discussed in 5.10).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on budget-constrained feature selection across multiple feature groups (group feature selection, multi-view feature selection) to place the allocation step.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on filter vs wrapper vs embedded feature selection in QSAR, to say which family the search belongs to (importance-based filtering inside each representation, wrapper-style scoring of the whole hybrid).
% - TODO(results): one sentence previewing where hybrids beat the best single representation and where they do not; numbers come from the full runs.
% Figure:
% - DIAGRAM: Figure 5.1, the search pipeline.
%   - Left: $K$ base representation matrices, labelled by type (fingerprint, descriptors, learned embedding).
%   - Middle, drawn inside one outer training fold: the inner group-aware split (about 80/20).
%   - Middle, per representation: a standalone score $\tilde{r}_k$ and a feature importance bar $s^{(k)}_j$.
%   - Middle: the allocation box turning $\tilde{r}_k$ into $b_k$ (bar widths proportional to $b_k$); top-$b_k$ features cut from each matrix; the concatenated hybrid $\mathbf{H}$.
%   - Right: downstream model fitted on $\mathbf{H}$, scored on the held-out fold.
%   - Around it: a bracket showing the loop over configurations (count, importance, allocation, evaluator, model, budget) and "best per (model, budget)".
%   - Bottom right: the blueprint saved from the winner.
%   - Mark the outer test fold as never seen by the search.
% - DIAGRAM (optional, small inset): nested cross-validation, outer folds as rows, each containing inner folds, with the search box inside the outer training part.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2 ----


\subsection{From base representations to a hybrid}
% OUTLINE 5.2.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: overview paragraph of the search, and fold-local preprocessing (median imputation, dropping all-missing features, standardising on training-fold statistics) with \citep{Ambroise2002, Vabalas2019}.
% Two corrections to the existing prose (checked against the code):
% - CORRECTION: the prose says the hybrid has "$b$ columns". The budget is a maximum. The hybrid has at most $B$ features (see Step 5).
% - CORRECTION: the prose says binary features (fingerprints) skip standardising. That holds only when one representation is scored on its own.
% - Once the hybrid is assembled, every feature in it is standardised on training-fold statistics, binary bits included.
% Inputs:
% - $K$ base representations of the same $n$ molecules: $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$, $k = 1,\dots,K$.
% - Widths $d_k$ differ widely, from tens of descriptors to thousands of fingerprint bits.
% - Labels $\mathbf{y}$ (regression or classification).
% - One group label per molecule (e.g. scaffold). The split is always group-aware, never random.
% - The dataset's metric $m$. It sets what "better" means and in which direction.
% - A downstream model and a list of budgets $B$ (the maximum number of features in the hybrid).
% Step 0, outer folds:
% - $F = 5$ folds by default: $\{(\mathcal{T}_f, \mathcal{V}_f)\}_{f=1}^{F}$. No group appears in both $\mathcal{T}_f$ and $\mathcal{V}_f$.
% - Classification: folds are also stratified by class, so class balance holds across folds while groups stay separate.
% - Fewer groups than folds: the number of folds drops to the number of groups.
% - A dataset with a single group is refused rather than split at random.
% - A representation learned from the labels (e.g. a fine-tuned embedding) is re-trained inside each training fold, on training rows only.
% - Why it matters: otherwise the held-out labels would leak into the features themselves.
% Step 1, internal split inside each training fold:
% - $\mathcal{T}_f = \mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{in}}_f \cup \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$.
% - $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$ is the first fold of a 5-way group-aware split of $\mathcal{T}_f$: about 20% of its molecules, whole groups only.
% - The split is deterministic (no random draw).
% - Everything the search learns (importance scores, standalone scores, allocation) comes from $\mathcal{T}_f$ only.
% - $\mathcal{V}_f$ is used only to score the finished hybrid.
% - Why it matters: selecting features on the same rows that score them gives optimistic numbers \citep{Ambroise2002}.
% - Here, features are ranked on $\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$ only, so ranking sees neither $\mathcal{V}_f$ nor $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$.
% Step 2, feature importance per representation:
% - For each $k$, fit the importance method on $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{in}}_f]$ and $\mathbf{y}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{in}}_f]$. This gives one score $s^{(k)}_j \ge 0$ per feature $j$.
% - Each representation is scored separately. Importance only ranks features within one representation; it is never compared across representations.
% - Cost limit: if a representation has more than 2000 features, the tree-based importance methods first keep its 2000 highest-variance features.
%   - Variance is measured before standardising (after standardising every feature has variance 1).
%   - The features outside those 2000 get $s^{(k)}_j = 0$.
% - The importance methods themselves (gain, TreeSHAP, DeepLIFT) are described in the next subsection.
% Step 3, standalone score per representation:
% - The internal evaluator (a model, possibly the downstream model itself) is fitted on $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{in}}_f]$ and scored on $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$ in the dataset's metric. This gives a raw score $r_k$.
% - The raw scores are rescaled so that 0 means "no better than a trivial predictor" and 1 means perfect.
% - Why it matters: weights are then on one scale whatever the dataset's metric.
%   - Spearman, Pearson, MCC and $R^2$: $\tilde r_k = \max(0, r_k)$.
%   - AUROC and concordance index (chance at 0.5): $\tilde r_k = \max\big(0,\ 2(r_k - 0.5)\big)$.
%   - RMSE: $\tilde r_k = \max\big(0,\ 1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_k / \mathrm{RMSE}_0\big)$, where $\mathrm{RMSE}_0$ is the error of predicting the training mean on $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{in}}_f$.
%   - MAE: same form, with the reference predicting the training median.
%   - Accuracy and weighted F1: $\tilde r_k = \max\big(0,\ (r_k - r_0)/(1 - r_0)\big)$, where $r_0$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class.
% - A representation whose fit fails gets weight 0. It is recorded as a failure, not as a score of 0.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on skill scores measured against a mean-predictor (climatology) reference, e.g. the MSE skill score in forecast verification.
% Step 4, choose representations, then split the budget:
% - Optional representation count $N$ (a swept axis, see next subsection): keep the $N$ representations with the highest $\tilde r_k$ on this fold; drop the rest.
% - Weights $w_k = g(\tilde r_k)$. The allocation method sets $g$.
%   - Linear: $w_k = \tilde r_k$.
%   - Squared: $w_k = \tilde r_k^{2}$.
%   - The other methods are listed in the next subsection.
% - Exact share: $b^{*}_k = B\, w_k / \sum_{k'} w_{k'}$.
% - Rounding by largest remainder:
%   - Set $b_k = \min(\lfloor b^{*}_k \rfloor, d_k)$.
%   - Hand out the leftover features one at a time, largest fractional part $b^{*}_k - \lfloor b^{*}_k \rfloor$ first.
%   - Skip any representation already at its full width $d_k$.
%   - Result: $\sum_k b_k = B$ exactly (unless every representation is full), and $b_k \le d_k$.
% - Any representation with $w_k > 0$ keeps at least one feature. It is taken from the largest share.
% - So a representation drops out because its weight is 0, not because of rounding.
% - If every weight is 0, the budget is split equally.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on largest-remainder (Hamilton) apportionment.
% Step 5, select features and assemble:
% - $S_k$ = indices of the top $\min\big(b_k,\ |\{j : s^{(k)}_j > 0\}|\big)$ features of representation $k$, ranked by $s^{(k)}_j$.
% - A representation stops at its last feature with positive importance. So $|S_k| \le b_k$.
% - The hybrid can be narrower than its budget: $\sum_k |S_k| \le B$.
% - If no feature of a representation scores above 0, it takes its full quota $b_k$.
% - Hybrid: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$. The same feature indices are applied to training and held-out rows.
% - Why it matters: the budget is a ceiling, not a target. A smaller hybrid that scores better is the better answer.
% - Why it matters: tree importance gives exactly 0 to every feature no tree split on. Filling the budget from those would pick features by their position in the matrix, since ties are broken by order.
% - Links to: augmentation (later subsection), which can add extra features outside the budget.
% Step 6, preprocess the hybrid (fold-local):
% - Standardise every feature: $\hat{x}_{ij} = (x_{ij} - \mu_j)/\sigma_j$, with $\mu_j, \sigma_j$ computed on $\mathcal{T}_f$ only.
% - Fill missing values with the training-fold median of each feature.
% Step 7, score the configuration and pick a winner:
% - A configuration $c$ is one choice of allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, representation count and budget.
% - Fit the downstream model on $\mathbf{H}[\mathcal{T}_f]$ and score it on $\mathbf{H}[\mathcal{V}_f]$.
% - $\mathrm{score}(c) = \frac{1}{F}\sum_{f=1}^{F} m\big(\mathbf{y}_{\mathcal{V}_f},\ \hat{f}_c(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{V}_f})\big)$.
% - Per-fold scores and their spread are kept. There are no significance tests.
% - The winner is picked separately for each (downstream model, budget) pair: $c^{*}_{\text{model},B} = \arg\max_c \mathrm{score}(c)$, with the sign flipped for lower-is-better metrics.
% - The winner is never picked across models. Each model gets its own best hybrid.
% - Configurations whose score cannot be computed are excluded.
% - If every configuration fails, no winner is reported.
% - DIAGRAM: pipeline figure (replaces the existing figure todo):
%   - Left: $K$ matrices of different widths.
%   - Middle, one outer fold: training part split 80/20 by group; importance bars inside each representation; standalone score per representation feeding a pie of $b_k$; top-$b_k$ features pulled from each matrix.
%   - Right: concatenated $\mathbf{H}$, then downstream model, then score on the held-out fold.
%   - Bottom: 5 folds, mean score, winner per (model, budget), saved as a blueprint.
% Step 8, the reported comparison (nested):
% - The Step 7 score has already been used to choose among many configurations, so it is optimistic as an estimate of performance \citep{Varma2006, Cawley2010}.
% - The reported comparison runs the whole search (Steps 0-7) inside each outer training fold.
% - The winning recipe is rebuilt on the full outer training fold and scored once on the untouched outer test fold (5 outer folds).
% - Every single representation is also scored on the same outer test folds.
% - The "best single representation" is chosen from the inner search's own scores, never from the outer test scores. Choosing on test scores would be an oracle.
% - Both the hybrid and the best single representation therefore choose their winner without seeing the outer test fold.
% - The gap between the inner search score and the outer test score is recorded per model and budget. It measures how optimistic the search score was.
% - TODO(results): size of the gap between search score and outer-fold score, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - The single representation is scored at its full width, while the hybrid is held to $B$. Both widths are recorded.
% - DIAGRAM: nested loop: outer fold -> inner 5-fold search -> internal 80/20 split. Show which rows each level is allowed to see.
% - Links to: Models and metrics section (which metric per dataset); Limitations (cost of repeating the search per outer fold).
% What comes out: the blueprint:
% - A blueprint is a recipe, not a list of features.
% - It stores the representations, allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, budget, representation count, metric and direction, objective and augmentation settings.
% - Re-applying a blueprint to new labelled data re-runs Steps 1-5 on those rows: new importance scores and a new budget split.
% - The per-fold allocations and chosen feature indices are stored for provenance only.
% - For unlabelled molecules, the features chosen by a labelled fit are reused as they are.
% - Links to: Blueprints subsection, and "Blueprints and what they tell a chemist".
% Why it matters, and links:
% - Why it matters: every quantity that decides the hybrid comes from training rows. So a gain over a single representation cannot come from seeing the test labels.
% - Why it matters: the method changes only the input. The downstream model is a standard one.
% - Why it matters: so the hybrid plugs into any existing workflow that takes a feature matrix.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Under label noise, all training-side quantities (importance, standalone scores) use the noisy labels.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Held-out scoring uses clean labels unless stated otherwise.
% - Links to: the similarity objective (Chapter 6). It replaces Step 7's model score with a score computed from nearest neighbours in the hybrid space. The rest of the pipeline is unchanged.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.1 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] from old Sec "Exploring the search space".
% TODO: review
Given $K$ frozen representation matrices $\mathbf{R}_1, \dots, \mathbf{R}_K$ for the same $n$ samples, a label vector $\mathbf{y}$, and optional group assignments for cross-validation, the search finds which features from which sources best predict for a specified metric and downstream model. The output is a hybrid feature matrix of $b$ columns, where $b$ is the feature budget, together with a Blueprint that records all parameters needed to reproduce the identical feature selection on new data. The search first scores each source independently, then scores feature importance on training data within each cross-validation fold, allocates the budget among sources according to baseline performance, and scores candidate hybrid configurations via cross-validation. We retain the best configuration for each combination of downstream model and budget. Every selected feature traces back to its source and importance rank. 

% [KB 151] fold-local preprocessing.
Inputs from different sources differ in scale, distribution, and missingness patterns, and naively pooling them before splitting would leak validation-set statistics into the training procedure, a well-documented source of optimistic bias in feature selection studies \citep{Ambroise2002, Vabalas2019}. All preprocessing therefore runs within each cross-validation fold. We impute missing values with per-feature medians computed on the training fold and drop columns that are entirely missing. Binary feature matrices (molecular fingerprints, for instance) skip standardization, since their $\{0,1\}$ encoding already carries a consistent scale. For all other features, we standardize to zero mean and unit variance using training-fold statistics only.

\subsection{The search space}
% OUTLINE 5.2.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: fold-local imputation and scaling (previous subsection); the matched internal evaluator and why importance depends on the model \citep{Dong2020}. The existing text under "Feature importance scoring" and "Feature allocation strategies" is out of date. It lists 5 importance methods (two of them are allocation names), 13 allocation strategies, greedy forward selection, and about 15,000 configurations. The bullets below replace those facts.
% Overview:
% - The search space is a grid. Every combination of the values below is built and scored.
% - Seven axes: budget, allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, downstream model, representation count, objective.
% - One configuration = one value on each axis.
% - Each configuration produces one hybrid per fold and one held-out score per fold.
% - The winner is chosen separately for each (downstream model, budget) pair.
% - The winner is the configuration with the best mean held-out score over the folds.
% - Why it matters: the chapter's claim is that no single setting suits every dataset. A grid tests that directly, because every axis is measured and none is assumed.
% - Links to: "Why each axis is swept" (evidence for each axis); "Fast tier and adaptive pruning" (how the grid is shortened); the appendix table of the search space and its defaults.
% - DIAGRAM: table, one row per axis. Columns: axis, what it controls, values in the full search, values in the fast tier.
% Budget $B$:
% - $B$ is the maximum number of features in the hybrid: $\sum_k |S_k| \le B$. It is a ceiling, not a quota.
% - Values: $B \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2000\}$ features.
% - The hybrid can come in under budget for two reasons.
%   - A representation cannot give more features than it has: $b_k \le d_k$.
%   - Features with importance exactly zero are never taken (see "Importance method" below).
% - No downstream model has its own cap on features. Every model is searched at every budget.
% - Why it matters: models differ in how many features they use well. Tree ensembles and neural networks can do best at different budgets on the same dataset.
% Representation count $N$:
% - $N \in \{2, 3, 5, K\}$, where $K$ is the whole pool of base representations for the dataset.
% - TODO(check): the pool size $K$ per dataset, for the text and the appendix table.
% - In each fold, the $K$ representations are ranked by their standalone score $r_k$ (defined under "Allocation method" below). Only the top $N$ go on to allocation.
% - Count and allocation are separate axes. So every allocation method is paired with every count.
% - Why it matters: some datasets may do best with a hybrid built from 2 representations, others with all of them. Feature selection over one pooled matrix has no such setting.
% Importance method (scores $s^{(k)}_j$):
% - Importance is computed separately for each representation $k$.
% - It is fitted on the internal training part of each fold (the same 80% used for $r_k$ below), never on held-out rows.
% - Every feature is standardised before fitting.
% - Three methods are searched: gain from gradient-boosted trees, TreeSHAP, and DeepLIFT.
% - Other importance methods were implemented and compared, but are not searched.
% - Gradient-boosted tree gain: fit a LightGBM model \citep{ke2017lightgbm} with 50 trees of depth at most 6.
%   - A feature's score is the total loss reduction over all splits that use it: $s^{(k)}_j = \sum_{t}\sum_{v \in \mathrm{splits}(t),\, f(v)=j} \Delta L_v$.
%   - A feature that no tree splits on scores exactly 0.
% - TreeSHAP: fit a random forest \citep{Breiman2001} with 50 trees of depth at most 10.
%   - Compute exact Shapley values for the trees \citep{Lundberg2020}.
%   - Score = mean absolute SHAP value over the $n$ training molecules: $s^{(k)}_j = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} |\phi_{ij}|$.
%   - Binary classification uses the positive class's values. Multiclass averages $|\phi_{ij}|$ over classes.
% - DeepLIFT \citep{Shrikumar2017}: train a small neural network. Two hidden layers of 64 and 32 units, ReLU, Adam, learning rate $10^{-3}$, 100 full-batch epochs.
%   - Each prediction is attributed to its inputs relative to an all-zero reference input. After standardisation, zero is the training mean of every feature.
%   - Implemented with Captum \citep{Kokhlikyan2020}.
%   - $s^{(k)}_j = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i |C_{ij}|$, where $C_{ij}$ is the contribution of feature $j$'s difference from the reference to molecule $i$'s output difference from the reference.
%   - Contributions sum to the output difference: $\sum_j C_{ij} = t(\mathbf{x}_i) - t(\mathbf{0})$.
%   - Multiclass averages $|C_{ij}|$ over classes.
% - Pre-screen for the two tree methods: when a representation has more than 2000 features, keep the 2000 with the highest variance before standardising. The rest get $s^{(k)}_j = 0$. DeepLIFT has no pre-screen.
% - The tree methods read where trees choose to split.
% - DeepLIFT reads a network's response to its inputs.
% - So a feature that no tree splits on can still score well under DeepLIFT.
% - Selection within representation $k$: take the top $b_k$ features by $s^{(k)}_j$, among those with $s^{(k)}_j > 0$:
%   - $S_k = \operatorname{top}_{b_k}\{\, j : s^{(k)}_j > 0 \,\}$.
%   - If no feature scores above zero, the full $b_k$ is taken anyway.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, "Most features do not vary on a given dataset". A feature that is constant on a dataset gets zero gain, so it is never selected.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing tree split-gain importance with SHAP values and gradient-based attributions on the same features (disagreement between feature-importance methods).
% Allocation method (budget split $b_k$):
% - Every allocation method starts from one standalone score per representation, $r_k$.
% - $r_k$ is measured on an internal split of each training fold: about 80% train, 20% held out, whole scaffold groups kept together.
% - The model that computes $r_k$ is the internal evaluator (below).
% - Under the default objective, $r_k$ is rescaled so that 0 means "no better than a trivial reference". This is a skill score:
%   - RMSE: $\tilde r_k = \max\!\left(0,\, 1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_k / \mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{mean}}\right)$. $\mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{mean}}$ is the error of always predicting the training mean.
%   - MAE: same form, with the training median as the reference.
%   - Spearman, Pearson, MCC, $R^2$: $\tilde r_k = \max(0, r_k)$.
%   - AUROC and concordance index: $\tilde r_k = \max\!\left(0,\, 2(r_k - 0.5)\right)$.
%   - Weighted F1 and accuracy: $\tilde r_k = \max\!\left(0, \frac{r_k - r_{\mathrm{maj}}}{1 - r_{\mathrm{maj}}}\right)$. $r_{\mathrm{maj}}$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class.
% - Under the other objectives, scores are shifted up by the most negative score when any is negative, so the worst representation sits at 0.
% - Why rescale: without it, the budget split depends on which metric the dataset happens to use. With it, weight is proportional to skill above the trivial reference.
% - Each method is a weighting $w_k = g(\tilde r)$ plus a rule for turning weights into features. Five methods are searched:
%   - Linear (performance-weighted): $w_k = \tilde r_k$.
%   - Power 2: $w_k = \tilde r_k^{\,2}$. It favours the stronger representations more than the linear weighting does.
%   - Softmax, temperature $T=1$: $w_k = \exp\!\left((\tilde r_k - \max_l \tilde r_l)/T\right)$. Because $\tilde r \in [0,1]$, this stays close to an equal split.
%   - Exponential rank decay: sort by $\tilde r_k$, best first, with rank $\rho_k = 0, 1, \dots$; then $w_k = e^{-0.3\,\rho_k}$. Only the order of the scores matters, not their size.
%   - Global pool with power-2 weights: described below.
% - Per-representation methods (the first four): $b_k \approx B\, w_k / \sum_l w_l$, then rounded.
%   - Rounding: take $\lfloor B w_k/\sum_l w_l \rfloor$. Then hand out the leftover features one at a time, largest fractional part first. The total is exactly $B$ unless the representations run out of features.
%   - Any representation with $w_k > 0$ keeps at least one feature.
%   - Every $b_k \le d_k$. Leftover features go to representations that still have room.
%   - If every weight is 0, the budget is split equally.
% - Global pool (power-2 weights): the weights size each representation's share of a candidate pool twice the budget.
%   - $c_k = \operatorname{round}\!\left(2B\, w_k / \sum_l w_l\right)$, with $c_k \le d_k$.
%   - Take each representation's top $c_k$ features by $s^{(k)}_j$. Join them into one matrix of about $2B$ features.
%   - Recompute importance once over the whole pool, with the same importance method. Keep the top $B$ features with non-zero importance.
%   - So $b_k$ is not fixed in advance. It is however many of representation $k$'s features survive the second ranking.
%   - Consequence: one tree model ranks the whole pool, and it may split on far fewer than $B$ features. So the hybrid can come in well under budget.
% - The contrast: a per-representation quota guarantees each representation its share. The global pool lets features from different representations compete directly.
% - The whole family assumes that a representation which predicts well on its own holds more useful features.
% - That is not always true: a strong representation may owe its score to a few features.
% - Cut from the search (one sentence each in the text):
%   - Greedy forward allocation: cut.
%   - Fixed "top $k$ representations" allocation names: replaced by the representation-count axis, so that any weighting can be paired with any count.
%   - Redundancy-penalised, thresholded and minimum-floor weightings: implemented, not searched.
%   - TODO(check with Adelaide): which of these to name in the thesis, and the reason for each.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the largest-remainder (Hamilton) apportionment method, for rounding proportional shares to integers.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on group-wise feature selection across several feature sets (multi-view or multi-block feature selection), to place the quota versus global-pool contrast against existing work.
% Internal evaluator:
% - The internal evaluator is the model that computes each representation's standalone score $r_k$.
% - So it sets both the budget split and the ranking used for the representation count $N$.
% - It does not score the final hybrid. The downstream model does that.
% - Values: matched (the same model type as the downstream model), random forest, LightGBM, and a dense neural network.
% - "Matched" adds nothing when it equals one of the other three.
%   - Random-forest, LightGBM and neural-network downstream models get 3 evaluators each.
%   - XGBoost and SVM downstream models get 4 each.
% - $r_k$ is computed once per fold, evaluator and metric. It is shared by every downstream model and budget.
% Downstream model:
% - Values: random forest, LightGBM, XGBoost, a dense neural network, support vector machine. Architectures and hyperparameters go in "Models and metrics".
% - The downstream model is fit on the hybrid built from each fold's training rows. It is scored on that fold's held-out rows.
% - This held-out score picks the winner.
% - The dense neural network replaced an earlier multilayer perceptron.
% - Linear models, CatBoost and other models are not searched.
% - The SVM is skipped above 10,000 training molecules. Its cost grows as $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^3)$ in the number of rows.
% - Under uncapped augmentation the SVM limit is 5,000 molecules, because augmentation adds rows (see "Augmentation").
% Objective:
% - The objective sets what $r_k$ measures, and so how the budget is split.
% - Predictive (default): $r_k$ is the dataset's own metric, rescaled as above.
% - Predictive correlation: $r_k$ is a correlation-scale prediction score, whatever the dataset's own metric. It puts prediction on the same scale as the blends below.
% - Similarity: $r_k$ is how well each held-out molecule's label is predicted from its five nearest neighbours in the representation's feature space. Scored by Spearman $\rho$ against the true labels (AUROC for binary classification). No model is fitted.
% - Structural fidelity: $r_k$ is how well distances in the feature space agree with maximum-common-substructure similarity between molecules.
% - Blends: a weighted mean of component scores, $r_k = \sum_c \lambda_c r^{(c)}_k$ with $\sum_c \lambda_c = 1$. Every component is on a $[-1,1]$ higher-is-better scale.
% - Each objective is its own search, not an axis inside one search.
% - Links to: Chapter 6, "Similarity in a hybrid" (full definitions of the similarity and structural-fidelity objectives).
% Size of the grid:
% - Per dataset, objective and augmentation setting: $|\mathcal{C}| = |\mathcal{A}| \times |\mathcal{B}| \times |\mathcal{I}| \times |\mathcal{N}| \times \sum_{m} |\mathcal{E}(m)|$.
%   - $\mathcal{A}$ allocation methods, $\mathcal{B}$ budgets, $\mathcal{I}$ importance methods, $\mathcal{N}$ representation counts, $\mathcal{E}(m)$ internal evaluators for downstream model $m$.
% - Full search: $5 \times 8 \times 3 \times 4 \times 17 = 8{,}160$ configurations. Here $17 = 3+3+3+4+4$ evaluators summed over the 5 downstream models.
% - Fast tier: 3 allocation methods (linear, power 2, rank decay), 1 importance method (tree gain), 2 representation counts ($N \in \{3, K\}$), 3 downstream models (random forest, LightGBM, neural network).
%   - $3 \times 8 \times 1 \times 2 \times 9 = 432$ configurations, with $9 = 3+3+3$ evaluators.
% - Each configuration is scored on every fold. With 5 folds the full search is $40{,}800$ downstream fits, before the importance and standalone-score fits.
% - Importance scores are computed once per fold and importance method, and reused by every configuration. Standalone scores are computed once per fold and evaluator.
% - So cost grows more slowly than the configuration count.
% - TODO(check): confirm the fold count and the fast-tier lists for the final runs before quoting these totals.
