\subsubsection{Feature importance scoring}

% TODO: this is out of date

% [KB 155] the three importance families and the 13 retained allocation strategies.
% TODO: citations??
% What were the qualifications for correlation/filtering allocation strategies? Can I formalize this a bit? 
Not all features from a given base representation are equally informative, and there are a variety of techniques that can assess each feature's relative importance. Tree-based feature importance measures favor features with high split gain, while gradient-based attributions reflect sensitivity in a neural network's loss landscape. \cite{??} 
We tested if, for the purposes of this task, there was a single feature importance method that worked across data sets and model architectures. Not only was there no clear winner, but there was evidence that certain networks preferred tree-based measures of importance while certain tree ensembles preferred gradient-based approaches. We landed on a set of five who's predictions did not correlate strongly with each other and who's selected features performed well as model input.

% TODO: background on each of these, also how do I name these in text
We score features using LightGBM gain, performance-weighted, Power2, DeepLift, and TreeSHAP. LightGBM gain is a tree based method which.... TreeSHAP is explanation-based, meaning it... DeepLift relies on neural attribution which... 

When searching for the features to use in the hybrid, thse feature importance scoring methods are swept.

\subsubsection{Feature allocation strategies}

% TODO: allocation strategies completely out of date
% Check framework doc? 
How to divide the total feature budget among sources is the role of the allocation strategy. We tested 31 candidates and retained 13 that appeared in top-performing configurations on multiple datasets and model types. The 13 retained strategies fall into three families. Score-based strategies divide the budget in proportion to transformed baseline scores: restricting features to the top $k$ sources, for example, or penalizing redundancy between correlated sources. A floor-based variant guarantees every source a minimum allocation, while a greedy forward approach iteratively adds features from whichever source most improves the internal evaluator, with early stopping. Once we have the allocation, we select the top features from each source by importance score and concatenate them to form the hybrid.

% [CR 810-847] the two original allocation strategies, spelled out as numbered procedures
% (greedy forward selection; performance-weighted). These are the ancestors of KIRBy's
% `greedy` and `performance-weighted` families.
% TODO: reconcile with the 13 strategies above -- these two are now a subset.
Given a total feature budget $B$, we test two strategies for allocating features from the given base representations. The first method uses greedy forward selection, iteratively adding top-ranked features from the representation that provides the largest performance improvement on the validation set. If the performance plateaus, it stops early.

% TODO: completely replace this is SO out of date, I need a version of this for all the allocation strategies I'm using, as well as the knobs

% \begin{enumerate}
%     \item \textbf{Split:} Divide training data into allocation-train (80\%) and validation (20\%) subsets using scaffold splitting.
    
%     \item \textbf{Initialize:} Set current feature set to empty, remaining budget to $B$.
    
%     \item \textbf{Rank features:} For each representation $r \in \{base_1, base_2, \dots base_m\}$, train RFs using the base representations individually, then rank features by mean decrease in impurity.
    
%     \item \textbf{Iterate:} While remaining budget $\geq$ step\_size and patience not exhausted:
%     \begin{enumerate}
%         \item For each representation, try adding the top step\_size candidates from its ordered list to the current feature set.
%         \item Train a RF using the tentative feature set.
%         \item Evaluate $R^2$ on the validation set.
%         \item Select the representation whose addition yields the highest validation $R^2$.
%         \item Permanently add step\_size features from the winning representation; remove from its candidate list.
%         \item If validation $R^2$ does not improve for patience consecutive rounds, terminate early.
%     \end{enumerate}
    
%     \item Re-compute importance scores for the features of the new hybrid on the full training data. For each representation, select the top $n_r$ features according to the allocation determined above, where $n_r$ is the number of features allocated to representation $r$ during iteration.
% \end{enumerate}

% This greedy approach works better on larger datasets, as the size of the validation set may not be enough to gain meaningful insights from. 

% TODO: this is not what's happening

The second method is performance-weighted allocation, where the distribution of features from each base representation is proportional to that representation's standalone predictive performance on the training set. This works under the assumption that representations with stronger predictive performance contribute more valuable features. It is worth noting that this is not always the case, as high-quality, large representations may rely on a small subset of valuable features. 

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Train a RF on each base representation independently using the full training data.
    \item Compute baseline $R^2$ for each representation via 5-fold cross-validation.
    \item Allocate features proportionally: 
    \[
    n_r = B \times \frac{R^2_r}{\sum_{r'} R^2_{r'}}
    \]
    \item For each representation, rank features by RF importance and select the top $n_r$.
    \item Concatenate selected features to form the hybrid representation.
\end{enumerate}

This method is a lot more computationally efficient than the greedy approach, however it cannot adapt allocation based on feature complementarity in the same way, and it relies on the assumption that many features in a well-performing representation are valuable.

\subsubsection{Search configuration and internal evaluation}

% [KB 161] the matched internal evaluator.
Features that improve predictions under RF may not necessarily help a kNN model, and vice versa. This is because variable importance depends fundamentally on the model, as Dong and Rudin showed \citep{Dong2020}, and as we found in our feature importance scoring analysis. To address this, we use an internal evaluator which scores each candidate configuration with a model whose inductive bias best fits the downstream model. We use the following evaluators: RF, DNN, SVM, LightGBM, XGBoost, and the special case of matched, which matches the internal evaluator to the final downstream evalutors. We fit each on the training fold and score it on the held-out fold. 

This internal evaluator is used to score candidate configurations in the internal selection process of features within the larger hybrid search. Thus, it is another axes we sweep while searching for the best set of features. 

% TODO: need additional files to specify architectures 

% TODO: does match always work? custom downstream model? 

% [KB 163] the combinatorial search and the Blueprint.
% TODO: these numbers are outdated
The full search tests all combinations of budgets, allocations, importance methods, downstream models, and internal evaluators under 5-fold cross-validation: about 15,000 configurations per dataset when using 7 downstream models. We approach this problem combinatorially as it allows us to measure which factors contribute most to performance variance (model choice, budget, allocation, or their interactions) rather than assuming any single axis dominates.

% TODO: mention representation knobs

The complete search result is cached to disk. The search runs once; everything after that is a lookup. Each search yields a Blueprint recording all parameters (source names, allocation strategy, importance method, budget, evaluator, metric) required to reproduce the hybrid on new data without re-running the search.


\subsection{Why each axis is swept}
% OUTLINE 5.2.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: the motivating story (expected one simple way to pick features; different tasks wanted different answers, so a search was needed).
% Core argument:
% - The search sweeps several axes instead of fixing one "best" setting.
% - Reason: on each axis, no setting can be shown to be best before seeing the data.
% - Three separate reasons back this:
%   - Feature importance depends on the model used to compute it.
%   - No learning method is best on every problem (no free lunch).
%   - Different datasets and downstream models may prefer different settings (to be shown by the full runs).
% - Why it matters: a fixed setting builds in an assumption about the data; sweeping measures which assumption the data supports.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (no single base representation is enough; different representations carry different information). This section says the same holds one level up, for how features are chosen.
% No free lunch:
% - Averaged over all possible target functions, no learning algorithm has lower off-training-set error than any other \citep{Wolpert1996}.
% - The optimisation version: averaged over all problems, no search algorithm beats another \citep{Wolpert1997}.
% - Plain implication: a method gains only by matching its assumptions to the problem. No default is safe everywhere.
% - Empirical echo: 179 classifiers were compared on 121 classification datasets \citep{FernandezDelgado2014}.
%   - The best families were random forests and Gaussian-kernel SVMs.
%   - Even these did not win on every dataset.
% - Caution for the text: no free lunch is a statement about averages over all problems. It does not say which setting wins on chemistry data. Use it as motivation, not proof.
% - The claim about her own datasets comes from the full runs (see the TODO(results) bullets below).
% Axis: downstream model:
% - Swept models: random forest, LightGBM, XGBoost, SVM, DNN.
% - Comparing several model families, rather than fixing one, is established practice in benchmarking \citep{FernandezDelgado2014}.
% - The model decides what counts as a good feature.
%   - A tree ensemble uses a feature through split thresholds, so feature scale does not matter to it.
%   - An SVM or a neural network is sensitive to feature scale and to redundant features.
% - So the best budget $B$ and the best allocation can differ by model on the same dataset.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature showing that the optimal feature subset differs between classifier types on the same dataset (tree ensembles vs kernel SVMs vs neural networks).
% - TODO(results): the best downstream model and budget per dataset, and one dataset where the best budget differs by model; numbers come from the full runs.
% Axis: budget $B$:
% - $B$ = total number of features in the hybrid, $\sum_k b_k = B$.
% - Too small: informative features are left out.
% - Too large: more uninformative features, and more room to overfit when $n$ is small.
% - Where the best $B$ sits depends on $n$, on how many informative features the base representations carry, and on the model.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how the optimal number of selected features depends on sample size and model class (the peaking phenomenon / Hughes effect).
% Axis: importance method $s^{(k)}_j$:
% - Variable importance is a property of a model, not of the data alone.
% - Many models fit the same data about equally well, yet rank variables differently (the "Rashomon set") \citep{Dong2020}.
% - Model class reliance: the range of a variable's importance over all well-performing models in a class \citep{Fisher2019}.
%   - $\mathrm{MCR}_-(j) = \min_{f \in \mathcal{R}(\epsilon)} \mathrm{VI}_j(f)$ and $\mathrm{MCR}_+(j) = \max_{f \in \mathcal{R}(\epsilon)} \mathrm{VI}_j(f)$.
%   - $\mathcal{R}(\epsilon)$ = the set of models whose loss is within $\epsilon$ of a reference model's loss.
% - Impurity-based importance in random forests is biased toward variables with more possible split points (continuous variables, or categorical variables with many categories) \citep{Strobl2007}.
%   - Sparse binary fingerprint bits and continuous descriptors differ in exactly this way.
%   - So a split-based ranking may favour descriptors for reasons unrelated to the label.
%   - Unverified: whether the same bias holds for gain importance in gradient boosting. TODO(lit): search for literature on bias of gain-based feature importance in gradient-boosted trees toward high-cardinality or continuous features.
% - The three swept methods come from different families:
%   - split gain in LightGBM \citep{ke2017lightgbm}
%   - SHAP values computed on trees (TreeSHAP)
%   - DeepLIFT attributions from a neural network
% - They are not expected to agree.
% - Why it matters: if the importance ranking changes, the selected set $S_k$ (top $b_k$ features of representation $k$) changes, so the hybrid changes.
% - TODO(results): how often each importance method gives the best hybrid, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Links to: 5.2.2 (definitions of each importance method); 5.2.6 (blueprints). The chemistry read off a blueprint depends on which importance method picked the features.
% Axis: allocation method (how $B$ is split into $b_k$):
% - Every method starts from $r_k$, the standalone score of a model trained on representation $k$ alone, computed inside the training fold.
% - Scores are oriented so that higher is better.
% - If any $r_k$ is negative, all are shifted up by the most negative value, so that $\min_k r_k = 0$.
% - Weights $w_k$ turn into budgets $b_k \approx B\, w_k / \sum_{k'} w_{k'}$, rounded to integers that sum to $B$.
% - Four per-representation methods:
%   - linear: $w_k = r_k$
%   - squared: $w_k = r_k^2$ (moves budget toward the strongest representations)
%   - softmax with temperature $T=1$: $w_k = \exp\big((r_k - \max_{k'} r_{k'})/T\big)$
%   - rank decay: $w_k = \exp(-0.3\,\rho_k)$, where $\rho_k = 0,1,2,\dots$ is the rank of representation $k$ (uses order only, ignores score gaps)
% - One global method:
%   - Build a candidate pool of about $2B$ features.
%   - Size each representation's share of the pool with $w_k = r_k^2$.
%   - Rank the whole pool once with the importance method and keep the top $B$.
%   - Here $b_k$ is an outcome of the ranking, not a fixed share.
% - The weightings encode different beliefs: "share the budget fairly evenly" vs "concentrate on the best one or two representations".
% - Which belief is right depends on whether the representations complement each other or one dominates. That is a property of the dataset.
% - Links to: 5.1.1 / 5.1.2 (pooling all features into one selection vs selecting within each representation); the Chapter 4 section on different representations carrying different information.
% - TODO(results): per dataset, how often each allocation method (and global pool vs per-representation share) gives the best hybrid; numbers come from the full runs.
% Axis: number of representations used (2, 3, 5 or all):
% - Only the top $m$ representations, ranked by $r_k$ within the fold, are kept before allocation.
% - For the others, $b_k = 0$.
% - This is a separate axis, so any weighting can be combined with any count.
% - Point from her brain dump: with 10 candidate representations, some datasets might do best with the top 2 or 3, others with all 10. Plain pooled feature selection over all features cannot express this choice directly.
% - TODO(results): per dataset, which representation count wins most often, and how much score is lost (in the dataset's own metric) if only one count were searched; numbers come from the full runs.
% Axis: internal evaluator:
% - The internal evaluator is the model that scores candidate hybrids inside the search.
% - This is the wrapper approach: judge a feature subset by training a model on it \citep{Kohavi1997}.
% - Options in the code: the downstream model itself ("match"), random forest, LightGBM, DNN.
% - TODO(check): the existing prose in the internal-evaluator section lists RF, DNN, SVM, LightGBM, XGBoost and match. The code sweeps match, RF, LightGBM and DNN only. Make the two agree.
% - "Match" is the obvious default.
% - A different evaluator might pick better features when data are small and a single internal split is noisy. This is a hypothesis for the results to test, not a finding.
% - Dong2020 is already cited in the internal-evaluator section. Fisher2019 could be added there as the class-level version of the same point.
% - TODO(results): which internal evaluator most often gives the best hybrid, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% Treating the search as one joint problem:
% - Choosing a model and its hyperparameters together is the combined algorithm selection and hyperparameter optimisation problem \citep{Thornton2013}.
% - The hybrid search does the same thing for building features.
% - The axes can interact. Examples: the best budget may depend on the model; the best allocation may depend on the representation count.
% - So tuning one axis at a time can miss the best joint setting.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on interaction effects between feature selection and classifier choice (selecting features and model jointly vs sequentially).
% Cost of sweeping, and how it is kept honest:
% - Choosing the best of many configurations on the same data gives an optimistically biased score (selection bias) \citep{Cawley2010}.
% - Remedy: the whole search runs inside each outer training fold, and the winner is scored on the untouched outer fold (nested cross-validation) \citep{Cawley2010}.
% - No significance tests between settings. Report per-fold scores and spread only.
% - The number of configurations is the product of the axis sizes:
%   - $|\mathcal{C}| = |\text{models}| \times |\text{budgets}| \times |\text{importance methods}| \times |\text{allocation methods}| \times |\text{representation counts}| \times |\text{evaluators}|$
% - Links to: 5.2.5 (the fast tier and adaptive pruning answer this cost). An axis is cut only when it is degenerate, broken or undefined, not on a theory about which setting wins.
% What was tried and dropped (one line each):
% - Greedy allocation: cut.
% - Allocation variants that fixed a number of representations inside the weighting were replaced by the separate representation-count axis.
% - TODO(check): decide whether the thesis mentions the larger first set of allocation methods that was narrowed to the five swept now, and on what evidence.
% Implications:
% - Why it matters: "the best setting differs by dataset" would itself be a finding, if the full runs show it. It argues against publishing one fixed feature-selection recipe for molecular property prediction.
% - Why it matters: the chosen configuration per dataset is recorded in the blueprint, so the choices can be inspected rather than hidden.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, "No single method fits every dataset".
% DIAGRAM: one small panel per axis (allocation method, representation count, importance method, internal evaluator). Datasets on the vertical axis. Bars = number of (downstream model, budget) combinations in which each setting gave the best hybrid. Purpose: show whether any setting wins on every dataset.
% DIAGRAM: for one dataset, held-out score (dataset's own metric) vs budget $B$ (log scale), one line per downstream model, showing where each model peaks.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.3 ----

When I began to implement this idea, I figured that there would be a fairly straightforward way to select the most informative features. However, I began to ask some questions. How do you measure feature importance? If you're starting with a handful of input representations, how many features do you take from each? How do you score these potential feature sets?  Unfortunately, my one-size-fits-all hypothesis rang true on multiple fronts. As I found out, different prediction tasks prefer different answers to all of those questions. So to figure out how to even go about selecting the best features, I realised I had to sweep a search space. 

% [RESTRUCTURE] was old Sec "Configurating the search".
% TODO: explain framework experiments
We ran a series of experiments to determine the best variables to include in the search space. 
When making a selection, we took into account how many potential hybrid representations would be gained by including that variable, as well as the compute time

% #   importance method        lightgbm_gain                       + deeplift, treeshap
% #   allocation               performance_weighted, power_2,      + softmax_T1,
% #                            rank_exp_d0.3                         wglobal_power2

\subsection{Augmentation}
% OUTLINE 5.2.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: small or specialised feature sets (MACCS, 3D pharmacophores, antibody mutation features) handled apart from the budget split.
% - Update needed: the existing paragraph says extras are ranked by random forest importance and appended one at a time if they improve the internal score. That is out of date. Extras are now ranked by correlation with what the base model gets wrong, and a whole block size is chosen at once (below).
% What augmentation is for:
% - Two uses, same mechanism.
%   - Use 1: one representation already dominates a dataset. Keep it at full width, then add a few features from the others.
%   - Use 2: small or specialised feature sets that cannot compete fairly inside the budget split (too few features, or only meaningful alongside something else). They are offered as extras after the main hybrid is built.
% - Terms: "base" = the feature matrix being improved (one full representation, or a finished hybrid $\mathbf{H}$). "Donors" = the feature sets the extras are drawn from.
% - Why it matters: a chemist who already trusts one representation (e.g. an ECFP model in use) can keep it. Augmentation reports which few outside features help, and how many.
% - Why it matters: this is a lower barrier to adoption than replacing the representation outright.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, "Different representations carry different information". Augmentation tests that claim directly: does another representation hold signal the dominant one lacks?
% - Links to: "Blueprints". The kept extras are stored in the blueprint with their source representation, so they can be read chemically where the donor allows it (e.g. MACCS keys).
% Which feature sets are offered as extras (set per dataset, not per domain):
% - Small molecules: MACCS structural keys (166 keys) \citep{durant2002maccs}, 3D pharmacophore features, USR shape descriptors \citep{ballester2007usr}, USRCAT (USR plus pharmacophore channels) \citep{schreyer2012usrcat}, Coulomb matrix eigenvalues \citep{rupp2012coulomb}.
%   - MACCS is a donor on most small-molecule datasets but sits in the main pool on one. Word this as "usually a donor".
%   - On a few datasets a learned embedding or torsion fingerprint is a donor instead of a main representation.
% - Antibodies: mutation features (differences from a parent sequence), protein language model log-likelihood ratios (ESM-2, AntiBERTy), amino acid composition, paratope features, structural features.
% - Proteins (drug-target sets): pocket descriptors, protein shape descriptors, residue contact-graph features, amino acid composition.
% - Nucleotides: codon usage, GC content, dinucleotide frequencies, DNA shape, sequence complexity.
% - Some datasets have no donors, so they are not augmented.
% - Common thread: each donor is low-dimensional or describes one aspect only (shape, the mutated positions). Useful as a supplement, weak as a whole representation.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on combining 3D shape/pharmacophore descriptors with 2D fingerprints in QSAR models.
% How it works:
% - Step 1, internal split: the training fold is split once more, about 80% internal-train and 20% internal-validation. The split is group-aware (scaffold or sequence groups kept together). The outer test fold is never touched.
% - Step 2, what the base gets wrong: fit a small random forest (50 trees, depth 10) on the base features. Use 3-fold group-aware cross-validation inside internal-train. Take the out-of-fold residuals: $r_i = y_i - \hat{y}^{\mathrm{oof}}_i$.
%   - Out-of-fold, not in-sample: a forest's in-sample residuals shrink towards zero and mostly reflect fitting noise.
%   - For binary classification, $\hat{y}$ is the predicted probability of the positive class.
%   - The residual model is always this cheap forest, not the downstream model. This keeps the ranking cost fixed.
% - Step 3, rank donor features: pool every feature from every donor. Score each by $c_j = |\rho_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathbf{x}_j, \mathbf{r})|$ (Pearson correlation, on internal-train). Keep the top $M = 50$.
%   - Why residuals, not labels: ranking against $\mathbf{y}$ promotes features that repeat what the base already knows. Ranking against $\mathbf{r}$ promotes features that explain the base model's errors.
%   - Same idea as fitting each new stage to the current residual in gradient boosting \citep{friedman2001greedy}.
%   - Same idea as picking the variable most correlated with the current residual in least angle regression \citep{efron2004lars}.
%   - Other ranking rules exist but are not the default: forest importance, LightGBM gain, mutual information and DeepLIFT against the labels, and DeepLIFT against the residuals.
%   - Limitation: $|\rho_{\mathrm{P}}|$ sees only linear, one-feature-at-a-time relations.
%   - Limitation: it does not see redundancy among the extras themselves.
%   - Limitation: by default every donor feature is pooled. A wide donor (thousands of features) gets more chances in the ranking than a 40-feature engineered set. An optional per-donor cap exists.
% - Step 4, choose how many: try block sizes $k \in \{0, 5, 10, 20, 40\}$, capped at the candidates available. Block $k$ = the top $k$ ranked features.
%   - Each block size is scored on internal-validation with the downstream model: score $S(k)$.
%   - Pick $k^* = \arg\max_k S(k)$, subject to $S(k) > S(0) + \delta$. Margin $\delta = 0$ by default.
%   - $k = 0$ (keep nothing) is scored first and wins ties. "No extras" is the default outcome, not a failure.
%   - Five whole-block choices replace about 50 one-by-one accept/reject decisions.
%   - Choosing among many options on a small validation set overfits the choice \citep{cawley2010overfitting}. Fewer choices leaves less room for this.
%   - Variant: instead of the fixed list, $k$ can be set as a fraction of the final width, $k = \mathrm{round}(f \cdot B)$.
% - Step 5, rebuild: the chosen block (donor name and feature indices) is stored. It is re-extracted from the full data for the outer fold, like the rest of the hybrid.
% Two ways to fit extras against the budget $B$:
% - Uncapped: extras are appended outside the budget. Final width $= |\mathbf{H}| + k^*$, up to $B + 40$.
% - Capped: extras must displace base features. Base features are ranked once by forest importance.
%   - The weakest $k^*$ base features are dropped. Final width $= (B - k^*) + k^* = B$.
%   - Any unspent budget is filled before any base feature is dropped.
%   - Only in capped mode must an extra feature beat a base feature to earn its slot.
% - Why it matters: uncapped and no-augmentation at the same nominal $B$ are not the same width. Comparing them mixes "better features" with "more features". Capped removes the width difference.
% - Dominant-representation use: the base is one representation and $B = d_k$ (its full width). Uncapped is the natural mode there. Capped would drop the base's own features.
% - In the main search, a dataset with two or more main representations and a donor list runs three separate conditions: no augmentation, uncapped, capped.
% - A dataset with a single main representation runs uncapped only.
% - Augmentation is a separate condition, not a swept axis inside one search. Every other axis (model, budget, allocation, importance) is searched once per condition.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on adding a few selected descriptors from a second feature set to a primary molecular fingerprint, chosen by the primary model's errors (residual-driven feature addition in QSAR).
% - A decoy check tests whether the rule rejects useless extras. Two fake donors are planted among the real ones.
%   - Decoy 1: pure Gaussian noise, as wide as the real donors.
%   - Decoy 2: a near-copy of 64 base features with 5% random jitter.
%   - The correct number of decoy features admitted is 0.
%   - Links to: "Complexity experiments" for the full method.
% - TODO(results): how often $k^*=0$ is chosen per dataset and model, and the per-fold score of base alone vs base plus extras (uncapped and capped); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): which donor feature sets are kept most often, per domain; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): how many decoy features are admitted in the decoy check; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: flow diagram. Left: one base matrix plus several donor blocks. Middle: base forest, then out-of-fold residuals $\mathbf{r}$, then donor features ranked by $|\rho_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathbf{x}_j,\mathbf{r})|$, then top 50. Right: two branches. Uncapped appends $k^*$ features. Capped drops the $k^*$ weakest base features and adds $k^*$. Internal-validation score picks $k^* \in \{0,5,10,20,40\}$.
% - DIAGRAM (results, later): per dataset, a bar of the chosen $k^*$ per fold, coloured by donor source.
% Big picture:
% - Implication: if $k^*$ is usually small but not zero, one dominant representation is close to enough, and a few cheap extras fix specific gaps.
% - Implication: if $k^*$ is usually zero, the dominant representation already covers what the others offer.
% - Links to: "Simple is often better" (Chapter 7). Augmentation is the minimal version of the hybrid: one representation plus a few features.
% - Links to: "No single method fits every dataset" (Chapter 7). Which donors help, and how many features, is expected to vary by dataset. That is why $k$ is chosen per fold and not fixed.
% - Links to: "Beyond small molecules". Antibody mutation features and log-likelihood ratios reach the model through augmentation. For some antibody datasets this is how the domain-specific signal gets in.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.4 ----

% [KB 157] augmentation: low-dimensional sources handled outside the budget.
% TODO: review, also I think this is out of date
Some features are too low-dimensional or too specialized to participate in the budget allocation stage: MACCS fingerprints (167 features), for instance, or log-likelihood ratios (3 features). Excluding them from the allocation would lose potentially useful signal; including them would distort the budget distribution because they cannot contribute many features. We handle these as augmentation: after the base hybrid is constructed, we pool the augmentation features, rank them by random forest feature importance, and append them only if they improve the internal evaluator score.

% TODO: I need a better list to describe augmentations
We also looked at smaller sets of features, or augmented features, which cannot stand on their own as a full representation but nonetheless may contain useful information. These range from smaller fingerprints like MACCS or Coloumb matrices, sets of structural information like 3D pharmacophores, and features describing only the differences between the sames like mutation features for antibodies. These were handled on a more per-feature basis, explained below. 
% TODO: add augmentation subsection


\subsection{Fast tier and adaptive pruning}
% OUTLINE 5.2.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: a short paragraph on adaptive pruning (allocation methods scored on the first fold, dropped if in the top 3 in fewer than 15% or 25% of groups). It is out of date. Votes now use the first two folds. The allocation rule is best-of-2 at 20%. Three more axes are voted on. There is a checkpoint at budget 100. Pruning is also off unless a study asks for it. Replace that paragraph rather than extend it.
%
% Framing (the two ways to cut cost are different things):
% - The full search is a grid. It covers every combination of allocation method, importance method, representation count, budget, downstream model and internal evaluator.
% - Each configuration is scored under $F$-fold cross-validation ($F = 5$).
% - Grid size: $|\mathcal{G}| = |A|\,|I|\,|R|\,|\mathcal{B}|\,|M \times E|$.
%   - $A$ = allocation methods, $I$ = importance methods, $R$ = representation counts, $\mathcal{B}$ = budgets.
%   - $M \times E$ = the (downstream model, internal evaluator) pairs.
% - Cost is roughly $|\mathcal{G}| \times F$ model fits, plus the importance scoring.
% - Cost grows multiplicatively with every axis added.
% - There are two ways to make the search cheaper. They answer different questions.
%   - Fast tier: shorter lists on some axes. The lists are fixed before the search starts and are the same for every dataset. The fast tier knowingly accepts a possibly worse answer in exchange for a much smaller grid.
%   - Adaptive pruning: decided during the search, from this dataset's own fold scores. It aims to find the same best answer while skipping configurations that have already clearly lost. Time saved is a side effect.
% - Why it matters: without these, the search is impractical on large datasets.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, Future directions, where speed is a stated limitation.
%
% Fast tier:
% - Full grid per budget: 5 allocation methods x 3 importance methods x 4 representation counts x 17 (downstream model, internal evaluator) pairs = 1,020 configurations.
%   - Over 8 budgets (10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000 and 2000 features), that is 8,160 configurations per dataset. Each is scored over 5 folds.
%   - The 17 pairs: 5 downstream models x 4 internal evaluators = 20, minus 3 duplicates.
%   - The duplicates come from the 'matched' evaluator. It uses the same model type as the downstream model. For random forest, LightGBM and the neural network (DNN), that repeats an evaluator already in the list.
% - The fast grid shortens three axes:
%   - Allocation: 3 methods instead of 5. It keeps linear performance weighting, power-law weighting with exponent 2, and rank-exponential decay with $d = 0.3$. It drops softmax weighting ($T = 1$) and the single global-pool method.
%   - Importance: LightGBM gain only. DeepLIFT and TreeSHAP are dropped.
%   - Representation count: 2 values (the top 3 representations, and all of them) instead of 4 (2, 3, 5, all).
%   - Internal evaluators: unchanged.
%   - Downstream models: in the runs, all five are scored. The model list is not shortened.
% - Fast grid: 3 x 1 x 2 x 17 x 8 = 816 configurations per dataset, a tenth of the full grid.
% - How the fast lists were chosen: from earlier measurement on several datasets. A member stays in the fast lists if it is cheap and wins often.
%   - Allocation: the fast list keeps the three methods that most often ranked in the top three when each (dataset, model, budget) was compared.
%   - A member that is expensive and wins belongs in the full grid only. The global-pool allocation is the example: it wins often but is costly.
%   - A member that is expensive and rarely wins belongs in neither list.
%   - TODO(results): the calibration behind the fast lists (each importance method's share of run time per dataset; the score change from dropping each member); numbers come from the full runs.
% - Which datasets use the fast tier: those where a full grid cannot finish.
%   - Criterion: more than about 10,000 samples, or an estimated full-grid run time beyond the cluster's job limits.
%   - Examples: QM9, the large logD set, the antibody expression set, the large genomic sequence sets, hERG, and the two trastuzumab HER2 sets.
%   - TODO: name these by their real dataset names in the thesis, and mark them in the dataset table (Section 5.3.1 / Appendix A).
% - A fast search is a different search, not a subset of a full one. Its results are kept apart from full-grid results. They cannot later be topped up into a full result.
% - Dropped members are still searched on every other dataset. The fast list is a compute compromise, not a claim that those members are useless. This is consistent with Section 5.2.3.
% - Links to: Section 5.2.3 (why each axis is swept); Appendix C (the search space and its defaults). The fast/full table can sit in Section 5.2.3 or here, but not in both.
%
% Adaptive pruning, in words:
% - Idea: after the first folds, some configurations are so far behind that scoring them on the remaining folds only confirms a loss. Stop scoring those.
% - Related to early stopping in hyperparameter search, which spends more compute on promising settings and drops poor ones early.
%   - Examples: successive halving \citep{Jamieson2016} and Hyperband \citep{Li2018Hyperband}.
%   - Difference: those methods cut after partial training, e.g. fewer iterations or less data. Here the cut comes after partial cross-validation (a subset of folds), by voting within matched groups.
% - Pruning is off unless a study switches it on. The main search that is computed once per dataset and reused by the experiments does not prune.
%   - TODO: confirm, study by study, which results in the thesis were produced with pruning on (some older settings used an allocation cut-off of 15% or 25%), and state it per study.
% - Pruning is judged on one criterion only: it must never delete a configuration that would have been the best.
% - The cut-offs were chosen as the settings that lost no winner on a calibration set of datasets, with a margin either side. They were not chosen for the largest saving.
%
% Adaptive pruning, step by step:
% - Step 1, voting folds: every configuration is scored on the first $v = 2$ folds.
%   - Every rule below ranks by the mean over these folds: $\bar{s}_c = \frac{1}{v}\sum_{f=1}^{v} s_{c,f}$.
%   - Budgets are processed from smallest to largest.
% - Step 2, checkpoint at budget 100:
%   - Group configurations by (downstream model, internal evaluator, representation count).
%   - Within each group, rank the (allocation, importance) pairs by $\bar{s}_c$, using configurations at budgets $\le 100$.
%   - Keep the top $\max(1, \mathrm{round}(n(1-\delta)))$ pairs, where $n$ is the number of pairs in the group and $\delta = 0.5$.
%   - Dropped pairs are never run at budgets above 100 for that group.
%   - This removes configurations inside a group. It never removes an axis value from the whole search.
% - Step 3, per-axis votes, one axis at a time:
%   - Take an axis $a$ with values $m$. Form groups $g \in G_a$ that fix every other axis (same model, budget, evaluator and so on). Values then compete only against each other.
%   - Points: $p_m = \sum_{g \in G_a} \mathbb{1}[\,m \in \text{top-}t \text{ of } g \text{ by } \bar{s}\,]$.
%   - Drop value $m$ from the remaining folds if $p_m < \tau\,|G_a|$.
%   - Settings $(t, \tau)$: allocation (2, 20%), representation count (1, 10%), importance (2, 20%), internal evaluator (3, 10%).
%   - A vote runs only if the axis has more than $t$ values.
%   - A vote can never delete every value of an axis.
%   - Configurations that failed to score are ranked last. They cannot take a top place.
% - Step 4: survivors are scored on the remaining folds. The best configuration is picked by the mean over all folds, as without pruning.
% - DIAGRAM: funnel.
%   - Top: the full grid on folds 1-2.
%   - Next: the checkpoint at budget 100 (half the (allocation, importance) pairs per group dropped for larger budgets).
%   - Next: the per-axis votes (allocation, representation count, importance, evaluator).
%   - Bottom: survivors on folds 3-5, then the best configuration.
%   - Annotate each stage with the share of configurations remaining.
% - DIAGRAM (optional): one voting group as a small table. Rows = allocation methods. Columns = groups (model x budget x ...). A tick marks a top-$t$ place. Show point totals and the $\tau|G_a|$ cut-off line.
%
% Why these design choices:
% - Voting within matched groups, not across the whole grid: a value that is best for a random forest at a small budget should not be deleted for losing to a neural network at a large budget.
% - Two voting folds, not one:
%   - On a single fold, many configurations can get identical scores. This happens most on classification with small test folds.
%   - Ties are then broken by list order, so a one-fold vote can delete good values by accident.
%   - TODO(results): the calibration showing that one-fold voting deleted winners and two-fold voting did not; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Why it matters: pruning keeps the choice per dataset. If an expensive method is winning on a dataset, pruning keeps searching it there. The fast tier does the opposite: it drops that method on every fast-tier dataset.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, 'No single method fits every dataset'. Which values survive pruning on each dataset is direct evidence that different datasets prefer different settings.
%
% Caveat on evaluation:
% - When pruning is on, the voting folds are used both to choose survivors and to score them.
% - The mean over all folds is then slightly optimistic. This is the selection bias from reusing the same data for selection and evaluation \citep{Cawley2010}.
% - Single representations are never pruned. A fair hybrid-vs-single comparison under pruning leaves out the voting folds' scores, or re-scores without them.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on selection bias from early elimination in cross-validated model selection, specifically racing algorithms (Hoeffding races, Maron and Moore) and early-stopped cross-validation.
%
% Results placeholders:
% - TODO(results): hours saved by pruning, and the number of (model, budget) best scores it changed, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): fast-tier vs full-grid best score on datasets where both were run; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO: some older notes call the pruning cut-offs uncalibrated. Later notes say they were re-set on a calibration record. State only the final settings in the thesis.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.5 ----

\subsubsection{Fast tier searching}

\subsubsection{Adaptive pruning}

% [KB 217] the adaptive pruning thresholds, from Supplementary Methods.
Adaptive pruning: allocation strategies are evaluated on fold 0 and pruned if they appear in the top-3 in fewer than a threshold fraction of (importance, evaluator, model, budget) groups. The primary hybrid searches (no augmentation and uncapped augmentation across general and top-tier representation sets) use a 15\% threshold. Capped augmentation searches and standalone experiment-specific searches use a more aggressive 25\% threshold to reduce computational cost; this higher threshold prunes more aggressively from the candidate allocation strategies without affecting the quality of the surviving strategies.


\subsection{Blueprints}
% OUTLINE 5.2.6: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.6 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: blueprint as a stored recipe; list of recipe settings; ECFP/MACCS mapped to substructures; embeddings return only representation name plus dimension index; explainability works both ways (discover vs confirm).
% Corrections to existing prose (checked against the code):
% - Prose says the blueprint recreates "this exact hybrid representation on any data". Not true on new molecules.
%   - A rebuild re-scores the representations and re-picks features on the rows it is given.
%   - Other molecules can therefore give other features.
% - Prose says the blueprint stores no selected feature indices. True for the base-representation features.
%   - It does store, per fold, how many features each representation received.
%   - It also stores, per fold, which augmentation features were kept.
% - Rebuilding on the same rows with the same seed and settings should repeat the same selection (expected from the design; not separately tested).
% - Missing from the prose's list of recipe settings: the objective (predictive vs similarity).
% - Missing: how many representations the configuration was allowed to use (2, 3, 5 or the whole pool).
% - Missing: the metric direction (lower or higher is better).
% - Missing: the augmentation settings in full (strategy, how candidates are ranked, per-source cap, fraction of budget, acceptance margin, candidate list length).
% What a blueprint is:
% - A blueprint separates two things: the recipe (how to build a hybrid) and the result (which features one build picked on one set of molecules).
% - Recipe = the representation pool $\{\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(K)}\}$, budget $B$, step size, allocation method, importance method for $s^{(k)}_j$, internal evaluator, objective, number of representations allowed, seed, fold count, metric and its direction, task type, augmentation settings.
% - The recipe acts as a function of the data: $\mathcal{R}: (\{\mathbf{X}^{(k)}\}, \mathbf{y}) \mapsto (b_1,\dots,b_K;\ S_1,\dots,S_K)$ with $|S_k| = b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k = B$.
% - The result = the fixed index sets $S_1,\dots,S_K$ from one fit.
% - Applying a result to new molecules only takes those features: $\mathbf{H}_{\text{new}} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{\text{new}}[:,S_1],\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{\text{new}}[:,S_K]]$.
% - Stored as data for inspection: each representation's score from the search; the per-fold budget split $b_k^{(f)}$ for each fold $f$; the per-fold indices of the augmentation features kept.
% - The per-fold split and kept augmentation features are not read when rebuilding.
% - The stored representation scores are not used to split the budget at rebuild; each rebuild scores the representations on its own training rows.
% - The recipe does not name which representations won. It stores the whole pool plus the allowed count.
%   - When the allowed count is smaller than the pool, the rebuild keeps the top-ranked representations up to that count, ranked by representation scores.
% - Why it matters: the per-fold split $b_k^{(f)}$ shows which representations actually fed the hybrid, and whether that is stable across folds.
% - Why it matters: a blueprint can be shared and rebuilt by someone else without repeating the whole search over allocation methods, importance methods, evaluators and budgets.
% Two ways to reuse a blueprint:
% - Re-run the recipe: needs labels $\mathbf{y}$ on the new molecules.
%   - Representation scores and feature importances are recomputed on those rows, so $b_k$ and $S_k$ adapt to the new dataset.
% - Reuse the exact features: take $S_1,\dots,S_K$ from a labelled fit and take the same features from the new molecules.
%   - No labels needed.
%   - The only option for unlabelled molecules (e.g. screening a library).
%   - Also what a model already trained on those features requires.
% - Re-running the recipe without labels is not allowed.
%   - With a constant label vector every feature importance $s^{(k)}_j$ is zero.
%   - The top-$b_k$ pick would then fall back to feature order and mean nothing.
% - A rebuild uses the same group-aware internal train/validation split as the search when groups are given; without groups it uses a random 20% validation split, which is not what the search used.
% - Trade-off, recipe: adapts to the new data, but costs a new selection per dataset and can give a different feature set.
% - Trade-off, exact features: fixed and cheap, but assumes the same features matter on the new data.
% - Links to: 5.1.4 (what a blueprint tells a chemist); 5.10 Limitations (a recipe rebuilt on other molecules picks other features).
% Transfer tests (methods only):
% - Cross-dataset transfer: find a blueprint on a large source dataset, apply it to a smaller target dataset using the representations both datasets share.
%   - Example source/target pair: lipophilicity (logD, about 7k molecules) to hERG inhibition constants (Ki, about 1.4k molecules).
%   - Four conditions per downstream model per target fold.
%   - Condition 1: best single representation, picked inside the fold.
%   - Condition 2: native hybrid, recipe searched inside the fold.
%   - Condition 3: recipe transfer, the source recipe re-run on the target training rows.
%   - Condition 4: exact-feature transfer, the source feature indices from a fit on source rows; the downstream model is retrained on target training rows.
%   - Source rows are never in a target fold, so neither transfer condition sees target test rows.
%   - Question it answers: can a recipe found once on a large dataset be published and reused on a small one without a new search?
% - Cross-fold transfer (check for a selection fitted to one training set):
%   - For fold pair $(k, j)$: search a recipe on the rows outside both fold $k$ and fold $j$, apply it to fold $j$.
%   - Compare with the recipe searched for fold $j$ alone.
%   - The two differ in how many rows the search saw (one fold fewer), not in whose recipe is applied.
%   - A small gap means the selection is not fitted to one particular training set.
% - Stability of the selected features across folds, Nogueira index \citep{Nogueira2018}.
%   - $M$ folds, $d$ = number of features in the pool, $\hat p_f$ = fraction of folds that select feature $f$, $\bar k$ = mean number of features selected per fold.
%   - $\hat\Phi = 1 - \frac{\frac{1}{d}\sum_{f=1}^{d} s_f^2}{\frac{\bar k}{d}\left(1-\frac{\bar k}{d}\right)}$, with $s_f^2 = \frac{M}{M-1}\hat p_f(1-\hat p_f)$.
%   - $\hat\Phi = 1$: the same features in every fold.
%   - $\hat\Phi \approx 0$: no more overlap than random subsets of the same size.
%   - $d$ is the pool width, not the union of the selected features.
% - TODO(results): recipe transfer vs exact-feature transfer vs native hybrid vs best single representation on the target, per model, per-fold scores and spread; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): cross-fold transfer gap and Nogueira stability per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on reusing feature subsets selected on one QSAR dataset for a related endpoint (transfer of feature selection across chemical datasets).
% Naming the selected features (what can be read chemically):
% - Each selected feature is traced back to its source representation $k$ and its index $j$ in $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$.
% - Physicochemical descriptor vector: 200 named RDKit descriptors (e.g. MolLogP, TPSA, BertzCT) \citep{rdkit}.
% - MACCS keys: each of the 166 keys has a SMARTS pattern and a name (e.g. key 154 = carbonyl, C=O) \citep{Durant2002}.
% - ECFP bits \citep{rogers2010}: a bit is resolved to the atom environment (centre atom plus radius) that sets it in an example molecule.
%   - The example is the first of up to 50 molecules in which the bit is set.
%   - Caveat: a hashed bit can be set by several different substructures (4.2.2), so one example environment is not the only meaning of that bit.
%   - Report it as "one substructure that sets this bit".
% - Non-molecular domains are also named: antibody sequence encodings by position and residue; gene-expression pathway scores by pathway.
% - Not named, reported as representation plus dimension index: atom-pair, Avalon, graph-kernel and Mordred features, and every learned embedding (4.2.4).
% - Other hashed fingerprints (e.g. MAP4, reaction fingerprints) are reported as bit index only.
% - Open decision (recorded in the outline): whether to add names for Mordred descriptors and atom-pair features before the blueprint results (5.6); both could be derived.
% - Why it matters: for named features the blueprint turns the selection into a chemical hypothesis (which groups or properties drive the endpoint) that a chemist can check against known structure-activity knowledge.
% - Why it matters: for embeddings it only says which model's embedding carried signal, not what chemistry.
% - Why it matters: blueprints from different downstream models on the same dataset can be compared, to see whether tree ensembles and neural networks prefer different feature sets and different numbers of features.
% - Links to: 5.6 (deep dives on specific datasets), 4.2.2 (hash collisions), 4.2.4 (embeddings cannot be read chemically), 5.1.4 (blueprints and chemists).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on mapping Morgan/ECFP fingerprint bits back to substructures for model interpretation (bit-to-environment visualisation in QSAR).
% DIAGRAM: two-panel schematic. Left: "re-run recipe" path, new labelled data -> score representations -> split budget $b_k$ -> pick top features per representation -> hybrid. Right: "reuse exact features" path, stored $S_1..S_K$ -> take the same features from unlabelled molecules -> hybrid. Mark which path needs labels.
% DIAGRAM: example blueprint card: recipe settings as a small table; stacked bar of $b_k$ per fold (x = fold, y = number of features, colour = representation); a list of top named features (MACCS/descriptor names, ECFP environment drawn on a molecule).
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.6 ----

% TODO: is this list for blueprint components complete? 
To be able to better examine the selected features and to regenerate them on new data sets, we produce blueprints. After a hybrid feature selection search is completed, it's results are stored as a blueprint containing the ``recipe'' required to recreate this exact hybrid representation on any data. These blueprints store the selected picks for the pre-specified budget,  input representations actually used, feature importance scoring method, allocation strategy, and internal evaluator. It contains other runtime information like the step size, number of folds, the seed, the metric being used to score, the task domain, whether augmented features were being used, and the per-representation scores. 

The blueprint does not store selected feature indices, those can only be recovered when the blueprint is refit on the data, which re-runs the feature selection process deterministically from that recipe and seed. 

It also provides a registry of selected features, stating where that feature came from. On some representations, we added an extension where features are mapped back to their function. For instance, ECFP and MACCS bits are mapped to SMARTS substructures. This blueprint will tell you which of those substructures were selected. This is not possible for learned embeddings for which the blueprint will return the bare representation name and dimension index. This mapping provides a layer of explainability. It can be used both to discover what features are useful for your task and to confirm that the features being selected are in fact lining up with the underlying known chemistry. 
% TODO what representations can it map to chemically related names specficially? 


\section{Methods: experimental setup}
% OUTLINE 5.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section does:
% - Short lead-in to the setup subsections: datasets, base representations, models and metrics, then the complexity experiments and the applied settings.
% - It says one thing the subsections do not: what one standard experiment looks like from start to finish.
% - In one line: build a hybrid on training rows only, then test it on held-out rows next to each base representation.
% - The chapter-specific experiments come later. This section only fixes the shared protocol they reuse.
% The datasets in one line each (details go in Datasets and splitting):
% - 16 datasets across 6 domains: small-molecule ADME and toxicity (5), quantum properties (1, QM9), reaction yield (2), antibodies (4), nucleotide sequences (2), cancer genomics from TCGA (2).
% - Why it matters: the method is not tied to molecules. Any supervised task with several vector-valued representations of the same samples fits.
% - Links to: Beyond small molecules (earlier in this chapter), Representations by domain, and the Datasets and curation appendix.
% The standard protocol, in order:
% - Outer loop: $F = 5$ group-aware folds. Samples that share a group never sit on both sides of a split.
%   - Groups by domain: Murcko scaffold (small molecules, QM9), additive (reactions), sequence-identity cluster, mutation position or edit-distance shell (antibodies), chromosome (nucleotides), tissue source site (TCGA).
%   - Regression uses group $k$-fold. Classification uses stratified group $k$-fold, which keeps class balance while groups stay whole.
%   - No random splits anywhere.
% - For outer fold $f$, the rows split into training rows $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}$ and test rows $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{test}}$.
% - The hybrid search sees only $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}$.
%   - It sweeps budgets $B \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2000\}$ features, capped per dataset, and the other search axes (see The search space).
%   - It scores each configuration with its own inner group-aware cross-validation on the training rows (5 inner folds by default).
% - Inside each inner fold, the scores that set the allocation $b_k$ come from a further held-out carve of the training rows.
%   - The carve is the first fold of a 5-fold group split: about 20% of the rows held out, deterministic, no random seed.
%   - Why it matters: the search itself never scores a configuration on rows that share a group with the rows it was built on.
% - The search returns the best configuration (the blueprint) for each downstream model.
% - The blueprint is rebuilt on all of $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}$. That gives the selected feature sets $S_k$.
% - The same $S_k$ are applied to the test rows: $\mathbf{H}_{\text{test}} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{\text{test},S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{\text{test},S_K}]$. No selection is redone on test rows.
% - Baselines: each base representation $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$ on its own. It is trained on the same $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}$ with the same model and scored on the same $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{test}}$.
%   - Baselines are individual representations. There is no concatenation baseline and no averaged baseline.
% - The comparator for fold $f$ is the best single representation $k^*_f$. It is picked using the training rows only, from the inner search's own single-representation scores.
%   - $k^*_f$ is then refit on $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}$ and scored on $\mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{test}}$, exactly like the hybrid.
%   - It is not the best representation on the test fold. Picking on test rows would give the baseline an advantage the hybrid does not get.
%   - $k^*_f$ can differ between folds.
% - Main comparison per fold: $\Delta_f = m_f(\mathbf{H}) - m_f(\mathbf{X}^{(k^*_f)})$, with the sign flipped for lower-is-better metrics like RMSE, so $\Delta_f > 0$ always means the hybrid is better.
% - Reported per dataset and model: the per-fold scores for the hybrid and for every single representation, $f = 1, \dots, F$, and their spread.
% - No significance tests and no confidence intervals. Per-fold scores and their spread only.
% - Why nested: choosing a configuration and scoring it on the same folds biases the score upward \citep{Varma2006, Cawley2010}.
%   - Why it matters: every hybrid-versus-baseline number in the results sections comes from test rows the search never saw.
%   - Why it matters: both sides pay the same cost. The hybrid and the best single representation are each chosen on training rows and judged on the same unseen test rows.
% - DIAGRAM: nested protocol as boxes. Outer 5-fold group split; training rows go to the hybrid search (inner group-aware folds, budgets swept); blueprint rebuilt on training rows; the same selected features applied to test rows. In parallel, the best single representation is picked on training rows, refit, and tested on the same fold. Output: per-fold scores for both and $\Delta_f$.
% How it fits with the rest:
% - Links to: Methods: the hybrid search defines what the search does. This section defines how its output is tested.
% - Links to: Chapter 6 (Hybrid Representations for Similarity and Label Noise) builds on this protocol. TODO(check): state there which rows receive injected noise.
% - Links to: Limitations. Cost grows with the number of outer folds times the full search. That is why the fast tier and adaptive pruning exist.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on nested cross-validation with grouped (scaffold) splits in QSAR model selection.
% - TODO(results): per-dataset table of the per-fold best single representation versus the hybrid, per model, with per-fold spread; numbers come from the full runs.
% Checks for the subsections (not for this intro):
% - The existing prose in Datasets and splitting says the internal split is a single random group-aware draw. The current method uses the first fold of a 5-fold group split of the training rows (about 20% held out, no random seed).
% - The existing prose says "5-fold GroupKFold throughout". Classification datasets use stratified group folds.
% - The existing prose gives $R^2$ as the reaction-yield metric. The method uses RMSE for reaction yield.
% - The existing prose lists no metric for the nucleotide datasets. They use MCC.
% - Already in prose: the domain-matched metrics and the no-random-splits rationale. Both sit in Datasets and splitting.
% ---- END NOTES 5.3 ----


\subsection{Datasets and splitting}
% OUTLINE 5.3.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section does:
% - Lists the datasets used in the hybrid experiments, grouped by domain: what is measured, number of samples, how folds are made, metric, source.
% - Why it matters: every hybrid comparison in this chapter is scored on held-out folds. How the folds are cut decides what "better" means.
% - Why it matters: a group split asks "does the model work on chemistry or sequences it has not seen?". A random split asks "can it recall near-copies?".

Columns: name and source; what is measured and its unit; task type (class balance if classification); number of samples after curation; metric and direction; what the folds are grouped by and number of groups; base representations. A line above the table saying one row is one dataset.

% Fixes needed in the existing prose (it disagrees with the code):

% - The TODO in prose says reactions are grouped by template. The code groups them by additive.
% - The TODO says cancer data is grouped by patient. The code groups TCGA samples by tissue source site.

% General split rule:
% - 5 outer folds per dataset.
% - Every sample $i$ carries a group label $g_i$.
% - A group is never split: $\{g_i : i \in \mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}\} \cap \{g_i : i \in \mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{test}}\} = \emptyset$ for every fold $f$.
% - Regression: group k-fold.
% - Classification: stratified group k-fold. Each fold's class balance stays close to the full dataset's.
% - There is never a random-split fallback. With fewer than 5 groups, the search lowers the number of folds to the number of groups. With one group, the run stops with an error.
% - Why: random splits put near-analogues on both sides of the split, and this inflates scores \citep{Yang2019, Wallach2018}.
% - Time splits are a stricter alternative that mimics prospective use \citep{Sheridan2013}. None of these datasets has usable dates, so none is used.
% - The grouping is chosen per domain to match what "unseen" means in that field (see below).
% - Results are per-fold scores and their spread. No significance tests (see Models and metrics).
%
% Small molecules (ADME and toxicity; regression; RMSE; grouped by Bemis–Murcko scaffold):
% - Scaffold = the ring systems plus the linkers between them, with side chains removed \citep{Bemis1996}.
% - All molecules with the same scaffold go to the same fold.
% - hERG inhibition (Ki): 1,412 molecules; label pKi. Source: ChEMBL target CHEMBL240 \citep{Zdrazil2023}.
%   - Kept: Ki records with exact "=" relations only.
%   - Repeat measurements of one molecule are collapsed to their median.
%   - Molecules whose repeats differ by more than 1.0 log unit are dropped.
%   - Why it matters: hERG block causes cardiac-safety failures in drug development. TODO(lit): search for literature on hERG channel blockade, QT prolongation and drug attrition or withdrawal.
%   - Public Ki data has an experimental error of its own \citep{Kramer2012}. This error sets a floor on how low RMSE can go.
% - Caco-2 efflux: 2,158 molecules; label log10 efflux ratio. Source: OpenADMET ExpansionRx data \citep{openadmet}.
% - LogD: 5,028 molecules; label LogD. Same source \citep{openadmet}.
%   - Both OpenADMET sets: duplicates by standardised SMILES are collapsed to the median.
%   - Both OpenADMET sets: labels are clipped at their 1st and 99th percentiles.
% - Rat liver microsome clearance: 3,053 molecules; label log CLint. Biogen ADME set \citep{fang2023}, obtained through Polaris \citep{polaris}.
% - MDR1-MDCK efflux: 2,641 molecules; label log efflux ratio. Same source \citep{fang2023}.
% - Molecules per scaffold differ between datasets. hERG has about 1.8 molecules per scaffold (769 scaffolds). The Biogen sets are close to one molecule per scaffold (2,625 and 2,318 scaffolds).
% - So on the Biogen sets, a scaffold split is close to holding out single molecules.
% - CHECK: Chapter 3 gives LogD 5,039, Caco-2 2,161 and hERG 1,415 molecules. This chapter's counts are 5,028, 2,158 and 1,412. The cause is probably a different deduplication (unverified). Appendix A should say which curation each chapter used.
%
% Quantum chemistry (regression; RMSE; scaffold groups):
% - QM9: HOMO–LUMO gap of small organic molecules, computed by DFT \citep{Ramakrishnan2014}.
% - The full set has about 134k molecules. 10,000 are drawn once with a fixed seed, to limit compute.
% - Folds are grouped by Murcko scaffold.
% - CHECK: 9,978 is quoted elsewhere as the count after curation (unverified).
% - The noise chapter uses ten replicate draws of QM9. This chapter uses one draw with 5 scaffold folds.
% - Why it matters: the label is computed, so it has no experimental noise. Any gap between hybrid and single representations reflects the representation, not label error.
%
% Reactions (regression; yield in %; RMSE; grouped by additive):
% - Buchwald–Hartwig C–N coupling, Doyle high-throughput set: 3,955 reactions \citep{Ahneman2018}. The loader documents 22 additive groups.
% - Second Buchwald–Hartwig set, taken from the Doyle lab's plate data table: 4,599 rows before curation. Rows with a missing aryl halide or yield are dropped.
% - The loader documents 23 additives for this set. The draft count of 24 groups may include a no-additive group (unverified).
% - CHECK: the second set comes from the public code repository of \citep{Ahneman2018}. The two sets may be largely the same reactions under two curations (unverified). If they are, they are not independent datasets.
% - Holding out whole additives follows the out-of-sample tests in the original paper \citep{Ahneman2018}.
% - With about 22–23 additive groups over 5 folds, each test fold holds 4–5 additives the model never saw in training.
% - The inputs are reactions, not molecules. Representations are computed on the reactants, the products, or their difference (see Representations by domain).
%
% Antibodies:
% - Koenig expression (deep mutational scan): variants of the anti-VEGF antibody G6; label expression enrichment; Spearman \citep{Koenig2017}. Taken from the FLAb benchmark \citep{Chungyoun2024}.
%   - Grouped by mutation position mod 5, following ProteinGym \citep{Notin2023}.
%   - All mutations at one residue position stay in one fold. The model cannot learn a per-position effect and look it up at test time.
%   - Only 5 groups exist, so fold $f$ is the residue class $\{p : p \bmod 5 = f\}$.
% - Nanobody thermostability: 672 nanobodies; label melting temperature (°C); Spearman. From NbThermo \citep{ValdesTresanco2023}, via FLAb \citep{Chungyoun2024}.
%   - Grouped by 95% sequence-identity clusters.
%   - Identity: $\mathrm{id}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{\mathrm{lev}(a,b)}{\max(|a|,|b|)}$, where $\mathrm{lev}$ is the Levenshtein edit distance.
%   - Clustering: single linkage at $\mathrm{id} \geq 0.95$. Heavy and light chain are concatenated where both exist.
%   - Why: grouping by exact sequence left point-mutant pairs on both sides of a split. 95% clustering keeps them together.
% - Humanness: 564 therapeutic antibodies; binary, human vs non-human; AUROC. From IMGT/mAb-DB via BioPhi \citep{Prihoda2022}. Grouped by 95% identity clusters.
%   - CHECK: the loader's own description says 549 antibodies; the dataset table says 564.
% - Trastuzumab–HER2 binding: 5,000 CDRH3 variants, drawn from about 524,000 \citep{Chinery2024}. Binary, binder vs non-binder; AUROC.
%   - The 5,000 are drawn stratified by label and by edit distance.
%   - Grouped by edit-distance shell: the number of mutations away from the trastuzumab wild-type CDRH3.
%   - A held-out fold therefore holds variants at mutation distances not seen in training.
% - Why it matters: the antibody sets test whether the method works on sequence inputs, not only on molecular graphs. Links to: Beyond small molecules.

%   - H3K4me3 histone-mark prediction: 18,244 sequences (dataset table count, unverified).
%   - TATA promoter detection: 5,274 sequences (dataset table count, unverified).
% - The benchmark's train and test files are merged, then regrouped by chromosome.
% - A whole chromosome is held out together.

% - TODO(lit): search for literature on homology leakage between train and test sets in genomic benchmarks.

% Cancer genomics (TCGA; classification; grouped by tissue source site; stratified group k-fold):
% - Lung adenocarcinoma vs lung squamous cell carcinoma: binary; AUROC. Data from TCGA \citep{TCGA2014LUAD, TCGA2012LUSC}, processed through the Toil pipeline \citep{Vivian2017}, downloaded from UCSC Xena \citep{Goldman2020}.
%   - CHECK: the dataset table says 1,016 samples; the draft said 1,017 loaded (unverified).
% - Breast cancer PAM50 subtype: 4 classes (Luminal A, Luminal B, Basal, HER2-enriched); weighted F1.
%   - Subtypes come from the PAM50 classifier \citep{Parker2009}. Data \citep{TCGA2012BRCA}, same processing \citep{Vivian2017, Goldman2020}.
%   - CHECK: the dataset table says 900 samples; code comments give 821 for the current download.
% - Tissue source site = the institution that supplied the sample, read from the TCGA barcode.
% - Expression is not batch-corrected. The site codes are used only as fold groups.
% - Why: grouping by site stops a model from scoring well by learning site-specific batch effects.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on tissue-source-site batch effects in TCGA gene-expression data.
%
% Choices to state explicitly:
% - Group definitions differ by domain on purpose. The one shared rule is that no group spans train and test.
% - Some groupings leave few groups: 5 for Koenig, about 22–23 for reactions, one per chromosome for nucleotides.
% - Fewer groups make the per-fold scores vary more. Read spread per dataset, not across datasets.
% - Links to: the similarity chapter uses the same folds. Predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours is scored on the same held-out groups.
% - Links to: Limitations. Scaffold splits can still leave close analogues across the split, because two scaffolds can differ by one atom. TODO(lit): search for literature comparing scaffold splits with similarity-cluster and time splits on how much they overestimate prospective performance.

\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{5.3.1: Table 5.2, one row per dataset, grouped by domain.}

% [KB 167] domain-matched primary metrics.
Different biological domains have established different conventions for what constitutes a good prediction, and applying a single metric everywhere would misrepresent performance in at least some of them. To this end, we assign each task a primary metric matched to its domain: RMSE for molecular property prediction, Spearman rank correlation for antibody regression and developability, AUROC for binary classification (including antibody classification and tumor subtype), weighted F1 for multiclass classification, concordance index for drug-target affinity, and $R^2$ for reaction yield.

% [KB 181] no random splits. Aligns with the group's standing requirement.
No random splits are used. Random splitting inflates performance estimates for molecular data by placing structurally similar compounds on both sides of the train/test boundary \citep{Yang2019, Wallach2018}, and position-level leakage in deep mutational scanning data allows models to memorise site-specific effects rather than learning generalisable sequence-fitness relationships \citep{Notin2023, Vieira2026}. Splits therefore match domain conventions and use 5-fold GroupKFold throughout.

% TODO: [KB 183-191] gives the per-domain splitting protocols in full (Murcko scaffold groups;
% TODO: antibody inter-family by VH identity; DMS by mutation position mod 5 per ProteinGym;
% TODO: SKEMPI by PDB complex; Ginkgo pre-computed hierarchical clusters; reaction by template;
% TODO: cancer genomics by patient with subtype stratification). Carry them in as an itemized list,
% TODO: or defer to Appendix A. They are verbatim in KIRBy/PAPER_DRAFT.md.

% [KB 191] the nested, group-aware internal split.
The hybrid construction step uses a nested internal train/validation split within each training fold to score representations for feature allocation. This internal split is group-aware, using GroupShuffleSplit with the same grouping variable as the outer cross-validation, to prevent the allocation evaluator from seeing related samples on both sides of its split.


\subsection{Representations by domain}

% What this section is for:
% - Purpose: say which base representations enter the hybrid in each domain, what each family encodes, and why a pool mixes families.
% - Why it matters: the hybrid can only pick features that some base representation already carries. A pool of look-alike representations gives it nothing to choose between.

% - Already in prose: any vector input can be a base representation.

% - Step 1: score every candidate representation on its own with a gradient-boosted tree model (LightGBM), under the dataset's real grouped folds. Drop the dataset if no representation clearly beats a trivial predictor.
% - Step 2: compare candidates by their out-of-fold predictions, not by their raw features. Spearman correlation between two representations' prediction vectors: $\rho_{kl} = \mathrm{corr}_S(\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}, \hat{\mathbf{y}}^{(l)})$.
% - Two representations with $\rho_{kl} \geq 0.95$ are treated as carrying the same signal, so only one is kept.
% - Step 3: keep at most 10 that span distinct signal. The author made the final choice per dataset.
% - Some datasets have fewer than 10 because fewer distinct candidates passed: three nucleotide tasks have 8 or 9, and one antibody binding-energy-change set has 8.

The Suzuki reaction set uses the ten that appear most often across the two pruned Buchwald-Hartwig datasets. State this openly.

% Small molecules (input: a SMILES string per molecule):
% - Hashed substructure fingerprints: ECFP4 \citep{rogers2010}, atom pairs \citep{Carhart1985}, topological torsions \citep{Nilakantan1987}, Avalon \citep{Gedeck2006}. All 2048 bits.
%   - ECFP4: each atom's neighbourhood up to radius 2 bonds is hashed to an integer, then folded into 2048 bits: bit $= h(\text{environment}) \bmod 2048$. Folding is where the hash collisions of 4.2.2 come from.
%   - Atom pairs: every pair of atoms, described by their two atom types and the number of bonds between them.
%   - Topological torsions: every linear path of four bonded atoms.
%   - Avalon: a hashed mix of paths, rings and atom environments.
%   - What they encode: presence of local substructures. No 3D information and no global properties.
% - Predefined keys: MACCS, 166 fixed substructure questions (167 bits in RDKit) \citep{Durant2002}. Each bit has a fixed chemical meaning, so no collisions. Augmentation on most molecular datasets; a base representation on one.
% - Graph-kernel histogram: Weisfeiler-Lehman relabelling \citep{Shervashidze2011}, 5 rounds.
%   - Relabelling rule: $\ell^{(t+1)}(v) = h\big(\ell^{(t)}(v), \{\!\{\ell^{(t)}(u) : u \in N(v)\}\!\}\big)$. The feature vector counts how often each label appears, over all rounds.
%   - Close in spirit to ECFP, but counts are kept and nothing is folded. Labels absent from more than 95% of molecules are dropped.
% - Computed descriptors: 200 RDKit 2D descriptors \citep{rdkit} (connectivity indices, surface-area contributions, fragment counts, logP, weight), and Mordred, about 1,600 2D descriptors \citep{Moriwaki2018}.
%   - What they encode: whole-molecule physical and topological properties that a chemist can name.
% - Pretrained graph encoders: GROVER, a graph transformer pretrained on about 10 million unlabelled molecules \citep{Rong2020GROVER}; GraphMVP, pretrained to align 2D graphs with 3D conformers, only the 2D encoder used at inference \citep{Liu2022GraphMVP}; MHG-GNN \citep{kishimoto2023}; MolCLR, contrastive graph pretraining (augmentation on one dataset only) \citep{Wang2022MolCLR}.

We combine a variety of component, or base representations to create these hybrids. The framework is generalized such that it can accept any vector-based input as a base representation. We list all of these representations alongside their associated data sets and prediction tasks below:
