For every data set in this study, we pre-selected ten base representations that spanned across different domains of information and showed some positive results when used on their own for the given prediction task. 


\subsection{Models and metrics}
% OUTLINE 5.3.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope: the downstream models that score every hybrid and every single representation, and the metric each dataset is scored in. The internal evaluators belong to the search-space section and get one link here.
% The existing prose is out of date. Fix these points when rewriting:
% - The prose says seven standard models, including GP, kNN and MLP. The default set is now five: random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, SVM and DNN.
% - The MLP was cut and the DNN replaces it everywhere. Ridge, CatBoost, the 1D CNN and the MC-Dropout BNN are not part of the experiments. Drop them from the list.
% - The prose gives the GP cap as 6,000 samples. The GP is not in the default set, and its cap is 1,000 training samples.
% - The prose says there is no per-dataset tuning. That is true by default.
%   - An optional per-fold tuning exists: random search, 20 draws, 3 inner folds, group-aware, on the outer training fold only.
%   - The DNN is never tuned this way. It relies on its own early stopping.
%   - If any experiment in Ch 5 uses tuning, say so in that experiment's methods.
% Downstream models (default set):
% - There are five downstream models. Together they cover bagged trees, boosted trees, a kernel method and a neural network.
%   - Random forest \citep{Breiman2001}: 100 trees, no depth limit, $\sqrt{d}$ features tried at each split, where $d$ is the number of features in the input.
%   - XGBoost \citep{Chen2016}: 100 boosted trees. Each tree sees a random half of the features (column subsample 0.5). Library defaults otherwise.
%   - LightGBM \citep{ke2017lightgbm}: 100 boosted trees. Library defaults otherwise.
%   - SVM \citep{Vapnik1995}: RBF kernel with library defaults, $k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}') = \exp(-\gamma\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}'\|^2)$.
%     - The classifier outputs probabilities, so AUROC can be computed.
%   - DNN: fully connected, three hidden layers of 256, 128 and 64 units, ReLU activations.
%     - Dropout 0.2 \citep{Srivastava2014}.
%     - Adam optimiser, learning rate $10^{-3}$ \citep{Kingma2015}, batch size 256.
%     - Trains for at most 200 epochs.
%     - Early stopping: it stops after 20 epochs with no improvement on a holdout of 20% of the training fold.
%     - The learning rate halves after 5 epochs with no improvement.
%     - The early-stopping holdout is group-disjoint, using the same grouping as the outer split, so no scaffold group sits on both sides.
%     - Regression targets are z-scored inside the fit: $\tilde{y} = (y - \bar{y}_{\text{train}})/s_{\text{train}}$.
% - Implementation: scikit-learn \citep{scikit-learn} for the random forest and the SVM. XGBoost and LightGBM come from their own libraries. The DNN is written in PyTorch.
%   - TODO(lit): add a citation for PyTorch. refs.bib has none; the standard reference is Paszke et al. 2019, NeurIPS.
% - Hyperparameters are the same on every dataset.
%   - So any difference between two inputs comes from the input, not from one model being tuned more than another.
%   - Why it matters: the hybrid changes the input, not the model. Fixed models keep that comparison clean.
% - Features are standardised in each fold: $x'_{ij} = (x_{ij} - \mu_j)/\sigma_j$.
%   - $\mu_j$ and $\sigma_j$ are fitted on the training fold only.
%   - Binary fingerprint features are left as 0/1.
% - Size caps: the SVM is not fitted above 10,000 training samples.
%   - The reason: the SVM keeps a cache of kernel values, and above this size the cache no longer fits in memory. The solver then recomputes kernel values, and its cost grows faster than quadratically in $n$.
%   - Under the augmentation that adds extra features on top of the budget $B$, the SVM cap is 5,000 training samples.
% - Why five models: the best set of features depends on the model that uses it.
%   - Tree ensembles can ignore irrelevant features, because a split only uses one feature at a time.
%   - SVMs and kNN cannot ignore them, because every feature enters the distance.
%   - Why it matters: a hybrid that helped only one model family would be a property of that model, not a better input.
%   - Links to: "Why each axis is swept" (the downstream model is one of the swept axes) and Ch 7 "No single method fits every dataset".
% - Tree-based models remain state of the art on medium-sized tabular data of about 10,000 samples \citep{Grinsztajn2022}.
%   - A hybrid is a tabular feature matrix, so tree ensembles are the baseline to beat.
% - Other models can be run by name but are not in the default set:
%   - kNN with $k=5$.
%   - A Gaussian process with an RBF plus white-noise kernel, capped at 1,000 training samples.
%   - NGBoost.
%   - A minimally regularised linear model. It is used only in the budget-overfitting experiment. It is the one model whose number of fitted weights equals the budget $B$.
% - The noise chapter uses two robust-loss models: LightGBM with Huber loss, and a random forest that splits on absolute error. Describe them in the Ch 6 methods, not here.
% - Links to: "The search space" for the internal evaluators. Those are the models the search uses to score a candidate hybrid. They are separate from the downstream models reported here.
% Metrics, one per dataset:
% - Each dataset is scored in the metric its field normally uses, so the numbers can be compared with published work on the same dataset.
%   - Small-molecule regression (ADME, hERG $K_i$, logD, QM9) and reaction yields use RMSE, lower is better: $\mathrm{RMSE} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_i (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}$.
%   - Antibody regression (developability, expression, deep mutational scans) uses Spearman $\rho$, higher is better: $\rho = 1 - \frac{6\sum_i d_i^2}{n(n^2-1)}$.
%     - $d_i$ is the difference between the true rank and the predicted rank of sample $i$. This form of the formula assumes no ties.
%     - Why Spearman: these assays are used to rank candidates. Their scales differ between labs, so getting the order right matters more than getting the units right.
%   - Exception: the antibody $\Delta\Delta G$ benchmark sets use Pearson $r$, to match the numbers reported in the benchmark paper.
%     - TODO(lit): cite the Graphinity paper (Hummer et al., antibody $\Delta\Delta G$ prediction from structure) and confirm that it reports Pearson $r$.
%   - Drug-target affinity (Davis, KIBA, BindingDB $K_d$, Metz) uses the concordance index.
%     - The concordance index was introduced as the evaluation metric for affinity prediction by \citep{Pahikkala2015}.
%     - $\mathrm{CI} = \frac{1}{Z}\sum_{y_i > y_j} h(\hat{y}_i - \hat{y}_j)$.
%     - $h(u) = 1$ if $u > 0$, $0.5$ if $u = 0$, and $0$ otherwise.
%     - $Z$ is the number of pairs whose true values differ.
%   - Binary classification (hERG blocker, TCGA LUAD vs LUSC, binarised antibody binding, humanness) uses AUROC.
%     - AUROC is the probability that a random positive scores higher than a random negative.
%   - Multi-class classification (TCGA BRCA PAM50 subtypes, trastuzumab-HER2 multi-class) uses support-weighted F1: $F_1^{w} = \sum_c \frac{n_c}{n} \cdot \frac{2P_cR_c}{P_c+R_c}$.
%     - $n_c$ is the number of samples in class $c$. $P_c$ and $R_c$ are the precision and recall for class $c$.
%   - Nucleotide classification uses the Matthews correlation coefficient: $\mathrm{MCC} = \frac{TP\cdot TN - FP\cdot FN}{\sqrt{(TP+FP)(TP+FN)(TN+FP)(TN+FN)}}$.
%     - MCC is high only when all four cells of the confusion matrix are good. That matters on imbalanced classes \citep{Chicco2020}.
%   - The ChEMBL per-target QSAR sets in the applied chapter use MAE: $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i |y_i - \hat{y}_i|$.
% - Two antibody binding sets have censored labels: most values sit at the assay's ceiling or floor.
%   - They are scored as binder vs non-binder, with AUROC.
%   - A rank correlation says little when most labels share one clamped value.
%   - Links to: Appendix "Datasets and curation" and Ch 6, where censoring is one of the noise types.
% - Table: one row per domain, giving the metric, its direction (higher or lower is better) and why the field uses it. This could be merged with the dataset table in "Datasets and splitting".
% - Metrics are never averaged across datasets or domains. Each result is read in its own dataset's metric.
% Reporting, error bars, no significance tests:
% - Every score is computed on an outer test fold that the search never saw.
%   - There are 3 to 5 outer folds, split by scaffold or group (see "Datasets and splitting").
% - For each configuration, report the per-fold scores, their mean, and their spread (the standard deviation across the $F$ fold scores).
% - The comparison with single representations is paired by fold.
%   - In each fold $f$, the best single representation is chosen using the training data only, then scored on the same test fold as the hybrid.
%   - Check this against the full-run protocol before writing it up; it has not been re-verified in the code.
%   - $\Delta_f = s^{\text{hyb}}_f - s^{\text{single}}_f$.
%   - For metrics where lower is better, flip the sign, so that $\Delta_f > 0$ always means the hybrid is better.
%   - Report $\bar{\Delta} = \frac{1}{F}\sum_f \Delta_f$ together with all $F$ values of $\Delta_f$.
%   - Why paired: differences in difficulty from fold to fold are often larger than the gap between methods. Pairing removes the part the two methods share.
% - No significance tests and no confidence intervals, within or across datasets.
%   - With 3 to 5 folds, a p-value or an interval would rest on too few values to mean much.
%   - Showing every fold lets the reader see the spread directly.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on why scores from cross-validation folds are not independent, and why standard tests on them understate the variance (for example the Nadeau and Bengio variance correction, and Bengio and Grandvalet on there being no unbiased estimator of the variance of k-fold CV). Use it for one sentence justifying the choice.
% - A gap is only interpreted if it is larger than the spread across folds. State this rule once, here, and apply it in every results section.
% - DIAGRAM: one example panel per domain.
%   - x-axis: fold, 1 to 5.
%   - y-axis: score in that dataset's metric.
%   - One line for the hybrid and one for the best single representation, with the two points in each fold joined.
%   - It shows why the paired difference is reported rather than two means.
% Compute:
% - TODO(results): hours per dataset for the full search, and the hardware used (CPU cores, memory, and a GPU where the DNN or the embeddings used one); numbers come from the full runs.
% - Links to: "Fast tier and adaptive pruning", for how compute is reduced without changing the default models.
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: five models, with one field-standard metric per dataset, let the thesis ask whether a better input helps every learner, not just one.
% - Links to: the Ch 5 Discussion (does the hybrid overfit the search?) and Ch 7 "Simple is often better". Fixed models with library defaults are a deliberately plain setting.
% ---- END NOTES 5.3.3 ----

% [KB 175] the downstream model pool.
For a fair comparison, the downstream model pool must cover a range of inductive biases, from methods that degrade in high dimensions (kNN, SVM, GP) to tree ensembles that tolerate irrelevant features. Thirteen models are available: tree ensembles (random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost, NGBoost), kernel methods (SVM, Gaussian process), nearest-neighbor (kNN), linear models (Ridge regression for regression, logistic regression for classification, both with cross-validated regularization), and neural networks (single-layer MLP, three-layer DNN, MC-Dropout BNN for uncertainty quantification, and a multi-scale 1D CNN). Standard experiments use seven of these: random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, kNN, SVM, Gaussian process, and MLP. We use the full set for model-matching and noise experiments, and skip models with cubic or higher scaling for large datasets: Gaussian processes above 6,000 samples, SVMs above 10,000. No per-dataset tuning is performed: all models use fixed hyperparameters throughout, so performance differences reflect the input, not the model configuration.

% [RESTRUCTURE 2026-09-28] The statistical-analysis paragraph (Nadeau-Bengio, Wilcoxon,
% Friedman/Nemenyi) was deleted: no significance tests, within or across datasets.


\subsection{Complexity experiments}
% OUTLINE 5.3.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Purpose of the section:
% - These experiments ask how the hybrid behaves as a learning method, not only whether it wins on a dataset.
% - Five main questions: does the search overfit its own selection; how does the gain change with dataset size; is fusing features better than fusing model predictions; is the hybrid better than standard feature reduction at the same number of features; does the model used inside the search need to match the final model.
% - A sixth question checks whether the selected features stay the same from fold to fold.
% - Why it matters: the hybrid chooses among many configurations (allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, budget). A reader will ask whether the reported gain is real or the reward for searching hard. These experiments are the answer to that.
% - Links to: Methods (the hybrid search: budget, allocation, internal evaluator); Discussion (overfitting on noise against robustness from a variety of features); Limitations (compute cost, re-running the search per model).
% - All experiments use the scaffold-group folds from Datasets and splitting. Every score is a held-out score per fold. Report per-fold scores and their spread. No significance tests.
%
% Shared protocol: pay the same selection cost on both sides:
% - Problem: the hybrid's score is the best of many configurations, while each single representation is one score. The best of many cross-validated scores is biased upwards \citep{Varma2006}.
% - Remedy used in several experiments: leave-one-fold-out reselection.
%   - For held-out fold $f$, pick the hybrid configuration with the best mean score on the other folds, $c^*_{-f} = \arg\max_c \frac{1}{F-1}\sum_{g\neq f} s_{c,g}$.
%   - Pick the best single representation in the same way, $k^*_{-f}$.
%   - Report $\Delta_f = s_{c^*_{-f},f} - s_{k^*_{-f},f}$, signed so that $\Delta_f>0$ means the hybrid is better (flip the sign for RMSE).
%   - It is arithmetic on stored per-fold scores. No extra model fits.
% - Both sides pay the same cost for being a maximum.
% - Selection inside folds: importance scores and the feature selection are always recomputed on the training rows of each fold only.
% - Where a configuration was chosen by a search over all rows and only the feature selection is redone per fold, the score is not fully held out. Say so wherever it applies (flagged below).
% - Already in prose (Discussion): Ambroise and McLachlan's result on selection outside the folds \citep{Ambroise2002}.
%
% 1. Does the search overfit? (budget against held-out score):
% - Nested cross-validation. The outer folds are never seen by the search. The whole search (allocation, importance method, internal evaluator, budget) runs inside each outer training set.
% - For every budget $B$, the winning configuration at that budget is rebuilt on the outer training rows and scored on the outer held-out fold.
% - Selection bias at each budget: $\text{bias}(B) = \bar s_{\text{inner}}(B) - \bar s_{\text{held-out}}(B)$, signed so a positive value means the inner search promised more than the held-out fold delivered (both metric directions).
% - Standard budgets: $B \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2000\}$ features, plus 4,000 and 8,000 features.
% - Where 8,000 exceeds the width of the whole pool, the hybrid becomes "use every feature". That is the no-selection endpoint, kept on purpose.
% - Extra budgets placed at the dataset's own training-set size: $B = r\cdot n_{\text{train}}$, $r \in \{0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 1.5, 2\}$, with $n_{\text{train}}$ the smallest outer-fold training set.
% - Three regions: under-parameterised $B < 0.75\,n_{\text{train}}$; around the threshold $0.75\,n_{\text{train}} \le B \le 1.5\,n_{\text{train}}$; over-parameterised $B > 1.5\,n_{\text{train}}$.
% - Why the extra budgets: test error can peak where the number of parameters equals the number of training points and then fall again ("double descent") \citep{Belkin2019}. A fixed budget grid can miss that peak entirely.
% - Caveat for the text: for most models the threshold does not sit at $B = n_{\text{train}}$.
%   - The DNN already has more weights than training molecules at the smallest budget.
%   - The SVM is fitted in the dual, at most one coefficient per training molecule, so it can never be over-parameterised.
%   - The random forest can grow one leaf per training row.
%   - So a flat curve from these models is not evidence against double descent.
% - A linear model with a negligible penalty (one weight per selected feature, nothing else) is added for this experiment only, because its threshold is exactly $B = n_{\text{train}}$. It is not one of the study's downstream models.
% - The two numbers to read per model: the budget each outer fold's search picked, and the budget with the best held-out score. If the search picks far above the best held-out budget, the search overfits.
% - Also compared: the hybrid at each budget against the best and the second-best single representation on the same held-out fold. A budget can beat the best and still lose to the second-best.
% - Links to: Discussion, "is this selection fitted to noise, or is a variety of features a robustness gain?".
% - DIAGRAM: nested CV scheme. Outer folds on top; inside each outer training set, the inner search; arrow from the chosen configuration to the untouched outer fold.
% - DIAGRAM: x = budget $B/n_{\text{train}}$ (log scale), y = held-out score; one line per model; second panel for $\text{bias}(B)$; vertical band at 0.75-1.5.
% - TODO(results): held-out score and selection bias against budget per model and dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% 2. Learning curves: gain against dataset size:
% - Subsample 10, 25, 50, 75 and 100% of the molecules. Draw whole scaffold groups until the target size is reached, so no scaffold is split between training and test.
% - Datasets above 5,000 molecules: absolute sizes of 250, 500, 1,000 and 2,000 molecules instead of percentages.
% - 3 random subsamples per size below 100%. At 100% every draw is the same, so one run.
% - A full hybrid search is run on each subsample. Budgets above the number of training rows are dropped.
% - Consequence: small subsamples search fewer budgets. Part of any rise in the gain with $n$ may come from searching more configurations, not from sample efficiency. State this.
% - Gain at each size: $\Delta(n)$ = hybrid minus best single representation, using the leave-one-fold-out reselection above.
% - Curve fit: $s(n) = s_\infty - a\,n^{-b}$, with $s_\infty$ the fitted score at unlimited data. For RMSE, fit on $-s$ so the curve rises, then turn $s_\infty$ back into RMSE.
% - Second curve: the configuration chosen on all rows, with only the feature selection redone on each subsample. It uses every label to pick the configuration, so it is not a held-out score. It asks "how well does a good configuration transfer to less data".
% - Why it matters: most drug-discovery datasets are small. The question is whether the hybrid is useful where data is scarce, or only once there is plenty of data.
% - Links to: Limitations (the hybrid struggles below about 200 molecules, already in prose there).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on power-law learning-curve fits in QSAR / molecular property prediction (held-out score against training-set size).
% - DIAGRAM: x = molecules (log scale), y = held-out score; hybrid and best single representation per model; shaded spread over the 3 subsamples.
% - TODO(results): $\Delta(n)$ per dataset and model; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% 3. Fusing features against fusing predictions (ensembles):
% - Ensemble: one model per representation, predictions pooled. The hybrid: one model on one fused feature vector.
% - Every arm is a full procedure run on the fold's training rows only. Choices are made on an inner 3-fold group split of the training rows. The held-out fold is touched once.
% - The hybrid arm runs its own search on each outer fold's training rows, so both sides choose their configuration from the same rows.
% - Two ensemble styles:
%   - the same model on every representation;
%   - each representation paired with its own best model, chosen on the inner split.
% - Members: the top 3 representations by inner score, or all of them.
% - Pooling rules for $M$ member predictions $\hat y^{(1)},\dots,\hat y^{(M)}$ (per molecule):
%   - mean $\frac1M\sum_m \hat y^{(m)}$, and median;
%   - 20% trimmed mean;
%   - Tukey-fence filtered mean, dropping members outside $[Q_1 - 1.5\,\text{IQR},\; Q_3 + 1.5\,\text{IQR}]$;
%   - MAD-filtered mean, dropping members with $|\hat y^{(m)} - \text{med}| > 3 \times 1.4826\,\text{MAD}$;
%   - weighted mean $\sum_m w_m \hat y^{(m)} / \sum_m w_m$ with $w_m$ the member's inner score;
%   - mean of the top 3 members by inner score;
%   - majority vote, for hard-label classification only.
% - For classification, pooling is on predicted class probabilities, not labels (except the vote).
% - Self-contained variants choose their own member set and pooling rule on the inner split. These are the fair comparison, because the hybrid also picks its own configuration from training data.
% - Controls:
%   - best single representation;
%   - all representations concatenated, then reduced by PCA to the hybrid's width;
%   - the best (model, representation) pair read off the held-out fold. That one is an upper bound, not a fair arm.
% - Cost is reported as several numbers, because no single cost number is reliable \citep{Dehghani2022}:
%   - number of representations to compute at prediction time;
%   - their summed width;
%   - number of models deployed;
%   - model input width;
%   - stored model size;
%   - model fits spent on making choices;
%   - fit and prediction time.
% - Point to make: the hybrid still has to compute every representation it takes a feature from. It saves only if it draws on fewer representations than the ensemble. PCA needs all of them plus a dense projection matrix.
% - A plain mean over all representations needs no search at all. It is the cheapest competitor.
% - Why it matters: an ensemble is the obvious simpler alternative. If it matches the hybrid at lower cost, that belongs in the "Simple is often better" chapter.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on late fusion (averaging predictions across molecular representations) against early fusion (feature concatenation) in QSAR.
% - TODO(results): held-out score and cost per arm; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% 4. The hybrid against standard feature reduction at the same width:
% - Same number of features $B$ for every method, taken from the pool of all base representations. Fitted on the fold's training rows. Regression datasets only.
% - The five methods, and what each ranks by:
%   - hybrid: the stored configuration at budget $B$, with the features re-selected per fold;
%   - PCA: the directions of largest variance, ignoring the labels;
%   - mRMR: greedy, adding at each step $j^* = \arg\max_{j\notin S}\big[I(x_j;y) - \frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S} I(x_j;x_i)\big]$ \citep{Peng2005}; its ranking stops at 200 features for run time;
%   - RFE: repeatedly drop the least important 10% of features using a 50-tree random forest of depth 5 \citep{Guyon2002};
%   - random: $B$ features drawn uniformly.
% - One downstream model for all five: a 100-tree random forest. The model is fixed so only the features differ.
% - Measured on the held-out rows:
%   - the held-out score in the dataset's metric;
%   - estimated mutual information with the label, $\hat I(\mathbf{X}_S;\mathbf{y}) \approx \sum_{c=1}^{C} \hat I(u_c;\mathbf{y})$, where $u_c$ are the first $C \le 50$ principal components of $\mathbf{X}_S$ and each term uses the Kraskov nearest-neighbour estimator with $k=5$ \citep{Kraskov2004};
%   - note: the sum over components is an approximation. It treats the components as independent in how they carry information about $y$.
%   - information per feature, $\hat I/B$;
%   - effective rank, $\text{erank}(\mathbf{X}) = \exp\!\big(-\sum_i p_i \log p_i\big)$ with $p_i = \sigma_i/\sum_j\sigma_j$ and $\sigma_i$ the singular values of the standardised matrix \citep{RoyVetterli2007}.
% - Caveat: the hybrid's configuration is chosen using all rows. Only its feature selection is redone per fold. So this arm is not fully held out.
% - Why it matters: the question is whether the gain comes from drawing on several representations, or only from reducing the number of features. If mRMR or RFE at the same width does as well, the multi-representation part adds little.
% - TODO(results): score and information per feature against $B$ for the five methods; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% 5. Does the internal evaluator need to match the final model?:
% - The search scores candidate hybrids with an internal model (the "evaluator"). It then hands the winner to a downstream model.
% - Nested cross-validation as in experiment 1. One search per outer fold per evaluator setting.
% - For each downstream model, the evaluator settings are:
%   - matched: the same model;
%   - near family: random forest scored by LightGBM, LightGBM by random forest, XGBoost by LightGBM, SVM by Gaussian process, DNN by a smaller DNN;
%   - far family: random forest, LightGBM and XGBoost scored by SVM; SVM by LightGBM; DNN by kNN;
%   - size mismatch: the DNN scored by a wider DNN;
%   - always one of the three fast tree models (random forest, LightGBM, XGBoost).
% - DNN widths: downstream hidden layers (256, 128, 64); smaller evaluator (64, 32); wider evaluator (512, 256, 128). Only the width differs.
% - Report the matched minus mismatched held-out score, paired per outer fold.
% - Why it matters: the answer sets whether one cheap evaluator is enough, or whether the search must be re-run for each model. That feeds Limitations.
% - Links to: tree ensembles and neural networks may prefer different numbers of features and different feature sets. Pair this with the per-model budget curves from experiment 1 and a comparison of chosen configurations across models.
% - TODO(results): matched against mismatched differences per model; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% 6. Stability of the selected features across folds:
% - Selection on each of the $M$ folds gives a binary vector $\mathbf{z}_m \in \{0,1\}^d$, where $d$ is the width of the whole pool.
% - Nogueira stability \citep{Nogueira2018}:
%   - $\hat\Phi = 1 - \dfrac{\frac1d\sum_{f=1}^d s_f^2}{\frac{\bar k}{d}\big(1-\frac{\bar k}{d}\big)}$;
%   - $s_f^2 = \frac{M}{M-1}\hat p_f(1-\hat p_f)$, where $\hat p_f$ is the fraction of folds that selected feature $f$;
%   - $\bar k$ is the mean number of features selected.
% - $\hat\Phi=1$ means the same features every fold. $\hat\Phi \approx 0$ is what random selection gives.
% - $d$ must be the pool width, not the union of selected features; otherwise identical selections give an undefined value.
% - Transfer across folds: for fold $j$, a configuration searched without fold $j$ and without one other fold $k$ is scored on fold $j$ and compared with fold $j$'s own configuration.
% - Caveat: the cross-fold search leaves out one more fold. So the two sides differ in how many rows the search saw, not only in which configuration is applied.
% - Why it matters: this is direct evidence for or against overfitting of the selection (Discussion). Unstable feature sets would also weaken any chemical reading of the chosen configurations.
% - TODO(results): $\hat\Phi$ per dataset and model; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Smaller experiments (one paragraph each, or move to the appendix):
% - Importance during training: the hybrid at 100 features, with random forest, LightGBM and XGBoost. Five conditions:
%   - unweighted;
%   - features rescaled, $\tilde x_j = x_j w_j$ with $w_j = \sqrt{s_j}\big/\frac1B\sum_i \sqrt{s_i}$, so the weights average 1; $s_j$ from LightGBM gain importance on the training rows;
%   - molecules weighted by agreement with their 10 nearest neighbours in the rescaled feature space, $r_i = \text{clip}\big(1 - |y_i - \bar y_{\mathcal N(i)}|/\text{range}(\mathbf y),\,0.1,\,1\big)$;
%   - both together;
%   - only the features strictly above the median importance kept.
%   - Caveat: the hybrid configuration here comes from a search over all rows; only the feature selection is redone per fold.
% - Adding a few features to one strong representation:
%   - Setup: one base representation at full width. Candidate extra features from other representations are ranked by correlation with the out-of-fold residuals of a small forest fitted on the base features, i.e. by what the base cannot already explain.
%   - The number of extras $k$ is chosen from $\{0, 5, 10, 20, 40\}$ on an internal split of the training rows. $k=0$ is allowed and wins ties.
%   - Two rules. In the capped rule extras replace base features, so the total width stays fixed. In the uncapped rule extras are added on top.
%   - Two decoys planted in the pool: a block of pure Gaussian noise as wide as the real candidate features combined, and near-copies of base features with noise at 5% of each feature's standard deviation.

\subsection{Applied settings: OPIG and federated}
% OUTLINE 5.3.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section covers:
% - Two applied settings sit outside the benchmark datasets used in the rest of the chapter: a set of collaborative prediction tasks, and a federated setting where molecules cannot be pooled.
% - Both are method sections. They say how each task was set up and scored, not what came out.
% - The purpose of both is the same: test whether the hybrid search is a general procedure for tabular supervised learning, not a small-molecule trick.
% - Requirement stated once for all of them: several numeric representations of the same objects, one supervised target, and a grouped split that keeps near-duplicates on one side.
% - Why it matters: every result earlier in the chapter comes from small-molecule property datasets, so a reader cannot tell whether the method or the domain is doing the work.
% - Links to: Representations by domain, which lists the protein, reaction and nucleotide representations these tasks draw on; Datasets and splitting, for the grouped-split rules reused here.
%
% Reusing a recipe across datasets:
% - Question: can a recipe found by searching on a large dataset be reused on a small one, instead of paying for a new search.
% - Source dataset is lipophilicity (about 5,000 molecules); target is hERG binding affinity (about 1,400 molecules).
% - The representation pool is restricted to the six representations present for both datasets at the same width, so the two searches range over the same feature space.
% - A recipe is the tuple (importance method, allocation rule, budget $B$, internal evaluator). It is a set of decisions, not a set of features.
% - The selected feature sets $S_1,\dots,S_K$ are the output of applying a recipe to a particular dataset.
% - Four arms are run inside every held-out fold of the target:
%   - best single representation, re-chosen from the fold's training rows;
%   - hybrid searched inside the fold's training rows, which is the ceiling;
%   - recipe transferred from the source, with $S_k$ re-solved on the target's training rows;
%   - the source's own $S_k$ applied unchanged, so no target label is used at selection time.
% - Five scaffold-group folds, root-mean-square error, several downstream models.
% - The two transfer arms separate two things that are usually confused: whether the search decisions generalise, and whether the chosen features generalise.
% - Why it matters: the search is the expensive part of the method, and a recipe that transfers turns it into a one-off cost paid on whichever dataset is largest.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on transferring a fitted feature-selection mask between QSAR datasets without refitting on the target.
% - TODO(results): whether a transferred recipe closes the gap between the best single representation and a hybrid searched in place.
%
% Describing a variant by what changed:
% - Single-wildtype mutational scans break whole-object encodings: every variant differs from the reference at one position, so an encoding of the whole sequence is nearly constant across the dataset.
% - Difference encoding of variant $i$ against wildtype: $\Delta\mathbf{x}_i = \mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{wt}}$, which is zero everywhere the variant did not change.
% - Two such representations are tested: a five-number summary of the change in physicochemical properties, and a position-resolved version of about 1,140 features.
% - The question is whether either earns a place in the pool alongside protein language-model embeddings and cheap sequence encodings, judged as a standalone representation.
% - Four antibody mutational scans with a single wildtype and mutation metadata, including one measuring expression \citep{Koenig2017}.
% - Nested cross-validation: the search runs on outer-training rows only; the hybrid and every single representation are scored on the untouched outer fold.
% - Why it matters: the whole method assumes the pool carries different information about the same object, and a difference encoding is the cleanest test of that assumption, because it carries information no whole-object encoding can express.
% - Links to: Flaws in standard base representations, which shows how often a representation cannot tell two objects apart.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on difference-based encodings of single point mutants for protein property prediction.
%
% Other applied tasks, one line each:
% - Activity-cliff prediction on three protein targets: dopamine D2 receptor, factor Xa, and the SARS-CoV-2 main protease, following a published comparison of representations and models \citep{Dablander2023}.
% - Reaction yield prediction, comparing representations built from the condensed graph of reaction \citep{HeidGreen2022} against representations built from reaction SMILES, on palladium-catalysed C--N cross-coupling yields \citep{Ahneman2018} and a plate-based high-throughput set.
% - Antibody developability, on the biophysical properties of a public competition set and using that competition's own cluster splits \citep{vanNiekerk2026}.
% - Antibody--antigen binding free-energy change, asking how close sequence representations get to a structure-based graph network \citep{Hummer2025}.
% - Humanness classification of therapeutic antibodies, extending a score-comparison study into a supervised setting with cross-validation \citep{Prihoda2022}.
% - Binder enrichment in a trastuzumab variant library, asking which embedding separates binders from non-binders rather than which generator designs more of them \citep{Chinery2024}.
% - Difference representations judged as similarity spaces, with distances graded against an independent substitution matrix rather than against a model score.
% - Two tasks in this group are reported in the similarity chapter instead, because they are about pairwise comparison, not about fitting a model.
%
% The federated setting: what it is:
% - Federated learning trains one model across data held by several parties without moving the data \citep{McMahan2017}.
% - It has been run at industrial scale across pharmaceutical companies on shared property-prediction targets \citep{Heyndrickx2024}.
% - The motivation in this field is not privacy in the abstract: compound structures and assay results are the asset, so pooling them is not on the table \citep{Hanser2023,Bassani2023}.
% - The collaborator's process is distillation-based rather than gradient-averaging: one teacher model per site, a shared public set of molecules, and one student trained on the teachers' combined labels \citep{Hanser2025}.
% - Distillation here means training a model on another model's outputs rather than on measurements \citep{Hinton2015}.
%
% How the teachers are combined:
% - Each teacher's vote on a public molecule is weighted by how close that molecule is to the teacher's own data, so a site speaks loudest about chemistry it has seen.
% - Raw distance for site $t$: mean Euclidean distance from the molecule to its eight nearest neighbours in a random 20\% sample of that site's fingerprints, written $d_t(\mathbf{x})$.
% - Rescaled to $[0,1]$ across sites: $\tilde d_t(\mathbf{x}) = (d_t(\mathbf{x}) - d_{\min})/(d_{\max}-d_{\min})$.
% - Weight: $w_t(\mathbf{x}) = \dfrac{(1-\tilde d_t(\mathbf{x}))^2}{\sum_u (1-\tilde d_u(\mathbf{x}))^2}$, so $\sum_t w_t(\mathbf{x}) = 1$.
% - Combined label: $\hat p(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_t w_t(\mathbf{x})\, p_t(\mathbf{x})$, where $p_t$ is site $t$'s predicted probability.
% - The student is trained on the most confident of these labels, taking the same number from each class.
% - A second weighting is also run, where every site gets one scalar weight from its own cross-validated score instead of a per-molecule distance.
% - Only predictions on shared public molecules cross a site boundary.
%
% Selecting features under federation:
% - The addition here is that feature selection, not only model fitting, happens at the sites.
% - Each site runs the hybrid search on its own molecules: features are chosen on 80\% of its rows and the resulting set is scored on the other 20\%, split by scaffold group so the score is not read off rows the features were chosen from.
% - Each site reports three things: feature indices, importance values, and one score $v_s$. No molecule and no feature value leaves.
% - Coordinator's split of the budget: $b_s = \max\!\left(1, \left\lfloor B \dfrac{v_s}{\sum_u v_u} \right\rfloor\right)$, with $\sum_s b_s \approx B$.
% - Alternative splitting rules are swept alongside it: equal shares $b_s = \lfloor B/k \rfloor$; squared score $v_s^2$; square-root score $\sqrt{v_s}$; and the site's rank rather than its score, which is immune to the scale of the scores.
% - The equal split is the null: if it is not beaten, the reported scores are buying nothing.
% - A feature another site has already taken is skipped, so the hybrid has $B$ distinct features rather than $B$ slots.
% - Why it matters: what crosses the network is a list of indices and importances, which is smaller and less invertible than a model's weights or a gradient.
% - Why it matters: the allocation question the rest of the chapter asks between representations is the same question asked between organisations, with the same machinery.
% - Links to: Feature allocation strategies, where the within-dataset version of the same split is defined.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on feature selection performed independently at each client in horizontal federated learning.
%
% The hERG comparison:
% - hERG channel blockade is a cardiotoxicity liability screened early in drug discovery, and its public data is assembled from many labs \citep{Czodrowski2013}.
% - Three real sources act as sites: one public activity database and two separately curated sets from the collaborator, one of which is a conservative call pooled over four independent published datasets.
% - This is source heterogeneity that exists in the world, not sites made by clustering one dataset, which is how the process is usually simulated.
% - Molecules held by more than one source are deduplicated on the standard molecular identifier, keeping the public copy.
% - Binary target: blocker if $\mathrm{p}K_i \ge 6$, where $\mathrm{p}K_i = -\log_{10} K_i$ with $K_i$ in molar, so the cut is $K_i \le 1\,\mu\mathrm{M}$.
% - Five scaffold-group folds over the pooled molecules.
% - The search is driven by area under the receiver-operating characteristic curve; the reported number is the Matthews correlation coefficient, which does not flatter an imbalanced split.
% - $\mathrm{MCC} = \dfrac{TP\cdot TN - FP\cdot FN}{\sqrt{(TP+FP)(TP+FN)(TN+FP)(TN+FN)}}$, in $[-1,1]$.
% - The comparison is built as a two-by-two: molecules pooled or kept at their sites, crossed with all features or a selected set.
%   - pooled molecules and all features is the unrealistic upper reference;
%   - pooled molecules and selected features isolates what the selection is worth;
%   - molecules at their sites and all features is federated training as usually done;
%   - molecules at their sites and selected features is the only arm where nothing but indices and predictions moves.
% - Reading the corners: selected minus all features, at fixed site structure, is what selection buys; pooled minus site-held, at fixed features, is what pooling buys.
% - Four further arms give context: the single best source picked on training rows, the two teacher-weighting schemes, a control that runs the same search on one fingerprint alone, and one teacher per representation.
% - The budget $B$ is swept, not fixed, so no comparison rests on one width.
% - Caution to state in the text: the all-features arm fits a 2,048-bit fingerprint while the selected arm fits features drawn from six representations, so those two arms differ in width and in source at once. The budget sweep is what makes the difference readable; a single width cannot separate the two.
% - The number of molecules held by two curators with opposite blocker calls is counted, and recounted at three cut-offs, so the reader can see how much of the disagreement is curation and how much is where the line was drawn.
% - Links to: the noise chapters, since two curators disagreeing on the same molecule is label noise with a known source rather than injected noise.
% - Why it matters: it is the only place in the thesis where the label disagreement is between real curators on real molecules.
%
% Stated limitations of the federated work:
% - One source's data is proprietary and cannot be redistributed, so the comparison is not reproducible from public data alone; the appendix says what is and is not available.
% - The comparison also depends on the collaborator's own implementation of the process, which is used unmodified so the numbers stay comparable to theirs.
% - The published process labels a large separate public set; the simulation here uses the training molecules in that role, and held-out molecules are excluded from it. This is a compromise and is stated as one.
% - Sites are unequal in size and in how their labels were derived, so a site's score reflects its curation as well as its chemistry.
%
% Figures:
% - DIAGRAM D5.4: two rows, same three sites in both. Top row: the standard process, sites to teacher models to a shared public set of molecules being labelled to one student model. Bottom row: the version with selection, sites to per-site feature search to a coordinator splitting the budget to per-site models on the shared feature list to distance-weighted predictions. Mark on each row exactly what crosses a site boundary (top: predictions on public molecules; bottom: feature indices, importances, one score, and predictions). Label $b_1,b_2,b_3$ with $\sum_s b_s = B$ on the bottom row.
% - DIAGRAM: the two-by-two of the hERG comparison. Horizontal axis molecules pooled or site-held, vertical axis all features or selected features, one cell per arm, with arrows marked as the selection difference and the pooling difference.
%
% Results placeholders:
% - TODO(results): whether keeping molecules at their sites and selecting features beats keeping molecules at their sites with all features; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): whether either teacher-weighting scheme beats the best single source; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): the cross-curator disagreement counts at each cut-off; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): per-task outcome for each applied task listed above, including which representations the selected features came from; numbers come from the full runs.
% ---- END NOTES 5.3.5 ----

\section{Discussion}
% OUTLINE 5.9: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.9 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Section scope: two discussion threads. (1) Does the search overfit, or does drawing on diverse features make the model more robust? (2) Datasets where the hybrid does not beat the best single representation.
% - Already in prose: selection bias from selecting before cross-validation (Ambroise and McLachlan), selection as a form of model selection (Cawley and Talbot), search degrees of freedom (Tibshirani), the budget as a limit on capacity, frozen features against fine-tuning (Kumar et al.), and the Nogueira stability index.
% - Citation keys used in the existing prose are NOT in refs.bib: Ambroise2002, Vabalas2019, Cawley2010, Tibshirani2015, Ng2004, Kumar2022, Nogueira2018. Verified bibtex for all seven is returned with these notes.
% - CHECK the existing prose against the sources:
%   - Kumar et al.: fine-tuning is on average 7% worse than linear probing out of distribution. The "10%" figure is their combined method (linear probe first, then fine-tune) against plain fine-tuning, not linear probing against fine-tuning.
%   - Ng2004 does not argue that the feasible set grows polynomially in the budget. Ng shows that with $L_1$ regularisation the number of training samples needed grows only with the log of the number of irrelevant features; with $L_2$ it grows at least linearly. Rewrite or drop that sentence.
%   - The Ambroise and McLachlan "11 out of 40" figure was not re-checked here; check it against the paper.
%
% The case that the search overfits:
% - Features are ranked by importance computed on the same training labels the model is then fitted to. Noise in those labels can steer which features are picked.
% - The search picks the best of many configurations: budgets, allocation methods, importance methods, number of representations. The best of many noisy scores is biased upwards, even when each score on its own is unbiased \citep{Cawley2010}.
%   - Define the optimism of the search as $\Delta = \hat{m}_{\text{inner}} - m_{\text{outer}}$.
%   - $\hat{m}_{\text{inner}}$ is the score the search reported for its chosen configuration.
%   - $m_{\text{outer}}$ is the score of the same configuration rebuilt on the outer training set and tested on a held-out outer fold.
%   - Sign it so that $\Delta > 0$ means the search over-promised, for either metric direction (flip the sign for error metrics such as RMSE).
% - Tuning by cross-validation and then reporting that same cross-validation error gives an optimistic estimate; nested cross-validation removes most of this bias \citep{Varma2006}.
% - Prior work in this field: Sort \& Slice, which uses no labels to choose fingerprint substructures, outperformed two supervised substructure-selection schemes (filtering and mutual-information maximisation) \citep{sns}.
%   - TODO(lit): check whether the Sort \& Slice paper or Dablander's thesis attributes the weaker supervised selection to overfitting; cite only if it says so.
% - Small datasets are the most exposed. With few molecules, one fold's importance ranking rests on few labels.
% - The budget can exceed the number of training molecules, $B > n_{\text{train}}$. The search allows this.
% - Test error can peak where the number of model parameters is close to the number of training samples, then fall again as parameters grow (double descent) \citep{Belkin2019}.
%   - For most downstream models the parameter count is not $B$, so that peak need not sit at $B = n_{\text{train}}$.
%   - Example: a neural network's first layer alone has $B \times h_1$ weights ($h_1$ = width of the first hidden layer), so it is past that point at small budgets.
%
% The case that it does not, or that the cost is outweighed:
% - Every importance score is computed inside each fold, on training data only. This is the standard remedy for the bias Ambroise and McLachlan showed.
% - The held-out test is nested cross-validation.
%   - The search runs on the outer training set only.
%   - The configuration it picks is rebuilt and scored on an outer fold the search never saw.
%   - The single representations are scored on the same outer folds.
% - Picking the best single representation out of $K$ is also a selection step with its own optimism.
%   - The hybrid search compares far more configurations than $K$, so its selection cost is larger. Nested cross-validation charges each side for its own selection.
% - The search does not search over arbitrary feature subsets. Within a configuration, representation $k$ contributes its top $b_k$ features by $s^{(k)}_j$.
%   - The number of candidate hybrids is the size of the configuration grid (budgets $\times$ allocation methods $\times$ importance methods $\times$ representation counts). It is not $\prod_k \binom{d_k}{b_k}$.
%   - The downstream model still fits its own parameters on $\mathbf{H}$. Selection adds degrees of freedom on top of the model's; it does not replace them \citep{Tibshirani2015}.
% - Stability across folds can be measured directly. Let $M$ be the number of folds, $d$ the total number of candidate features, $\hat{p}_f$ the fraction of folds that select feature $f$, and $\bar{k}$ the mean number of features selected per fold.
%   - $\hat{\Phi} = 1 - \dfrac{\frac{1}{d}\sum_{f=1}^{d} s_f^2}{\frac{\bar{k}}{d}\left(1-\frac{\bar{k}}{d}\right)}$, with $s_f^2 = \frac{M}{M-1}\hat{p}_f(1-\hat{p}_f)$ \citep{Nogueira2018}.
%   - $\hat{\Phi}$ near 1: the same features are picked in every fold.
%   - $\hat{\Phi}$ near 0: the overlap between folds is what chance would give.
%   - Caveat: correlated features can swap between folds while carrying the same information. A low $\hat{\Phi}$ does not by itself show overfitting.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on feature-selection stability measures that account for correlated or redundant features.
% - Blueprint transfer is a second check. A blueprint chosen on one dataset or split and reused on another keeps its score only if the selection was not fitted to noise. Links to: the Blueprints method and results sections.
%
% Robustness from diverse features:
% - A hybrid draws its features from several representations. The model then does not depend on one representation's blind spots.
%   - Examples: hash collisions in folded fingerprints; molecules a representation cannot tell apart. Links to: Chapter 4, flaws in standard base representations.
% - A related idea from ensembles: decision forests built on different random subsets of features generalise better than a single tree \citep{Ho1998,Breiman2001}.
%   - Difference here: the feature subsets come from different sources, chosen by importance, not from random draws.
% - If the representations make errors that are not correlated, combining them should average part of those errors out.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on error correlation between molecular representations and how it relates to gains from combining them.
% - The tradeoff: part of any chosen set of $B$ features may be picked on noise. The question is whether the gain from strong, varied features outweighs that.
%   - The held-out outer folds answer this, not the inner search.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Under label noise the importance ranking is itself computed on noisy $\mathbf{y}$, so that chapter tests the overfitting worry directly.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, "Simple is often better". The winning hybrids often use fewer features than the largest budget.
% - TODO(results): for each dataset and budget, $\hat{m}_{\text{inner}}$ against $m_{\text{outer}}$, and the sign and size of $\Delta$; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): whether the held-out score peaks and then falls once $B$ exceeds $n_{\text{train}}$ (extra budgets are placed at set ratios of each dataset's $n_{\text{train}}$ for this); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): the Nogueira stability index across folds for each dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: x-axis budget $B$ on a log scale, vertical line at $n_{\text{train}}$. y-axis metric in the dataset's own units. Two lines: score the inner search reported, and held-out outer-fold score. One panel per dataset. Individual fold scores as points; no error bars.
%
% Where the hybrid does not help:
% - On some datasets the best single representation scores as well as the hybrid, or better. Expected when one representation is clearly the strongest in the pool.
% - If one $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$ already holds most of the signal, adding features from the others adds little.
%   - A search that still reported a large gain there would be the warning sign of overfitting.
% - A tie on these datasets is consistent with the search not inflating scores. Scoring on held-out outer folds is what prevents inflation.
% - Detail for the reader: the smallest number of representations the search combines is 2 (other settings: 3, 5, and all of them).
%   - The single representations are scored beside the hybrid as baselines.
%   - "Hybrid does not help" therefore means the best single representation equals or beats every hybrid on the held-out folds.
% - Practical signal: score each $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$ alone before searching. If one stands well clear of the others, expect little gain from the hybrid.
%   - Links to: Limitations, where the search has a high upfront cost for an unknown gain.
% - No free lunch theorems: averaged over all problems, no search algorithm beats any other \citep{Wolpert1997}. By analogy, no single representation or combination should be expected to win on every dataset.
%   - Links to: Chapter 7, "No single method fits every dataset".
% - TODO(results): the datasets where the hybrid does not beat the best single representation, and which representation dominates on each; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on when combining molecular fingerprints or descriptors gives no gain over the best single one.
%
% Why it matters:
% - Why it matters: gains from a feature-selection method are only credible if they hold on data the search never saw. This section is where the thesis shows that check.
% - Why it matters: "the hybrid does not help here" is useful to a practitioner. It says one representation is enough for this task and saves the cost of the search.
% ---- END NOTES 5.9 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Ch 5 Sec 5.7 (overfitting).
% [KB 135] selection-overfitting controls. Belongs with Sec 6.4 (stability).
Ambroise and McLachlan \citep{Ambroise2002} showed the extreme case of feature selection overfitting: an SVM achieved near-zero cross-validated error on random labels when gene selection was performed on the full dataset before cross-validation, but when selection was nested inside each fold the median error was 11 out of 40, close to the 50\% expected by chance. Vabalas et al.\ \citep{Vabalas2019} showed that the bias introduced by pre-selection can exceed the bias from hyperparameter tuning, and Cawley and Talbot \citep{Cawley2010} argued that feature selection is itself a form of model selection whose complexity cost must be accounted for. KIRBy follows the standard remedy: we compute all importance scores within each cross-validation fold, on training data only. But the problem of ``search degrees of freedom'' \citep{Tibshirani2015} remains. Searching over many possible feature subsets adds complexity even when each individual selection is unbiased. The budget acts as a hard capacity constraint: selecting 100 features from a pool of 5,000 limits the space of possible selections far more tightly than $L_1$ regularization over the same pool, because the feasible set grows polynomially in the budget while the continuous parameter space of a regularized model is unbounded \citep{Ng2004}. Important here is a distinction: the features being selected are frozen embeddings, not parameters being fitted. Choosing which pre-existing knowledge to trust is, we think, a less expressive operation than learning new features, and Kumar et al.\ \citep{Kumar2022} showed that frozen embeddings fed through a linear probe can outperform full fine-tuning by up to 10\% in out-of-distribution accuracy precisely because fine-tuning distorts pretrained structure. And stability can be measured directly: the Nogueira index \citep{Nogueira2018} quantifies the consistency of selected feature sets between folds. If selection were overfit, feature sets would differ wildly from fold to fold.


\section{Limitations}
% OUTLINE 5.10: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.10 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: small datasets, the ceiling on frozen embeddings, sparse sequence encodings, the engineering burden of many foundation models, and the noise ceiling from Kramer2012. The bullets below add the four points from the brain dump and do not repeat these.
% - TODO(check): the prose says there is no gain below about 200 molecules. That claim is older than the small-dataset allocation work. Re-check it against the full runs before keeping it.
%
% Compute and run time:
% - The search is a full grid. Every configuration is scored on every fold.
% - Number of configurations per dataset: $|\mathcal{C}| = |\mathcal{A}|\cdot|\mathcal{I}|\cdot|\mathcal{R}|\cdot\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}|\mathcal{B}_m|\,|\mathcal{E}_m|$
%   - $\mathcal{A}$ = allocation methods; $\mathcal{I}$ = importance methods; $\mathcal{R}$ = settings for how many base representations may enter the hybrid; $\mathcal{M}$ = downstream models; $\mathcal{B}_m$ = budgets allowed for model $m$; $\mathcal{E}_m$ = internal evaluators tried for model $m$.
%   - Budgets and evaluators are summed over models, not multiplied. Some models have a budget cap, so they see fewer budgets.
%   - The default evaluators are: the same model type as the downstream model, a random forest, LightGBM and a neural network. When the downstream model is already one of the three fixed ones, it adds nothing new, so $|\mathcal{E}_m|$ is 3 or 4.
%   - Default grid for scale: 5 allocation methods, 3 importance methods (tree gain, TreeSHAP, DeepLIFT), 4 settings for the number of representations (2, 3, 5, all). This gives $5\cdot3\cdot4 = 60$ configurations per budget per (model, evaluator) pair.
% - Total model fits $\approx |\mathcal{C}| \times F_{\text{inner}} \times F_{\text{outer}}$ under nested cross-validation. The search default is 5 inner folds. The search runs again inside every outer fold, so the outer loop multiplies the whole grid.
% - Importance scores $s^{(k)}_j$ are computed once per importance method per fold, on training data only. All allocation methods and budgets in that fold reuse them.
% - Importance methods differ in cost. SHAP values and neural-network attributions (DeepLIFT) cost far more than tree gain importance.
% - Per-dataset cost grows with $n$ (every fit), with $\sum_k d_k$ (importance scoring over every feature) and with $K$ (per-representation scores and the allocation over representations).
% - TODO(results): wall-clock hours per dataset for the full search, and which axis dominates the time. Numbers come from the full runs.
% - Caching repeated work (fold splits, importance scores, per-representation baseline scores) cuts the cost of a re-run. It does not cut the cost of the first run.
% - DIAGRAM: run time (hours, log scale) against dataset size $n$ for the full search. One point per dataset, coloured by domain. A horizontal reference line for the time to fit the best single representation once.
% - Why it matters: the search costs orders of magnitude more than fitting one model on one representation. It needs a cluster, not a laptop, which limits who can use it.
% - Links to: "Fast tier and adaptive pruning" (Methods, this chapter), the partial answer already built. Links to "Future directions" (Chapter 7), where speeding up the search is a next step.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the energy use and carbon cost of large hyperparameter or architecture searches in ML, and whether any cheminformatics or QSAR paper reports compute cost.
%
% The search must be repeated when inputs change:
% - A search result is fitted to one dataset, one set of base representations, one downstream model and one split.
% - New molecules or different folds: the importance scores $s^{(k)}_j$ and the per-representation scores change, so $b_k$ and $S_k$ can change.
% - A new base representation added to the pool: every $b_k$ is set relative to the others, since $\sum_k b_k = B$. The whole search reruns, not just the part for the new representation.
% - A new downstream model: the best configuration is chosen per model, so a new model needs its own search.
% - This is the same kind of cost as re-tuning hyperparameters, but over a larger space.
% - Why it matters: datasets grow over time (new assay rounds, new chemical series). Each update means paying for the search again.
% - Links to: "Blueprints" (Methods, this chapter). Reusing a blueprint on a related dataset is the partial workaround; its limits belong here.
% - TODO(results): how far a blueprint carries to a new split or a related dataset (overlap of selected features, change in score). Numbers come from the full runs.
%
% High upfront cost, unknown gain:
% - The gain is only known after the search has run.
% - Some datasets are not helped. When one base representation dominates, the hybrid falls back to it or ties it.
% - A standout single representation in the pool hints that the gain will be small. It is not a guarantee either way.
% - No cheap test run before the search predicts whether the hybrid will beat the best single representation.
% - Why it matters: on some datasets a user pays the full cost to learn there is no gain. That result still says the single representation is enough, but it is expensive to learn.
% - TODO(results): the fraction of datasets where the hybrid does not beat the best single representation beyond the fold-to-fold spread, with per-fold scores. Numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: per dataset, hybrid score minus best single representation score (in the dataset's own metric), against search cost in hours. Per-fold points shown, no error bars.
% - Links to: "Discussion" (this chapter), on where hybrids do not help. Links to "No single method fits every dataset" (Chapter 7).
%
% Reproducibility:
% - A hybrid result depends on many settings: the representation pool and the version of each representation, the fold seed, the importance method, the allocation method, the budget, the internal evaluator, and library versions.
% - Feature selection is unstable under resampling. Small changes to the training set can change which features are selected \citep{Kalousis2007}.
% - Stability between two selected sets can be measured, e.g. Jaccard: $J(S, S') = \frac{|S\cap S'|}{|S\cup S'|}$. The Nogueira stability index is already cited in the Discussion.
% - Some importance methods are stochastic (neural-network attributions, subsampled tree ensembles). The same seed and the same library versions are needed to rebuild the same hybrid.
% - The blueprint stores the recipe: which representations, the method choices, the budget and the seed. It does not store the selected feature indices.
% - Rebuilding from a blueprint re-runs the selection on the molecules given. It needs their labels, and on other molecules it can select different features $S_k$ and a different split $b_k$.
% - Pretrained embeddings add model-version dependence. A re-released checkpoint changes $\mathbf{X}^{(k)}$.
% - ML reporting norms ask authors to release code and data and to report hyperparameter ranges, number of runs and compute used \citep{Pineau2021}. The hybrid adds more settings to report.
% - Why it matters: a reader who wants to reproduce a number needs the full recipe, not just "a hybrid of ECFP and descriptors".
% - Links to: Appendix "Data and code availability", and Appendix "The search space and its defaults".
%
% Simpler selection may be enough:
% - Simpler options exist:
%   - Pick the best single representation by cross-validation.
%   - Concatenate all representations, then use a filter method (variance or univariate correlation) \citep{Guyon2003}.
%   - Concatenate, then use an L1 penalty: $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = \arg\min_{\boldsymbol\beta} \|\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta\|_2^2 + \lambda\|\boldsymbol\beta\|_1$ \citep{Tibshirani1996}.
%   - Rely on a tree ensemble's built-in feature selection.
% - Each option costs about one model fit, or one short tuning run, not a grid.
% - They may reach much of the hybrid's gain on many datasets at a much lower cost.
% - Any selection tuned by cross-validation needs nested cross-validation for an honest error estimate \citep{Varma2006}.
% - This applies to the simple options too, so the cost gap is smaller than it first looks.
% - Practical rule to state: with limited compute or a routine task, start with the best single representation. Run the search when a gain beyond the fold-to-fold spread would matter.
% - TODO(lit): search for benchmarks comparing L1 or filter feature selection on concatenated fingerprints and descriptors against the best single fingerprint or descriptor set in QSAR regression.
% - Links to: "Simple is often better" (Chapter 7). Links to "Combining representations: what exists" (introduction of this chapter), where the simpler baselines are introduced.
%
% Scope of claims:
% - The search space is finite. A better hybrid may exist outside the grid (other budgets, other importance methods). The search only finds the best configuration within the grid.
% - The hybrid is built from fixed (frozen) features. It does not learn new features end to end.
% - Why it matters: together these limits say the method is a tool for choosing features when a gain is worth the compute. They do not make it a default for every dataset.
% - Links to: "Limitations" in Chapter 7, which covers the thesis as a whole; keep this section to the hybrid method.
% ---- END NOTES 5.10 ----

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Ch 7 Limitations.
% [KB 137] the KIRBy limitations paragraph, md -> tex. Wording unchanged apart from
% 'KIRBy' -> 'the method' where it reads as a product name mid-sentence.
We want to be clear about KIRBy's limitations. For small datasets (below about 200 samples), the hybrid search cannot reliably outperform the best single source. We suspect this is because the internal validation set within each fold shrinks to roughly 20 samples, and at that size the evaluator cannot distinguish genuine improvements from noise. Tuning cannot fix this. And at the other extreme, frozen embeddings have a ceiling. No signal can be added beyond what the inputs already contain, and on tasks where the true signal requires end-to-end learning from raw inputs (certain 3D quantum-mechanical properties, for example, where SchNet outperforms any frozen embedding by a wide margin), this ceiling binds. Although the methodology naturally accommodates embeddings from sequence-trained models (ESM-2, ProtBERT, etc.), encoding raw sequences directly as one-hot or $k$-mer features disperses the positional and compositional information that makes sequences useful in the first place. Integrating 20 to 50 foundation models into a single pipeline, each with its own dependencies, GPU requirements, and version constraints, is genuinely difficult engineering. Embeddings need only be computed once per dataset and everything is cached. Yet the initial integration burden is real and scales with the number of models in the pool. Finally, on endpoints with high experimental noise, even a well-constructed hybrid may not improve on simpler baselines. Kramer et al.\ \citep{Kramer2012} documented that experimental uncertainty in heterogeneous public $K_i$ data is approximately 0.54 log units. This sets a natural accuracy ceiling of $R^2 \approx 0.81$ regardless of model sophistication; the budget constraint plausibly guards against overshoot, but it cannot push past the noise floor.
