\section{Introduction}
% OUTLINE 6.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Purpose of this introduction (keep it short, about one page):
% - Ties Chapter 4 (what is broken) to Chapter 5 (the tool built) and says what this chapter tests.
% - Chapter 4 finding, in one line: similarity computed on a single standard representation (e.g. ECFP4 bits compared by Tanimoto) mixes a few task-relevant features with many that are constant or irrelevant on the dataset.
% - Consequence carried over from Chapter 4: methods that trust a molecule's neighbours (noise detection, noise estimation, neighbour-based LNL) inherit that error.
% - Already in prose (Chapter 4, exploratory LNL study): adding distance metrics to LNL methods hurt performance; neighbour-based ground-truth estimation looked too weak under uniform noise. Refer back; do not restate it here.
% - Chapter 5 finding, in one line: a hybrid picks a budget of $B$ features from $K$ base representations for a given task and model; it is an input, not a model.
% - The question of this chapter: if the features are chosen for the task, does the neighbourhood of each molecule become more meaningful, and do the methods that depend on neighbourhoods improve?
%
% The original motivation (her own history, worth one or two sentences):
% - The hybrid idea started as a fix for similarity: select the task-relevant features first, then compute distances only on those.
% - It turned out hybrids also work as ordinary model inputs (Chapter 5). This chapter goes back to the original motivation.
% - Why it matters: this is the test of the hypothesis that motivated the thesis method in the first place.
%
% Two ways a hybrid can be used for similarity (defined fully in the Methods section "Similarity in a hybrid"; name them only here):
% - Ordinary hybrid: the search is scored by a downstream model's held-out score; distances are then computed in the hybrid $\mathbf{H}$.
% - Similarity hybrid: same search, but each candidate is scored by how well each molecule's label is predicted from its 5 nearest neighbours in $\mathbf{H}$.
%   - Neighbours are taken only from other scaffold groups (the held-out molecules are split by scaffold group, and each group is predicted from the others).
%   - Neighbour prediction: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j \in \mathcal{N}_5(i)} y_j / d_{ij}}{\sum_{j \in \mathcal{N}_5(i)} 1 / d_{ij}}$, with $d_{ij}$ the Manhattan distance between standardised rows $i$ and $j$ of $\mathbf{H}$: $d_{ij} = \sum_{m} |H_{im} - H_{jm}|$.
%   - Score: Spearman $\rho(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$ for regression; $2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$ for binary classification, so chance is 0 in both cases.
% - Option available to both: importance-weighted distance, where each feature's term in the distance is multiplied by its normalised importance $w_m$ (from $s^{(k)}_j$).
%   - Manhattan form: $d^{w}_{ij} = \sum_{m} w_m |H_{im} - H_{jm}|$, so a feature with zero importance drops out.
%   - Euclidean form: $d^{w}_{ij} = \sqrt{\sum_{m} w_m (H_{im} - H_{jm})^2}$, equivalent to scaling each feature by $\sqrt{w_m}$.
% - Why it matters: the ordinary hybrid asks whether a representation chosen for prediction is also a better similarity space as a side effect; the similarity hybrid asks whether it is worth optimising for neighbourhoods directly.
%
% Where neighbourhoods are used in this chapter (one bullet each; details in Methods):
% - Similarity itself: predicting each molecule's activity from its nearest neighbours in the space.
% - Noise detection and estimation: a molecule whose label disagrees with its neighbours' labels is flagged (the neighbour residual is defined in the Methods; only point to it).
% - Noise mitigation: LNL methods, correcting labels clipped at a detection limit, and cleaning real labels with nothing injected.
% - Classical root of neighbour-based noise filtering: editing a training set by removing samples that disagree with their nearest neighbours \citep{Wilson1972}.
% - Model-based filtering as the other classical route: flagging samples that classifiers trained on other data misclassify \citep{Brodley1999}.
% - Modern version of the same idea: Confident Learning estimates which labels are wrong from out-of-sample predicted probabilities \citep{northcutt2021confident}.
% - Similarity principle the neighbour methods rely on: similar molecules tend to have similar properties \citep{Cherkasov2014}.
% - Activity cliffs are the known exception: structurally similar pairs with large activity differences \citep{Dablander2023}.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature showing activity cliffs are mistaken for label noise by neighbour-based or model-based noise detection in QSAR data.
%
% Research questions (as bullets, one per results section):
% - RQ1 (similarity): Is predicting each molecule's activity from its nearest neighbours more accurate in a hybrid than in the single representations it is built from?
% - RQ2 (similarity): Does optimising the search for neighbourhoods (the similarity hybrid) give a better neighbour space than an ordinary hybrid? Does it cost predictive accuracy?
% - RQ3 (detection and estimation): Are noisy labels easier to find, and their size easier to estimate, when neighbours come from a hybrid?
% - RQ4 (mitigation): Do noise-mitigation methods recover more of the clean score when run on a hybrid, and does the answer depend on the noise type (Gaussian, outliers, grouped offsets, censoring, real labels)?
% - Comparison for every question: each downstream model's own top two single representations, not a fixed baseline.
%
% Expectation to state up front (from the brain dump, keep neutral):
% - Better similarity cannot fix every noise type. Under Gaussian noise every label is noisy, including the neighbours used to judge it.
% - So a more relevant neighbourhood does not remove that problem.
% - Noise that stands out from its neighbours (outliers, labels clipped at a limit) is where better neighbourhoods have a mechanism to help.
% - Links to: the results section "By noise type" and Chapter 7 "Handling noise realistically", which return to this.
%
% Links to:
% - Chapter 2, NoiseInject section: noise conditions are defined there, not re-defined here.
% - Chapter 3: the representation and model noise-robustness results; this chapter adds the hybrid as one more representation under the same noise model.
% - Chapter 4, "Consequences for methods that rely on similarity" and "What a representation would need": this chapter tests whether a hybrid meets those requirements.
% - Chapter 5, "The adaptive hybrid representation": where both uses of similarity were first named.
%
% Status note for the reader (one sentence in prose):
% - This chapter is planned as the second hybrid paper; methods are final, results are preliminary.
% - TODO(results): one-sentence preview of the main pattern per research question; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% DIAGRAM: one schematic linking the three chapters. Left: single representation -> many irrelevant features -> noisy neighbourhoods -> neighbour-based noise methods fail (Chapter 4). Middle: several representations -> hybrid of $B$ selected features (Chapter 5). Right: hybrid -> nearest neighbours -> similarity, noise detection, noise mitigation (Chapter 6), each box tagged with its research question.
%
% Literature to find:
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on task-specific or supervised molecular similarity (similarity metrics learned or weighted using activity labels, e.g. metric learning for QSAR).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on feature selection or feature weighting improving k-nearest-neighbour prediction in QSAR.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on how irrelevant features degrade nearest-neighbour distances in high dimensions (distance concentration), to back the Chapter 4 link in one sentence.
% ---- END NOTES 6.1 ----


\section{Methods}
% OUTLINE 6.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section does:
% - Short signpost for the chapter's methods. It has three parts: similarity in a hybrid, the noise conditions, and the noise-mitigation methods.
% - Every hybrid in this chapter comes from the hybrid search in Chapter 5 (Section "Methods: the hybrid search"). How a hybrid is built does not change.
% - Two things are new: what each candidate hybrid is scored on, and what is done to the labels.
% - Already in prose: the move from sample removal to weighting and smoothing, the estimators, the smoothing and weighting formulas, and the recovery rate (all under "Noise mitigation methods").
%
% The three questions the methods answer (one per part):
% - Similarity: does a hybrid chosen for the task give better nearest neighbours than a standard fingerprint does? This targets the similarity flaws shown in Chapter 4.
% - Noise detection and estimation: do better neighbours help flag noisy labels, or estimate how large the noise is?
% - Noise mitigation: once labels are flagged or sized, does cleaning them, weighting them, or training with a noise-aware method improve the held-out score?
% - Why it matters: Chapter 4 argued that neighbour-based noise handling fails because the representation space is poor. This chapter tests the direct fix: change the space and keep the noise method.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, "Consequences for methods that rely on similarity".
% - Links to: Chapter 3 (noise robustness), for the noise model and for the NGBoost and GP uncertainty results.
%
% Two kinds of hybrid used throughout:
% - Predictive hybrid: the search scores each candidate by a downstream model's held-out score. This is the Chapter 5 hybrid.
% - Similarity hybrid: the same search, but each candidate is scored without fitting any downstream model.
%   - The score predicts each held-out molecule's activity from its 5 nearest neighbours in the hybrid space.
%   - The details and the formula belong in "Similarity in a hybrid". Here only name both kinds.
% - Importance-weighted distance: each hybrid feature's contribution to the distance is multiplied by its importance score.
%   - Under the Manhattan distance, the default for the similarity hybrid: $d(\mathbf{h}_a,\mathbf{h}_b) = \sum_j s_j\,|h_{aj}-h_{bj}|$. The features are scaled by $s_j$.
%   - Under the Euclidean distance: $d^2(\mathbf{h}_a,\mathbf{h}_b) = \sum_j s_j\,(h_{aj}-h_{bj})^2$. The features are scaled by $\sqrt{s_j}$, i.e. $\mathbf{H}' = \mathbf{H}\,\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{\mathbf{s}})$.
%   - The same importance vector gives a different weighting under each distance, because the power applied to $s_j$ changes.
%   - Note: the existing prose gives only $x' = x \odot \sqrt{w}$. That form is correct for Euclidean only. Update it to state the distance.
%   - The weight vector is normalised before use. Negative importance scores are set to 0.
%   - TODO: confirm which distance and which power on $s_j$ the final runs use. Both were still being chosen when these notes were written.
%
% Shared protocol across all experiments in the chapter:
% - The datasets, the scaffold-group-aware cross-validation folds and the metrics are the same as in Chapter 5 (Sections "Datasets and splitting" and "Models and metrics"). No random splits.
%   - TODO: state which datasets each of the three noise-mitigation experiments runs on. The final list comes from the full runs.
% - Noise is injected into the training labels only.
% - Held-out labels are never changed, so every score is measured against the recorded labels.
%   - Noise injection uses the NoiseInject package \citep{noiseinject}.
%   - The noise conditions are defined once in Chapter 2 (Section "Artificial noise injection") and are not redefined here.
% - One exception: the "labels as they come" experiment injects no noise. It cleans the recorded labels and asks only whether the held-out score improves.
%   - QM9 is the control in that experiment. Its labels are computed, not measured.
%   - So a cleanup that "helps" on QM9 is removing hard molecules, not measurement error.
% - When the labels are noisy, the hybrid never sees clean labels. It is built within each fold's training molecules, using their noisy (or uncleaned) labels.
%   - Why it matters: a hybrid searched on clean labels would give the noise method access to the true labels, which a real user never has.
% - Comparison baseline: each downstream model's own top 2 single representations, ranked by that model's own held-out score. It is never a fixed list, and never one chosen fingerprint.
%   - Why it matters: comparing against a weak single representation would inflate any gain from the hybrid.
% - Reporting: per-fold scores and their spread only. No significance tests and no confidence intervals.
%   - Where a cleanup is judged, report the number of folds on which it beat the uncleaned model, out of the total number of folds. Put the mean difference and the per-fold spread beside it.
% - The common summary of a mitigation method is the recovery rate (already in prose): $\mathrm{Recovery} = \frac{P_{\text{treated}} - P_{\text{noisy}}}{P_{\text{clean}} - P_{\text{noisy}}}$, where $P$ is the held-out score in the dataset's own metric.
%   - For a metric where lower is better, such as RMSE, the sign flips: $\mathrm{Recovery} = \frac{P_{\text{noisy}} - P_{\text{treated}}}{P_{\text{noisy}} - P_{\text{clean}}}$.
%   - It is not reported when the gap between the clean and the noisy score is under 2% of the clean score. A tiny gap makes the ratio meaningless.
%   - Note: the existing prose defines recovery with $R^2$ and a random forest on ECFP4 only. The final experiments use each dataset's own metric and each model's top 2 representations. Update the prose to match.
%
% Framing bullets:
% - The chapter separates three tasks that are often treated as one: finding noisy labels, estimating how large the noise is, and improving the model.
% - A method can do the first task well and still fail the third.
% - Links to: Chapter 7, "Handling noise realistically" and "No single method fits every dataset".
% - Background on learning with noisy labels: the survey by \citep{Song2022}.
% - Confident learning is the standard label-cleaning baseline \citep{Northcutt2021}.
% - The three families of label-noise methods (noise-robust models, cleaning or filtering the data, noise-tolerant learning) are laid out in \citep{Frenay2014}. Cite it once for this grouping.

%   - Middle: two branches, the predictive hybrid and the similarity hybrid, each built per fold on the noisy labels. Beside them, the top 2 single representations.

\subsection{Similarity in a hybrid}
% OUTLINE 6.2.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.2.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: importance-weighted distance, $x' = x \odot \sqrt{w}$ (the Feature importance weighting subsubsection).
% - CHECK (existing prose): $\sqrt{w}$ is only right for Euclidean (and cosine) distance. Under Manhattan, the default, each feature is multiplied by $w$ itself.
%   - The rule is the exponent that makes the weight enter the distance once. Euclidean: $w^{1/2}$. Manhattan: $w^{1}$. Minkowski with exponent $p$: $w^{1/p}$.
%   - The prose needs either the general form or a statement that it assumes Euclidean.
% Two ways to use a hybrid for similarity:
% - Way 1: build an ordinary hybrid (features chosen to help a downstream model predict the label), then compute distances in it.
% - Way 2: change what the search optimises. The search picks the hybrid whose distances best predict each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours. Call this a similarity hybrid.
% - Same search in both: same importance scores, allocation methods, budgets and folds. Only the score used to rank candidate hybrids differs.
% - Way 2 fits no downstream model during the search. The score comes from the geometry of the space and the labels alone.
% - CHECK (scope): the search can also rank hybrids by structural agreement with maximum common substructure (defined below), or by a weighted mean of several scores, each on $[-1,1]$. Decide whether the thesis uses either; if not, leave them out.
% - Why it matters: Chapter 4 argued that the space defines the neighbours, and the neighbours drive similarity-based methods. Way 2 tests whether choosing features for neighbourhood quality fixes that more directly than choosing them for prediction.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, section "Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations"; Chapter 5, subsection "The search space" (the objective is one axis of the search).
% - DIAGRAM: two parallel pipelines with a shared first half. Shared: base representations -> importance -> allocation -> hybrid. Top branch: hybrid -> downstream model -> held-out predictions -> dataset metric. Bottom branch: hybrid -> 5 nearest neighbours from other scaffold groups -> distance-weighted mean activity -> Spearman against truth. Caption: same search, different score.
% Preparing the hybrid for distances:
% - Hybrid: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times B}$, with $|S_k| = b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k = B$.
% - Each feature is z-scored with the mean and standard deviation of the training molecules only: $z_{ij} = (h_{ij} - \mu_j)/\sigma_j$.
% - Held-out molecules use the training $\mu_j$ and $\sigma_j$.
% - Z-scoring stops features with large units (e.g. molecular weight) from dominating the distance.
% - Default distance is Manhattan: $d(\mathbf{z}_a,\mathbf{z}_b) = \sum_{j=1}^{B} |z_{aj} - z_{bj}|$.
% - Alternatives available: Euclidean $\sqrt{\sum_j (z_{aj}-z_{bj})^2}$; cosine $1 - \frac{\mathbf{z}_a\cdot\mathbf{z}_b}{\|\mathbf{z}_a\|\|\mathbf{z}_b\|}$; fractional Minkowski $\left(\sum_j |z_{aj}-z_{bj}|^{p}\right)^{1/p}$ with $p=0.5$.
% - Reason for Manhattan over Euclidean: in high dimensions, distances under larger $p$ vary less in relative terms, so nearest and farthest neighbours become hard to tell apart; smaller $p$ keeps more contrast \citep{Aggarwal2001}.
% - TODO(results): comparison of the four distances at predicting activity from five nearest neighbours; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(decision): the choice of distance and of neighbour weighting ($1/d$ vs other schemes) are still being swept together. Confirm the final choice before writing it as settled.
% Width is weight:
% - In a z-scored concatenation, every feature adds one term of similar size to a Manhattan distance.
% - So a representation's influence on the distance grows with its number of features.
% - Worked example: a 2048-feature fingerprint next to a 200-feature descriptor set supplies $2048/2248 \approx 91\%$ of the terms. The fingerprint alone decides the neighbours.
% - The fix when stacking whole representations: divide each z-scored representation of width $m$ (number of features) by $m$ under Manhattan or $\sqrt{m}$ under Euclidean. General rule: $m^{1/p}$ for exponent $p$.
% - In a hybrid, representation $k$ contributes $b_k$ of the $B$ terms. Its share of the distance is roughly $b_k/B$.
% - Big picture: in a hybrid, the allocation doubles as a weighting of the representations inside the distance. Choosing how many features each representation gets also sets how much it counts in similarity.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 subsection "Different representations carry different information"; Chapter 5 subsection "From base representations to a hybrid" (allocation).
% Importance-weighted distance (optional, on top of the hybrid):
% - Negative importance scores are clipped to 0.
% - The remaining scores are rescaled to average 1: $\tilde{w}_j = w_j \cdot \frac{B}{\sum_{l} w_l}$.
% - Why average 1: features stay near their own z-scored scale.
% - Nearest-neighbour rankings are the same whether weights average 1 or sum to 1. A fixed distance cut-off or kernel width is not.
% - Weights are applied after z-scoring: $z'_{ij} = \tilde{w}_j^{\,e}\, z_{ij}$, with $e = 1$ for Manhattan, $1/2$ for Euclidean and cosine, and $1/p$ for Minkowski.
% - The resulting Manhattan distance: $d_w(\mathbf{z}_a,\mathbf{z}_b) = \sum_j \tilde{w}_j\,|z_{aj}-z_{bj}|$.
% - Pitfall worth one sentence: weighting before z-scoring does nothing, because z-scoring divides the weight back out.
% - A weight of 0 removes a feature. Two molecules that differ only in removed features sit at distance 0. The distance is then a pseudometric.
% - The search score for a similarity hybrid uses the plain z-scored hybrid, with no importance weights.
% - CHECK: say which Chapter 6 experiments use weighted distances downstream.
% - Prior art: nearest-neighbour QSAR with variable selection picks features for a kNN predictor \citep{Zheng2000}. It is the closest earlier idea to a similarity hybrid. The difference here: several representations and a fixed budget.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on "feature-weighted distance metrics for k-nearest-neighbour regression".
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on "metric learning for molecular similarity".
% The similarity search score (the similarity objective):
% - Scored on each held-out validation block inside a fold. Features are selected on the training part only.
% - The block's scaffold groups are split into $\min(5, G)$ parts, where $G$ is the number of scaffold groups in the block. A group is never split across parts.
% - For each molecule $i$ in one part, its 5 nearest neighbours $N_5(i)$ are found among molecules in the other parts. So neighbours always come from a different scaffold group.
% - Why other scaffolds: a neighbour sharing the scaffold is an easy match and inflates the score. Requiring a different scaffold asks whether the space generalises across chemical series.
% - Links to: Chapter 5 subsection "Datasets and splitting".
% - Predicted activity is the inverse-distance weighted mean: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} y_j / d_{ij}}{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} 1/d_{ij}}$.
%   - If some neighbours sit at distance 0, only those neighbours are used.
%   - Distance-weighted kNN goes back to \citep{Dudani1976}. Dudani's weights are linear in distance, not $1/d$.
% - Regression score: $S_{\text{sim}} = \rho_s(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$, the Spearman correlation between the neighbour predictions and the true labels.
% - Binary classification: $\hat{y}_i$ is the distance-weighted fraction of the 5 neighbours in class 1, and $S_{\text{sim}} = 2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$.
%   - Why not AUROC as is: AUROC is 0.5 at chance, a correlation is 0. The rescale puts both on $[-1,1]$ with 0 at chance.
%   - The rescale does not change which hybrid ranks first.
%   - It matters because the budget split (below) divides by these scores, so the zero point must mean "no signal".
% - A candidate hybrid's search score is the mean of $S_{\text{sim}}$ over the folds where the score is defined.
% - The score is undefined (a gap, not a zero) when a block has fewer than 10 molecules, fewer than 2 scaffold groups, constant labels or constant predictions.
%   - Reason: a real correlation of 0 means no neighbourhood structure. An undefined fold must not look like that.
% - Undefined for multiclass labels: a mean of class indices is not a score for any one class.
% - Also undefined when a held-out block holds only one group.
% - So the similarity objective is not run on the multiclass datasets, or on the binary antibody dataset whose groups are edit-distance shells.
% - Relation to published work: $S_{\text{sim}}$ is a graded, regression form of the modelability index (MODI), which counts molecules whose single nearest neighbour has the same activity class \citep{Golbraikh2014}.
% - MODI itself was not used: it keeps only a hard class match with one neighbour and discards the graded fraction.
% - CHECK: whether to mention the second candidate score that was dropped (the correlation between pairwise distance and pairwise activity difference).
% - Why it matters: $S_{\text{sim}}$ turns the similarity principle into a number \citep{Cherkasov2014}.
% - Choosing the space that maximises it is choosing the space where "similar molecules have similar activity" holds best, on molecules and scaffolds not used to pick the features.
% Budget split under the similarity objective (keep short; detail belongs in Chapter 5):
% - When the allocation method splits the budget by per-representation scores, each representation is scored alone by $S_{\text{sim}}$ on its own z-scored features, in place of its predictive score.
% - Linear scheme: if every score is non-negative, $b_k \propto S^{(k)}_{\text{sim}}$.
% - If some scores are negative, all are shifted up by the lowest: $b_k \propto S^{(k)}_{\text{sim}} - \min_l S^{(l)}_{\text{sim}}$. The worst representation then gets 0 features.
% - A representation whose score is undefined gets 0 features.
% - The same distance and the same neighbour rule are used for the budget split and for the final score. Otherwise a hybrid would be judged by one similarity and have its budget split by another.
% - CHECK (unverified): the outline says similarity runs always use the whole representation pool and never search the number of representations. Confirm before stating it.
% How similarity quality is evaluated (outer folds):
% - Features are chosen on the outer training part, and the z-scoring is fitted there. Scoring is on the untouched outer test fold.
% - Number 1: $S_{\text{sim}}$ on the outer test fold (predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours in the space).
% - Number 2, structural agreement (molecules only). Maximum-common-substructure (MCS) distance for a pair $(a,b)$: $d_{\text{MCS}}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{n_{\text{MCS}}}{n_a + n_b - n_{\text{MCS}}}$.
%   - $n_a$, $n_b$ are atom counts. $n_{\text{MCS}}$ is the atom count of the largest shared substructure.
%   - Structural agreement: $F = \rho_s\big(d_{\mathbf{H}}(a,b),\, d_{\text{MCS}}(a,b)\big)$ over a precomputed set of molecule pairs with both molecules in the fold.
%   - Only reported with at least 10 pairs in the fold.
%   - Higher $F$ means the space's distances track shared atoms and bonds.
%   - $d_{\text{MCS}}$ grows with the combined size of the two molecules. A second version of $F$ removes combined heavy-atom count $n_a + n_b$ from both sides (partial Spearman).
%   - CHECK: $F$ always uses Euclidean distance, even when the neighbour search uses Manhattan. Decide whether to align them or state it.
% - Number 3: the dataset's own metric, from a downstream model fitted on the same similarity-hybrid features. Stored next to the geometry numbers, so both objectives can be compared in one table.
% - The comparison is always against single representations. Each is scored the same way: $S_{\text{sim}}$ on its z-scored features, same folds, same scaffold groups.
% - Only per-fold scores and their spread are reported. No significance tests.
% - TODO(results): per dataset, $S_{\text{sim}}$ of the similarity hybrid vs the ordinary hybrid vs the best single representation, per fold; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): structural agreement $F$ for the same three spaces; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): the dataset metric of a downstream model fitted on the similarity hybrid, i.e. how much prediction is lost when optimising for neighbours; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Links to: Chapter 6 section "Results: similarity", where these numbers go.
% - Links to: Chapter 6 subsection "Noise mitigation methods". The spaces evaluated here are the spaces the neighbour-based noise detection runs in.
% - Links to: Chapter 7 section "No single method fits every dataset", on whether the best similarity space differs from the best prediction space.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on "activity landscape analysis comparing descriptor-space distance to maximum common substructure similarity".
% ---- END NOTES 6.2.1 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{6.2.1: objective=similarity. [DIAGRAM D6.1]}

\subsubsection{Feature importance weighting}

% [CR 954-956] importance-weighted distance -- the 'importance-weighted distance' the
% outline's Sec 7.2 makes its central positive claim about.
Beyond simply using the feature importance scores for allocation, we investigate whether feature weighting by importance during distance computation improves noise detection. For a feature importance vector $w$, weighted distances are computed by scaling features: $x' = x \odot \sqrt{w}$. 


\subsection{Noise conditions}
% OUTLINE 6.2.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.2.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope and link to Chapter 2:
% - The conditions are defined once in Chapter 2 (Artificial noise injection, with the dose-matching and grouped-shifted equations). This subsection says which ones this chapter uses, what is new here, and which real source of error each one stands for.
% - Already in prose (Ch 2): the seven conditions, the dose-matching equation, the grouped-shifted equation, and where $\lambda=3$, $p=0.10$ and $\rho=0.62$ come from. Refer back to them; do not re-derive.
% - New in this chapter compared with Ch 2: two more Student-$t$ shapes ($\nu=10$ and $\nu=3$), two more outlier rates ($p=0.01$ and $p=0.05$), the outlier-only family, and a real-label setting with nothing injected.
% - Links to: Ch 3 (NoiseInject results) used the same generator, so conditions and levels are comparable across the two chapters.
% - DIAGRAM/TABLE: one row per noise condition. Columns: real-world source it stands for; what is injected; parameters; how the level is expressed (fraction of label SD, or fraction clipped); zero-mean or not; does a "corrupted subset" exist (yes/no). Rows: Gaussian, Student-$t$ ($\nu=10,5,3$), Laplace, outlier ($p=0.01,0.05,0.10$), outlier-only ($p=0.01,0.05,0.10$), grouped-wider, grouped-shifted, censoring, real labels (nothing injected).
%
% Shared set-up:
% - Noisy label: $\tilde{y}_i = y_i + \epsilon_i$.
% - Only training labels are corrupted. Test labels are never corrupted in the injected conditions.
% - Level for every condition except censoring is a fraction $\ell$ of the SD of the clean training labels of that fold: $\tau = \ell \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\mathbf{y}_{\text{train}})$.
% - Levels swept: $\ell \in \{0, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.5\}$, the same grid on every dataset.
%   - Why: the area under the score-vs-level curve can then be compared between datasets.
% - $\ell = 0$ is the clean control inside every sweep.
% - Everything that depends on labels is computed from the training molecules of the fold only: the SD that sets $\tau$, the censoring limit, which scaffold families are affected, and the constant $G$.
%   - Why it matters: otherwise held-out labels would decide how much noise the training labels get, so held-out information would leak into training.
% - The random draws are made once over the whole dataset and then subset per fold. A molecule gets the same draw in every fold it trains in; only the training-derived constants change between folds.
% - Which molecules are hit is seeded separately from the shape draws. The affected molecules stay the same across levels, so a higher level is more noise on the same molecules, not a different set.
% - Dose matching (from Ch 2): $\epsilon_i = \frac{\tau}{G} s_i z_i$ with $G = \sqrt{\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_j s_j^2}\,\sigma_z$. The delivered noise has root-mean-square $\tau$ whatever the shape or targeting.
% - The level is matched on the second moment (root-mean-square), not on mean $|\epsilon|$, because RMSE and $R^2$ are second-moment quantities.
%   - At equal root-mean-square, $\mathbb{E}|\epsilon|/\mathrm{RMS} = \sqrt{2/\pi} \approx 0.80$ for a Gaussian and $2/\pi \approx 0.64$ for Student-$t$ with $\nu=3$.
%   - Consequence: matching on mean $|\epsilon|$ would give heavy-tailed shapes more noise at the same nominal level.
% - Scaffold families for the grouped conditions are Bemis--Murcko scaffolds \citep{Bemis1996}, with every acyclic molecule its own family.
%   - Why: an acyclic molecule has an empty scaffold. Without this rule all acyclic molecules would share one family, and one draw would move all of them together.
%   - This grouping is separate from the scaffold grouping used to make the CV folds, so changing the noise scheme never changes the folds.
%
% Gaussian and other shapes (every label noisy):
% - Gaussian: $z_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$ and $s_i = 1$ for all $i$, so $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\tau^2)$.
% - Heavier tails at the same root-mean-square: Student-$t$ with $\nu = 10, 5, 3$ ($\sigma_z = \sqrt{\nu/(\nu-2)}$) and Laplace ($\sigma_z = \sqrt{2}$).
% - Stands for: random repeat-measurement error of an assay.
% - Every molecule has the same noise scale, so "which molecules are noisy" is undefined.
%   - Consequence: detection precision and recall are not reported under these conditions.
% - Why it matters: every label is wrong by an unknown amount in an unknown direction, and the neighbours used to judge a label are noisy too. The expectation is that cleaning methods have little to find here (brain-dump point; links to 6.5.5 By noise type and 7.2 Handling noise realistically).
% - TODO(results): whether cleaning helps under the shape conditions; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Outliers (a subset badly wrong):
% - Outlier: each molecule is selected with probability $p$ and gets $s_i = \lambda = 3$; the rest get $s_i = 1$. $p \in \{0.01, 0.05, 0.10\}$. Every label still moves.
% - New in this chapter, outlier-only: the same selection, but unselected molecules get $s_i = 0$ and are left exactly clean.
%   - For outlier-only, $G \approx \lambda\sqrt{p}\,\sigma_z$ (exact when the realised selected fraction equals $p$).
% - Share of the injected squared error carried by the selected molecules: $\frac{p\lambda^2}{p\lambda^2 + (1-p)\beta^2}$, where $\beta$ is the multiplier on unselected molecules.
%   - $\beta=1$, $p=0.10$, $\lambda=3$: $0.9/1.8 = 0.5$. A perfect detector that removes every selected molecule still leaves half the injected error.
%   - $\beta=0$: the share is 1. Removing the selected molecules removes all injected error.
% - Why both are swept: the pair separates "the detector cannot find the bad labels" from "finding them does not help". A null result under outlier alone cannot tell these apart.
% - Stands for: gross errors such as transcription mistakes, unit mix-ups and failed runs.
% - Why it matters: here detection is well defined and a practitioner can hope to remove bad points. Contrast with Gaussian, where no bad subset exists.
%
% Systematic / grouped (errors shared within a family):
% - Grouped-wider: whole scaffold families are chosen in random order until the chosen families cover the number of training molecules closest to 20%. Those molecules get $s_i = \lambda = 3$; the rest get $s_i = 1$.
%   - Selection counts molecules, not families, because scaffold families differ greatly in size.
%   - Stands for: a less reliable laboratory or assay covering part of chemical space.
%   - The 20% is a stated choice; no published figure was found.
% - Grouped-shifted: $\epsilon_i = \frac{\tau}{G}\left(\sqrt{\rho}\,b_{g(i)} + \sqrt{1-\rho}\,w_i\right)$ with $\rho = 0.62$, one offset $b_g$ per family and one $w_i$ per molecule (Ch 2 equation).
%   - Notation clash: $b_g$ here is a family offset, not the allocation $b_k$. Rename one of them in the thesis.
%   - Every molecule in a family is moved by the same offset, so labels within a family stay consistent with each other.
%   - Stands for: laboratory offsets when data from many sources are pooled. Combining $K_i$ or IC$_{50}$ values from different sources adds substantial noise \citep{landrum2024}.
%   - The noise scale per molecule is constant, so "which molecules are noisy" is undefined here too.
% - Why it matters: the nearest neighbours of a shifted molecule are usually from the same family and are shifted the same way. Methods that compare a label with its neighbours' labels cannot see this error (links to 4.4.2 Noise detection by neighbours and 6.2.1 Similarity in a hybrid).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on what share of a pooled public bioactivity dataset (e.g. one ChEMBL target) comes from a single laboratory or assay, to justify or replace the 20% family coverage.
%
% Censoring at a detection limit:
% - The level is the fraction $f$ of training labels clipped, not a dose.
% - The limit comes from the clean training labels: $c = Q_{1-f}(\mathbf{y}_{\text{train}})$ (upper quantile), and $\tilde{y}_i = \min(y_i, c)$.
%   - Exactly $\mathrm{round}(f\,n_{\text{train}})$ molecules count as censored: the top-ranked ones, with ties broken by position. This matters for coarsely recorded labels where many values sit on the limit.
% - $\epsilon_i = \min(y_i,c) - y_i \le 0$: one-sided and not zero-mean, a bias rather than a scatter. So it cannot be dose-matched and gets its own axis.
% - Levels: $f \in \{0, 0.10, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30, 0.40\}$. Detection-and-repair experiments use the subset $\{0, 0.10, 0.25, 0.40\}$. $f=0$ is the control.
% - 0.5 is not used: clipping half the labels removes the top of the label range outright rather than corrupting single values.
% - No randomness: the clip is deterministic, so there is one realisation per level.
% - Clipped molecules are visible to a practitioner, because their recorded label equals the limit. No detector is needed; the open question is what to do with them (links to 6.2.3 Noise mitigation methods).
% - Stands for: assay range limits, e.g. "> 10 µM" recorded as 10 µM. Censored labels occur in industrial drug-discovery assay data \citep{Svensson2025}.
% - TODO(lit): confirm the share of censored labels per assay reported in Svensson et al. 2025 before quoting a percentage.
%
% Real noise (nothing injected):
% - Labels are used as they come. Training and test labels both carry whatever error the assay has.
% - No list of wrong labels exists. Success can only be measured as a better held-out score after cleaning, fold by fold, not as detection accuracy.
% - A win means the cleaning helped the model. It does not prove it found errors; it may have removed molecules that are simply hard to predict.
% - QM9 as control: its labels come from a quantum-chemical calculation, with no random experimental error. A cleaning method that "helps" on QM9 is removing something other than measurement error.
% - Real error sizes, marked on the shared axis as $\tau_{\text{ref}}/\mathrm{SD}(\mathbf{y})$ for each dataset:
%   - pK$_i$ in heterogeneous public data: about 0.54 log units \citep{Kramer2012}. For hERG this is a ChEMBL-wide stand-in; no hERG-specific figure is used.
%   - Caco-2: about 0.35 log$_{10}$ units, computed by the author from digoxin efflux ratios reported by 11 laboratories \citep{Bentz2013}. State that it is computed, not quoted.
%   - logD: 0.48 log units repeat-test error after curation \citep{Niu2024}.
%   - TODO(check): confirm the 0.48 figure against Table 5 of Niu et al. 2024 before quoting it.
% - Why it matters: the injected conditions show what a method can do when the truth is known. The real-label setting shows whether it helps on data a chemist actually has. Both are needed (links to 7.2 Handling noise realistically).
%
% Classification datasets:
% - The level is a flip rate, the share of training labels switched to a wrong class: $\{0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.20, 0.30, 0.40\}$.
% - Kept below 0.5: at 0.5 in a binary task the label is independent of the molecule; above 0.5 the labels are inverted.
% - TODO(check): decide whether classification noise is reported in this chapter at all. If yes, add one line per flip model (uniform, class-imbalance, binary-asymmetric, instance-dependent, class-dependent, confusion-directed).
%
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: the conditions split into two groups. In outlier, outlier-only, grouped-wider and censoring a "bad subset" exists. In Gaussian, the other shapes and grouped-shifted every label is wrong. Detection and cleaning of a subset are only defined for the first group.
% - Why it matters: the practical reading for a user is to know where the data came from. Many pooled laboratories suggest grouped-shifted. One controlled assay suggests censoring plus Gaussian repeat error. Hand-curated data suggest outliers.
% - Links to: 6.5.5 By noise type (results broken down along these same rows); 7.3 No single method fits every dataset.
% - TODO(results): per-condition score-vs-level curves for each mitigation method; numbers come from the full runs.
% ---- END NOTES 6.2.2 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{6.2.2: Table 6.1, one row per noise type.}


\subsection{Noise mitigation methods}
% OUTLINE 6.2.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.2.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Framing: two families of methods
% - Already in prose: sample removal (neighbour label spread, LOF), noise-magnitude estimators, label smoothing and weighting formulas, recovery rate. The bullets below cover only what is new or changed for the final methods.
% - Two families, kept apart throughout the chapter:
%   - Learning with noisy labels (LNL): leave the labels as they are; change how the model trains (which samples it trusts, how much weight each gets, what loss it uses).
%   - Data cleaning: change the training labels, or drop or reweight molecules, before any model sees them; the model then trains as normal.
% - Key difference: LNL never has to say which direction a label is wrong in.
% - Cleaning has to say it, per molecule, whenever it moves a label.
% - Why it matters: this split supports the Discussion section "Handling noise realistically": LNL as the safer default, and per-molecule correction as hard when every label carries unknown noise.
% - Three method sets, one per question:
%   - (1) a known, structured defect: labels clipped at a detection limit;
%   - (2) a survey of LNL methods under injected noise;
%   - (3) cleaning real labels, with nothing injected.
% - Shared comparison in all three: the hybrid against each downstream model's own two best single representations. The ranking uses that model's own score in the hybrid search. Never a fixed list of representations.
% - Test labels are never changed in any of the three. Every score is against the original held-out labels.
% - Only per-fold scores and the spread across folds are reported. No significance tests.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the distinction between noise-robust training and data cleaning for regression (continuous) labels, not classification.
% - Links to: "Noise conditions" section of this chapter (censoring, Gaussian, outliers etc. are defined there); "The exploratory learning-with-noisy-labels study" in Chapter 4 (where these methods started).
%
% Method set 1: labels clipped at a detection limit (censoring)
% - Setup: every training label above a limit $c$ is recorded as $c$: $\tilde{y}_i = \min(y_i, c)$. Test labels untouched.
%   - $c$ = the $(1-\ell)$ quantile of the full label column.
%   - So the level $\ell$ is the fraction of labels clipped, not a noise size in label units.
%   - Clipping has no randomness, so one draw per level.
% - Which molecules count as clipped: those whose recorded label equals the largest training label.
%   - Read from the training labels alone, which is what a user without the true values can see.
%   - Needs at least 1% of training molecules, and at least 2, sitting on that value.
% - Why it is a different problem: the affected molecules are known exactly.
% - The direction of the error is also known: the true value is at or above $c$.
% - So no detector is needed. Only the size of the correction is unknown.
% - Link: real assays report some values only as "> limit" or "< limit".
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the frequency of qualified (censored, ">" or "<") activity values in ChEMBL bioactivity records.
% - Reference prediction used by the corrections: $p_i$ = out-of-fold gradient-boosted tree prediction for training molecule $i$.
%   - Inner folds keep scaffold groups together.
%   - Fitted in the same feature space as the condition being scored.
% - Residual spread: $s = \operatorname{sd}(y_i - p_i)$, over training molecules not at the limit only.
%   - Including the clipped molecules would mix in the distance the clip moved them.
% - Standardised limit: $a_i = (c - p_i)/s$.
% - $\phi$, $\Phi$ = standard normal density and CDF; inverse Mills ratio $\lambda(a) = \phi(a)/(1-\Phi(a))$.
% - Label treatments (each a separate condition):
%   - Clean: trained on labels before clipping. Upper reference.
%   - Clipped: trained on clipped labels as they are. Lower reference; every other condition is paired against it.
%   - Constant lift: every clipped label raised by one shared amount, $\tilde{y}_i = c + \frac{1}{|C|}\sum_{j\in C} s\,\lambda(a_j)$, where $C$ = set of clipped molecules.
%   - Per-molecule lift: $\tilde{y}_i = c + s\,\lambda(a_i)$.
%   - Shuffled per-molecule lift (control): the same lifts dealt to different clipped molecules at random.
%     - Separates "labels were raised" from "each molecule got its right amount".
%   - Truncated-normal mean: $\tilde{y}_i = \max\big(c,\; p_i + s\,\lambda(a_i)\big)$ = the mean of $\mathcal{N}(p_i, s^2)$ above $c$, floored at $c$.
%     - The two lift formulas differ by $(c - p_i)$.
%     - The per-molecule lift adds the Mills-ratio term to $c$; the textbook conditional mean adds it to $p_i$. Both are kept.
%   - Drop clipped: remove clipped molecules from training.
%   - Residual winsorising, applied to every training label, not only clipped ones: $\tilde{y}_i = p_i + \operatorname{clip}(y_i - p_i,\,-k\hat\sigma,\,k\hat\sigma)$.
%     - $\hat\sigma = 1.4826\,\operatorname{median}_i|y_i - p_i|$; $k \in \{1.0, 1.25, 1.5\}$.
%     - A label within $k\hat\sigma$ of its prediction is not moved.
% - Truncated normal mean: $E[Y \mid Y > c] = \mu + \sigma\,\phi(\alpha)/(1-\Phi(\alpha))$, with $\alpha=(c-\mu)/\sigma$.
% - The censored (Tobit) regression model is built on this \citep{Tobin1958}.
% - The constant 1.4826 makes the median absolute deviation a consistent estimate of the standard deviation under a normal distribution \citep{Rousseeuw1993}.
% - Model changes instead of label changes. These are comparators, not proposals: the thesis changes the input, not the model.
%   - Censored likelihood: gradient-boosted trees with an accelerated-failure-time objective and normal errors \citep{Barnwal2022}.
%     - A clipped molecule enters as the interval $[c, +\infty)$; others as exact values.
%     - Likelihood (Tobit form \citep{Tobin1958}): $\prod_{i\notin C}\frac{1}{s}\phi\!\left(\frac{y_i-f(x_i)}{s}\right)\prod_{i\in C}\left[1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{c-f(x_i)}{s}\right)\right]$.
%     - Labels enter as $t_i = \exp(y_i - \bar{y})$, with $\bar{y}$ a centring constant. The objective models $\log t$, so the normal applies to the label itself.
%     - Paired against plain boosted trees on clipped labels, and against plain boosted trees on the constant lift.
%   - Random forest with absolute-error splits, paired against the ordinary random forest.
%   - Gradient boosting with Huber loss \citep{Huber1964}: $L_\delta(r) = \tfrac12 r^2$ if $|r|\le\delta$, else $\delta(|r| - \tfrac12\delta)$. Paired against ordinary gradient boosting.
% - Feature spaces: each model's two best single representations, and the hybrid.
%   - The hybrid is rebuilt in each fold from the clipped training labels.
%   - All treatments share that one hybrid, so no label treatment reaches the feature choice.
% - Score: recovery rate (same idea as in the existing prose), $R = \frac{m_{\text{treated}} - m_{\text{clipped}}}{m_{\text{clean}} - m_{\text{clipped}}}$ for a metric $m$ where higher is better; numerator and denominator sign-flipped for RMSE.
%   - Not computed when $|m_{\text{clean}} - m_{\text{clipped}}|$ is under 2% of the clean score: the gap is too small to divide by.
%   - For a model change, the denominator is the replaced model's own clean-to-clipped gap.
% - Why it matters: shows what is possible when the noise type and the affected molecules are known.
% - Why it matters: the contrast with Gaussian noise (every label wrong by an unknown amount, in an unknown direction) is the chapter's central point.
% - TODO(results): recovery rate per treatment, per model, per feature space, per censoring level; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: panel 1, true label (x) vs recorded label (y), showing the pile-up at $c$, with arrows for constant lift, per-molecule lift and truncated-normal mean. Panel 2, recovery rate per treatment (x), coloured by feature space.
%
% Method set 2: learning with noisy labels, twelve methods
% - Question: does changing the input from a single representation to the hybrid change which LNL methods work, and how well?
% - This is a relative question, not a claim of absolute robustness.
% - Noise injected into training labels only; validation and test labels clean.
% - Default noise condition: Gaussian. The other conditions from the "Noise conditions" section run the same way.
% - Base network for every method: fully connected, hidden layers 256-128-64, ReLU, dropout 0.2, Adam with learning rate $10^{-3}$, batch 256, up to 200 epochs, early stopping patience 20.
% - Regression: z-scored labels, MSE loss. Classification: cross-entropy.
% - Six conditions: {best single representations, hybrid, importance-weighted hybrid} x {plain network, network + each LNL method}.
% - The single representations are the network's own two best, ranked by the network's score in the hybrid search.
% - The hybrid is re-searched in every fold and noise level on the noisy training labels, so feature choice never sees clean labels.
%   - Reduced budget grid: $B \in \{50, 100, 250\}$ features.
%   - The search scores candidates with the same neural network every condition is trained with.
% - Importance-weighted hybrid: feature $j$ scaled by $\sqrt{w_j}$, then all scale factors divided by their mean: $x'_j = x_j \sqrt{w_j} \big/ \tfrac{1}{d}\sum_{l}\sqrt{w_l}$, $d$ = number of hybrid features.
%   - Mean-one scaling keeps the inputs at roughly unit scale. Weights that sum to one would shrink every feature towards zero.
%   - Same weighting idea as "Feature importance weighting" in "Similarity in a hybrid".
% - Methods, grouped (one table, one line per method):
%   - Filter, then retrain:
%     - Small loss: warm up 10 epochs, keep the 70% of molecules with the smallest loss, retrain \citep{Han2018}.
%     - Confident learning, adapted to regression: an ensemble of 3 networks. A molecule is flagged if its error is above the 85th percentile AND the ensemble spread is below the 25th percentile ("confidently wrong"). Flagged molecules removed, then retrain \citep{northcutt2021confident}.
%     - Neighbour-based selection: $d_i = |y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_{10}(i)}| / \operatorname{sd}(y_{\mathcal{N}_{10}(i)})$, over the 10 nearest neighbours in feature space. Top 15% removed, then retrain.
%     - Neighbour-based selection is the only one of the twelve that uses similarity in feature space. It is the direct link to Chapter 4.
%   - Change the training loop, simple:
%     - Multistage cleaning: 3 stages; each stage removes the 10% of molecules with the highest loss \citep{zhao2025}.
%     - Early-learning reweighting: every epoch, each molecule with loss above the median has its weight multiplied by $\gamma = 0.95$. Weights floored at 0.01 \citep{zhang2025}.
%     - TODO(lit): confirm zhang2025 (a meta-learning reweighting paper) is the right source for this per-epoch weight-decay rule, or find the original.
%     - Uncertainty curriculum: after 20 warm-up epochs, train on the most confident 25%, then 50%, 75%, 100% of molecules \citep{bengio2009curriculum}.
%   - Change the training loop, complex:
%     - DivideMix, adapted to regression: two networks, 10 warm-up epochs. Each epoch, a two-component Gaussian mixture is fitted to $\log(\text{loss})$. The low-loss component counts as "clean". Each network trains on the other network's clean set \citep{li2020}.
%     - Meta-Weight-Net: a small network maps a molecule's loss to its weight. Updated every 5 epochs to reduce loss on a held-out split \citep{shu2019metaweightnet}.
%     - MentorNet: a mentor network takes the current loss, the last 5 losses and the distance to the nearest neighbours, and outputs a training weight \citep{jiang2018mentornet}.
%     - Contrast-to-divide style: 5 small Gaussian input perturbations ($\sigma = 0.01$). High loss with low prediction variance under perturbation = likely noisy label, since the model confidently disagrees \citep{zheltonozhskii2021contrast}.
%     - The original contrast-to-divide uses self-supervised pre-training; this version is a looser adaptation.
%   - Model the noise directly (regression only):
%     - Heteroscedastic GP: GP mean fitted on up to 1,000 molecules, plus a network predicting per-molecule noise variance \citep{liu2020}.
%     - Deep evidential regression: the network outputs Normal-Inverse-Gamma parameters $(\mu, v, \alpha, \beta)$ \citep{amini2020deep}.
%       - Loss $= \mathcal{L}^{\text{NLL}} + \lambda\,|y-\mu|(2v+\alpha)$, with $\lambda = 0.01$.
%       - $\mathcal{L}^{\text{NLL}} = \tfrac12\log\frac{\pi}{v} - \alpha\log\Omega + (\alpha+\tfrac12)\log\big(v(y-\mu)^2+\Omega\big) + \log\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\frac12)}$, with $\Omega = 2\beta(1+v)$.
% - Most of these methods were designed for image classification with flipped class labels \citep{Song2022}.
% - Here they are adapted to continuous labels: MSE in place of cross-entropy, loss-based splits in place of label agreement.
% - Why it matters: image datasets are large, their labels are discrete, and a wrong label is fully wrong.
% - Bioactivity data are small and continuous, and every label is a little wrong.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature applying image-domain LNL methods (co-teaching, DivideMix) to molecular property regression.
% - Reported per noise level: $\Delta = m_{\text{noisy}} - m_{\text{clean}}$, per method and feature space.
% - TODO(results): change in each method's score, and in the ranking of methods, from single representation to hybrid; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: grid with rows = the 12 methods (grouped as above), columns = feature space, cell = score at one noise level. Alternative: lines of score vs noise level, one per method, faceted by feature space.
%
% Method set 3: cleaning real labels, with nothing injected
% - Question: does a cleanup improve a model trained on the labels as they come?
% - No ground truth about which labels are wrong is needed.
% - Protocol per fold:
%   - fit a model on the uncleaned training labels and score it on the held-out labels;
%   - apply the cleanup to the training labels only;
%   - fit a fresh model of the same kind and score it again.
% - Held-out labels keep their own assay error. That makes a win harder, not easier.
% - Headline: the number of folds where the cleaned model beats the uncleaned one, out of the folds run. Mean difference and spread across folds reported beside it.
% - A win shows the cleanup helped the model. It does not show the cleanup found errors: it may have removed molecules that were only hard to predict.
% - Control dataset: QM9. Its labels come from a quantum-chemical calculation, with no experimental error.
% - A "gain" on QM9 means the cleanup removed hard molecules, not errors.
% - Suspicion scores (which molecules to act on) use two kinds of evidence:
%   - Neighbour residual: $\frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_{10}(i)}|}{\sigma_y}$ (formula already in prose). Neighbours drawn from other scaffold groups only.
%   - Cross-validation residual: $|y_i - p_i|$, with $p_i$ from a model trained on other molecules, using scaffold-disjoint inner folds.
% - Share of molecules flagged, swept over 1%, 5% and 20%.
%   - 1% is meant to sit near the share of compounds whose repeat measurements disagree by more than 1 log unit.
%   - 20% is deliberately aggressive.
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on the fraction of ChEMBL compounds whose repeat measurements differ by more than 1 log unit.
%   - Related known figure: public Ki data carry an estimated standard deviation of 0.54 pKi units between independent measurements \citep{Kramer2012}.
% - Cleanups ($\hat{s}_i$ = suspicion score min-max scaled to $[0,1]$):
%   - Remove: drop the flagged molecules. Uses the flag share.
%   - Downweight: $w_i = 1/(1 + \beta\hat{s}_i)$, normalised to mean 1.
%     - $\beta \in \{1, 3, 9\}$, a weight ratio of 2:1, 4:1 and 10:1 between the least and most suspect molecule.
%   - Neighbour smoothing: $\tilde{y}_i = y_i + \alpha\hat{s}_i(\bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}(i)} - y_i)$, with $\alpha \in \{0.25, 0.5, 1\}$.
%   - Neighbour replacement: flagged labels replaced by their neighbours' mean. Uses the flag share.
%   - Model-prediction smoothing: the same formula as neighbour smoothing, with target = out-of-fold boosted-tree prediction $p_i$. Same $\alpha$ values.
%   - GP smoothing: the same formula, with target = GP posterior mean \citep{Rasmussen2006}. Same $\alpha$ values.
%   - Quantile clipping: each molecule gets the 10th-90th percentile range of labels from a quantile regression forest \citep{Meinshausen2006}.
%     - Labels inside that range are untouched; labels outside are moved to the nearer edge.
%   - Model-prediction smoothing, GP smoothing and quantile clipping are regression only.
% - On regression data only remove and neighbour replacement use the flag share.
%   - Downweighting and the three smoothers act on every molecule in proportion to $\hat{s}_i$, so they sweep their strength ($\beta$ or $\alpha$) instead.
%   - Quantile clipping has no setting, so it runs once.
% - Shuffled-score control: every cleanup is rerun with the suspicion scores dealt to molecules at random.
%   - Same number of molecules removed; same set of weights and label shifts.
%   - Separates "which molecules" from "how many".
% - Feature spaces: each model's two best single representations, the hybrid, and the similarity hybrid (from "Similarity in a hybrid").
%   - Each is built once per fold from the uncleaned training labels.
%   - The detector is rerun in each space, so different spaces flag different molecules.
% - Why it matters: this is the practical test a user can run on their own data.
% - Why it matters: it connects noise detection (which depends on similarity, Chapter 4) to the question of whether cleaning helps at all.
% - TODO(results): folds won out of folds run, per cleanup, setting, suspicion score and feature space; QM9 reported separately; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: flow chart of the protocol. Main path: train on raw labels -> score; score molecules -> clean training labels -> retrain -> score; compare per fold. Side branch: the shuffled-score control.
%
% Links to other chapters:
% - Links to: "Results: noise mitigation by noise type" in this chapter uses all three method sets.
% - Gaussian noise is expected to be hardest: every label is wrong by an unknown amount, and the neighbours used to judge it are noisy too.
% - Links to: Discussion sections "Simple is often better" (complex LNL methods vs the plain network) and "No single method fits every dataset".

% [RESTRUCTURE] moved from old Sec 4.1.
% [CR 790] the pivot from sample removal to weighting and smoothing.
In addition to evaluating hybrid representation quality for distance-based reasoning, our investigation evolved to explore alternative noise handling strategies. Initial experiments with sample removal methods revealed limitations when applied to Gaussian noise affecting all samples. This motivated a pivot toward sample weighting and label smoothing approaches, which do not require accurate binary classification of noisy versus clean samples but instead leverage continuous noise magnitude estimates.

\subsubsection{Sample removal methods}

% [CR 849-861] why removal was abandoned for weighting/smoothing; kNN filtering; LOF.
Initially, I attempted to adapt noise mitigation techniques that focused on removing noisy samples. However given that in these experiments, I'm adding noise to all of the data, this task because not just difficult but impractical as all samples are noisy, it's more about understanding the magnitude of noise. Therefore, I shifted my to sample weighting and label smoothing which I believed would be better suited for handling Gaussian noise affecting all samples.

Distance-based approaches rely on the assumption that similar molecules have similar properties \citep{Cherkasov2014}. Presumably, noisy data would break this pattern, making it a method for detecting anomalous data. k-Nearest neighbor filtering examines the variance of a sample's neighborhood. For sample $x_i$ with $k$ nearest neighbors $\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)$, we compute the standard deviation of neighbor labels:
$$
\sigma_i = \text{std}(\{y_j : x_j \in \mathcal{N}_k(x_i)\})
$$
Samples with $\sigma_i$ exceeding the 90th percentile are marked as potentially noisy. We test $k \in \{5, 10, 20\}$.

Local Outlier Factor (LOF) identifies anomalous samples by detecting local density estimates between a sample and its neighbors \citep{Breunig2000}. These density estimates are based on the distance to its $k$ nearest neighbors. If a point's density differs from that of its neighbors such that the LOF is substantially greater than 2, it is flagged as an outlier.

We also tested the following noise removal methods:  distance-weighted prediction filtering, neighborhood consensus filtering, AC detection, Mahalanobis outlier detection, cross-validation disagreement, bootstrap ensemble filtering, co-teaching \citep{Han2018}, and DivideMix \citep{li2020}.

\subsubsection{Noise magnitude estimation}

% [CR 863-906] neighbour-based, model-based, and uncertainty-based estimators.
% TODO(xref): [CR 906] 'as discussed in Chapter 3' -> now ch.4 (uncertainty).
% TODO: [CR 863] carries the author's own '% TODO: get sources' marker. Left in place.
We now examine the core of the noise mitigation research: estimating the magnitude of noise. This enables proportional handling rather than complete removal. We used the following neighbor-based estimators:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Neighbor residual: Estimates noise as deviation from k-NN mean, normalized by global label standard deviation:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\sigma_y}$$
    
    \item Local variance ratio: Measures deviation relative to local label variance, identifying samples that deviate from neighbors more than neighbors deviate from each other:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\text{std}(y_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}) + \epsilon}$$
    
    \item AC-aware: Distinguishes noise from activity cliffs by weighting deviation by neighborhood consistency. High deviation with high neighbor agreement suggests noise while high deviation with neighbor disagreement suggests an AC \citep{Dablander2023}:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\sigma_y} \cdot \frac{1}{1 + \text{std}(y_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)})/\sigma_y}$$
\end{itemize}

And we used the following model-based estimators using prediction disagreement:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Cross-validation residual: Uses out-of-fold predictions; high residuals indicate labels inconsistent with the model learned from other samples.
    \item Ensemble disagreement: Trains multiple models and combines prediction variance with mean residual.
\end{itemize}

We used probabilistic methods that directly predict noise magnitude using uncertainty estimates:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Gaussian Process: Provides natural uncertainty estimates via posterior variance, with learned noise level $\sigma^2$ from the kernel \citep{gauche}.
    
    \item Heteroscedastic Neural Network: Predicts both $\mu(x)$ and $\sigma^2(x)$ using $\beta$-NLL loss \citep{Seitzer2022}:
    $$\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(y - \mu)^2}{\sigma^2} + \log \sigma^2\right) \cdot \sigma^{2\beta}$$
    where $\beta = 0.5$ prevents the variance from hiding the mean errors.
    
    \item Conformal prediction: Uses split conformal prediction to obtain prediction intervals. Wider intervals should indicate higher uncertainty \citep{Shafer2008}.
    
    \item Deep ensemble: Trains $M$ independent networks and uses the variance of means as epistemic uncertainty plus the mean of variances as aleatoric uncertainty \citep{lakshminarayanan2017}.
    
    \item Bayesian Random Forest: Uses tree prediction variance and out-of-bag residuals as uncertainty estimates \citep{taddy2015}.
    
    \item Quantile regression: Fits models for multiple quantiles and uses interval width as noise estimate.
    
    \item NGBoost: Natural gradient boosting that predicts distributional parameters $(\mu, \sigma)$ directly \citep{Duan2020}.
\end{itemize}

NGBoost in particular was selected due to its promising uncertainty values with respect to the correlation between noise and prediction error, as discussed in Chapter 3.  

\subsubsection{Label smoothing and sample weighting}

% [CR 908-935] proportional/threshold/soft-threshold/confidence smoothing; inverse,
% exponential and soft weighting.
Given the fact that all labels contain some magnitude of noise, we focused strongly on label smoothing over removal using the following methods:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Proportional: $y_{\text{smooth}} = y + \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}} \cdot (\text{target} - y)$
    
    \item Threshold: Only correct samples above the $(1-\alpha)$ percentile of scores.
    
    \item Soft threshold: Applies sigmoid-like correction strength:
    $$\text{strength} = \alpha \cdot \sigma\left(5 \cdot (\text{score}_{\text{norm}} - 0.5)\right)$$
    
    \item Confidence: For methods that output raw uncertainty $\sigma$ (e.g., NGBoost):
    $$\text{correction} = \frac{\alpha \cdot \sigma}{1 + \alpha \cdot \sigma}, \quad y_{\text{smooth}} = (1 - \text{correction}) \cdot y + \text{correction} \cdot \mu$$
\end{itemize}


Rather than directly modifying labels, we weighted more trustworthy samples during training:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Inverse: $w = \frac{1}{1 + \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}}}$
    
    \item Exponential: $w = \exp(-\alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}})$
    
    \item Soft: $w = 1 - \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}}^2$, clipped to $[0.1, 1.0]$
\end{itemize}

All weights are normalized to mean 1 to preserve effective sample size.

\subsubsection{Recovery rate}

% [CR 972-982] the evaluation protocol and the recovery-rate definition.
For noise detection accuracy, we compute precision, recall, and F1 score, treating noise injection as ground truth.

We have a standard RF on ECFP4 train and test on the clean dataset, producing $R^2_{\text{clean}}$. Noise is then added to the clean data, RF on ECFP4 is then freshly trained and tested on this noisy data, producing $R^2_{\text{noisy}}$. Using the given estimation method and representation to test, noise estimation and label smoothing is applied to the noisy data, resulting in the denoised set. RF on ECFP4 is yet again run, this time on the denoised data, to produce $R^2_{\text{denoised}}$. From these performance values, we derive recovery rate, or the fraction of performance loss recovered after denoising, as follows: 

$$
\text{Recovery} = \frac{R^2_{\text{denoised}} - R^2_{\text{noisy}}}{R^2_{\text{clean}} - R^2_{\text{noisy}}}
$$

A recovery rate of 1.0 indicates complete restoration to clean performance; negative values indicate the denoising procedure made performance worse than the noisy baseline.

% Limits:
% - Most noise here is injected. Injected noise has a known form; real noise is mixed and of unknown form.
% - In the noise experiments, each fold ranks features and splits the budget using that fold's noisy training labels, so feature choice sees the noise.
% - The search settings (allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, budget) come from searches on clean labels, which is a head start the hybrid gets and a real user would not.
% - A clean-label feature space is kept as a comparison, so the size of that head start can be read off rather than assumed.
% - Similarity results cover the six small-molecule datasets only.
% - Each noise experiment compares the hybrid against each downstream model's own top two single representations, not against a fixed weak baseline.
% - No significance tests; per-fold scores and fold spread only.
% - Compute: the feature ranking and budget split are repeated per fold and per noise draw, which multiplies the cost of every noise experiment.
% Bridge:
% - Links to: Chapter 7 "Handling noise realistically" (model the noise before choosing a method).
% - Links to: Chapter 7 "No single method fits every dataset" (which method helps depends on noise type, amount, dataset and space).
% - Links to: Chapter 7 "Simple is often better" (plain models and simple corrections against complex learning-with-noisy-labels methods).
% - Links to: Chapter 3, where the best representation changed with the noise condition; this chapter asks the same question with a representation built for the task.
% DIAGRAM: one panel per noise type (Gaussian, outlier, grouped offset, censoring); x-axis true label, y-axis observed label; shade the part that can be detected and the part that can be corrected. Could be shared with the by-noise-type section instead of repeated here.
% ---- END NOTES 6.6 ----

\section{Summary}
% OUTLINE 7.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.

% Purpose of this section:

% - DIAGRAM: one chain of boxes across the thesis. Chapter 3 (the model sets robustness; the representation shares in setting clean accuracy) -> Chapter 4 (standard representations, and the distances computed from them, are flawed) -> Chapter 5 (build a representation chosen for the task: the hybrid) -> Chapter 6 (does that representation repair similarity and noise handling?) -> Chapter 7 (no single method fits; simple is often better). Label each arrow with the question it passes on.
%
% The thread in one line:
% - The thesis started with label noise. It found that the representation and the model matter in different ways. It found that methods which compare molecules to each other fail on standard representations. It then built a way to choose features for the task.

% - Found: model robustness rankings barely change across noise types (Kendall's $W = 0.92$, already in prose).
