\section{Handling noise realistically}
% OUTLINE 7.2: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 7.2 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: noise sources and published evidence for non-Gaussian, grouped and censored noise are in Chapter 2 (Noise in molecular data); do not repeat the assay-level sources here.
% Main message:
% - Handling noise realistically = first ask where the noise comes from, then pick a method that fits that source.
% - The default assumption in most noise studies: independent, zero-mean Gaussian error on every label, $\tilde{y}_i = y_i + \epsilon_i$, $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \tau^2)$.
% - Real assay data breaks each part of that assumption.
%   - Shape: heavy tails.
%   - Independence: shared offsets per laboratory or per scaffold family.
%   - Zero mean: censoring at detection limits.
% - Why it matters: a method chosen for the wrong noise model can make the data worse, not better.
% - Links to: Chapter 2 (noise model and its sources), Chapter 6 (hybrid results by noise type), Section "No single method fits every dataset".
% Noise types as models (write each as a generating process):
% - Gaussian: every label shifted, size and sign unknown; $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\tau^2)$.
% - Heavy-tailed: same targeting as Gaussian, but $\epsilon_i$ drawn from a Student-t ($\nu = 10, 5, 3$) or a Laplace distribution.
% - Outliers (Huber contamination): most labels near-clean, a fraction $p$ badly wrong; $\epsilon_i \sim (1-p)\,\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2) + p\,\mathcal{N}(0,(\lambda\sigma)^2)$ \citep{huber1964robust}.
%   - Values used in this thesis: $\lambda = 3$, $p \in \{0.01, 0.05, 0.10\}$.
%   - A second version leaves the other $1-p$ labels untouched: $\epsilon_i = 0$ unless selected.
%   - Why both: the second version tells apart "a detector cannot find the corrupted labels" from "finding them does not help", because removing exactly the selected labels removes all the injected error.
% - Grouped, shifted: one shared shift per scaffold family $g$; $\epsilon_i = b_{g(i)} + w_i$, $\mathrm{Var}(b) = \rho\tau^2$, $\mathrm{Var}(w) = (1-\rho)\tau^2$, with $\rho = 0.62$.
% - Grouped, wider: whole scaffold families chosen until about 20% of molecules are affected; those molecules get error scale $\lambda = 3$ times larger, still centred on the true value.
% - In the injected conditions the groups are Murcko scaffold families; in real pooled data the matching group is often the laboratory or source document.
% - Censoring: labels past a limit $c$ replaced by the limit; $\tilde{y}_i = \min(y_i, c)$ (upper limit used here; $\max$ for a lower limit).
%   - Its level is the fraction of labels clipped, not a noise size; it is not zero-mean.
% - All other conditions are matched on root-mean-square noise, $\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_i \epsilon_i^2}$, so differences between them are differences of shape, not of amount.
% - TODO(lit): find the exact sources behind the parameter values: $\lambda = 3$ (between-lab vs within-lab error, Avdeef 2019), $p$ = 1-10% gross errors in routine data (Hampel 2001), $\rho = 0.62$ (group-level share of variance, Bentz et al. 2013, Table 7); the choice of 20% affected molecules has no published source and should be stated as a choice.
% - Pooling measurements from different sources adds noise on top of any single assay's error \citep{Kramer2012, landrum2024}.
% - DIAGRAM: four panels (Gaussian, outlier, grouped offset, censoring); x-axis true label, y-axis observed label; shade the part of the error that can be detected and the part that can be corrected. Keep it a one-row summary so it does not duplicate the by-noise-type figure in Chapter 6.
% Why Gaussian noise is the hardest case:
% - Every label is wrong by an unknown amount in an unknown direction; there is no clean subset to learn from.
% - A neighbour-based estimate of the true value uses neighbours' labels, which carry the same noise.
%   - Neighbour mean of $k$ neighbours: $\bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}} = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j \in \mathcal{N}(i)} \tilde{y}_j$.
%   - Its error variance: $v = \tau^2/k + \sigma^2_{\text{struct}}$, where $\sigma^2_{\text{struct}}$ is the real activity difference between the molecule and its neighbours.
%   - Best linear correction (shrinkage): $\hat{y}_i = \frac{v\,\tilde{y}_i + \tau^2\,\bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}}}{v + \tau^2}$.
%   - Correction helps only when $v < \tau^2$, i.e. when neighbours are genuinely similar in activity.
%   - Activity cliffs and a poor similarity measure make $\sigma^2_{\text{struct}}$ large.
% - Even a correctly flagged label has no known correction; the direction is a guess.
% - Outliers are easier: a few points stand far from the rest, so detection is feasible and removal or down-weighting is a reasonable action.
% - Censoring is the most correctable: the location ($c$) and the direction ($y_i \ge c$) are both known.
%   - Expected true value above the limit under a normal model: $\mathbb{E}[y \mid y \ge c] = \mu + \sigma\frac{\phi(a)}{1-\Phi(a)}$, $a = (c-\mu)/\sigma$ (inverse Mills ratio).
%   - Censored (Tobit) likelihood uses clipped labels instead of discarding them: $L = \prod_{i \notin C} \frac{1}{\sigma}\phi\!\left(\frac{\tilde{y}_i-\mu_i}{\sigma}\right) \prod_{i \in C} \left[1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{c-\mu_i}{\sigma}\right)\right]$ \citep{Tobin1958}.
%   - Censored labels in drug-discovery assays carry usable information when modelled with a Tobit likelihood \citep{Svensson2025}.
%   - Common curation keeps only exact measurements (the "=" relation) and throws censored records away.
%   - The ChEMBL-derived datasets in this thesis were curated the same way; state this as a limitation of the data, not only of other studies.
% - Paradox to state plainly: noise can sometimes be detected or estimated, yet removing it still fails.
% - Better similarity does not fix this; the missing piece is the direction of the error, not the neighbour search.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (the exploratory learning-with-noisy-labels study: distance-based methods were the worst performers; neighbour-based estimation stayed poor under Gaussian noise regardless of representation).
% - TODO(results): per noise type, whether correction beat leaving labels as they are (censoring vs Gaussian vs outlier vs grouped offset); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): cleaning real, un-injected labels (small molecules and antibodies) against no cleaning; numbers come from the full runs.
% Recommendation: learning with noisy labels over direct cleaning:
% - Direct cleaning is two steps: estimate each label's error $\hat{\epsilon}_i$, then change the label, $y_i^{\text{clean}} = \tilde{y}_i - \hat{\epsilon}_i$ (or remove it).
% - Any error in step one is fixed into the data before training starts.
% - Learning with noisy labels (LNL) changes how the model trains instead of changing the data: loss, sample weights, sample selection, regularisation \citep{Song2022}.
% - Example, robust loss (Huber): $\ell_\delta(r) = \tfrac{1}{2}r^2$ if $|r| \le \delta$, else $\delta(|r| - \tfrac{1}{2}\delta)$ \citep{huber1964robust}.
%   - Large residuals get linear, not quadratic, weight.
%   - Robust losses under label noise for deep networks: \citep{Ghosh2017} (classification setting).
% - Example, heteroscedastic likelihood: $\mathcal{L}_i = \tfrac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(\tilde{y}_i-\mu(\mathbf{x}_i))^2}{\sigma^2(\mathbf{x}_i)} + \log\sigma^2(\mathbf{x}_i)\right)$.
%   - A noisy sample can be absorbed by a larger $\sigma^2(\mathbf{x}_i)$ instead of pulling $\mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$.
% - Small-loss selection relies on networks fitting clean patterns before memorising noise \citep{arpit2017closer}.
% - Networks can fit fully random labels given enough training \citep{Zhang2017}.
% - Why prefer LNL: the model re-weights samples throughout training using all the data, instead of one fixed guess per sample made beforehand.
% - Cost of LNL: extra training time and extra hyperparameters.
% - Cost of cleaning: paid once, and the cleaned data work with any model. State this trade-off.
% - Exceptions where direct correction is justified: known direction (censoring), or a clearly separate subset (gross outliers, systematic offsets that can be tied to a source).
% - No single algorithm was best at keeping performance under noise across datasets and noise levels \citep{Cortes2015}; ties to Section "No single method fits every dataset".
% - TODO(results): whether LNL methods beat a plain network at low vs high noise, and whether the input (hybrid vs single representation) moved the score more than the method; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature comparing label-cleaning pipelines against noise-robust training on the same regression data (not image classification).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on why LNL methods developed on image classification (label flips, class structure) transfer poorly to continuous-valued assay regression.
% Uncertainty as a partner to LNL:
% - Two levels of the same question: does uncertainty track noise per molecule, and does it track the dataset's overall noise level?
% - Per molecule: $\rho_{\text{Spearman}}(u_i, |\epsilon_i|)$ between predicted uncertainty $u_i$ and injected noise magnitude.
%   - Chapter 3: strongest for GP and NGBoost with fingerprints or descriptors; near zero or negative for the learned embeddings.
% - Population level: mean predicted uncertainty $\bar{u} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i u_i$ rises with the noise level $\tau$ (Chapter 3; \citep{Kolmar2021} for GPs).
% - Implication: use uncertainty to estimate how noisy a dataset is, not to pick out which labels are wrong.
% - A GP's learned observation noise $\sigma_n^2$ in $k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}') + \sigma_n^2\,\delta_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}'}$ is one dataset-level noise estimate.
%   - It could set how strongly an LNL method down-weights samples (e.g. the Huber $\delta$ or a smoothing strength). This is a proposal, not something tested.
% - Noise sets a ceiling on achievable accuracy; a dataset-level noise estimate tells you when a model is already near it \citep{Kolmar2021, Crusius2024}.
% - Contrast to state: in Chapter 3, model robustness rankings barely changed across noise types; which mitigation works depends strongly on noise type.
%   - So choosing a model is close to noise-type-agnostic; choosing a fix is not.
% - Links to: Chapter 3 (Uncertainty under label noise), Section "Limitations" (per-sample tracking is weak).
% Practical guidance (turn into a short list or table in the text):
% - Question 1: Are the data pooled from many laboratories or documents? Expect grouped offsets; check for source metadata (assay, document, lab).
% - Question 2: Do the data span very different chemical series? Neighbour-based detection is least reliable across series.
% - Question 3: Is it one controlled assay with a detection limit? Expect censoring; keep censored records and use a censored likelihood or a known-direction correction.
% - Question 4: No metadata and unknown noise level? Default to LNL with a robust loss or a heteroscedastic model; estimate dataset-level noise from uncertainty; avoid per-sample relabelling.
% - DIAGRAM: decision flowchart from "what do you know about the data source" to "suggested handling" (censoring correction / outlier removal / group offset modelling / LNL default).
% - Why it matters: gives a reader a procedure, not a single method; fits the thesis theme of testing options per dataset.
% - Links to: Section "Future directions" (classification and label flipping, where noise is discrete and different methods apply).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on estimating or correcting per-laboratory (per-source) offsets in pooled bioactivity data, e.g. mixed-effects or random-effects models for ChEMBL assays.
% ---- END NOTES 7.2 ----


\section{No single method fits every dataset}
% OUTLINE 7.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 7.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section argues:
% - No setting of the method is best on every dataset. The settings are the allocation method, the number of base representations, the importance method, the internal evaluator, the downstream model and the budget.
% - The same holds for noise handling. Which noisy-label learning method or mitigation method helps depends on the data.
% - So the contribution is not one fixed recipe. It is a procedure that tries the candidate settings on each task and keeps the one that scores best inside the training data.
% - Framing from the brain dump: admit that part of the answer comes down to the data. The problems are too varied for one architecture or training method tuned to one problem.
%
% What the choice depends on (one factor per bullet):
% - The type of noise: Gaussian, outlier, grouped-shifted or censored (Chapter 3; Chapter 6, "By noise type").
% - The amount of noise: the fraction of labels affected and the size of each error.
% - The number of samples $n$. A small $n$ gives a noisy internal score, so the chosen setting is less stable.
% - Whether any feature describes the property. If no base representation carries the relevant information, no allocation can create it.
% - The metric the task is scored on (RMSE, Spearman $\rho$, AUROC, weighted F1). The winner can change with the metric.
% - The downstream model. The best hybrid for a tree ensemble need not be the best hybrid for a neural network.
%   - Already in prose (Future directions): neural networks did well with small hybrids and tree methods with larger ones.
%
% No free lunch:
% - Supervised learning version: averaged uniformly over all possible target functions, any two learning algorithms have the same expected off-training-set error \citep{Wolpert1996}.
% - Search and optimisation version: averaged over all objective functions, all search algorithms perform equally \citep{Wolpert1997}.
% - Implication: one algorithm can only beat another on a restricted class of problems. Choosing that class is an assumption about the data.
% - Caveat for the prose: no free lunch is about all possible problems. Real chemical datasets are not drawn uniformly from all functions. The claim here is weaker: there is no a priori reason to expect one setting to transfer from one assay to another.
% - Chemistry evidence: 12 machine-learning algorithms were compared on 12 bioactivity datasets with simulated experimental error added; how the algorithms compared depended on the dataset and the noise level \citep{Cortes2015}.
%   - Check the exact wording of their conclusion before writing it.
% - Representation evidence: a systematic study of molecular property prediction compared many representations and models across datasets; which one did best depended on the dataset \citep{Deng2023}.
%   - Check the exact finding in the paper before writing it.
%   - TODO(lit): search for benchmark studies showing that the best molecular representation (fingerprint, descriptor, graph embedding, language-model embedding) changes from one property-prediction dataset to another.
% - Noisy-label methods: the survey groups deep-learning methods into five families: robust architecture, robust regularisation, robust loss function, loss adjustment, sample selection \citep{Song2022}.
%   - TODO(lit): search for benchmark comparisons of noisy-label learning methods showing the ranking of methods changes with noise type and noise rate.
%
% Where the thesis shows it (collected here; per-axis evidence lives in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6):
% - Chapter 3: the best representation and model pair changed with the noise condition and the dataset.
%   - Already in prose: the published NoiseInject results.
%   - Some pairs stood out on one dataset but did not hold on others.
% - Allocation method (Chapter 5).
%   - TODO(results): per dataset, the share of (downstream model, budget) combinations won by each allocation method; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Number of base representations (Chapter 5). The search tries 2, 3, 5 or all of them.
%   - TODO(results): the winning number of base representations per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Importance method (Chapter 5). The candidates are gradient-boosting gain, TreeSHAP and DeepLIFT.
%   - TODO(results): the winning importance method per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Internal evaluator (Chapter 5). This is the model used inside the search to score candidate hybrids: the downstream model itself, a random forest, gradient boosting, or a neural network.
%   - TODO(results): the internal evaluator that won most often, per dataset and per downstream model; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Budget and downstream model (Chapter 5).
%   - TODO(results): the winning budget and downstream model per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
%   - TODO(results): how often the winning hybrid used fewer features than the largest budget; numbers come from the full runs. Links to "Simple is often better".
% - Two ways of allocating (Chapter 5): a fixed quota of features per base representation, against one pooled ranking of candidate features from all representations.
%   - TODO(results): per dataset, which of the two won; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Noise mitigation (Chapter 6): which method helped depended on the noise type.
%   - TODO(results): the methods that helped under each noise type and the ones that did nothing; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Applied settings (antibodies; the multi-site setting where each owner keeps their data).
%   - TODO(results): whether the preferred settings differ from the small-molecule datasets; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: grid with datasets as rows and search settings as columns (allocation method, number of base representations, importance method, internal evaluator, downstream model, budget). Each cell is coloured by the winning option. The point is that no column is a single colour.
%
% What the method does about it:
% - The search treats each setting as something to choose per task, not something fixed in advance.
% - For each candidate configuration $c$ (allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, number of base representations, budget $B$), build $\mathbf{H}_c = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ with $|S_k| = b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k \le B$.
%   - The budget is a maximum, not a target. A hybrid that scores better with fewer features is kept.
% - Pick the configuration with the best score inside the training data: $c^* = \arg\max_c \; \mathrm{score}_{\text{internal}}(\mathbf{H}_c, \mathbf{y}_{\text{train}})$.
%   - Report it only on held-out folds. Folds are scaffold-group-aware; no random splits.
% - This is the same idea as automated machine learning, where the choice of algorithm and its hyperparameters is one search problem solved per dataset \citep{Thornton2013}.
%   - The difference: the hybrid search chooses the input features, not the model.
%   - TODO(lit): search for automated feature construction and feature selection inside AutoML pipelines, compared against a fixed feature set.
% - Adding a new representation (for example a new foundation-model embedding) needs no redesign. Add its matrix to the set and run the search again.
%   - Already in prose: the "what this means in practice" paragraph in Future directions. Do not repeat it here.
% - Cost: sweeping every axis multiplies the number of model fits. Stated in Limitations; a faster search is in Future directions.
% - Risk: choosing among many configurations on the same internal data can overfit that data. The risk grows as $n$ shrinks.
%   - TODO(lit): search for selection bias when many model or feature configurations are compared on the same cross-validation folds (overfitting in model selection).
%
% Why it matters:
% - For a practitioner: there is no default to trust. The useful question is which settings to try and how to compare them fairly.
% - For benchmark papers: a method that wins on one dataset is weak evidence that it will win on the next. Results should be reported per dataset, with the spread across folds.
% - For noise: the right response depends on knowing the noise type. Links back to "Handling noise realistically".
%
% Links to:
% - "Handling noise realistically": characterise the noise before choosing a method.
% - "Simple is often better": the per-dataset winner is often the simpler option. Neither complex nor simple settings transfer reliably between tasks.
% - Chapter 5, "Why each axis is swept", holds the per-axis evidence. This section only collects it.
% - Chapter 3 results and Chapter 6, "By noise type".
% - "Limitations": the cost of the search.
%
% Keep out:
% - No significance tests, and no claim that one setting is significantly better. Per-fold scores and spread only.
% - Do not average across datasets to name an overall winner. That would contradict this section's argument.
% ---- END NOTES 7.3 ----


\section{Simple is often better}
% OUTLINE 7.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 7.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section argues:
% - Main claim: across the thesis, the simpler choice often did as well as the complex one, or better. This holds for the model, the number of features, the neighbour method and the noise handling.
% - It is a synthesis section. It points back to Chapters 3 to 6 and adds no new results.
% - Why it matters: it tells a practitioner where to start. Start with a simple model on a small, relevant feature set, then try anything more elaborate.
% - Parsimony, the general principle: do not use more terms or a more complicated method than the data need. Breaking it is one definition of overfitting \citep{Hawkins2004}.
% - Links to: "No single method fits every dataset" (7.3). The two sections pull against each other, so write them as a pair (see the last group below).
%
% Simple models under label noise (Chapter 3):
% - Already in prose (Chapter 3, model-choice paragraph): tree-based ensembles fill the top 10 model-representation configurations on QM9, ranked by the Gaussian noise degradation slope. NGBoost appears five times, XGBoost and RF twice each and LightGBM once. No neural network appears.
% - NGBoost counts as a tree ensemble here. In Chapter 3 its base learner is a depth-3 regression tree, which is the library default and the setting in the original paper \citep{Duan2020}.
% - Chapter 3's conclusions name NGBoost and SVM as the most noise-robust models. So word the claim as "tree ensembles and SVM, not plain neural networks". Do not write "RF was the most robust".
% - Caveats from Chapter 3, one per bullet:
%   - RF is robust only with some representations.
%   - QRF is less robust than RF.
%   - XGBoost degrades on the three assay datasets.
%   - Full BNNs stay robust across all representations. So "neural networks are not robust" is too strong. Say "plain neural networks varied widely".
% - For plain neural networks, the main problem is high variance across representations and noise types. A low average is not the main problem.
% - Robust is not the same as accurate. NGBoost and SVM were the most robust models, but they were not the best on clean data (Chapter 3). The "simple" advice is about robustness to noise, not about top clean accuracy.
% - Why tree ensembles resist noise: averaging many decorrelated trees lowers variance. Take $T$ trees, each with variance $\sigma^2$ and pairwise correlation $\rho$. Then $\mathrm{Var}\big(\tfrac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T} f_t(\mathbf{x})\big) = \rho\sigma^2 + \tfrac{1-\rho}{T}\sigma^2$ \citep{Hastie2009, Breiman2001}.
%   - A noisy label moves only the leaves it falls in, and only in the trees whose bootstrap sample contains it. Averaging over the trees dilutes it.
% - Networks can memorise noisy labels. With enough capacity they reach zero training error even on random labels \citep{Zhang2017}. They learn the clean patterns first and the noise later \citep{arpit2017closer}.
% - Outside chemistry: tree-based models still beat deep networks on medium-sized tabular data of about 10k samples \citep{Grinsztajn2022}.
%   - One reason they give is that uninformative features hurt networks more than trees \citep{Grinsztajn2022}.
%   - Molecular fingerprints and descriptors are tabular data of this kind.
% - QSAR precedent: random forests have been a strong default for QSAR since \citet{Svetnik2003}.
%   - Deep networks gave gains over random forests on QSAR data, but the gains were modest \citep{Ma2015}.
%   - A systematic study of molecular property prediction found that learned representations do not reliably beat fixed ones \citep{Deng2023}.
% - Links to: Chapter 3's first conclusion. Robustness is set by how the model is trained (ensembling, margin maximisation, priors), not by how complex the model is.
%
% Fewer features is often better (Chapter 5):
% - The hybrid search sweeps the budget $B$, the total number of features. It does not assume that more features are better.
% - The chosen budget is $B^{*} = \arg\max_{B \in \mathcal{B}} \mathrm{score}(B)$, where $\mathcal{B}$ is the set of budgets tried. Check how often $B^{*}$ is below the largest budget tried, $B_{\max} = \max \mathcal{B}$.
% - TODO(results): how often the best hybrid uses fewer features than the largest budget, per dataset and model; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Why fewer features can win: irrelevant features add variance without adding signal.
%   - Hawkins counts a model that includes irrelevant predictors as one form of overfitting \citep{Hawkins2004}.
% - The hybrid is itself a way to shrink the feature space. It filters and combines in one step: $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ with $|S_k| = b_k$ and $\sum_k b_k = B \le \sum_k d_k$. In practice $B$ is usually much smaller than $\sum_k d_k$.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (most features in a standard representation do not vary on a given dataset).
% - Links to: Chapter 5 (plain concatenation of representations tends to land between its parents; confirm this with the Chapter 5 results before stating it).
% - Different models prefer different budgets. This is expected, since feature importance depends on the model \citep{Dong2020} (already cited in Chapter 5).
%   - TODO(results): which models preferred smaller budgets and which preferred larger ones; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: x-axis = budget $B$ (log scale); y-axis = score in the dataset's own metric; one line per model; the best budget marked on each line; one panel per dataset.
%
% k-nearest neighbours on a small, relevant feature set (Chapter 6):
% - kNN is the simplest model that uses similarity. The plain version is $\hat{y}_i = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j \in N_k(i)} y_j$, where $N_k(i)$ is the set of the $k$ training molecules closest to molecule $i$ in feature space.
% - When the search scores a hybrid by similarity, it uses $k = 5$ and weights each neighbour by inverse distance: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j \in N_5(i)} w_{ij} y_j}{\sum_{j \in N_5(i)} w_{ij}}$, with $w_{ij} = 1/d_{ij}$.
% - Claim to test in Chapter 6: predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours works better in the hybrid space than in the raw representation space.
% - TODO(results): kNN score on hybrid features against raw features, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Mechanism: in high dimensions, distances concentrate.
%   - For many data distributions, the relative contrast $\frac{D_{\max} - D_{\min}}{D_{\min}} \to 0$ as the dimension $d \to \infty$. "Nearest" then loses its meaning \citep{Beyer1999}.
%   - Fewer, task-relevant features make the neighbours meaningful again.
% - The model stays simple. Any gain comes from the input, not from a new architecture.
%   - This matches the thesis's position that the hybrid changes the representation, not the model.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (similarity on standard representations is unreliable) and Chapter 6 (similarity in a hybrid).
%
% Complex noise-handling methods that did not help:
% - Say which kinds of method failed. Leave out method-level detail.
%   - Distance-based methods in the exploratory study of learning with noisy labels. All 20 of the worst configurations used a distance metric (already in Chapter 4 prose).
%   - Noise detection by neighbours (Chapter 4).
%   - The noise-cleaning methods in Chapter 6, by noise type.
%   - TODO(results): how many of the complex noise-handling methods beat the plain model, per noise type; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Most methods for learning with noisy labels were built and tested on image classification with flipped class labels \citep{Song2022, han2018coteaching}.
% - Why they may not carry over to molecular assay data (one reason per bullet):
%   - Image tasks have thousands to millions of samples. Assay datasets often have hundreds to a few thousand molecules.
%   - Image label noise is a discrete class flip. Assay noise is continuous, is present on every label and has an unknown direction.
%   - Real human label noise on images already differs from the synthetic noise used to benchmark these methods \citep{Wei2022}. The gap to assay noise is likely larger still.
%   - Many methods rely on neighbours, or on how easily a sample is learned. Both need a feature space where "close" means "similar activity". Chapter 4 shows that standard molecular representations do not give one.
% - QSAR precedent: Cortes-Ciriano et al. added simulated Gaussian error to 12 bioactivity datasets and measured how RMSE rose for 12 algorithms \citep{Cortes2015}.
%   - TODO: read that paper's results. Cite it for which algorithms were least sensitive only if its text says so.
% - Links to: "Handling noise realistically" (7.2). Keep the details of why mitigation fails there. Here, make only the simple-versus-complex point.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on noisy-label methods from financial time series or tabular finance data applied to regression targets, and on whether anyone has tested them on chemical or biological assay data.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the transfer of learning-with-noisy-labels methods from computer vision to regression on small scientific datasets.
%
% Over-fitting a method to a problem:
% - Point: a method tuned closely to one dataset or one task loses generality. The same risk applies to research methods, not only to model fitting.
% - The tasks in the thesis cover a wide range: binding affinity, ADME endpoints, quantum properties and antibodies. They differ in noise, size and structure.
% - That variety is why learned models are used at all. The patterns are too many for a person to track by hand.
% - Simple methods do not generalise automatically either. Feature selection needs tuning per dataset, which is why the hybrid search sweeps its settings instead of fixing them.
% - The balance: fix the simple, robust parts, such as a tree ensemble or kNN on a small feature set. Search only over the choices that vary by dataset: budget, allocation method, importance method and which representations to include.
% - No free lunch: averaged over all problems, no optimiser beats any other \citep{Wolpert1997}. So a method's advantage on one class of problems says nothing about other classes.
% - The hybrid search can be turned against itself. A heavily optimised feature selection could over-fit the validation folds.
%   - The per-fold scores and their spread reported in Chapter 5 are the check on this.
%   - Links to: Chapter 5 Discussion (over-fitting of the search against the benefit of varied features) and Chapter 5 Limitations.
% - Why it matters: this frames the thesis's contribution as a procedure for testing simple options on each dataset, not as a new complex model.
% - Links to: "No single method fits every dataset" (7.3). The two sections are one argument. 7.3 says the best settings differ by dataset. 7.4 says the settings worth trying are mostly simple ones.
% - DIAGRAM (optional): a 2x2 grid. Rows = model complexity (simple / complex). Columns = feature set (small and relevant / large and raw). Each cell holds the chapters whose evidence falls there. An arrow points to the cell for a simple model on a small feature set.
% ---- END NOTES 7.4 ----


\section{Limitations}
% OUTLINE 7.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 7.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope of this section:
% - Thesis-level limits only. Limits of the search method itself (compute, re-running the search when data, representations or model change, reproducibility, small datasets, frozen embeddings) are in the Chapter 5 Limitations section; point there, do not repeat.
% - Already in prose (Chapter 5 Limitations): small datasets, the ceiling set by frozen embeddings, the engineering cost, the noise floor from \citep{Kramer2012}.
% - Links to: Chapter 3 (noise robustness and uncertainty), Chapter 4 (flaws in similarity), Chapter 6 (noise mitigation with hybrids), Future directions (next section).
% Limitation 1: noise mitigation without ground truth:
% - The thesis set out to mitigate label noise. The author's conclusion: it is a hard problem with no clear general solution.
% - TODO(results): how many mitigation methods improved on the uncorrected baseline, per noise condition; numbers come from the full runs.
% - Every experimental label carries some unknown noise. There is no clean copy of the labels to check a correction against.
% - Observed label: $\tilde{y}_i = y_i + \epsilon_i$. Only $\tilde{y}_i$ is observed; $y_i$ and $\epsilon_i$ are both unknown.
% - Correcting a label needs both the size $|\epsilon_i|$ and the sign of $\epsilon_i$.
% - Detecting a noisy label needs only a ranking of $|\epsilon_i|$. So correction is the harder task.
% - Under Gaussian noise every label is noisy: $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2)$ for all $i$. No clean subset exists to learn from.
% - Neighbour-based noise estimates use the neighbours' labels, which are noisy too.
% - Example estimate: $\hat{e}_i = \tilde{y}_i - \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j \in \mathcal{N}_k(i)} \tilde{y}_j$, with $\mathcal{N}_k(i)$ the $k$ nearest neighbours of molecule $i$.
% - Expanding it: $\hat{e}_i = \epsilon_i + \big(y_i - \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j} y_j\big) - \frac{1}{k}\sum_{j} \epsilon_j$. The second term is real structure-activity difference; the third is the neighbours' noise.
% - Without ground truth these terms cannot be separated. A large $\hat{e}_i$ can be noise or a real activity cliff.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (similarity flaws make $\mathcal{N}_k(i)$ itself unreliable); Chapter 6 (a better similarity from hybrids does not remove the noise in the neighbours' labels).
% - A cleaned dataset can give a worse model than the noisy one it came from. The recovery rate in Chapter 6 is negative in that case.
% - TODO(results): whether and where recovery rate went negative; numbers come from the full runs.
% - What is possible: outlier detection (a label far from what its neighbours or a model predict) and detection of systematic errors.
% - Systematic error examples: a shift shared by one group (one laboratory, assay or scaffold family); labels clipped at a detection limit (censoring).
% - Why these are easier: the error has a known structure. Censoring has a known direction and a known limit. A group shift is shared by many molecules and can be estimated from all of them.
% - Real data context: combining IC50 or Ki values from different sources adds substantial noise \citep{landrum2024}.
% - Heterogeneous public Ki data have an experimental uncertainty of about 0.54 log units \citep{Kramer2012}.
% - Consequence: noise limits how well a model can be evaluated, not only trained. Noisy test labels cap the measurable performance \citep{Crusius2024}.
% - In the controlled experiments on assay data, noise was injected on top of labels that already had unknown noise. The added noise is known; the original noise is not.
% - So "clean" in those experiments means "no added noise", not "true". Only QM9 is close to noise-free.
% - DIAGRAM: one small panel per noise condition (Gaussian, Student-t, Laplace, outlier, grouped-wider, grouped-shifted, censoring). x-axis true label, y-axis observed label. Shade what can be detected and what can be corrected. May reuse the Chapter 6 by-noise-type figure.
% Recommendation that follows:
% - Recommend learning with noisy labels over cleaning labels first.
% - Learning with noisy labels: change the model or its loss so training tolerates noise. Examples: robust losses \citep{Ghosh2017}; two networks that pick low-loss samples for each other \citep{Han2018}. Survey: \citep{Song2022}.
% - Reason: the model adapts during training. Direct cleaning guesses the error's direction one molecule at a time.
% - Cleaning is cheaper: done once, reused by every later model.
% - The cost of cleaning: a wrong guess is fixed into the data for every later model.
% - Deep networks fit clean patterns before they memorise noisy labels \citep{arpit2017closer}.
% - Deep networks can memorise completely random labels \citep{Zhang2017}. Training-time methods rely on the gap between these two phases.
% - Pair with uncertainty estimation where it is reliable.
% - Population level: mean predicted uncertainty rises with the amount of label noise \citep{Kolmar2021}. Chapter 3 found the same.
% - Per molecule: the link between predicted uncertainty and label noise depends on model and representation. Chapter 3: strongest for GPs and NGBoost with fingerprints or descriptors, near zero for learned embeddings.
% - So per-molecule uncertainty is a weak basis for correcting single labels; the overall uncertainty level is a usable check on how noisy a dataset is.
% - TODO: re-check the per-molecule correlation range against the final Chapter 3 text before quoting a number. The outline says -0.16 to 0.16 under six of seven conditions; the Chapter 3 table shows higher values for the best model-representation pairs.
% - Why it matters: practitioners with noisy assay data should prefer training-time robustness plus a check on overall uncertainty, rather than a cleaned copy of the dataset.
% Limitation 2: classification:
% - The noise benchmarking in this thesis is on regression. Chapter 3's conclusion already asks for classification (Already in prose there; refer back, do not repeat).
% - The noise-injection tool from Chapter 3 already supports label flipping, including different flip rates per class. These were not benchmarked.
% - Class-conditional flips: $P(\tilde{y}=1 \mid y=0) = \rho_{0}$, $P(\tilde{y}=0 \mid y=1) = \rho_{1}$, with $\rho_0 \neq \rho_1$ \citep{natarajan2013learning}.
% - Label noise in classification, background survey: \citep{frenay2014classification}.
% - Possible source in assay data: a molecule measured close to an activity cut-off $t$ can land on either side. Flip probability would then depend on $|y_i - t|$. Unverified; needs a source.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on label noise in binarised bioactivity data caused by thresholding continuous assay values (active/inactive cut-offs on pIC50).

% Limitation 3: datasets:
% - Controlled noise was injected into QM9 plus three experimental sets (LogD, Caco-2 efflux, hERG Ki). The experimental sets are small and cover a narrow part of chemical space (Already in Chapter 3 prose; refer back).

% - No dataset in the thesis has repeated measurements with a known true value for every molecule. That is the dataset needed to test label correction directly.
% - TODO(lit): search for public bioactivity datasets with many replicate measurements per compound that could serve as a noise ground truth.

% Limitation 4: compute (thesis level):

% Limitation 5: injected noise is a model of noise:
% - Each noise condition is an assumption about how real errors arise: distribution shape, grouping, shared offset, censoring.
% - Real data mix several of these at unknown amounts.
% - A method that works under one injected condition may fail on real data whose noise type is unknown.

\section{Future directions}
% OUTLINE 7.6: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 7.6 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: more base representations, other importance-scoring methods, the model-versus-representation complexity trade-off, estimators and neighbour methods under other noise types, and the paragraph on adding a new foundation model's embeddings to the pool.
% - Prose fix: the practice paragraph says "A researcher using KIRBy". Change it to "the adaptive hybrid representation" or "the hybrid search". KIRBy should appear only in the code-availability appendix.
% - Prose fix: TreeSHAP, KernelSHAP and gradient attribution are now implemented as importance methods (Chapter 5). Rewrite that sentence as done work, or cut it.
%   - Drop-column importance was not found in the code. Keep it only if it is still planned.
%   - Small-loss selection is a learning-with-noisy-labels method (Chapter 4 study), not an importance method. Move it or cut it from that list.
% Scope note:
% - The brain dump names three next steps: classification and label flipping, hybrids as inputs to generators, and a faster search.
% - The outline keeps three more from the old outline: forward compatibility tested both ways, blueprints carried across domains, and a fuller federated deployment with Lhasa.
%
% Classification and label flipping:
% - Most of the noise work in the thesis is regression: continuous labels with additive, censored, outlier or grouped noise.
% - In classification, noise is a flipped class, not a shifted value.
% - The error is discrete. Once the true class is known, so is the direction of the error.
% - Standard description: the noise transition matrix $T_{ij} = P(\tilde{y} = j \mid y = i)$, with $\sum_j T_{ij} = 1$ \citep{frenay2014classification}.
% - Uniform (symmetric) flipping at rate $\rho$ over $C$ classes: $T = (1-\rho)\mathbf{I} + \frac{\rho}{C-1}(\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top - \mathbf{I})$.
% - Class-dependent flipping: $T_{ij}$ depends on the true class $i$. Example: inactives mislabelled as actives more often than the reverse.
% - Instance-dependent flipping: the flip probability depends on the molecule, $P(\tilde{y} \neq y \mid \mathbf{x})$. This is closest to real assay error.
% - The noise-injection framework already has six classification conditions, each with the flip rate as its level: uniform, class-imbalance, binary asymmetric, instance-dependent, class-dependent, and confusion-directed.
% - The hybrid search already handles classification: class-stratified, scaffold-grouped folds, scored with AUROC.
%   - What is missing is running the classification conditions at the same scale as the regression noise study.
% - Many QSAR classification labels come from cutting a continuous measurement at a threshold $t$. Example: hERG blocker or non-blocker from a pKi cut-off.
%   - With Gaussian measurement noise of standard deviation $\sigma$ on the true value $y$, the chance that the class flips is $P(\text{flip}) = \Phi\!\left(-\frac{|y - t|}{\sigma}\right)$.
%   - So flips concentrate on molecules near the threshold. This is instance-dependent noise, not uniform noise.
%   - Links to: Chapter 3 (noise on the continuous label) and the federated hERG work in Chapter 5 (labels binarised at a pKi cut-off).
% - Open question: do the regression findings carry over? Example: whether the best representation still changes with the noise condition.
% - Open question: learning-with-noisy-labels methods were mostly built for flipped class labels, e.g. co-teaching and confident learning \citep{han2018coteaching,Northcutt2021}.
%   - They may behave differently on flipped classes than on continuous QSAR labels.
%   - Why it matters: this would test whether their results in the Chapter 4 study come from the methods or from the regression setting.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on class-dependent label noise in binarised bioactivity data (active/inactive thresholds in ChEMBL or PubChem HTS).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on estimating the noise transition matrix from noisy labels alone (anchor-point and confident-learning estimators).
% - Limitation to state: a neighbour-based score measured with AUROC is undefined when a held-out group holds only one class.
%   - Multiclass and small-scaffold datasets hit this.
%
% Hybrids as inputs to generative models:
% - In the thesis the hybrid is only an input to supervised prediction.
% - It was also used in the federated setting (Chapter 5). Each silo chose its own features.
%   - Silos shared only predictions, which features they chose, importance weights and validation scores. No raw data left a silo.
% - Next use: goal-directed molecular generation. A generator proposes molecules and a scoring model rewards them.
%   - Example: reinforcement-learning fine-tuning of a SMILES generator \citep{Olivecrona2017}.
%   - The hybrid could be the input to that scoring model, so the reward comes from a feature set chosen for the task.
% - Known risk: generators exploit the scoring model.
%   - Molecules optimised against one model can score highly on it but lower on control models trained on other splits of the same data \citep{Renz2019}.
%   - Open question: does a small, task-chosen feature set make this easier (fewer features to game) or harder (fewer irrelevant features to exploit)?
% - What would the search optimise for this task? Options:
%   - Predictive score only, as now: $J = m(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$.
%   - A blend that also rewards structure. The search can already score a feature subset by how well its distances track maximum common substructure (MCS) distance.
%   - Inside a blend, prediction is scored as a correlation (Spearman for regression, $2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$ for classification), so the parts share one scale.
%   - Blend as a weighted mean per fold: $J = \frac{w_p J_{\text{pred}} + w_s J_{\text{struct}}}{w_p + w_s}$.
%   - A third option: agreement between scoring models trained on different folds, as a guard against exploitation (after the control-model idea in \citep{Renz2019}).
% - Practical cost: every generated molecule needs its hybrid features computed. For learned embeddings that means running the encoder on each candidate.
% - Fingerprint bits cannot be decoded back into a molecule directly.
%   - So the hybrid can score candidates, but it cannot be the generator's latent space without a decoder.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on reconstructing molecules from ECFP/Morgan fingerprints (fingerprint inversion or decoding).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the choice of molecular representation for the scoring (oracle) model in goal-directed generation.
% - DIAGRAM: generation loop. A generator produces a SMILES; it is converted to base representations; features are selected into the hybrid; a scoring model returns the reward to the generator. Mark where the search objective $J$ enters.
%
% A faster search:
% - As it stands, the search is too heavy for routine use.
% - Cost grows as the product of the axis sizes: $N_{\text{fits}} \approx |\mathcal{A}|\,|\mathcal{I}|\,|\mathcal{E}|\,|\mathcal{B}|\,|\mathcal{M}|\,F$.
%   - $\mathcal{A}$ = allocation methods, $\mathcal{I}$ = importance methods, $\mathcal{E}$ = internal evaluators, $\mathcal{B}$ = budgets, $\mathcal{M}$ = downstream models, $F$ = folds.
% - Two ways the current search limits cost (Chapter 5):
%   - A fast tier: shorter lists on four axes (downstream models, internal evaluators, importance methods, allocation methods). The lists are fixed before the search and are the same for every dataset. It knowingly accepts a possibly worse answer for a smaller grid.
%   - Adaptive pruning: after the first folds, it drops configurations that are far behind on this dataset's own fold scores. It is tuned so that it never removes the eventual winner; time saved is a side effect.
% - Adaptive pruning is close to successive halving and Hyperband. There, resources go to promising configurations and the rest are stopped early \citep{Li2018Hyperband}.
%   - Next step: use such a scheduler formally, with folds or budget as the resource.
% - Model-based search instead of a grid: e.g. tree-structured Parzen estimators with pruning, as in Optuna \citep{akiba2019optuna}.
%   - It samples configurations rather than enumerating them all.
%   - Caution: the thesis argues against cutting the search space. A sampler must still be able to reach every configuration; it only visits fewer.
% - Within one representation, top-$b$ selection from a fixed importance ranking is nested: $S_k(b) \subseteq S_k(b')$ for $b < b'$.
%   - The search already computes the importance ranking once per fold and reuses it at every budget.
%   - What remains expensive is the model fits at each budget.
% - Warm start from blueprints: begin the search from the blueprint of a similar dataset and search nearby, instead of the full grid.
%   - Links to: blueprints (Chapter 5) and blueprint transfer below.
% - TODO(results): wall-clock hours and number of fits for a full search against the fast tier, per dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on multi-fidelity hyperparameter optimisation where cross-validation folds are the fidelity (fold-wise early stopping of configurations).
%
% Carried from the old outline:
% - Forward compatibility, tested both ways.
%   - Current claim: a new embedding can be added to the pool, and the search decides whether it contributes.
%   - Other direction: does the hybrid improve a weak new embedding? Compare $m(\mathbf{H}_{\text{pool} \cup \{\text{new}\}})$ against $m(\mathbf{X}^{\text{new}})$ alone and against $m(\mathbf{H}_{\text{pool}})$.
%   - Why it matters: new foundation models often appear before anyone has benchmarked them on a given assay. This would show whether the hybrid protects against a poor choice of embedding.
% - Blueprints across domains.
%   - A blueprint records which representations were used, how many features came from each ($b_k$), and which features.
%   - Open question: does a small-molecule blueprint transfer to antibodies or proteins at the level of representation families (e.g. "mostly a learned embedding plus some counts")?
%   - Individual features cannot transfer, because the representations differ between domains.
%   - Links to: the antibody and protein results in Chapter 5.
% - A fuller federated deployment with Lhasa.
%   - The federated work so far is a simulation on public data plus one proprietary source.
%   - Next step: real silos, where each partner runs the silo-level selection on their own data.
%   - Background: federated learning in molecular discovery \citep{Hanser2023}; an industrial computational-toxicology hackathon \citep{Bassani2023}; cross-pharma federated QSAR across ten companies \citep{Heyndrickx2023}.
%   - Open question: the silo-level selection shares feature indices and importances. Can importances reveal anything about a partner's private chemistry?
%   - TODO(lit): search for literature on information leakage from shared feature-importance scores in federated learning.
%
% Noise, looking forward:
% - Pair learning with noisy labels with uncertainty estimation at the dataset level rather than per molecule.
%   - Links to: Limitations (7.5) and Handling noise realistically (7.2).
% - An objective that rewards noise robustness as well as accuracy: $J = J_{\text{pred}}(\text{clean}) - \lambda\,[J_{\text{pred}}(\text{clean}) - J_{\text{pred}}(\text{noisy})]$.
%   - $\lambda \geq 0$ sets how much a drop under noise costs.
%   - This extends the existing prose line on optimising for noise robustness. It is not built yet.
%
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: every direction here reuses the same core idea: choose features for the task, per dataset.
%   - They apply it to a new label type (classes), a new consumer (generators), a new setting (real federated silos), or make it cheaper (speed).
% - Links to: No single method fits every dataset (7.3). Each extension still needs a per-dataset search, not a fixed recipe.
% - DIAGRAM: hub-and-spoke figure. The hybrid search sits in the centre. Spokes go to classification and flipped labels, generative scoring, federated silos, faster search, and new embeddings or domains. Each spoke is tagged with the chapter it extends.
% ---- END NOTES 7.6 ----

% [CR 1150,1152,1154] the confirmation report's Next Steps. Much of this is now DONE
% (TreeSHAP/KernelSHAP/gradient attribution are implemented; the complexity trade-off is
% ch.5). Kept as raw material.
% TODO: prune the parts that ch.4-6 now deliver.
% TODO(xref): [CR 1150] ends 'small-loss selection, as discussed in Chapter 4'. Small-loss
% selection is an LNL method; the LNL study is now Section 4.1. DONE 2026-09-27: retargeted with \ref{sec:lnl_motivation}.
% TODO(xref): [CR 1156] 'Chapters 4 and 5' / 'Chapter 3' -- old numbering throughout.
I also plan to extend the hybrid research to additional base representations and datasets. Some alternative representations include additional GNN embeddings, sequential encoders, augmented features such as embedded distances from molecular graphs, and transformed representations (i.e., normalization, binarization of continuous features). Additionally, I want to explore alternative feature importance scoring methods, especially ones that involve different families of models. Specifically, I plan to look at TreeSHAP, KernelSHAP, drop-column importance for noise robustness, gradient-based attribution for NN embeddings, and small-loss selection, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:lnl_motivation}. 

As mentioned earlier, although I do not currently have a full set of results on hybrid, they have been showing promise throughout a rank of QSAR and noise mitigation tasks. I would like to explore the complexity tradeoff between model and representation complexity, as I've seen a trend of complicated models like NNs having stronger predictive performance with smaller hybrids and more simplistic tree-based methods benefiting from larger hybrids. This noise mitigation experiment relied on a very small default hybrid and used RF-based feature importance. I would like to leverage my allocation strategies to adapt hybrid models to datasets \textit{and} models. These strategies can also be modified to optimize for noise robustness in addition to predictive performance. 

Various estimation methods showed promise, meriting further investigation. Additionally, neighbor-based noise mitigation strategies which are not fit for Gaussian noise may perform differently on outlier-based strategies. The dominance of estimators with label smoothing and sample weighting may be challenged for different types of noise. 

% [KB 131] what this means in practice -- the forward-compatibility argument.
What does this mean in practice? A new foundation model appears every few months in molecular, protein, or antibody modeling. A researcher using KIRBy does not need to decide whether to replace their current encoding or retrain their pipeline; they compute the new model's embeddings, add the matrix to the pool, and re-run the search. The search determines whether the new embedding contributes useful features and, if so, how many and which ones. In a field where encoding choice is often determined by familiarity or lab convention rather than by systematic comparison, this is a substantive change. It replaces a single bet with a principled combination, tailored to each task, that updates as the pool grows.

\chapter{Datasets and curation}\label{app:datasets}
% ---- NOTES A (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope and layout:
% - Already in prose: the one-paragraph availability statement for QM9, LogD, Caco-2 and hERG Ki (Chapter 3 datasets).
% - This appendix adds one entry per dataset used in Chapters 5 and 6: source, citation, accession or DOI, what the label is, and each curation step in order.
% - Links to: the datasets table in the experimental-setup section of Chapter 5 (one row per dataset); this appendix holds the curation detail that table leaves out.
% - Why it matters: every curation step changes which molecules are in the set and what the labels mean.
% - Why it matters: a reader needs these steps to rebuild the exact sets and to compare with other papers that use the same source.
% - Why it matters: the sets span small molecules, reactions, antibodies, DNA and tumour gene expression. The claim that the hybrid is domain-agnostic rests on this spread, so each domain needs its own documented set.
% - Why it matters: every set has a grouping rule for its folds (scaffold, additive, mutation position, sequence cluster, chromosome, hospital site). The grouping decides how far the test set is from the training set, so it belongs next to the data, not only in the methods.
% - Links to: Chapter 6 (noise). Some sets have label steps (median of repeats, clipping) that already remove or flatten noise before any noise is injected.

%
% Shared steps for the OpenADMET and hERG Ki sets:
% - SMILES strings are standardised with RDKit \citep{rdkit}, in this order: cleanup, keep the largest fragment (removes salts and solvents), neutralise charges, canonical tautomer, reject any molecule that contains a metal.
% - The Biogen (Polaris) sets and QM9 are not standardised; they are only checked to parse in RDKit.
% - After loading, molecules with the same InChIKey \citep{Heller2015} count as duplicates. The first one is kept.
%   - Reason: two different SMILES strings can describe the same molecule. Without this step, one molecule could sit in both the training fold and the test fold.
%   - This step is applied to every small-molecule set, including the unstandardised ones.
% - Folds are grouped by Bemis–Murcko scaffold \citep{BemisMurcko1996}. All molecules that share a scaffold go into the same fold.
% - CHECK: Chapter 3 gives LogD 5,039, Caco-2 2,161 and hERG 1,415 molecules. The Chapter 5 sets have 5,028, 2,158 and 1,412. The InChIKey step is the likely cause, but this has not been checked. Say which curation each chapter used.
%
% hERG Ki (ChEMBL):
% - Source: ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023}, target CHEMBL240 (hERG, KCNH2). Retrieved through the ChEMBL web API.
%   - TODO: record the ChEMBL release number and the download date.
% - Query filters:
%   - standard type Ki;
%   - a pChEMBL value is present;
%   - the relation is "=" (records reported as ">" or "<" are dropped);
%   - no curator data-validity comment;
%   - binding assays only (assay type B).
% - Label: $\mathrm{pKi} = -\log_{10}(K_i/\mathrm{M})$, taken as ChEMBL's pChEMBL value.
% - Repeat measurements are merged per compound:
%   - label = the median of the compound's pKi values;
%   - compounds whose repeat measurements have standard deviation $> 1.0$ pKi unit are dropped.
% - Order of steps in the Chapter 5 version: repeats are merged on ChEMBL's own SMILES, then SMILES are standardised, then InChIKey deduplication.
%   - Consequence: two ChEMBL entries that standardise to the same molecule are not merged by median. The InChIKey step keeps the first and drops the second.
%   - CHECK: the Chapter 3 (noise) version adds a second median merge on the standardised SMILES. Say which version each chapter uses.
% - Rows: about 1,412 molecules. CHECK.
% - Background for the noise chapter: public Ki values carry an experimental uncertainty of about 0.54 pKi units \citep{Kramer2012}. Combining Ki or IC50 values from different sources adds noise of its own \citep{landrum2024}.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Censored records (">" or "<") never enter this set, because the query keeps only "=". The censoring experiment in Chapter 6 therefore injects censoring rather than finding it in these labels.
%
% LogD and Caco-2 efflux (OpenADMET–ExpansionRx):
% - Source: the OpenADMET–ExpansionRx challenge training data on Hugging Face, licence CC-BY-4.0 \citep{openadmet}.
% - Steps, in order:
%   - drop rows with no value;
%   - standardise SMILES;
%   - merge rows with the same standardised SMILES, keeping the median label;
%   - (Caco-2 only) take $\log_{10}$ of the efflux ratio;
%   - clip labels at their own 1st and 99th percentiles: $y_i \leftarrow \min(\max(y_i, q_{0.01}), q_{0.99})$;
%   - InChIKey deduplication.
% - Rows: LogD about 5,028 molecules; Caco-2 about 2,158. CHECK.
% - Links to: Chapter 6. Both sets already have their extreme labels clipped before any noise is injected. The censoring section should say this.
%
% Liver microsome clearance and MDR1–MDCK efflux (Biogen, via Polaris):
% - Source: the Biogen ADME set \citep{fang2023}, downloaded from Polaris \citep{polaris} as "biogen/adme-fang-v1" (3,521 molecules).
% - Endpoints available and loaded as released (all already $\log_{10}$):
%   - human liver microsome intrinsic clearance, $\log_{10}$ CLint;
%   - rat liver microsome intrinsic clearance, $\log_{10}$ CLint;
%   - MDR1–MDCK efflux ratio, $\log_{10}$.
%   - CHECK: which of the two clearance endpoints (human, rat, or both) are in the final dataset list.
% - Steps: drop rows with no value or with SMILES that RDKit cannot parse; InChIKey deduplication.
%   - No standardisation, no median merge and no clipping. This differs from the OpenADMET sets.
% - Rows: about 3,054 for rat clearance, about 3,087 for human clearance, about 2,642 for efflux. CHECK.
%
% QM9 (Chapter 5 version):
% - Source: QM9 \citep{Ramakrishnan2014, qm9_dataset}, read through PyTorch Geometric \citep{pytorchGeometric}.
% - Steps:
%   - keep molecules that RDKit can parse (129,428 of 130,831);
%   - draw 10,000 at random, seed 42 (a random subsample of molecules, not a random split);
%   - label = HOMO–LUMO gap, $\Delta\epsilon = \epsilon_{\mathrm{LUMO}} - \epsilon_{\mathrm{HOMO}}$, in eV.
% - Labels are scaled inside each fold only, never on the whole set.
% - Folds: scaffold-grouped over this one draw.
% - CHECK: the noise chapter uses ten draws of 10,000 molecules split 8,000 / 1,000 / 1,000. That is a different setup from this one; say which one each chapter uses.
%
% hERG blockers (Lhasa Limited, federated setting):

%   - Molecules found at more than one site are merged by InChIKey, and the ChEMBL copy is kept.
% - CHECK: the molecule counts above.
%
% Reaction yields:
% - Buchwald–Hartwig C–N coupling, high-throughput yields \citep{Ahneman2018}.
%   - File: the version released with \citet{Schwaller2021}. CHECK against the download URL.
%   - Label: yield, 0–100 %.
%   - About 3,955 reactions.
%   - Each reaction is written as reactants (aryl halide, amine, ligand, base, additive) and product.
%   - The amine is always 4-methylaniline.
%   - The product is generated by applying a C–N coupling reaction template to the aryl halide.
% - Second Buchwald–Hartwig set: the table in the Doyle lab's public repository.
%   - 4,599 rows; 287 dropped for a missing aryl halide, leaving about 4,312.
% - Folds for both: grouped by additive (leave additives out), which is the out-of-sample test used for these data \citep{Ahneman2018}.

% - Why it matters: here a "molecule" is a whole reaction (several reactants plus a product), so the representations and the grouping differ from the single-molecule sets.
%
% Antibodies:
% - FLAb benchmark \citep{Chungyoun2024}, CSV files downloaded from its repository.
%   - TODO: record the commit hash.
%   - Rows with a missing heavy chain or a missing label are dropped.
% - Expression, Koenig set:
%   - Label: expression enrichment ratio from a deep mutational scan of the anti-VEGF antibody G6 \citep{Koenig2017}.
%   - About 4,275 variants, with paired heavy and light chains.
%   - Folds grouped by (position of the first mutation) mod 5, following the modulo split of ProteinGym \citep{Notin2023}.
%     - Reason: mutations at the same residue stay on one side of the split.
% - Thermostability, NbThermo set:
%   - Label: melting temperature $T_m$ in °C, for 672 nanobodies (heavy chain only) \citep{ValdesTresanco2023}.
%   - Folds grouped by clusters at 95 % sequence identity.
%   - Reason: many entries are near-duplicates (one or two residues apart). Grouping only identical sequences would put most of these pairs on opposite sides of a split.
% - Humanness:
%   - Source: BioPhi's IMGT/mAb-DB therapeutic antibodies \citep{Prihoda2022}.
%   - Label 1 = human; label 0 = everything else, including humanised, chimeric and mouse antibodies.
%   - Heavy chain (VH) only; about 564 antibodies.
%   - CHECK: the count, because the loader's own documentation says 549.
%   - CHECK: humanised antibodies are labelled non-human. Say so in the text.
% - Trastuzumab–HER2 binding:
%   - Source: \citet{Chinery2024}, over 524,000 variants of the 10-residue CDR-H3 (the third complementarity-determining region of the heavy chain) of trastuzumab.
%   - Earlier screen of trastuzumab variants for HER2 binding: \citet{Mason2021}.
%     - CHECK: whether the Chinery library reuses the Mason library design; not confirmed.
%   - Label: binder / non-binder.
%   - 5,000 variants drawn, stratified by label and edit distance from the wild-type CDR-H3 (each stratum keeps its share of the full set).
%   - The full heavy chain is rebuilt by inserting each CDR-H3 into the trastuzumab framework; the light chain is the fixed trastuzumab light chain.
%   - Folds grouped by edit distance from the wild type.
% - Sequence identity used for the 95 % clusters:
%   - $\mathrm{id}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{\mathrm{lev}(a,b)}{\max(|a|,|b|)}$, where $\mathrm{lev}$ is the Levenshtein (edit) distance.
%   - Clusters are single-linkage at $\mathrm{id} \ge 0.95$: two sequences share a cluster if a chain of pairs each at $\ge 0.95$ joins them.
%   - For paired sets, heavy and light chains are joined into one string before clustering.
%   - Clustering paired antibodies at 95 % identity on joined heavy and light chains follows \citet{Kenlay2024}.
%   - On panels of distinct antibodies, almost every cluster holds one antibody, so the folds hold out antibodies, not germline families.
% - Why it matters: antibody sets range from single-parent mutant scans (Koenig, trastuzumab) to panels of unrelated antibodies (humanness, NbThermo). The same grouping rule means very different things in the two cases.
%
% Nucleotide sequences:
% - Source: the revised Nucleotide Transformer downstream tasks \citep{DallaTorre2025}, from Hugging Face.
%   - TODO: record the dataset revision.
% - Tasks: promoter-TATA (about 5,274 sequences) and the histone mark H3K4me3 (about 18,244 sequences). Both are binary.

% [NI 642] her own dataset-availability paragraph.
The datasets used in this study are publicly available. The QM9 dataset is available on Figshare \citep{qm9_dataset} and is also redistributed through the OpenADMET benchmark suite \citep{Ramakrishnan2014, openadmet}. The LogD and Caco-2 datasets are available in the OpenADMET repository \citep{openadmet}. The hERG-Ki dataset was derived from ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023} following the curation protocol described in Section~\ref{sec2}, and can be regenerated from ChEMBL using that protocol.
