\chapter{The search space and its defaults}\label{app:searchspace}
% ---- NOTES C (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope and status:
% - Purpose of this appendix: the full list of options the hybrid search can choose from, their default values, and what a saved blueprint records. Chapter 5 (Building Adaptive Hybrid Representations) gives the reasons; this appendix is the reference.
% - The outline extract under this heading is out of date. Remove from the text: greedy, dominant-greedy, two-stage and stability-selection allocation; MLP and ridge as downstream models; MLP and SVM as listed internal evaluators. None of these is searched any more.
% - Check against Chapter 5: its methods prose still describes greedy allocation, 13 retained allocation methods, and pruning on the first fold with a top-3 rule. The values below are the current ones; the two places should agree before submission.
% - Why it matters: this appendix is what lets a reader rebuild any hybrid in the thesis. Every choice the search makes is one of the values listed here.
% - Links to: Chapter 5, sections "Methods: the hybrid search" and "Results: blueprints on specific datasets"; Appendix A (datasets and their groups); Appendix D (extended result tables).
% - TABLE C.1: one row per axis. Columns: axis, values in a full search, values in a fast search, other values available but not searched. The bullets below fill it.
% Notation used throughout:
% - $K$ base representations $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$; budget $B$; allocation $b_k$ with $\sum_k b_k = B$ (less only when representations are narrower than their share); importance of feature $j$ in representation $k$: $s^{(k)}_j$; hybrid $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$ with $|S_k| = b_k$.
% - Within a representation, $S_k$ holds the $b_k$ features with the highest $s^{(k)}_j$: $S_k = \operatorname{top}_{b_k}\{ s^{(k)}_j : j = 1, \dots, d_k \}$.
% Axis 1, downstream models (the model that is finally trained and scored on the hybrid):
% - Full search: random forest \citep{Breiman2001}, LightGBM \citep{ke2017lightgbm}, a feed-forward neural network (DNN), XGBoost \citep{Chen2016}, support vector machine \citep{Vapnik1995}.
% - Fast search: random forest, LightGBM, DNN.
% - Available by name but never chosen automatically: k-nearest neighbours ($k=5$), Gaussian process \citep{Rasmussen2006}, NGBoost \citep{Duan2020}, a near-unregularised linear model, and two robust-loss variants (LightGBM with a Huber loss, random forest with an absolute-error split rule).
%   - The linear model: least squares with penalty $\alpha = 10^{-6}$ (minimum-norm fit when $d > n$); logistic regression with $C = 10^{6}$ for classification. Its parameter count equals the budget, which is why it is kept for the budget-versus-sample-size question.
% - Removed from the experiments: MLP (replaced by the DNN), ridge regression, CatBoost, a 1D CNN, a Bayesian neural network.
% - Default settings. Random forest: 100 trees, $\sqrt{d}$ features tried per split. LightGBM: 100 trees, library defaults otherwise. XGBoost: 100 trees, half the features sampled per tree. SVM: RBF kernel, library defaults (probability outputs for classification).
% - DNN: three hidden layers of 256, 128 and 64 units; dropout 0.2; Adam, learning rate $10^{-3}$, halved when validation loss stalls for 5 epochs; batch 256; up to 200 epochs; early stopping with patience 20 on an internal 20% hold-out.
% - Training-set size limits: SVM is skipped above 10,000 training molecules (5,000 when augmentation appends features beyond the budget); Gaussian process above 1,000; the absolute-error random forest above 5,000.
%   - Reason: kernel methods scale roughly as $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^3)$ in the number of training molecules; the Gaussian process factorises the full $n \times n$ kernel matrix.
% - No model has a cap on budget. Every model is searched at every budget.
% - Hyperparameters are defaults unless tuning is switched on. When it is, tuning uses only the training molecules of each outer fold (20 random draws, on all base representations joined). The DNN is not tuned; it relies on early stopping.
% Axis 2, budget $B$ (maximum number of features in the hybrid):
% - Values: $B \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2000\}$.
% - The best result per model is the best over all budgets, so a small budget can win.
% - Budgets larger than the number of training molecules are kept and searched, not dropped.
% - Why it matters: the budget sets the size of the input the model sees, and for the similarity uses it sets how many features enter the distance between molecules. Links to: Chapter 5 results on when the hybrid helps; Chapter 6.
% Axis 3, number of representations $N$:
% - Values: $N \in \{2, 3, 5, K\}$; $K$ = the whole pool (10 on most datasets). Fast search: $\{3, K\}$.
% - For each fold, the $N$ representations with the highest standalone score (see Axis 4) are kept; the rest get $b_k = 0$.
% - Older allocation variants fixed "top 2", "top 3" or "top 5" inside the weighting rule. Now the count and the weighting are separate choices, so any weighting can be paired with any count.
% Axis 4, allocation method (how $B$ is split across representations):
% - Step 1, standalone score: each representation alone is scored by the internal evaluator on an internal validation split of the training molecules, giving $s_k$ in the dataset's own metric.
% - Step 2, rescale so 0 means "no better than a trivial model":
%   - correlation metrics (Spearman, Pearson, MCC, $R^2$): $\tilde{s}_k = \max(0, s_k)$
%   - AUROC and concordance index: $\tilde{s}_k = \max(0,\, 2(s_k - 0.5))$
%   - RMSE and MAE: $\tilde{s}_k = \max\!\left(0,\, 1 - \mathrm{err}_k / \mathrm{err}_0\right)$, where $\mathrm{err}_0$ is the error of predicting the training mean for every held-out molecule
%   - weighted F1 or accuracy: $\tilde{s}_k = \max\!\left(0,\, \frac{s_k - s_0}{1 - s_0}\right)$, where $s_0$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class
% - Step 3, weights $w_k$ from $\tilde{s}_k$. The five searched methods:
%   - linear: $w_k = \tilde{s}_k$
%   - squared: $w_k = \tilde{s}_k^{\,2}$
%   - softmax with temperature $T=1$: $w_k = \exp\!\left((\tilde{s}_k - \max_j \tilde{s}_j)/T\right)$
%   - rank with exponential decay: $w_k = \exp(-0.3\, r_k)$, where $r_k = 0$ for the best representation, 1 for the second, and so on
%   - global pool with squared weights (described below)
% - Step 4, the first four methods give each representation a fixed number of features: $b_k = \frac{w_k}{\sum_j w_j} B$, capped at $d_k$.
%   - Rounding uses the largest-remainder rule: floor every $b_k$, then give the leftover features one at a time in order of the largest fractional part.
%   - A representation with $w_k > 0$ keeps at least one feature.
%   - If every weight is zero, the budget is split equally and a warning is recorded.
% - Global pool method:
%   - Candidate slots per representation: $c_k = \operatorname{round}\!\left(2B \cdot \frac{w_k}{\sum_j w_j}\right)$ with $w_k = \tilde{s}_k^{\,2}$, capped at $d_k$.
%   - Take the top $c_k$ features of each representation by importance and join them into one pool of about $2B$ features.
%   - Re-rank the whole pool with one importance fit and keep the top $B$.
%   - So $b_k$ is set by competition inside the pool, not fixed ahead of time.
% - Fast search: linear, squared, rank with exponential decay.
% - Available but not searched: other softmax temperatures (0.5, 2, 5, 10), cube power, thresholded weights, top-$k$ weights, linear rank weights, score-gap cut-offs, redundancy-penalised weights, a minimum floor per representation, an equal split, and the global pool with linear weights.
% - Not used in any thesis run: the greedy family (adding features step by step against a running score).
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on the largest-remainder (Hamilton) method of proportional apportionment, as the source for the rounding rule for $b_k$.
% Axis 5, importance method (how $s^{(k)}_j$ is computed):
% - Always fitted separately per representation, on training molecules only. Features are standardised first.
% - LightGBM gain: total loss reduction from splits on feature $j$; 50 trees, depth 6 \citep{ke2017lightgbm}.
% - TreeSHAP: mean absolute SHAP value over molecules, $s_j = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i |\phi_{ij}|$, from a 50-tree random forest of depth 10 \citep{Lundberg2020}.
% - DeepLIFT: mean absolute attribution against an all-zero reference input, from a small network (hidden layers 64 and 32, 100 epochs, Adam) \citep{Shrikumar2017}.
%   - Because features are standardised, the all-zero reference is the training mean of every feature.
% - For the two tree methods, a representation with more than 2,000 features is first cut to its 2,000 highest-variance features. Variance is measured before standardising. The rest get importance 0.
% - Fast search: LightGBM gain only.
% - Available but not searched: XGBoost gain, permutation importance, Kernel SHAP, integrated gradients, GradientSHAP.
% - Links to: Chapter 5 "The search space" (her prose on why importance depends on the model).
% Axis 6, internal evaluator (the model that scores representations and candidate hybrids inside the search):
% - Values: "match" (the same model type as the downstream model), random forest, LightGBM, DNN. The fast search uses the same four.
% - The evaluator models are lighter than the downstream ones: random forest 50 trees, depth 10; LightGBM 50 trees, depth 4; DNN the same as downstream.
% - "match" becomes XGBoost (50 trees, depth 4) or SVM (RBF kernel) for those downstream models. So five evaluator types appear in a full search, although four are listed.
% - Why it matters: tests whether the features a model prefers depend on that model's own inductive bias (tree ensemble, boosted trees, neural network). Links to: Chapter 5 "Why each axis is swept".
% Axis 7, objective (what the search maximises; chosen per run, not swept inside one run):
% - Prediction (default): the downstream model's score in the dataset's metric.
% - Similarity: no model is fitted. Each held-out molecule's activity is predicted from its five nearest neighbours in the hybrid. The score is the Spearman correlation with the true values (AUROC for two-class datasets). Needs scaffold groups on the held-out molecules.
%   - Not defined for datasets with more than two classes, because AUROC needs two classes.
% - Structural fidelity: Spearman correlation, over sampled molecule pairs, between Euclidean distance in the hybrid and maximum-common-substructure (MCS) distance.
%   - MCS distance: $d_{\mathrm{MCS}}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{m_{ab}}{m_a + m_b - m_{ab}}$, where $m_{ab}$ is the number of atoms in the MCS (rings matched only to complete rings) and $m_a$, $m_b$ the atom counts.
% - Prediction as a correlation: the same as prediction but scored as Spearman (regression) or $2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$ (classification), so it sits on the same scale as blends.
% - Blend: a weighted mean of component scores, all in $[-1, 1]$: $J = \sum_c \alpha_c J_c$, with $\sum_c \alpha_c = 1$.
% - An objective that correlated pairwise distance with pairwise activity difference was tried and removed; it is not available.
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (why similarity in fixed representations fails); Chapter 6 (Hybrid Representations for Similarity and Label Noise).
% Axis 8, augmentation (a separate run per setting):
% - Purpose: small feature sets (for example MACCS keys) that cannot compete as whole representations get a separate route into the hybrid. Already in prose: the motivation (Chapter 5 "Augmentation").
% - Settings: none; uncapped (extra features appended, total can exceed $B$); capped (extra features replace the base's weakest, total stays $\le B$).
% - Candidate ranking (default): absolute correlation between each candidate feature and the out-of-fold residuals of a 50-tree random forest (depth 10) fitted on the base hybrid. Residuals come from a grouped 2- or 3-fold split, so a molecule's scaffold-mates are not used to predict it.
% - The top 50 candidates are kept. Block sizes $k \in \{0, 5, 10, 20, 40\}$ (clipped to the number available) are each scored on the internal validation split. $k=0$ always takes part and wins ties.
% - Capped: the base features dropped are those with the lowest random-forest importance on the base hybrid.
% - Alternative candidate rankings exist (random-forest importance, LightGBM gain, mutual information, DeepLIFT, DeepLIFT on residuals). Not the default.
% - No admission test is applied beyond the block comparison. An earlier test (near-copy and shuffled-label checks) was removed.
% - Why it matters: ranking against residuals asks what the base hybrid cannot already explain, rather than what predicts the label on its own.
% Size of the search:
% - Configurations per (downstream model, budget), full: $|\text{allocation}| \times |\text{importance}| \times |\text{evaluator}| \times |N| = 5 \times 3 \times 4 \times 4 = 240$.
% - Fast: $3 \times 1 \times 4 \times 2 = 24$.
% - Per fold, full: $5 \text{ models} \times 8 \text{ budgets} \times 240 = 9{,}600$ configurations. Fast: $3 \times 8 \times 24 = 576$.
% - TODO(check): confirm the counts after duplicates (for example "match" equals random forest when the downstream model is random forest), then multiply by folds, objectives and augmentation settings to give the total number of fits.
% - A fast search is its own search, stored separately. It is not a subset that can later be extended to a full one.
% Splitting inside the search:
% - Outer: 5 folds, grouped (scaffold, sequence cluster, or another dataset-specific grouping); classification folds are also stratified by class. Never a random split.
% - Inner: inside each outer training fold, the first fold of a grouped 5-fold split gives a 20% internal validation set. The standalone scores and the augmentation blocks are measured on it.
% - Median imputation and standardisation use training-fold statistics only. Already in prose (Chapter 5 "From base representations to a hybrid").
% - Links to: Appendix A (groups per dataset).
% Adaptive pruning (off unless switched on):
% - TODO(check): state whether the full thesis runs used pruning.
% - Every configuration is scored on the first two outer folds before anything is cut.
% - At budget 100: within each (model, evaluator, $N$) group, the worse half of (allocation, importance) pairs is dropped for higher budgets. At least one configuration per model is always kept.
% - Then one vote per axis. A value scores a point in a group if it is among the best $t$ in that group. A value is dropped for the remaining folds if it scores in fewer than a share $\tau$ of groups:
%   - allocation: $t=2$, $\tau=0.20$
%   - importance: $t=2$, $\tau=0.20$
%   - number of representations: $t=1$, $\tau=0.10$
%   - internal evaluator: $t=3$, $\tau=0.10$
% - A vote can never remove every value of an axis.
% - Purpose: pruning drops options this dataset has already shown will lose. A cut-off is judged only on whether it ever removes a winner. Saving time is a side effect.
% - DIAGRAM: funnel. All configurations on folds 1 and 2, then the budget-100 cut, then the four per-axis votes, then the survivors on folds 3 to 5. Label each stage with its rule.
% Blueprint fields (what is saved for each winning configuration):
% - The recipe: the list of representations searched; the allocation method; the importance method; the budget; the internal evaluator; the number of representations used $N$; the objective; the metric and whether lower is better; the task type; the number of folds; the random seed.
% - Augmentation settings: strategy (none, uncapped, capped); candidate ranking rule; candidate list length (50); optional cap per source; margin a block must clear over $k=0$; optional fraction of the budget used instead of the block sizes.
% - Data recorded alongside the recipe (not part of the recipe):
%   - each representation's standalone score from the search
%   - the number of features each representation got, per fold ($b_k$ per fold)
%   - the extra features kept by augmentation, per fold
% - Rebuilding from a blueprint re-runs the selection on the molecules given, so it needs labels. On new molecules it can choose different features.
% - To apply a hybrid to unlabelled molecules, reuse the features chosen by a labelled fit.
% - Why it matters: the blueprint is what makes a hybrid readable. It says which representations and which named features (for example descriptors, substructure keys) were chosen. Links to: Chapter 5 "Blueprints" and "Results: blueprints on specific datasets".
% - TODO(results): per-dataset table of the winning blueprint (model, budget, allocation, importance, evaluator, $N$); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): per-axis count of how often each value wins, by dataset (with Table C.1 or in Appendix D); numbers come from the full runs.
% ---- END NOTES C ----

%      to 2,000, per-model capped); allocation methods (greedy, performance-weighted with its

% [NI 629-640] her NoiseInject availability statement + the Springer-style itemised
% block the outline asks for. Two more such blocks are needed (precompute pipeline, KIRBy).
The NoiseInject benchmarking framework, implementing all six regression and six classification noise injection strategies together with the robustness metrics (noise degradation slope and retention) and uncertainty-calibration metrics (ECE, coverage at $1\sigma$/$2\sigma$, mean interval width, and uncertainty--error / uncertainty--noise correlation) described in this work, is available as an open-source Python package under an MIT license \citep{noiseinject}. It also contains optional reference wrappers (a Gaussian process, split-conformal prediction, and Monte Carlo dropout) that produce per-sample uncertainty for use with these metrics. NoiseInject is model-agnostic, operates on label arrays (NumPy arrays or pandas Series), and is designed to integrate into existing cheminformatics or other data pipelines. Worked examples covering molecular-descriptor, fingerprint, and GNN-embedding workflows are provided in the repository.

\begin{itemize}
    \item Project name: NoiseInject
    \item Project home page: \url{https://github.com/adpunt/noise_inject}
    \item Archived version: \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20532710}
    \item Operating system(s): Platform independent
    \item Programming language: Python
    \item Other requirements: See repository \texttt{requirements.txt} for the full dependency list
    \item License: MIT
    \item Any restrictions to use by non-academics: None
\end{itemize}

% [NI 654] authors' contributions, for the front-matter declaration.
A.P. implemented the NoiseInject framework, conducted all experiments, and drafted the manuscript. T.H., S.W., and G.M. supervised the project, contributed to the experimental design, and revised the manuscript. All authors read and approved the final manuscript.
